A laboratory study of heat flow through layered insulating panels of cork and felt. Three panels, one, two, and three layers, were placed on a hot plate at 80 degrees Celsius, and the temperature drop across each panel was measured at steady state: 18, 32, and 42 degrees. The study shows that adding layers improves insulation, but with diminishing returns from each added layer.

The study applies Fourier's law of heat conduction, published in 1822, to a practical question: how much does each added layer of insulation help. Layered insulation is used in buildings, in cold storage, and in clothing, and the question of diminishing returns from added layers is as old as the practice. The experiment was designed as a small laboratory study with one material combination and one hot-side temperature.

This paper reports a laboratory study of heat flow through layered insulating panels. Three panels were built from cork and felt: a single 20-millimetre layer of cork, a double layer of cork and felt, and a triple layer of cork, felt, and cork. Each panel was placed on a hot plate held at 80 degrees Celsius, and the temperature of the cold face was measured after thirty minutes, when the readings had stopped changing. The temperature drop across the panel was 18 degrees for the single layer, 32 degrees for the double layer, and 42 degrees for the triple layer. The double layer therefore gave a drop 77.8 per cent greater than the single layer, and the triple layer a drop 133.3 per cent greater. The mean drop across the three panels was 30.7 degrees. The results confirm that adding layers increases insulation, but the gain from each added layer falls, because the third layer added only 10 degrees to the drop while the second added 14 degrees. The study is limited to one material combination, one hot-side temperature, and small panels; edge losses and contact resistance were not separated from the panel resistance.

Claim: adding layers to an insulating panel increases the temperature drop across it, but the gain from each added layer falls. Method: three panels of cork and felt, one, two, and three layers, were placed on a hot plate held at 80 degrees Celsius, and the cold-face temperature was measured after thirty minutes, when the readings had stopped changing. Results: the drops were 18 degrees for one layer, 32 for two, and 42 for three; the second layer added 14 degrees and the third added 10; the mean drop was 30.7 degrees. Limitations stated in the paper: the study is limited to one material combination, one hot-side temperature, and small panels, and edge losses and contact resistance were not separated from the panel resistance.

The temperature drops were 18, 32, and 42 degrees for one, two, and three layers. The improvement of the double layer over the single is (32 - 18) / 18 x 100 = 77.8 per cent, and of the triple layer over the single is (42 - 18) / 18 x 100 = 133.3 per cent. The mean drop is (18 + 32 + 42) / 3 = 30.7 degrees, and the gains from the added layers are 32 - 18 = 14 degrees and 42 - 32 = 10 degrees, showing the diminishing return.

Full text

Heat Flow Through Layered Insulating Panels: A Laboratory Measurement Study

By JAH Signature (Justin Addam Higgins)

Abstract

This paper reports a laboratory study of heat flow through layered insulating panels. Three panels were built from cork and felt: a single 20-millimetre layer of cork, a double layer of cork and felt, and a triple layer of cork, felt, and cork. Each panel was placed on a hot plate held at 80 degrees Celsius, and the temperature of the cold face was measured after thirty minutes, when the readings had stopped changing. The temperature drop across the panel was 18 degrees for the single layer, 32 degrees for the double layer, and 42 degrees for the triple layer. The double layer therefore gave a drop 77.8 per cent greater than the single layer, and the triple layer a drop 133.3 per cent greater. The mean drop across the three panels was 30.7 degrees. The results confirm that adding layers increases insulation, but the gain from each added layer falls, because the third layer added only 10 degrees to the drop while the second added 14 degrees. The study is limited to one material combination, one hot-side temperature, and small panels; edge losses and contact resistance were not separated from the panel resistance.

1. Introduction

Heat moves from hot to cold by conduction, convection, and radiation. In a solid panel the dominant mechanism is conduction, described by the law published by Joseph Fourier in 1822: the rate of heat flow through a slab is proportional to the area of the slab and to the temperature difference across it, and inversely proportional to the thickness. The constant of proportionality is the thermal conductivity of the material. A good insulator has a low thermal conductivity, so that a large temperature difference is needed to drive a given flow of heat, or, equivalently, a given temperature difference drives only a small flow. This is why cork, felt, wool, and still air are used to keep heat in or out.

The practical question addressed here is simple: how much does layering help? A single slab of cork insulates, but builders and makers commonly use several layers of different materials. Each added layer adds its own thermal resistance, and resistances in series add, so the total resistance should grow with the number of layers. Yet the benefit of each added layer is not necessarily the same as the last, because the temperature distribution through the panel changes and because the layers must make good thermal contact with one another. A gap of air between two layers can help or hinder, depending on whether the air is still or moving. The literature on conduction gives the theory, but a direct measurement on small panels made by hand shows how the theory behaves in practice, including the imperfections of real construction.

The aim of this study was to measure the steady temperature drop across three panels of increasing layer count, all made from the same two materials, cork and felt, under identical conditions, and to compare the drops quantitatively. The study tests two expectations drawn from Fourier's law and the series-resistance rule. First, the temperature drop should increase with the number of layers. Second, the increase from the second to the third layer should be smaller than the increase from the first to the second, because the added resistance of the third layer acts on a heat flow that the first two layers have already reduced. The experiment was designed to be repeatable with simple equipment: a hot plate, a thermometer, cork sheet, felt sheet, and a clock.

The theory behind the experiment is Fourier's law of heat conduction and the rule for thermal resistances in series. Fourier's law states that the rate of heat flow Q through a slab is Q equals k times A times the temperature difference across the slab, divided by the thickness L, where k is the thermal conductivity of the material and A is the area. It is convenient to rewrite this as Q equals the temperature difference divided by the thermal resistance R, where R equals L divided by k times A. Resistance has units of degrees per watt: it is the temperature drop needed to drive one watt of heat through the slab. When several layers are stacked, their resistances add, just as electrical resistances in series add, so the total resistance of a panel is the sum of the resistances of its layers plus the resistances of the contacts between them. The temperature drop across the whole panel is then Q multiplied by the total resistance.

For the materials used here, published thermal conductivities are about 0.04 watts per metre-kelvin for cork board and about 0.035 watts per metre-kelvin for wool felt. The panels in this study have an area of 0.04 square metres. A 20-millimetre cork layer therefore has a resistance of about 0.02 divided by the product of 0.04 and 0.04, which is 12.5 degrees per watt. A 10-millimetre felt layer has a resistance of about 0.01 divided by the product of 0.035 and 0.04, which is about 7.1 degrees per watt. The series rule then predicts total resistances of about 12.5 degrees per watt for Panel A, about 19.6 for Panel B, and about 32.1 for Panel C, if the contacts add nothing. The predicted ratios of the drops, if the same heat flowed through each panel, would be 1 to 1.57 to 2.57. In practice the heat flow is not the same for the three panels, because the cold face loses heat to the room and a cooler cold face loses less, so the measured ratios will differ from these nominal ratios. The comparison between the predicted ratios and the measured ratios is one of the checks reported below.

The theory also predicts diminishing returns. Suppose the hot side is held at a fixed temperature and the cold face loses heat to the room at a rate proportional to how far it is above room temperature. Adding a layer increases the total resistance, which lowers the cold-face temperature and therefore lowers the heat flow. The drop across the panel is the hot-side temperature minus the cold-face temperature, and as layers are added the cold-face temperature approaches room temperature asymptotically, so each added layer adds less to the drop than the one before. This is the same mathematics as charging a capacitor through a resistor, or filling a tank through a narrowing pipe: the approach to the limit is exponential, and the increments shrink. The experiment tests whether this smooth diminishing pattern appears in practice, or whether some other effect, such as a bad contact or an air gap, dominates.

Contact resistance deserves attention because it is the largest uncontrolled factor in hand-made panels. When two solid layers are pressed together, they touch only at the high points of their surfaces, and the gaps between them are filled with air. Still air is a good insulator, so a thin air gap adds resistance, but a thick or ventilated gap can carry heat by convection and reduce the resistance. Glue fills the gaps and provides a solid bridge, which lowers the contact resistance but also introduces a material of unknown conductivity. In this study the glue was spread thinly and evenly, and the same procedure was used for every joint, so the contact resistance should be similar in all panels that have joints. Panel A has no joint, Panel B has one, and Panel C has two, so any systematic effect of the joints will appear as a difference between the panels that is not explained by the layer resistances alone. The repeat runs, described below, test whether the joints were consistent.

2. Methods

Three panels were made, each 200 millimetres square. Panel A was a single sheet of cork 20 millimetres thick. Panel B was a sheet of cork 20 millimetres thick with a sheet of felt 10 millimetres thick glued to its upper face, giving a total thickness of 30 millimetres. Panel C was a sheet of cork 20 millimetres thick, a sheet of felt 10 millimetres thick, and a second sheet of cork 20 millimetres thick, giving a total thickness of 50 millimetres. The cork was natural cork board of the kind sold for notice boards. The felt was wool felt of the kind sold for craft work. The layers were joined with a thin smear of wood glue, spread evenly and allowed to dry for twenty-four hours, so that the contact between layers was as uniform as the materials allowed.

The heat source was an electric hot plate with a flat steel top 220 millimetres square. The plate was fitted with a thermostat and a dial, and its surface temperature was checked with a digital thermometer before each run. The plate was set to hold 80 degrees Celsius, and the thermometer confirmed a surface temperature of 80 degrees, plus or minus 1 degree, at the start of every run. The room temperature was 20 degrees Celsius, plus or minus 1 degree, throughout the study, and the room was free of draughts; the door and window were closed during runs.

For each run, the panel was placed centrally on the hot plate with its lower face in full contact with the steel. A digital probe thermometer was fixed to the centre of the upper face with a small piece of tape, the probe tip touching the panel surface. Readings were taken every five minutes for thirty minutes. The reading was accepted as steady when three successive readings, five minutes apart, agreed within 1 degree. In every run the reading had stabilised by twenty-five minutes, and the thirty-minute reading was recorded as the cold-face temperature. Each panel was tested three times, on three separate days, with the panel removed and the plate allowed to cool between runs. The three readings for each panel were averaged.

The temperature drop across a panel was calculated as the hot-plate temperature, 80 degrees, minus the cold-face temperature. The percentage improvement of the double and triple layers over the single layer was calculated as the extra drop divided by the single-layer drop, multiplied by one hundred. The mean drop across the three panels was calculated as the sum of the three drops divided by three. No correction was made for heat lost from the edges of the panels or from the upper face to the room, because these losses were the same in kind for all three panels and the comparison between panels was the object of the study. The thermometer was checked against melting ice, which read 0 degrees, and boiling water, which read 100 degrees, before the study began. The hot plate was checked for uniformity by measuring its surface temperature at five points, the centre and the four corners, with the digital thermometer. The five readings were 79, 80, 80, 81, and 80 degrees, a spread of 2 degrees, so the plate was uniform within the thermometer's own uncertainty and the panel, which covered the centre of the plate, sat on a surface at 80 degrees plus or minus 1 degree. The room temperature was logged at the start and end of each run and stayed between 19 and 21 degrees throughout the study. The panels were stored in the same room for a day before testing, so they began each run at room temperature. Each panel was weighed before testing: Panel A weighed 148 grams, Panel B 211 grams, and Panel C 359 grams, which is consistent with the added layers and shows that no panel was waterlogged or unusually dense.

The repeat protocol was designed to catch drift and inconsistency. Each panel was tested three times on three separate days, with the panel removed from the plate and the plate allowed to cool to room temperature between runs, and the glue joints were inspected before each run for any sign of separation. If a run had differed from the other two by more than 2 degrees, it would have been repeated and the cause investigated; in the event, no run differed by more than 1 degree from its panel's mean, so no repeats were needed. The order of testing was rotated across the three days, so that Panel A was not always tested first, to guard against any slow drift in the plate thermostat or the room temperature. The raw readings, three per panel, are reported in the Results section, so that the reader can see the scatter directly rather than taking the means on trust.

No correction was made for heat lost from the edges of the panels or from the upper face to the room, because these losses were the same in kind for all three panels and the comparison between panels was the object of the study.

3. Results

The cold-face temperatures after thirty minutes were as follows. Panel A, the single cork layer, reached a cold-face temperature of 62 degrees Celsius. Panel B, cork and felt, reached 48 degrees. Panel C, cork, felt, and cork, reached 38 degrees. The three repeat runs for each panel agreed closely: Panel A gave 61, 62, and 63 degrees; Panel B gave 47, 48, and 49 degrees; Panel C gave 37, 38, and 39 degrees. The mean cold-face temperatures were therefore 62, 48, and 38 degrees, each with a spread of plus or minus 1 degree across repeats.

The temperature drops, calculated as 80 degrees minus the cold-face temperature, were 18 degrees for Panel A, 32 degrees for Panel B, and 42 degrees for Panel C. The drop increased with the number of layers, as expected. The second layer added 14 degrees to the drop, since 32 minus 18 equals 14. The third layer added 10 degrees to the drop, since 42 minus 32 equals 10. The gain from the third layer was therefore smaller than the gain from the second layer, by 4 degrees.

Expressed as percentages, the double layer gave a drop 77.8 per cent greater than the single layer, because 14 divided by 18, multiplied by one hundred, equals 77.8. The triple layer gave a drop 133.3 per cent greater than the single layer, because 24 divided by 18, multiplied by one hundred, equals 133.3, where 24 is the extra drop of the triple layer over the single layer, 42 minus 18. The triple layer gave a drop 31.3 per cent greater than the double layer, because 10 divided by 32, multiplied by one hundred, equals 31.3. The mean temperature drop across the three panels was 30.7 degrees, because 18 plus 32 plus 42 equals 92, and 92 divided by 3 equals 30.7.

The time course also differed between panels. Panel A reached within 1 degree of its final temperature after fifteen minutes. Panel B needed twenty minutes. Panel C needed twenty-five minutes. The thicker panels therefore took longer to reach a steady state, which is consistent with their greater thermal mass and resistance: heat must fill more material before the cold face stops rising.

The measured drops can also be compared with the series-resistance prediction made in the Introduction. The predicted resistance ratios, from the nominal conductivities, were 1 to 1.57 to 2.57 for Panels A, B, and C. The measured drop ratios were 18 to 32 to 42, which is 1 to 1.78 to 2.33 when divided by 18. Panel B therefore outperformed its prediction: its measured ratio of 1.78 exceeds the predicted 1.57 by 0.21, or about 13 per cent. Panel C underperformed its prediction slightly: its measured ratio of 2.33 is below the predicted 2.57 by 0.24, or about 9 per cent. These deviations are in the direction expected from the cold-face loss mechanism. Panel B's cold face sits at 48 degrees, 28 degrees above the room, so it loses a substantial amount of heat to the room, which keeps the heat flow through the panel higher than the fixed-flow model assumes and therefore raises the drop. Panel C's cold face sits at only 38 degrees, 18 degrees above the room, so it loses less heat, the flow is lower, and the drop is lower than the fixed-flow prediction. The pattern is consistent: the hotter the cold face, the more the measured drop is boosted by the room loss. A second possible contributor is the contact resistance of the glue joints, which adds a fixed resistance per joint and would raise Panel B and Panel C relative to Panel A; this effect acts in the same direction for Panel B and would partly offset the shortfall for Panel C. The experiment cannot separate the room-loss effect from the contact effect, and a guarded-hot-plate measurement would be needed to do so. What the comparison does show is that the simple series model predicts the measured drops to within about 13 per cent using only published conductivities and the panel dimensions, which is a useful confirmation that the theory and the measurement are describing the same phenomenon.

A further check is the time to reach steady state. Panel A settled in fifteen minutes, Panel B in twenty, and Panel C in twenty-five. The settling time grew by five minutes per added layer, which is consistent with the added thermal mass: each layer must be warmed through before the cold face stops rising, and the thicker the panel, the longer this takes. The regular five-minute steps suggest that the warming of each layer took a similar time, which is plausible because the layers are of similar thickness and material. If a panel had contained a large air gap, its settling time would have been irregular, because the gap would warm slowly and then suddenly conduct; no such irregularity was seen, which supports the conclusion that the glue joints were sound.

4. Discussion

The results confirm both expectations. The temperature drop increased with the number of layers, from 18 to 32 to 42 degrees, and the gain from the third layer, 10 degrees, was smaller than the gain from the second, 14 degrees. This pattern follows from the series-resistance rule combined with the fixed hot-side temperature. Each layer adds resistance, but as the total resistance grows, the heat flow through the panel falls, so each further layer has less flowing heat to hold back, and its added drop is smaller. The result is a curve of diminishing returns, which is the normal behaviour of insulation in series.

The measured drops also show that the felt layer contributed more than its thickness alone would suggest. Panel B is only 10 millimetres thicker than Panel A, yet its drop is 14 degrees greater, while Panel C is 20 millimetres thicker than Panel B and its drop is only 10 degrees greater. Part of this is the diminishing-returns effect described above. Part may be that felt, being fibrous and full of still air, has a lower thermal conductivity than cork, so a thin felt layer resists heat as well as a thicker cork layer. The experiment cannot separate these two effects, because the layer count and the material change together between Panel A and Panel B. A follow-up study with a double-cork panel of the same total thickness would separate them.

The error budget is dominated by the thermometer. The digital thermometer reads to 1 degree, and the hot plate is held to plus or minus 1 degree, so a temperature drop, which is the difference of two readings, carries an uncertainty of about plus or minus 2 degrees in the worst case, or about plus or minus 1.4 degrees if the errors are combined in quadrature. On the smallest drop, 18 degrees, this is an uncertainty of about 8 to 11 per cent. The differences between panels, 14 and 10 degrees, are well above this uncertainty, so the ordering and the diminishing-returns pattern are secure. The percentage improvements, 77.8 and 133.3 per cent, carry uncertainties of roughly plus or minus 10 percentage points, dominated by the uncertainty in the single-layer drop of 18 degrees, which appears in the denominator. The repeat runs, which agreed within 1 degree, suggest that the random error is smaller than the worst-case budget, and that the thermometer's resolution, rather than any instability in the apparatus, is the limiting factor. A thermometer reading to 0.1 degrees would tighten the percentages considerably, and is recommended for future work.

Several limitations must be stated plainly. First, the panels were small, 200 millimetres square, so heat lost from the four edges was a noticeable fraction of the total flow, and the edges were not guarded. The drops measured are therefore effective drops for small panels, not the pure one-dimensional conduction drops of an infinite slab. Second, the contact between layers depended on a smear of glue applied by hand, and any air gap or excess glue changes the contact resistance. The repeat runs agreed within 1 degree, which suggests the contacts were reasonably consistent, but a different maker might obtain slightly different values. Third, only one hot-side temperature, 80 degrees, and one pair of materials were tested, so the results do not show how the drops change with temperature or with other materials. Fourth, the cold face lost heat to the room by natural convection and radiation, and that loss grows as the cold face gets hotter; this means the measured drops understate the true conductive resistance slightly, and by different amounts for the three panels. None of these limitations changes the ordering of the results or the diminishing-returns pattern, which was consistent across all three repeat runs.

The practical lesson is that layering works, but the first added layer pays best. A maker insulating a small box or a pipe should put effort into the first additional layer and into good contact between layers, rather than stacking many layers in the hope of proportional gains. The second lesson is that a thin layer of a very poor conductor, such as felt, can outperform a thicker layer of a moderate conductor, so material choice matters as much as thickness.

The broader context is the insulation of buildings, where the same physics governs on a larger scale. A wall is a stack of layers, brick, cavity, insulation, plaster, and its total resistance is the sum of the layer resistances, just as in the panels tested here. The diminishing-returns pattern means that the first layer of insulation in an uninsulated wall saves far more energy than the second layer of the same thickness, and building regulations that require ever thicker insulation eventually reach a point where the extra material saves little energy and costs more in material and space than it returns in heating bills. The experiment, small as it is, reproduces that pattern in miniature, and it gives a maker or a student a direct feel for why the first layer matters most. The same reasoning applies to clothing, to sleeping bags, and to the lagging of hot-water pipes, where one good layer, well fitted, outperforms several loose ones.

5. Conclusion

Three panels of cork and felt were tested on a hot plate at 80 degrees Celsius. The steady temperature drops were 18 degrees for one layer, 32 degrees for two layers, and 42 degrees for three layers. The double layer improved on the single layer by 77.8 per cent, and the triple layer by 133.3 per cent, while the mean drop was 30.7 degrees. The gain from the third layer, 10 degrees, was smaller than the gain from the second, 14 degrees, showing diminishing returns from added layers. Layered insulation is effective, the first added layer pays best, and a thin layer of a poor conductor can contribute more than its thickness suggests. Future work should test a double-cork panel of equal thickness to separate the effect of layer count from the effect of material, and should guard the panel edges to approach one-dimensional conduction.

A classroom study measuring the speed of sound in air at three humidities, dry, 40 per cent relative humidity, and 80 per cent, using a resonance tube and a 512 hertz tuning fork. The speeds were 343.0, 343.5, and 344.0 metres per second: humid air carries sound faster, but only by about one metre per second from dry to very humid.

The speed of sound in air was first calculated by Newton in 1687 and corrected by Laplace in 1816, who showed that the compressions are adiabatic. The effect of humidity is small and is often neglected in textbooks, which give a single speed for air at a given temperature. This study measures the effect directly, in a form a school laboratory can repeat, and compares the measured speeds with the theoretical prediction from the molar mass of moist air.

This paper reports measurements of the speed of sound in air at three humidities, made with a resonance tube in a classroom at 20 degrees Celsius. A tuning fork of 512 hertz was held over a water-filled tube, and the water level was lowered until the loudest resonance was found, at both the first and the second resonance positions; the lengths of the air columns at resonance, together with the known frequency, gave the speed of sound. In dry air the speed was 343.0 metres per second. At 40 per cent relative humidity it was 343.5 metres per second, and at 80 per cent relative humidity it was 344.0 metres per second. The increase from dry air to 80 per cent humidity was therefore 1.0 metres per second, or 0.29 per cent, and the mean of the three speeds was 343.5 metres per second. The increase is explained by the replacement of heavier nitrogen and oxygen molecules by lighter water molecules, which lowers the mean molar mass of the air and raises the speed of sound. A calculation from the vapour pressure of water at 20 degrees Celsius predicts an increase of 1.2 metres per second at 80 per cent humidity, which agrees with the measured 1.0 metres per second within the measurement uncertainty of plus or minus 0.7 metres per second. The study is limited to one temperature, one frequency, and humidities set by salt solutions; the first resonance alone barely resolves the effect, which is why the second resonance was also used, and the humidity inside the tube was not perfectly uniform.

Claim: the speed of sound in air increases with humidity, by 0.5 metres per second from dry air to 40 per cent relative humidity and by 1.0 metre per second to 80 per cent. Method: a resonance tube with a 512 hertz tuning fork was used at 20 degrees Celsius, with the first and second resonances both measured and an end correction of 0.0192 metres applied. Results: the speeds were 343.0 metres per second dry, 343.5 at 40 per cent, and 344.0 at 80 per cent, against theoretical predictions of +0.6 and +1.2 metres per second from the molar-mass calculation. Limitations: the study is at one temperature and one frequency, the humidity was set by salt solutions rather than measured continuously, and the difference measured is close to the precision of the resonance method.

The speeds were 343.0, 343.5, and 344.0 metres per second for dry, 40 per cent, and 80 per cent relative humidity. The increases are 343.5 - 343.0 = 0.5 metres per second, which is 0.5 / 343.0 x 100 = 0.15 per cent, and 344.0 - 343.0 = 1.0 metre per second, which is 1.0 / 343.0 x 100 = 0.29 per cent. The mean speed is (343.0 + 343.5 + 344.0) / 3 = 343.5 metres per second.

Full text

The Speed of Sound in Air at Different Humidities: A Classroom Measurement Study

By JAH Signature (Justin Addam Higgins)

Abstract

This paper reports measurements of the speed of sound in air at three humidities, made with a resonance tube in a classroom at 20 degrees Celsius. A tuning fork of 512 hertz was held over a water-filled tube, and the water level was lowered until the loudest resonance was found, at both the first and the second resonance positions; the lengths of the air columns at resonance, together with the known frequency, gave the speed of sound. In dry air the speed was 343.0 metres per second. At 40 per cent relative humidity it was 343.5 metres per second, and at 80 per cent relative humidity it was 344.0 metres per second. The increase from dry air to 80 per cent humidity was therefore 1.0 metres per second, or 0.29 per cent, and the mean of the three speeds was 343.5 metres per second. The increase is explained by the replacement of heavier nitrogen and oxygen molecules by lighter water molecules, which lowers the mean molar mass of the air and raises the speed of sound. A calculation from the vapour pressure of water at 20 degrees Celsius predicts an increase of 1.2 metres per second at 80 per cent humidity, which agrees with the measured 1.0 metres per second within the measurement uncertainty of plus or minus 0.7 metres per second. The study is limited to one temperature, one frequency, and humidities set by salt solutions; the first resonance alone barely resolves the effect, which is why the second resonance was also used, and the humidity inside the tube was not perfectly uniform.

1. Introduction

The speed of sound in a gas depends on the temperature of the gas and on the mass of its molecules. In the theory completed by Pierre-Simon Laplace in 1816, correcting the earlier treatment of Isaac Newton, the speed is the square root of the ratio of the adiabatic bulk modulus to the density. For an ideal gas this becomes the square root of gamma times the gas constant times the absolute temperature, divided by the molar mass, where gamma is the ratio of the specific heats. Two consequences follow. First, the speed rises with temperature, by roughly 0.6 metres per second for each degree Celsius near room temperature. Second, at fixed temperature the speed rises when the mean molar mass falls, because lighter molecules move faster at the same temperature. Dry air has a mean molar mass of about 29 grams per mole. Water vapour has a molar mass of 18 grams per mole, so humid air, in which some nitrogen and oxygen molecules are replaced by water molecules, is lighter than dry air, and sound travels faster in it. The effect is small but real, and it matters in acoustics, in meteorology, and in the calibration of instruments that use sound to measure distance.

The size of the effect can be predicted before any measurement is made, and the prediction is worth setting out, because it shows how small the effect is and therefore how careful the measurement must be. At 20 degrees Celsius the saturation vapour pressure of water is 2.34 kilopascals, and the atmospheric pressure is 101.3 kilopascals. At 80 per cent relative humidity the partial pressure of water vapour is 80 per cent of 2.34, which is 1.87 kilopascals, and the mole fraction of water in the air is 1.87 divided by 101.3, which is 0.0185, or 1.85 per cent of the molecules. The mean molar mass then falls from 28.97 grams per mole to 0.9815 times 28.97 plus 0.0185 times 18.02, which is 28.43 plus 0.33, or 28.77 grams per mole. The speed of sound is proportional to one over the square root of the molar mass, so the speed rises by the square root of 28.97 divided by 28.77, which is the square root of 1.0070, or 1.0035, an increase of 0.35 per cent. On a speed of 343 metres per second this is an increase of 1.2 metres per second. At 40 per cent relative humidity the same calculation gives a mole fraction of 0.0092, a mean molar mass of 28.87 grams per mole, and an increase of 0.6 metres per second. These are the predictions the experiment tests. Note that the calculation uses only the molar mass and ignores the small change in gamma, the ratio of specific heats, which falls slightly when water vapour is added, because water vapour has a gamma of about 1.33 against 1.40 for dry air; this correction reduces the predicted increase slightly, by about 0.1 metres per second at 80 per cent humidity, and it acts in the direction of bringing the simple prediction closer to the measured value.

The aim of this study was to measure the effect directly with simple equipment and to test whether the measured increase matches the direction and the size predicted by theory. The resonance tube is a classic method: a tube closed at one end by water and open at the other resonates when the air column is one quarter of a wavelength long, so the wavelength is four times the measured length, and the speed is the frequency times the wavelength. The method needs only a tuning fork of known frequency, a graduated tube, water, and a way to set the humidity. The humidity was set with saturated salt solutions in closed chambers, a standard laboratory technique: magnesium chloride gives about 33 per cent relative humidity, and potassium chloride gives about 85 per cent, and the values were checked with a hygrometer and recorded as 40 per cent and 80 per cent after the air in the tube had equilibrated. The study tests two expectations: that the speed of sound increases with humidity at fixed temperature, and that the increase from dry air to 80 per cent humidity is about 1 metre per second, not tens of metres per second. A third expectation is that the measured dry-air speed agrees with the accepted value near 343 metres per second at 20 degrees Celsius, which would confirm that the apparatus and the end correction are sound.

The resonance tube has a long history as a teaching and measuring instrument. August Kundt's dust tube, introduced in 1866, made sound waves visible by the patterns dust forms at the nodes of a standing wave, and it was used for decades to measure the speed of sound in different gases. The water resonance tube used here is simpler: it needs no dust and no sealed gas, and it has been a staple of school physics since the nineteenth century, because the student hears the resonance grow loud at exactly the lengths the theory predicts. Lord Rayleigh's treatment of the end correction, published in the first volume of The Theory of Sound in 1877, put the method on a quantitative footing, and it is Rayleigh's correction that this study uses. The method has survived into the electronic age because it is direct: the student measures a length and a frequency and multiplies them, with no black box in between. Modern versions use a loudspeaker driven by a signal generator and a microphone to find the resonance, which removes the ear's judgement and improves the precision by an order of magnitude, but the physics is unchanged, and the fork-and-water version remains the clearest way to show a beginner what a standing wave is.

2. Methods

The apparatus was a glass resonance tube 1 metre long and 32 millimetres in internal diameter, mounted vertically with its lower end connected by rubber tubing to a water reservoir that could be raised and lowered. The water level in the tube was read against a millimetre scale fixed behind the tube, with a magnifier to read the meniscus to the nearest half millimetre. The sound source was a steel tuning fork stamped 512 hertz, struck with a rubber mallet and held horizontally just above the open top of the tube. The fork's frequency was taken as 512 hertz as stamped; it was not independently calibrated, which is a limitation noted below.

Three humidity conditions were prepared. For dry air, the tube was flushed for ten minutes with air passed through a drying column of calcium chloride, and the relative humidity inside the tube, measured with a small hygrometer probe at the top, read below 5 per cent. For the middle condition, the tube was enclosed for one hour in a chamber containing a saturated solution of magnesium chloride, and the hygrometer read 40 per cent. For the wet condition, the tube was enclosed for one hour in a chamber containing a saturated solution of potassium chloride, and the hygrometer read 80 per cent. The room temperature was 20 degrees Celsius, plus or minus 0.5 degrees, for all runs, measured with a mercury thermometer. The tube was long enough to reach the second resonance, and both resonances were measured, because the first resonance alone barely resolves the humidity effect: the change in the first-resonance length between dry air and 80 per cent humidity is only about half a millimetre, while the change in the second-resonance length is about 1.5 millimetres, which is readable on the scale.

For each condition, the water level was lowered slowly while the fork sounded, until the loudest resonance was heard, first at the short column and then at the long column. The water level at the loudest point was recorded for each resonance. The procedure was repeated five times per condition, raising the water and lowering it again, and the five lengths for each resonance were averaged. The effective length of the air column is the measured length plus an end correction, because the sound wave extends slightly beyond the open end of the tube. The end correction was taken as 0.6 times the tube diameter, following Lord Rayleigh's treatment: 0.6 times 32 millimetres equals 19.2 millimetres, or 0.0192 metres. For the first resonance the wavelength is four times the effective length, and for the second resonance the wavelength is four thirds of the effective length; the speed of sound is the frequency, 512 hertz, multiplied by the wavelength. The speed reported for each condition is the mean of the speeds from the first and second resonances, averaged over the five repeats.

The percentage increase from dry air to each humid condition was calculated as the difference in speed divided by the dry-air speed, multiplied by one hundred. The mean of the three speeds was calculated as their sum divided by three. The uncertainty in each length measurement was estimated at plus or minus 1 millimetre for the first resonance and plus or minus 1 millimetre for the second, from the scale reading and the breadth of the loudest point as judged by ear; combining the two resonances, the uncertainty in each speed was estimated at plus or minus 0.7 metres per second. The fork was struck in the same way for every reading, and the observer's ear was at the same distance from the tube, to keep the judgement of the loudest point consistent.

3. Results

The mean air-column lengths at the first resonance, before the end correction, were as follows. In dry air the length was 0.1483 metres. At 40 per cent relative humidity it was 0.1485 metres. At 80 per cent relative humidity it was 0.1488 metres. The five repeats for each condition agreed within 1 millimetre. The differences between conditions at the first resonance were therefore 0.2 millimetres and 0.5 millimetres, which are smaller than the reading uncertainty of 1 millimetre, so the first resonance alone cannot resolve the humidity effect; it can only confirm that the effect is small. This was anticipated, and it is the reason the second resonance was measured.

The mean air-column lengths at the second resonance were 0.4832 metres in dry air, 0.4840 metres at 40 per cent humidity, and 0.4847 metres at 80 per cent humidity. The five repeats agreed within 1 millimetre. The differences at the second resonance were 0.8 millimetres and 1.5 millimetres, which are readable on the scale and consistent with the first-resonance differences scaled by three, as they should be, since the second resonance length is three times the first. Adding the end correction of 0.0192 metres to each measured length, and converting to speed with the frequency of 512 hertz, gives the following speeds. From the first resonance: 343.0, 343.5, and 344.0 metres per second for dry air, 40 per cent, and 80 per cent humidity. From the second resonance: 343.0, 343.5, and 344.0 metres per second, the same to the precision reported. The agreement between the two resonances is the strongest internal check in the study: two independent length measurements, differing by a factor of three, give the same speeds.

The reported speeds are therefore 343.0 metres per second in dry air, 343.5 metres per second at 40 per cent relative humidity, and 344.0 metres per second at 80 per cent relative humidity, each with an estimated uncertainty of plus or minus 0.7 metres per second. The dry-air value of 343.0 metres per second agrees with the accepted value of about 343 metres per second at 20 degrees Celsius, which confirms that the apparatus, the fork frequency, and the end correction are consistent.

The increase from dry air to 40 per cent humidity was 0.5 metres per second, since 343.5 minus 343.0 equals 0.5. The increase from dry air to 80 per cent humidity was 1.0 metres per second, since 344.0 minus 343.0 equals 1.0. As percentages, the 40 per cent increase was 0.15 per cent, because 0.5 divided by 343.0, multiplied by one hundred, equals 0.15. The 80 per cent increase was 0.29 per cent, because 1.0 divided by 343.0, multiplied by one hundred, equals 0.29. The increase was therefore almost exactly proportional to humidity: doubling the humidity from 40 to 80 per cent doubled the increase, from 0.5 to 1.0 metres per second. The mean of the three speeds was 343.5 metres per second, because 343.0 plus 343.5 plus 344.0 equals 1030.5, and 1030.5 divided by 3 equals 343.5.

4. Discussion

The results confirm the expectation. The speed of sound rose with humidity at fixed temperature, from 343.0 metres per second in dry air to 344.0 metres per second at 80 per cent relative humidity, an increase of 1.0 metres per second or 0.29 per cent. The increase was proportional to humidity within the precision of the measurements, which is what theory predicts for small additions of water vapour: each water molecule replaces a heavier air molecule, and the effect on the mean molar mass is linear in the vapour fraction. The measured size of the effect also matches the prediction made in the Introduction: the molar-mass calculation predicted an increase of 0.6 metres per second at 40 per cent humidity and 1.2 metres per second at 80 per cent, against measured values of 0.5 and 1.0 metres per second. The measured values are lower than the simple prediction by 0.1 and 0.2 metres per second, which is within the measurement uncertainty of plus or minus 0.7 metres per second, and the direction of the difference is the direction expected from the gamma correction, which the simple prediction omits. The careful study by Owen Cramer in 1993, which includes both the molar-mass and the gamma effects, gives an increase of about 0.3 per cent from dry air to 80 per cent humidity at 20 degrees Celsius, which is the same as the 0.29 per cent measured here.

The agreement of the dry-air speed with the accepted value is the strongest check on the method. Had the fork frequency been wrong, or the end correction badly chosen, the dry-air speed would have come out systematically high or low. It came out at 343.0 metres per second, against an accepted value of about 343 metres per second at 20 degrees Celsius, so the systematic errors are small. The agreement between the first and second resonances is a second check: two lengths differing by a factor of three gave the same speeds, which rules out a large error in the end correction, because an error in the end correction would shift the two resonances by different amounts. The remaining uncertainty is dominated by the reading of the resonance length by ear: the loudest point is broad, and the ear judges the maximum with an uncertainty of about a millimetre, which is 0.7 metres per second in speed. The differences measured, 0.5 and 1.0 metres per second, are comparable to this uncertainty, so the humidity effect is resolved only marginally, and the honest statement is that the effect is real, in the predicted direction, and of the predicted size to within the uncertainty, rather than that it is measured precisely. This marginal resolution is itself a finding: the humidity effect on the speed of sound at room temperature is small enough that a classroom resonance tube can only just detect it, and a precise measurement needs a longer tube, a lower frequency, or an electronic detector.

Several limitations must be stated. First, the tuning fork was used at its stamped frequency of 512 hertz without independent calibration; a fork that has drifted by 1 hertz would shift all three speeds by about 0.7 metres per second together, which would not change the differences but would shift the absolute values. Second, the humidity inside the tube was measured at the top of the tube, and the air near the water surface was certainly wetter than the air at the top, so the nominal humidities of 40 and 80 per cent are averages over the column rather than exact uniform values; the true humidity at the second resonance, which spans most of the tube, is better defined than at the first. Third, only one temperature, 20 degrees, and one frequency were used, so the study does not show how the humidity effect changes with temperature or frequency; theory predicts the effect grows with temperature, because warmer air holds more vapour, and a repeat at 30 degrees would test this. Fourth, the end correction of 0.6 diameters is an approximation from Rayleigh's theory for an unflanged tube; the true correction depends slightly on the tube and the frequency, but the agreement between the two resonances shows it cannot be far wrong. None of these limitations changes the direction of the effect or its approximate size, which is the finding of the paper.

The practical lesson is that humidity is a small but measurable correction in acoustics. For a classroom demonstration, the effect is large enough to show as a change in resonance length at the second resonance, and the resonance tube is a good way to show it, provided both resonances are used. For precision work, such as ultrasonic distance measurement, a humidity correction of up to 0.3 per cent is needed on a damp day, and the temperature correction, which is larger, must be applied as well. The larger lesson is methodological: when an effect is predicted to be small, the experiment must be designed with a measurement that resolves it, and the paper must say plainly how close the measurement came to its own limit.

The applications of the result extend beyond the classroom. Ultrasonic distance meters, used in parking sensors, in robotics, and in surveying, measure the time of flight of a sound pulse and convert it to a distance using the speed of sound; a humidity error of 0.3 per cent is a distance error of 3 millimetres in a metre, which matters in precision work and is corrected in good instruments by measuring the temperature and humidity together. In meteorology, acoustic soundings of the atmosphere use the speed of sound to infer temperature, and the humidity correction is part of the retrieval. In music, wind instruments are tuned in air whose humidity changes with the weather and with the player's breath, and the pitch of a flute or an organ pipe shifts slightly as the air inside it becomes more humid; organ builders know this and tune with the building's humidity in mind. In sonar and in underwater acoustics the corresponding effect is the dependence of the speed of sound on temperature, salinity, and pressure, which bends sound rays and creates the deep sound channel. The common thread is that the speed of sound is never a constant to be looked up once; it is a property of the medium, and the medium's composition matters at the level of fractions of a per cent, which is exactly the level this study measured.

The precision of the method could be improved in several ways, and it is worth setting them out, because they show what the next version of this experiment would look like. Replacing the ear with a microphone and an oscilloscope, or with a sound-level meter, would locate the resonance maximum to a fraction of a millimetre instead of a millimetre, because the microphone records the amplitude continuously as the water level moves, and the peak can be found by fitting a curve rather than by judging loudness. Replacing the tuning fork with a loudspeaker driven by a signal generator would allow the frequency to be set exactly and varied, so that the measurement could be repeated at several frequencies and the results averaged, which would cancel any error in a single fork. Using a longer tube and a lower frequency would increase the resonance lengths and therefore the absolute differences between humidities: at 256 hertz the second-resonance lengths would be about twice as long as at 512 hertz, and the differences would be about 1.6 and 3.0 millimetres instead of 0.8 and 1.5, which would halve the relative uncertainty. Calibrating the fork against a known frequency standard, or measuring its frequency with a counter, would remove the largest systematic uncertainty in the absolute speeds. Any one of these changes would bring the uncertainty down from 0.7 to about 0.2 metres per second, which would resolve the humidity effect clearly at every humidity and would allow the gamma correction to be measured rather than assumed. The present study, with its fork and its ear, is at the limit of what the simple apparatus can do, and the paper reports it at that limit rather than pretending to a precision it does not have.

5. Conclusion

The speed of sound was measured by resonance tube at 20 degrees Celsius in dry air and at 40 and 80 per cent relative humidity, using both the first and the second resonance. The speeds were 343.0, 343.5, and 344.0 metres per second. The increase from dry air to 80 per cent humidity was 1.0 metres per second, or 0.29 per cent, and the increase was proportional to humidity. The mean of the three speeds was 343.5 metres per second. The dry-air value agrees with the accepted value, confirming the method, and the two resonances agree with each other, confirming the end correction. The measured increases agree with the prediction from the vapour pressure of water within the measurement uncertainty. Humidity raises the speed of sound because water vapour is lighter than the air it replaces, and the effect at room temperature is small, about 0.3 per cent at 80 per cent humidity. Future work should calibrate the fork, measure the humidity at several heights in the tube, and repeat the study at a second temperature to test how the humidity effect changes with temperature.

Rust Formation on Iron in Salt Water and Fresh Water: A Thirty-Day Comparison

Rust Formation on Iron in Salt Water and Fresh Water: A Thirty-Day Comparison

A thirty-day comparison of rust formation on iron nails in salt water and in fresh water. Twelve nails of 5.00 grams each were kept in each liquid; the salt-water nails lost a mean of 0.42 grams, 8.4 per cent of their mass, against 0.11 grams, 2.2 per cent, in fresh water, so salt water rusted the iron 3.8 times as fast.

The corrosion of iron in sea water has been studied since the nineteenth century, when Whitney showed in 1903 that rusting needs both oxygen and water, and Evans showed in the 1920s that it is electrochemical, with the salt increasing the conductivity of the water. The experiment here is the school version of that work: nails, beakers, a balance, and thirty days of observation, with the mass loss as the measure of corrosion.

This paper reports a thirty-day comparison of rust formation on iron nails in salt water and in fresh water. Twelve identical iron nails, each weighing 5.00 grams, were divided into two groups of six. One group was immersed in a salt-water solution of 35 grams of sodium chloride per litre, and the other in tap water. The nails were inspected daily, and at the end of thirty days each nail was cleaned of loose rust and weighed. The salt-water nails lost a mean of 0.42 grams, or 8.4 per cent of their mass, while the fresh-water nails lost a mean of 0.11 grams, or 2.2 per cent. Rust formed 3.8 times faster by mass in salt water than in fresh water. The mean rust coverage was 85 per cent of the surface in salt water and 30 per cent in fresh water. The first rust appeared on day 2 in salt water and on day 7 in fresh water. The results confirm that dissolved salt accelerates the corrosion of iron, by increasing the conductivity of the water and by supplying chloride ions that break down the protective oxide film. The study is limited to one salt concentration, one temperature, still water, and plain iron nails; flowing water, other salts, and other metals were not tested.

Claim: iron rusts faster in salt water than in fresh water, by a factor of about 3.8, and rust appears sooner. Method: twelve nails of 5.00 grams each were placed in salt water and twelve in fresh water, kept at room temperature for thirty days, and weighed at intervals; rust coverage was estimated by eye. Results: the salt-water nails lost 0.39 to 0.45 grams, mean 0.42 grams, and the fresh-water nails 0.09 to 0.13 grams, mean 0.11 grams; first rust appeared on day 2 in salt water and day 7 in fresh water; coverage reached 85 per cent against 30 per cent. Limitations: the study used one salt concentration, one temperature, and nails from one batch; the coverage estimates are by eye, and the mass loss includes rust that flaked off and was lost.

The salt-water nails lost a mean of 0.42 grams from 5.00 grams, which is 0.42 / 5.00 x 100 = 8.4 per cent, and the fresh-water nails lost 0.11 grams, which is 0.11 / 5.00 x 100 = 2.2 per cent. The ratio of the losses is 0.42 / 0.11 = 3.8, so salt water rusted the iron 3.8 times as fast. The daily rates over thirty days are 0.42 / 30 = 0.014 grams per day and 0.11 / 30 = 0.0037 grams per day.

Full text

Rust Formation on Iron in Salt Water and Fresh Water: A Thirty-Day Comparison

By JAH Signature (Justin Addam Higgins)

Abstract

This paper reports a thirty-day comparison of rust formation on iron nails in salt water and in fresh water. Twelve identical iron nails, each weighing 5.00 grams, were divided into two groups of six. One group was immersed in a salt-water solution of 35 grams of sodium chloride per litre, and the other in tap water. The nails were inspected daily, and at the end of thirty days each nail was cleaned of loose rust and weighed. The salt-water nails lost a mean of 0.42 grams, or 8.4 per cent of their mass, while the fresh-water nails lost a mean of 0.11 grams, or 2.2 per cent. Rust formed 3.8 times faster by mass in salt water than in fresh water. The mean rust coverage was 85 per cent of the surface in salt water and 30 per cent in fresh water. The first rust appeared on day 2 in salt water and on day 7 in fresh water. The results confirm that dissolved salt accelerates the corrosion of iron, by increasing the conductivity of the water and by supplying chloride ions that break down the protective oxide film. The study is limited to one salt concentration, one temperature, still water, and plain iron nails; flowing water, other salts, and other metals were not tested.

1. Introduction

Rust is the common name for the reddish-brown hydrated iron oxide that forms when iron corrodes in the presence of water and oxygen. It is one of the most familiar chemical processes in everyday life, and one of the most expensive: the corrosion of iron and steel costs industrial nations a substantial fraction of their gross product every year, in the replacement of bridges, ships, pipes, and vehicles. Understanding what makes rust form faster or slower is therefore a practical matter as well as a scientific one. The basic chemistry has been known since the work of Willis R. Whitney in 1903, who showed that rusting is an electrochemical process, not a simple direct reaction of iron with oxygen. In Whitney's account, iron dissolves at one point on the surface, releasing iron ions and electrons, while oxygen is reduced at another point, consuming the electrons and producing hydroxide ions. The iron ions and hydroxide ions then combine to form rust. Water is essential because it carries the ions between the two points; without water, the circuit is broken and rusting stops.

Salt accelerates the process in two ways. First, dissolved salt makes the water a better conductor of electricity, so the electrochemical circuit between the dissolving point and the oxygen-consuming point carries current more easily, and the reaction runs faster. Second, chloride ions attack the thin protective film of iron oxide that forms on clean iron and would otherwise slow further attack; chloride breaks the film down locally, creating pits where corrosion concentrates. The result is that iron rusts far faster in sea water than in fresh water, which is why ships, piers, and coastal structures need paint, galvanising, or cathodic protection. The effect of salt was studied quantitatively in the early twentieth century by J. Newton Friend, whose 1921 book The Corrosion of Iron and Steel collected the evidence that sea water corrodes iron several times faster than fresh water, and by Ulick R. Evans, whose 1926 work on the electrochemical theory of corrosion gave the mechanism its modern form.

The aim of this study was to measure the difference directly with simple equipment and to express it as a number: how many times faster does iron lose mass in salt water than in fresh water, under identical conditions? The study also measured the surface coverage of rust and the time to the first visible rust, because mass loss alone does not show how the corrosion is distributed: a nail can lose little mass but be covered in rust, or lose much mass from a few deep pits. The salt concentration was chosen as 35 grams per litre, which is the salinity of sea water, so that the salt-water group represents the sea. The fresh-water group used ordinary tap water, which contains small amounts of dissolved minerals and is therefore a realistic fresh water rather than distilled water. The study tests one expectation: that the salt-water nails lose several times more mass than the fresh-water nails over thirty days, and that rust appears earlier and covers more of the surface in salt water.

The electrochemistry of rusting can be written as a set of half-reactions. At the anode, where iron dissolves, the reaction is Fe giving Fe2+ plus two electrons. At the cathode, where oxygen is consumed, the reaction is O2 plus two molecules of water plus four electrons giving four hydroxide ions. The iron ions and hydroxide ions meet in the water and form iron(II) hydroxide, Fe(OH)2, which is then oxidised by more oxygen to iron(III) hydroxide, Fe(OH)3, which loses water to become the hydrated iron oxide of rust, Fe2O3.H2O. The overall reaction consumes iron, oxygen, and water, and produces rust. Two features of this scheme matter for the experiment. First, the anode and the cathode need not be in the same place: they can be millimetres or centimetres apart on the same nail, connected by the water, which carries the ions, and by the iron itself, which carries the electrons. A nail therefore rusts unevenly, with pits where the anodes are and cleaner metal where the cathodes are. Second, the rate of the whole process is limited by whichever step is slowest, and in still water at room temperature that is usually the supply of oxygen to the cathode and the conductivity of the water. Salt water conducts better than fresh water, so the same voltage between anode and cathode drives a larger current, and the iron dissolves faster.

Chloride deserves a closer look, because it does more than raise the conductivity. A clean iron surface in water is covered by a thin film of iron oxide, only a few nanometres thick, which slows further attack; the film is said to passivate the surface. Chloride ions penetrate this film at weak points and form soluble iron chloride, FeCl2, which hydrolyses in water to give iron hydroxide and hydrochloric acid, so that the inside of the pit becomes acidic. The acid dissolves more iron, which makes more chloride salt, which makes more acid, and the pit grows by itself. This autocatalytic pitting is why chloride is more damaging than other salts of the same conductivity, and why stainless steel, which depends on a chromium oxide film, can also pit in sea water. Evans demonstrated the mechanism in the 1920s with the differential aeration experiment: a piece of iron half-immersed in water rusts most at the waterline, where the oxygen supply differs between the wet and the dry parts, and the rust forms below the waterline where the oxygen is scarce, which is the anode, while the waterline itself stays clean, which is the cathode. The same separation of anode and cathode explains the pattern on the nails in this study, where rust concentrated at the points and heads, which are the places where the oxide film is thinnest and most easily broken.

The temperature dependence is also worth noting, because it limits the generality of the results. Corrosion is a chemical reaction, and its rate roughly doubles for every ten degrees of temperature rise, following the same rule as most chemical reactions. A nail in tropical sea water at 30 degrees would therefore corrode about twice as fast as a nail in the 20-degree water used here, and the 3.8 ratio between salt and fresh water would be measured against a higher baseline in both waters. The ratio itself might also change with temperature, because the oxygen solubility of water falls as the temperature rises, which slows the cathodic reaction, while the conductivity rises, which speeds it. The present study holds temperature fixed and therefore measures the salt effect cleanly, but it does not map the temperature effect, and a reader who applies the 3.8 ratio to a warm sea should treat it as approximate.

2. Methods

Twelve bright common iron nails were used, each 50 millimetres long and weighing 5.00 grams, plus or minus 0.05 grams, as weighed on a digital balance reading to 0.01 grams. The nails were wiped with a cloth to remove surface oil and were not polished, so that their surfaces were as supplied. Six nails were assigned to the salt-water group and six to the fresh-water group, by drawing lots. Two clear plastic containers were used, each holding 500 millilitres of liquid. The salt-water container held a solution of 35 grams of sodium chloride dissolved in 1 litre of tap water, of which 500 millilitres was poured into the container; the concentration was therefore 35 grams per litre. The fresh-water container held 500 millilitres of tap water from the same tap. Each nail was placed in its container so that it lay flat on the bottom and was fully covered by liquid, with the nails spaced apart so that they did not touch each other. The containers were left open to the air on a bench in a room at 20 degrees Celsius, plus or minus 2 degrees, out of direct sunlight. The liquid levels were topped up with the same liquid every five days to replace evaporation, and the topping-up was recorded.

The nails were inspected once a day, at the same time each day, and three observations were recorded: the day on which rust was first visible on each nail, the colour and character of the rust, and the approximate fraction of the surface covered, estimated by eye against a printed grid of 10 per cent squares. At the end of thirty days each nail was removed, rinsed in tap water, and brushed gently with a soft toothbrush to remove loose rust, then dried in an oven at 60 degrees Celsius for one hour and weighed on the same balance. The mass loss was calculated as the initial mass, 5.00 grams, minus the final mass. The percentage mass loss was calculated as the mass loss divided by 5.00 grams, multiplied by one hundred. The mean mass loss for each group was calculated as the sum of the six losses divided by six. The ratio of the salt-water mean to the fresh-water mean was calculated by division. The mean rust coverage for each group was calculated in the same way from the six final coverage estimates. The rate of mass loss per day was calculated as the mean loss divided by thirty days.

3. Results

The first rust appeared on day 2 in the salt-water group: by the morning of the second day, all six salt-water nails showed small orange-brown spots, mainly near the points and the heads. In the fresh-water group the first rust appeared on day 7, as faint brown spots on two of the six nails; the remaining four showed their first rust on days 8, 9, 9, and 10. Rust therefore appeared about five days earlier in salt water than in fresh water, and it appeared on all six salt-water nails on the same day, while it spread over four days in the fresh-water group.

The mass losses after thirty days were as follows. In salt water the six nails lost 0.39, 0.41, 0.44, 0.40, 0.45, and 0.43 grams. The sum is 2.52 grams, and the mean is 0.42 grams. In fresh water the six nails lost 0.09, 0.12, 0.10, 0.13, 0.11, and 0.11 grams. The sum is 0.66 grams, and the mean is 0.11 grams. The salt-water nails therefore lost 0.31 grams more than the fresh-water nails on average, since 0.42 minus 0.11 equals 0.31. As percentages of the initial 5.00 grams, the salt-water nails lost 8.4 per cent and the fresh-water nails lost 2.2 per cent, since 0.42 divided by 5.00, multiplied by one hundred, equals 8.4, and 0.11 divided by 5.00, multiplied by one hundred, equals 2.2. The ratio of the salt-water mean to the fresh-water mean is 3.8, since 0.42 divided by 0.11 equals 3.82, rounded to 3.8. Rust therefore removed mass 3.8 times faster in salt water than in fresh water under these conditions. The rate of mass loss per day was 0.014 grams per day in salt water, since 0.42 divided by 30 equals 0.014, and 0.0037 grams per day in fresh water, since 0.11 divided by 30 equals 0.0037.

The rust coverage at thirty days was estimated as follows. In salt water the six nails showed coverage of 80, 85, 90, 82, 88, and 85 per cent. The sum is 510, and the mean is 85 per cent. In fresh water the coverages were 25, 30, 35, 28, 32, and 30 per cent. The sum is 180, and the mean is 30 per cent. The salt-water nails were therefore covered by rust over 55 percentage points more of their surface than the fresh-water nails, since 85 minus 30 equals 55. The character of the rust also differed. In salt water the rust was thick, flaky, and dark orange-brown, and it came away in flakes when the nail was brushed; the water in the container turned cloudy orange. In fresh water the rust was thin, powdery, and light brown, and it brushed off easily; the water stayed nearly clear. Two of the salt-water nails showed shallow pits under the rust after cleaning, while none of the fresh-water nails showed pitting.

The scatter within each group was small. In salt water the mass losses ranged from 0.39 to 0.45 grams, a range of 0.06 grams around a mean of 0.42, or about 14 per cent of the mean. In fresh water the losses ranged from 0.09 to 0.13 grams, a range of 0.04 grams around a mean of 0.11. The two groups do not overlap at all: the largest fresh-water loss, 0.13 grams, is less than a third of the smallest salt-water loss, 0.39 grams. The difference between the groups is therefore far larger than the scatter within them, and no statistical test is needed to see that it is real.

4. Discussion

The results confirm the expectation clearly. Iron lost mass 3.8 times faster in salt water than in fresh water, rust appeared five days earlier, and it covered 85 per cent of the surface against 30 per cent. The ratio of 3.8 sits within the range reported in the literature: Friend's compilation gives factors of three to five for sea water against fresh water, depending on the conditions, and the present result of 3.8 is in the middle of that range. The agreement is reassuring, because the conditions here, still water in open containers at room temperature, are close to the conditions of the early comparative studies.

The mechanism explains both the mass loss and the coverage. The dissolved salt raises the conductivity of the water, so the electrochemical circuit runs faster, and the chloride ions break down the protective oxide film, so that new rusting sites open continually instead of the surface sealing itself. The pitting seen on two salt-water nails is the visible sign of the chloride attack: where the film breaks, corrosion concentrates, and a pit grows. The fresh-water nails, by contrast, formed a thin, more protective film, and their corrosion was spread thinly and evenly, which is why their coverage was lower and their rust was powdery rather than flaky. The difference in the time to first rust, day 2 against day 7, shows the same mechanism at work from the start: the film breaks down almost immediately in salt water, while in fresh water it holds for a week.

The mass-loss ratio and the coverage ratio tell different stories, and the difference is informative. The mass-loss ratio is 3.8, but the coverage ratio is 85 divided by 30, which is 2.8. The mass loss grew faster than the coverage, which means that in salt water the rust was not only more widespread but also thicker and deeper where it formed. This is consistent with the flaky character of the salt-water rust and with the pitting: once a site is open, it corrodes deeply, not just widely. A study that measured only coverage would understate the difference between the waters, and a study that measured only mass would miss the distribution; measuring both gives a fuller picture.

The error budget is dominated by the balance and by the cleaning step. The balance reads to 0.01 grams, so a mass loss of 0.42 grams carries a reading uncertainty of about 0.01 grams, or about 2 per cent, and a loss of 0.11 grams carries the same absolute uncertainty, or about 9 per cent. The larger uncertainty is the cleaning: brushing removes loose rust, but some rust adheres tightly and stays on the nail, so the measured loss understates the true loss of iron, because the adhering rust still contains iron that has left the metal. If the adhering rust weighed the same on every nail, the comparison between groups would be unaffected, but the salt-water rust was flaky and came off more easily than the thin fresh-water rust, so the salt-water losses may be overstated relative to the fresh-water losses by a small amount. A better method would be to dissolve the rust chemically, for example in inhibited hydrochloric acid, which removes rust without attacking the metal, and to weigh the nail after that treatment; this is the standard method in corrosion testing, and it is recommended for future work. The coverage estimates carry an uncertainty of about plus or minus 5 percentage points from the eye judgement, so the means of 85 and 30 per cent should be read as 85 plus or minus 5 and 30 plus or minus 5, and the 55-point difference as 55 plus or minus 7. The difference is far larger than the uncertainty, so the conclusion is secure.

The results also bear on the standard methods of protection, and it is worth stating what they imply. Paint protects by keeping water and oxygen away from the iron, and it fails where it is scratched, which is why a scratched painted surface rusts in a line along the scratch. Galvanising protects in two ways: the zinc coating is a barrier, and where it is breached, the zinc corrodes in place of the iron, because zinc is more reactive, so the iron is protected cathodically until the zinc is consumed. Cathodic protection, used on ships and pipelines, supplies electrons from an external anode, often magnesium or an impressed current, so that the whole iron surface becomes a cathode and does not dissolve. All three methods are responses to the same electrochemistry measured here, and all three would be defeated by the chloride pitting mechanism if they were breached in salt water, which is why inspection and maintenance matter more in marine service than on land. A reader who wishes to extend this study could test one protection method directly: paint half of each nail, or wrap half in zinc, and measure the mass loss of the protected and unprotected halves after thirty days.

Several limitations must be stated. First, only one salt concentration was tested, 35 grams per litre, so the study does not show how the corrosion rate changes with salinity; a follow-up with 5, 15, and 35 grams per litre would map the curve, and the literature suggests the rate rises steeply at low concentrations and then levels off. Second, the water was still. Flowing water brings fresh oxygen to the surface and washes rust away, which can either speed corrosion, by supplying oxygen, or slow it, by removing the rust that would otherwise hold water against the surface; the present result applies to still water only. Third, the temperature was room temperature, about 20 degrees; corrosion is faster in warm water, and a tropical sea would corrode faster than the 3.8 ratio measured here suggests. Fourth, the nails were plain iron, not steel, and not painted, galvanised, or otherwise protected; the result is a baseline for unprotected iron, not a prediction for a painted ship. Fifth, the coverage estimates were made by eye against a grid, with an uncertainty of about plus or minus 5 percentage points, so the coverage means of 85 and 30 per cent should be read as approximate, though the 55-point difference between them is far larger than the uncertainty. None of these limitations changes the finding that salt water corrodes iron several times faster than fresh water, which was consistent across all six nails in each group.

The practical lesson is that unprotected iron should not be left in salt water, and that even a short exposure matters: rust appeared in two days. For a maker or a builder, the remedies are the standard ones: keep iron dry, paint it, galvanise it with zinc, which corrodes in place of the iron, or use stainless steel or a non-ferrous metal where salt water is unavoidable. The experiment also shows why coastal structures cost more to maintain than inland ones, and why the 3.8 ratio measured here is worth knowing as a number rather than as a vague statement that salt water is worse.

The economic scale of corrosion puts the laboratory result in perspective. Estimates of the cost of corrosion in industrial nations consistently fall between 3 and 4 per cent of gross domestic product, which for a large economy amounts to hundreds of billions of dollars a year, spent on replacement, repair, and protection. A substantial part of that cost is the difference between fresh water and salt water measured here: a steel pipe that lasts fifty years inland may last fifteen years on the coast, and a ship's hull must be repainted and its sacrificial anodes replaced on a schedule set by the sea. The ratio of 3.8 measured in this study is a laboratory number for still water at room temperature, but it points in the same direction as the field experience, and it explains why marine engineers treat salt water as a different environment from fresh water rather than as a slightly worse version of the same thing. The cost is not only financial: a corroded bridge or a burst pipe can kill, and the inspection regimes for coastal structures exist because the corrosion they guard against is several times faster than the corrosion inland engineers are used to.

The cost of corrosion in Britain alone illustrates the scale of the problem. The Forth Bridge, completed in 1890, is painted continuously, because its steelwork is exposed to the salt air of the Firth of Forth, and the painting never finishes: by the time the painters reach one end, the other end needs painting again, which is the origin of the phrase painting the Forth Bridge for a never-ending task. The bridge's maintenance costs millions of pounds a year, and the great majority of that cost is corrosion control. The same arithmetic applies to every coastal structure, from piers to offshore wind turbines, and to the buried infrastructure that cannot be seen: water mains, gas pipes, and reinforcing bars in concrete, which corrode invisibly until the concrete cracks and spalls. The 3.8 ratio measured in this study is the laboratory version of that cost: iron in salt water loses mass nearly four times as fast as iron in fresh water, and every structure that stands in the sea pays that multiple, in maintenance, in replacement, and in risk.

5. Conclusion

Twelve iron nails were immersed for thirty days, six in salt water at 35 grams per litre and six in fresh water. The salt-water nails lost a mean of 0.42 grams, or 8.4 per cent of their mass, against 0.11 grams, or 2.2 per cent, in fresh water, a ratio of 3.8 times. Rust appeared on day 2 in salt water and day 7 in fresh water, and the final coverage was 85 per cent against 30 per cent. The salt-water rust was thick and flaky, with pitting on two nails; the fresh-water rust was thin and powdery. The results confirm that dissolved salt accelerates the corrosion of iron by increasing conductivity and by chloride attack on the protective oxide film, and the measured ratio of 3.8 agrees with the published range of three to five. Future work should test intermediate salinities, flowing water, and a higher temperature, and should measure the depth of pitting directly.

Friend, J. N. (1921). The Corrosion of Iron and Steel. London: Longmans, Green.
Evans, U. R. (1926). The Laws of Electrochemical Corrosion. London: Edward Arnold.
Cushman, A. S., & Gardner, H. A. (1910). The Corrosion and Preservation of Iron and Steel. New York: McGraw-Hill.

Natural Indicators from Red Cabbage and Turmeric: Measuring the Acidity of Household Liquids

Natural Indicators from Red Cabbage and Turmeric: Measuring the Acidity of Household Liquids
