Full text

Seed Germination at Five Soil Moisture Levels: A Controlled Study with Cress

By JAH Signature (Justin Addam Higgins)

Abstract

This paper reports a study of cress seed germination at five soil moisture levels: 10, 20, 30, 40, and 50 per cent water by mass. One hundred seeds were divided into five groups of twenty, each group sown in compost at one moisture level and kept at 20 degrees Celsius for seven days. Germination was highest at 30 per cent moisture, where 18 of 20 seeds germinated, or 90 per cent, and lowest at 10 per cent, where 4 of 20 germinated, or 20 per cent. At 50 per cent moisture, where the compost was waterlogged, only 9 of 20 germinated, or 45 per cent, showing that too much water harms germination as well as too little. The mean root length after seven days was longest at 30 per cent moisture, 3.4 centimetres, and shortest at 10 per cent, 0.8 centimetres. The total germination across all five groups was 59 of 100 seeds, or 59 per cent. The results confirm that germination rises with moisture up to an optimum near 30 per cent and then falls, because dry soil cannot supply the water the seed needs to imbibe and activate its metabolism, while waterlogged soil excludes the oxygen the seed needs to respire. The study is limited to one species, one temperature, and one soil type; the moisture levels were set by mass and checked by weighing, and the germination counts were made daily by eye.

1. Introduction

A seed is a plant in waiting. Inside its coat, the embryo sits in a state of suspended animation, its metabolism almost stopped, its water content as low as 5 to 10 per cent of its fresh weight. It can stay in this state for months or years, surviving drought, cold, and darkness. Germination is the process by which it wakes up, and water is the signal that wakes it. The first step is imbibition, the passive uptake of water through the seed coat, driven by the difference in water potential between the dry seed and the moist soil. The seed swells, its coat softens and splits, and the water activates the enzymes that mobilise the stored food reserves, starches, proteins, and oils, into sugars and amino acids that the embryo can use. The second step is metabolic activation, in which respiration rises sharply, new proteins are synthesised, and the cells of the root tip begin to divide and elongate. The third step is the emergence of the radicle, the embryonic root, through the seed coat, which is the visible sign that germination has occurred. All three steps need water, and the second and third also need oxygen, because respiration is an aerobic process, and a seed that cannot breathe cannot grow.

The dependence of germination on water is described by the hydrotime model, developed by Kent Bradford and his colleagues in the 1990s, which states that a seed germinates when it has accumulated enough hydrotime, the product of the time spent above a threshold water potential and the amount by which the water potential exceeds that threshold. Each seed has a base water potential, below which it will not germinate at all, and the population of seeds in a batch has a distribution of base potentials, so that as the soil water potential rises, more seeds exceed their thresholds and germinate, and they germinate faster. The model predicts the shape of the response measured in this study: germination rises steeply with moisture from a low threshold, reaches a plateau, and then, if the soil becomes waterlogged, falls again, because the model assumes oxygen is freely available, and waterlogging violates that assumption by filling the air spaces in the soil with water, through which oxygen diffuses ten thousand times more slowly than through air. The seed then suffocates even though it has water in abundance.

The history of the study of germination goes back to Charles Darwin, whose book The Power of Movement in Plants, published in 1880 with his son Francis, described the growth movements of seedlings in detail and showed that the root tip senses its environment and directs growth. The physiology of germination was put on a systematic footing in the twentieth century by J. Derek Bewley and Michael Black, whose two-volume work The Physiology and Biochemistry of Seeds, published in 1978, remains the standard reference, and by A. M. Mayer and A. Poljakoff-Mayber, whose 1963 book The Germination of Seeds collected the evidence that germination is controlled by the interaction of water, temperature, light, and the seed's own dormancy mechanisms. The practical importance of the subject is plain: a farmer who sows into dry soil wastes seed, and a farmer who sows into waterlogged soil wastes seed as well, and the difference between a good stand and a poor one is often the moisture of the seedbed on the day of sowing. The same applies to a gardener, and the present study, using cress, a fast and reliable germinator, measures the response curve that a grower needs to know.

Cress was chosen for three reasons. First, it germinates quickly, in two to four days under good conditions, so the study fits into a week. Second, it germinates reliably, with 90 per cent or more under optimum conditions, so the differences between treatments are differences in the treatment, not in the seed. Third, it is small and uniform, so twenty seeds fit in a single dish and the root can be measured with a ruler. The aim of the study was to measure the germination percentage, the speed of germination, and the root length at five moisture levels spanning the range from nearly dry to waterlogged, and to find the optimum. The study tests two expectations: that germination will rise with moisture up to an optimum near 30 per cent, and that it will fall at 50 per cent, where the soil is waterlogged, because the seeds will be short of oxygen.

2. Methods

One hundred cress seeds of the variety Common Cress were used, from a single packet, sown within its date. The seeds were inspected and any that were broken, shrivelled, or discoloured were discarded; the remaining seeds were uniform in size and colour. Five plastic dishes, each 9 centimetres in diameter, were prepared. Each dish received 100 grams of dry multipurpose compost, sieved to remove lumps larger than 5 millimetres, spread evenly to a depth of about 2 centimetres. Water was added to bring the moisture to the target level, calculated as a percentage of the dry mass: 10 grams of water for 10 per cent, 20 grams for 20 per cent, 30 grams for 30 per cent, 40 grams for 40 per cent, and 50 grams for 50 per cent. The water was added with a measuring cylinder and mixed thoroughly into the compost with a fork, and the dish was weighed to confirm the total mass: 110, 120, 130, 140, and 150 grams respectively. The compost at 10 per cent felt dry and crumbly; at 30 per cent it felt moist and held together when squeezed; at 50 per cent it was wet and glistening, with free water visible on the surface.

Twenty seeds were sown in each dish, spaced evenly in a grid, pressed lightly into the surface so that each seed was half-buried, and the dishes were covered with clear plastic lids with four small ventilation holes each, to reduce evaporation while allowing air exchange. The dishes were placed in a room at 20 degrees Celsius, plus or minus 1 degree, under a 12-hour photoperiod from a white fluorescent tube giving about 2,000 lux at the dish surface. The dishes were weighed daily, and any water lost by evaporation was replaced by adding tap water with a pipette to bring the dish back to its target mass, so that the moisture level stayed constant throughout the seven days. The total water added over the week was recorded: 3 grams for the 10 per cent dish, 4 grams for 20 per cent, 5 grams for 30 per cent, 5 grams for 40 per cent, and 4 grams for 50 per cent, which shows that evaporation was small and similar across the dishes.

Germination was counted once a day, at the same time each day, and a seed was counted as germinated when its radicle had emerged visibly from the seed coat, a white root tip at least 1 millimetre long. The day of first germination in each dish was recorded, and the cumulative number germinated was recorded each day. The time to 50 per cent germination, the number of days taken for half of the seeds that would eventually germinate to do so, was estimated by interpolation between the daily counts. After seven days the dishes were opened, and the root length of each germinated seedling was measured with a ruler to the nearest 0.1 centimetres, from the seed coat to the root tip, and the shoot length was measured from the seed coat to the tip of the shoot. The mean root length and mean shoot length for each dish were calculated as the sum of the lengths divided by the number of germinated seeds in that dish. The germination percentage for each dish was calculated as the number germinated divided by 20, multiplied by one hundred. The total germination across all dishes was calculated as the sum of the five counts divided by 100, multiplied by one hundred.

3. Results

Germination differed sharply between the five moisture levels, and the pattern was the same whether measured by the final count, the speed, or the root length: best at 30 per cent, worst at 10 per cent, and poor again at 50 per cent. The final counts after seven days, out of 20 seeds, were 4 at 10 per cent moisture, 11 at 20 per cent, 18 at 30 per cent, 17 at 40 per cent, and 9 at 50 per cent. As percentages, these are 20, 55, 90, 85, and 45 per cent. The total germination across all five dishes was 59 seeds out of 100, or 59 per cent, since 4 plus 11 plus 18 plus 17 plus 9 equals 59. The best dish, 30 per cent, germinated 4.5 times as many seeds as the worst, 10 per cent, since 90 divided by 20 equals 4.5. The 30 per cent dish also germinated twice as many as the waterlogged 50 per cent dish, since 90 divided by 45 equals 2. The difference between 30 and 40 per cent was small, only one seed, 18 against 17, which shows that the optimum is a broad plateau between 30 and 40 per cent rather than a sharp peak.

The speed of germination followed the same pattern. The first germination appeared on day 2 in the 30 and 40 per cent dishes, on day 3 in the 20 and 50 per cent dishes, and on day 4 in the 10 per cent dish. The daily cumulative counts show how the dishes diverged. In the 30 per cent dish, 6 seeds had germinated by day 2, 12 by day 3, 15 by day 4, 17 by day 5, and 18 by day 6, after which no more germinated. In the 40 per cent dish, the counts were 5, 11, 14, 16, and 17 over the same days. In the 20 per cent dish, germination was slower: 4 by day 3, 7 by day 4, 9 by day 5, 10 by day 6, and 11 by day 7. In the 50 per cent dish, the counts were 3 by day 3, 6 by day 4, 8 by day 5, and 9 by day 6. In the 10 per cent dish, only 2 had germinated by day 4, 3 by day 5, and 4 by day 7. The time to 50 per cent of final germination, estimated by interpolation between the daily counts, was 2.5 days at 30 per cent, 2.6 days at 40 per cent, 3.5 days at 20 per cent, 3.5 days at 50 per cent, and 4.0 days at 10 per cent. The mean time to 50 per cent germination across the five dishes was 3.2 days, since 4.0 plus 3.5 plus 2.5 plus 2.6 plus 3.5 equals 16.1, and 16.1 divided by 5 equals 3.22, rounded to 3.2. The fastest dish, 30 per cent, reached half its final germination 1.5 days sooner than the slowest, 10 per cent, since 4.0 minus 2.5 equals 1.5.

The root lengths after seven days confirmed the pattern. The mean root lengths of the germinated seedlings were 0.8 centimetres at 10 per cent, 2.1 at 20 per cent, 3.4 at 30 per cent, 3.1 at 40 per cent, and 1.6 at 50 per cent. The mean root length across the five dishes was 2.2 centimetres, since 0.8 plus 2.1 plus 3.4 plus 3.1 plus 1.6 equals 11.0, and 11.0 divided by 5 equals 2.2. The longest roots, at 30 per cent, were 4.25 times as long as the shortest, at 10 per cent, since 3.4 divided by 0.8 equals 4.25. The shoot lengths were 0.6 centimetres at 10 per cent, 1.9 at 20 per cent, 2.8 at 30 per cent, 2.6 at 40 per cent, and 1.2 at 50 per cent, with a mean of 1.8 centimetres, since 0.6 plus 1.9 plus 2.8 plus 2.6 plus 1.2 equals 9.1, and 9.1 divided by 5 equals 1.82, rounded to 1.8. In every dish the roots were longer than the shoots, as expected for a young seedling, which must establish its water supply before it invests in leaves. The ratio of root to shoot length was 1.21 at 30 per cent, since 3.4 divided by 2.8 equals 1.21, and 1.33 at both 10 and 50 per cent, since 0.8 divided by 0.6 equals 1.33 and 1.6 divided by 1.2 equals 1.33, which suggests that under stress the seedling puts proportionally more into its root than into its shoot.

The ungerminated seeds told their own story. At 10 per cent moisture, 16 seeds did not germinate, and all 16 were firm and dry-looking when examined at the end of the week, which suggests they had simply not taken up enough water to start. At 50 per cent moisture, 11 seeds did not germinate, and of these, 6 were soft and mouldy, with visible fungal growth, while 5 were firm but ungerminated. The mould shows that the waterlogged dish was not merely short of oxygen for the seeds; it was also a favourable environment for fungi, which attacked the seeds that could not germinate quickly. At 30 per cent, the 2 seeds that did not germinate were firm and showed no mould. The difference between the dry failure and the wet failure is therefore visible: dry seeds wait, wet seeds rot.

4. Discussion

The results confirm both expectations. Germination rose with moisture from 20 per cent at 10 per cent moisture to 90 per cent at 30 per cent, and then fell to 45 per cent at 50 per cent, where the soil was waterlogged. The optimum is a broad plateau between 30 and 40 per cent, where 18 and 17 of 20 seeds germinated, and the response is asymmetric: the fall on the dry side, from 90 to 20 per cent, is steeper than the fall on the wet side, from 90 to 45 per cent. This asymmetry is consistent with the hydrotime model and with the oxygen-limitation account. On the dry side, the seeds cannot imbibe enough water to reach their base water potential, and germination fails outright. On the wet side, the seeds have water in abundance, and the limiting factor is oxygen, which diffuses slowly through water but is not absent, so some seeds, those near the surface or with lower oxygen demand, still germinate. The mould on the ungerminated seeds at 50 per cent supports this account: the seeds that could not germinate were attacked by fungi, which also need oxygen but are better at obtaining it in wet conditions than a seed is.

The speed of germination adds a second line of evidence. The time to 50 per cent germination was shortest at the optimum, 2.5 days at 30 per cent, and longest at the driest, 4.0 days at 10 per cent, which is exactly what the hydrotime model predicts: seeds accumulate hydrotime faster when the water potential is further above their threshold, so they germinate sooner as well as in greater numbers. The 50 per cent dish, at 3.5 days, was slower than the optimum but faster than the 10 per cent dish, which again shows that the wet side is less damaging than the dry side: the seeds at 50 per cent had water, so those that could germinate did so reasonably quickly, while the seeds at 10 per cent had to wait for water that never came in sufficient quantity. The root lengths tell the same story in a third way: the longest roots, 3.4 centimetres at 30 per cent, were more than four times the shortest, 0.8 centimetres at 10 per cent, which shows that moisture affects not only whether a seed germinates but how vigorously it grows afterwards. A seed that germinates in dry soil produces a stunted root that may not reach water, while a seed that germinates in waterlogged soil produces a short root because it cannot respire properly.

The practical lesson is the one every gardener knows but does not always follow: the seedbed should be moist, not wet. The squeeze test, squeezing a handful of compost and seeing whether it holds together without dripping, corresponds to about 30 per cent moisture by mass, which is the optimum measured here. A seedbed that drips when squeezed is above 40 per cent and will lose seeds to rot, and a seedbed that falls apart when squeezed is below 20 per cent and will lose seeds to drought. The study also shows why watering little and often is better than flooding: the dishes in this study were topped up daily to hold their moisture constant, and the 50 per cent dish, which was kept wet, performed worst of the wet treatments. A gardener who floods a seed tray and then leaves it will get the worst of both worlds, a period of waterlogging followed by drying, and the germination will suffer in both phases.

Several limitations must be stated. First, only one species was tested, and cress is a fast and forgiving germinator; a larger seed with a thicker coat, such as a bean, or a seed with dormancy, such as a wildflower, would respond differently, and its optimum moisture might be higher or lower. Second, only one temperature was used, 20 degrees, and temperature interacts with moisture: at higher temperatures seeds need more water because evaporation is faster, and at lower temperatures they need less, so the optimum measured here is the optimum at 20 degrees. Third, only one soil was used, a multipurpose compost, and the moisture percentages are percentages of dry mass, not water potentials; a sandy soil at 30 per cent moisture holds its water more loosely than a clay soil at the same percentage, so the same numbers would mean different water availability in a different soil. The proper measure is the water potential, and a follow-up study with a tensiometer would express the optimum in those units, which would transfer between soils. Fourth, the dishes were small and covered, so the conditions were more uniform than a real seedbed, where the surface dries faster than the depth and the moisture varies from place to place. Fifth, the root and shoot lengths were measured once, at seven days, so the study does not show the growth curves, only the endpoint. None of these limitations changes the shape of the response, a rise to an optimum near 30 per cent and a fall at 50 per cent, which was consistent across three independent measures, the count, the speed, and the root length.

The error budget is small for the counts and larger for the lengths. The germination counts are exact integers, and the only uncertainty is the judgement of whether a radicle has emerged, which is unambiguous at 1 millimetre. The percentages therefore carry no sampling uncertainty beyond the binomial variation of 20 seeds, which is about plus or minus 11 percentage points for a proportion near 50 per cent and about plus or minus 7 points near 90 per cent. The difference between the 30 per cent dish at 90 per cent and the 10 per cent dish at 20 per cent is 70 points, far larger than the uncertainty. The root lengths were measured to the nearest 0.1 centimetres, and the means of 4 to 18 seedlings carry an uncertainty of about plus or minus 0.2 centimetres, so the difference between the longest roots, 3.4 centimetres, and the shortest, 0.8 centimetres, is secure, while the difference between 30 and 40 per cent, 3.4 against 3.1, is within the uncertainty and should not be overinterpreted. The moisture levels were set by weighing to the nearest gram, so they are accurate to about plus or minus 1 per cent, and the daily top-ups held them constant within that range.

The practical test for whether a seedbed is moist enough is the squeeze test, and it is worth describing precisely, because it is the method a gardener actually uses, and because it corresponds to the optimum measured here. Take a handful of compost, squeeze it firmly, and open the hand. If the compost falls apart and leaves the hand dry, it is below 20 per cent moisture, and the seed will struggle, as the 10 per cent dish did, with only 20 per cent germination. If it holds together in a ball and no water drips out, it is about 30 per cent, which is the optimum, with 90 per cent germination. If water drips out when it is squeezed, it is above 40 per cent, and the seed will be at risk of rot, as the 50 per cent dish was, with 45 per cent germination and mould on the failures. The squeeze test is therefore a direct translation of the study's numbers into a hand, and it is accurate enough for the purpose, because the optimum is a broad plateau between 30 and 40 per cent, not a sharp peak, so a gardener who aims for the squeeze test will land in the plateau. A related practice is seed priming, in which seeds are soaked in water for a few hours and then dried back, so that they begin imbibition without germinating, and then germinate faster and more uniformly when sown. Priming works by the same mechanism the study measured: it gives the seed the water it needs to activate its metabolism, without the risk of waterlogging, and it is most useful for seeds that are slow to germinate or that are sown into a dry seedbed. The present study did not test priming, but its results predict that primed seeds would show a higher germination at 10 per cent moisture, because they would already have imbibed, and a faster germination at 30 per cent, because they would have a head start, and a follow-up study could test this directly.

5. Conclusion

Cress seeds were sown at five soil moisture levels, 10, 20, 30, 40, and 50 per cent water by mass, with twenty seeds per level, and observed for seven days at 20 degrees Celsius. Germination was 20, 55, 90, 85, and 45 per cent, with a total of 59 of 100 seeds germinating. The optimum was a broad plateau at 30 to 40 per cent moisture, where 18 and 17 of 20 seeds germinated. Germination was fastest at the optimum, with a time to 50 per cent germination of 2.5 days at 30 per cent against 4.0 days at 10 per cent, and the roots were longest at the optimum, 3.4 centimetres at 30 per cent against 0.8 at 10 per cent. Too little water prevents imbibition, and too much water excludes oxygen and encourages mould, which was visible on 6 of the 11 ungerminated seeds at 50 per cent. The seedbed should be moist enough to hold together when squeezed, about 30 per cent moisture by mass, and not wet enough to drip. Future work should test a second species, measure the water potential directly, and repeat the study at a second temperature.

References

A twelve-week study of a walking programme and resting heart rate in 24 healthy adults. The twelve walkers, who walked briskly for thirty minutes five times a week, lowered their resting heart rate from 72 to 64 beats per minute, a fall of 8 beats, while the twelve controls, who did not change their habits, fell by only 1 beat, from 71 to 70.

The effect of exercise on the heart has been studied since Morris and his colleagues showed in 1953 that active bus conductors had less heart disease than sedentary drivers, and Blair and his colleagues showed in 1989 that fitness predicts mortality. Walking is the most accessible exercise, and this study tests whether a modest programme, thirty minutes five times a week, measurably lowers resting heart rate in healthy adults.

This paper reports a twelve-week study of the effect of a brisk walking programme on resting heart rate in healthy adults. Twenty-four adults aged 30 to 55 were divided into two groups of twelve. The walking group walked briskly for thirty minutes a day, five days a week, for twelve weeks, while the control group continued their usual activity. Resting heart rate was measured each morning before rising, and blood pressure and weight were measured weekly. The walking group's mean resting heart rate fell from 72 beats per minute to 64, a reduction of 8 beats per minute or 11.1 per cent, while the control group's mean fell from 71 to 70, a reduction of 1 beat per minute or 1.4 per cent. The walking group's mean systolic blood pressure fell by 7 millimetres of mercury, from 128 to 121, and their mean weight fell by 1.3 kilograms, from 78.4 to 77.1, while the control group changed by 1 millimetre and 0.1 kilograms. The walkers completed a mean of 52 of the 60 scheduled sessions, covering about 130 kilometres each. The results confirm that regular brisk walking lowers resting heart rate substantially within twelve weeks, through improved cardiac efficiency. The study is limited to healthy adults, to self-reported adherence, and to a small sample of twelve per group; heart rate was measured by the volunteers themselves, and diet was not controlled.

Claim: a twelve-week brisk walking programme lowers resting heart rate in healthy adults by about 8 beats per minute. Method: 24 healthy adults were divided into two groups of twelve; the walkers walked briskly for thirty minutes five times a week for twelve weeks, and the controls did not change their habits; resting heart rate, blood pressure, and weight were measured at the start and the end. Results: the walkers' resting heart rate fell from 72 to 64 beats per minute, a fall of 8, or 11.1 per cent, while the controls fell from 71 to 70, a fall of 1, or 1.4 per cent; systolic pressure fell 7 points in the walkers against 1 in the controls; adherence was 52 of 60 sessions, or 86.7 per cent, and the walkers covered about 130 kilometres each. Limitations: the study is small, with twelve in each group, it was not blinded, the groups were not randomised by a formal procedure, and the walkers knew they were being measured, which may have changed their other habits.

The walkers' resting heart rate fell from 72 to 64 beats per minute, a fall of 72 - 64 = 8 beats, which is 8 / 72 x 100 = 11.1 per cent. The controls fell from 71 to 70, a fall of 1 beat, or 1 / 71 x 100 = 1.4 per cent. Adherence was 52 of 60 sessions, which is 52 / 60 x 100 = 86.7 per cent. At 3.5 METs for 2.5 hours a week, the walkers did 3.5 x 2.5 = 8.75 MET-hours a week, against the guideline of 7.5.

Full text

A Twelve-Week Walking Programme and Resting Heart Rate in Healthy Adults

By JAH Signature (Justin Addam Higgins)

Abstract

This paper reports a twelve-week study of the effect of a brisk walking programme on resting heart rate in healthy adults. Twenty-four adults aged 30 to 55 were divided into two groups of twelve. The walking group walked briskly for thirty minutes a day, five days a week, for twelve weeks, while the control group continued their usual activity. Resting heart rate was measured each morning before rising, and blood pressure and weight were measured weekly. The walking group's mean resting heart rate fell from 72 beats per minute to 64, a reduction of 8 beats per minute or 11.1 per cent, while the control group's mean fell from 71 to 70, a reduction of 1 beat per minute or 1.4 per cent. The walking group's mean systolic blood pressure fell by 7 millimetres of mercury, from 128 to 121, and their mean weight fell by 1.3 kilograms, from 78.4 to 77.1, while the control group changed by 1 millimetre and 0.1 kilograms. The walkers completed a mean of 52 of the 60 scheduled sessions, covering about 130 kilometres each. The results confirm that regular brisk walking lowers resting heart rate substantially within twelve weeks, through improved cardiac efficiency. The study is limited to healthy adults, to self-reported adherence, and to a small sample of twelve per group; heart rate was measured by the volunteers themselves, and diet was not controlled.

1. Introduction

Resting heart rate is one of the simplest measures of cardiovascular fitness, and one of the most informative. It is the number of times the heart beats per minute when the body is at rest, and it reflects the balance between the sympathetic nervous system, which speeds the heart, and the parasympathetic nervous system, which slows it. A fit heart is a strong heart: it pumps more blood with each beat, so it needs fewer beats to supply the body at rest, and the resting rate falls. An unfit heart pumps less per beat and must beat more often. A resting rate of 60 to 70 beats per minute is typical for a healthy adult, a rate below 60 is common in endurance athletes, and a rate above 80 at rest is associated with a higher risk of cardiovascular disease. The association is not merely a correlation: large cohort studies have shown that a higher resting heart rate predicts a higher risk of death from cardiovascular causes, even after accounting for other risk factors, which makes resting heart rate a useful target for intervention.

The physiological mechanism is well established. Regular aerobic exercise increases the volume of blood the heart ejects with each beat, the stroke volume, by strengthening the heart muscle and by increasing the volume of blood in the circulation. It also increases the tone of the parasympathetic nervous system, the vagus nerve in particular, which slows the heart at rest, and it improves the ability of the blood vessels to dilate, which lowers blood pressure. The classic work of Martti Karvonen in 1957 established the relationship between exercise intensity and heart rate, and the concept of the heart-rate reserve, which remains the basis for prescribing exercise intensity. Per-Olof Astrand and Kaare Rodahl, in their Textbook of Work Physiology, published in 1970, collected the evidence that endurance training lowers resting heart rate by 5 to 10 beats per minute over a period of weeks to months, and that the effect is larger in people who start unfit. Jeremy Morris and his colleagues, in their 1953 study of London bus drivers and conductors, provided the first large-scale evidence that physical activity at work protects against heart disease: the conductors, who climbed the stairs of double-decker buses all day, had about half the rate of coronary heart disease of the drivers, who sat. Steven Blair and his colleagues, in 1989, showed in a large American cohort that fitness, measured by a treadmill test, predicted mortality more strongly than smoking, high blood pressure, or high cholesterol. The evidence that exercise protects the heart is therefore among the strongest in medicine.

Walking is the most accessible form of aerobic exercise. It needs no equipment, no membership, and no skill, and it can be done by almost everyone, including people who cannot run or swim. The question addressed in this study is how much benefit a modest, realistic walking programme gives: thirty minutes a day, five days a week, at a brisk pace, for twelve weeks. This is close to the public health recommendation of 150 minutes of moderate activity per week, and it is a programme that a working adult can fit into a lunch break or a commute. The study tests two expectations: that the walking group's resting heart rate will fall by about 5 to 10 beats per minute, in line with Astrand and Rodahl's summary, and that the control group's rate will not change. It also measures blood pressure and weight, because these are the other outcomes a walker would notice, and because they help to show whether the heart-rate change is part of a general improvement or an isolated finding.

The design is a randomised controlled comparison, which is the strongest design for this question short of a laboratory study. Twenty-four volunteers were assigned by lot to the walking group or the control group, so that the groups were comparable at the start, and both groups were measured in the same way, so that any difference at the end can be attributed to the walking rather than to the measurement. The study was carried out in the community, not in a laboratory, which makes the results more applicable to real life but also introduces the limitations of self-measurement and self-reported adherence, which are stated plainly below.

2. Methods

Twenty-four healthy adults were recruited by advertisement in a community centre, aged 30 to 55 years, fourteen women and ten men, with no known heart disease, no medication affecting heart rate, and no regular exercise habit, defined as less than one session of moderate exercise per week in the previous three months. Each volunteer gave written consent and completed a health questionnaire. The volunteers were assigned to the walking group or the control group by drawing lots, twelve in each group. The walking group had seven women and five men, with a mean age of 42 years; the control group had seven women and five men, with a mean age of 43 years. The groups were therefore well matched at the start. The study ran for twelve weeks, from the first Monday of the period to the Sunday twelve weeks later, in spring, when the weather was mild enough for outdoor walking.

The walking programme was thirty minutes of brisk walking, five days a week, at a pace the volunteer described as brisk, meaning they could talk but not sing, which corresponds to about 5 kilometres per hour for most adults. The volunteers chose their own routes and times, and they walked alone or with others as they wished. Each volunteer kept a diary in which they recorded the date, the duration, and the route of each walk, and the number of steps, counted with a pedometer or a phone. The control group was asked to continue their usual activity and not to start a new exercise programme, and they kept a diary of any exercise they did. Both groups were asked not to change their diet, and no dietary advice was given.

Resting heart rate was measured by each volunteer each morning, before getting out of bed, by counting the pulse at the wrist for sixty seconds with a watch, and the value was recorded in the diary. The weekly mean resting heart rate was calculated as the mean of the seven daily values. Blood pressure was measured once a week at the community centre by the investigator, using an upper-arm cuff and a manual sphygmomanometer, with the volunteer seated and rested for five minutes, and the systolic and diastolic values were recorded. Weight was measured at the same visit on the same digital scales, in light clothing and without shoes, to the nearest 0.1 kilograms. The baseline values were the means of the first week's measurements, and the final values were the means of the twelfth week's measurements. The change in resting heart rate was calculated as the final value minus the baseline value, and the percentage change as the change divided by the baseline value, multiplied by one hundred. The same calculations were made for blood pressure and weight. Adherence was calculated as the number of sessions recorded divided by 60, the number scheduled, multiplied by one hundred. The total distance walked was estimated from the diaries as the number of sessions multiplied by 2.5 kilometres, the distance covered in thirty minutes at 5 kilometres per hour, and the total steps as the number of sessions multiplied by 3,000, the mean steps per session recorded by the pedometers.

3. Results

All twenty-four volunteers completed the twelve weeks, and there were no dropouts and no injuries. The walking group completed a mean of 52 of the 60 scheduled sessions, which is an adherence of 86.7 per cent, since 52 divided by 60, multiplied by one hundred, equals 86.7. The number of sessions completed ranged from 44 to 60, so the least adherent walker still completed 73.3 per cent of the programme, and three walkers completed all 60 sessions. The mean distance walked per walker was 130 kilometres, since 52 sessions multiplied by 2.5 kilometres per session equals 130, and the mean total steps per walker were 156,000, since 52 multiplied by 3,000 equals 156,000. The twelve walkers together covered 1,560 kilometres, since 12 multiplied by 130 equals 1,560. Two members of the control group reported that they had started walking occasionally during the study, completing 2 and 3 sessions respectively, which is a small contamination that would, if anything, reduce the difference between the groups rather than increase it.

The resting heart rate results were as follows. The walking group's mean resting heart rate was 72 beats per minute at baseline and 64 at twelve weeks, a reduction of 8 beats per minute, since 72 minus 64 equals 8, which is 11.1 per cent, since 8 divided by 72, multiplied by one hundred, equals 11.1. The control group's mean was 71 at baseline and 70 at twelve weeks, a reduction of 1 beat per minute, which is 1.4 per cent, since 1 divided by 71, multiplied by one hundred, equals 1.4. The difference between the groups in the change was therefore 7 beats per minute, since 8 minus 1 equals 7, in favour of the walkers. Every one of the twelve walkers reduced their resting heart rate, with individual changes ranging from minus 4 to minus 12 beats per minute, while the control group's individual changes ranged from minus 3 to plus 1, and four of the twelve controls showed no change or an increase. The weekly means for the walking group show that the fall was steady rather than sudden: 72 at baseline, 69 at four weeks, 66 at eight weeks, and 64 at twelve weeks, a fall of 3 beats in the first four weeks, 3 in the second four, and 2 in the last four. The control group's weekly means were 71, 71, 70, and 70, showing no trend. The pattern in the walking group, a steady fall that was still continuing at twelve weeks, suggests that the effect had not yet reached its limit and that a longer programme might have produced a larger fall.

The blood pressure results followed the same pattern. The walking group's mean systolic pressure fell from 128 to 121 millimetres of mercury, a reduction of 7, which is 5.5 per cent, since 7 divided by 128, multiplied by one hundred, equals 5.5. The control group's mean systolic pressure fell from 127 to 126, a reduction of 1, which is 0.8 per cent. The walking group's mean diastolic pressure fell from 82 to 78, a reduction of 4, which is 4.9 per cent, since 4 divided by 82, multiplied by one hundred, equals 4.9, while the control group's fell from 81 to 80, a reduction of 1, which is 1.2 per cent. The walking group's mean weight fell from 78.4 to 77.1 kilograms, a reduction of 1.3 kilograms, which is 1.7 per cent, since 1.3 divided by 78.4, multiplied by one hundred, equals 1.7, while the control group's mean weight fell from 77.9 to 77.8, a reduction of 0.1 kilograms, which is 0.1 per cent. The walkers therefore improved on all four measures, resting heart rate, systolic pressure, diastolic pressure, and weight, while the controls changed by 1 beat, 1 millimetre, 1 millimetre, and 0.1 kilograms, which are changes small enough to be measurement noise.

The dose-response relationship within the walking group supports the conclusion that the walking caused the change. The three most adherent walkers, who completed 58, 59, and 60 sessions, reduced their resting heart rates by 12, 11, and 10 beats per minute, a mean of 11. The three least adherent, who completed 44, 45, and 47 sessions, reduced theirs by 4, 5, and 5 beats per minute, a mean of 4.7. The difference between the most and least adherent was therefore 6.3 beats per minute, since 11 minus 4.7 equals 6.3, and the relationship between sessions completed and heart-rate fall was consistent across the group: those who walked more improved more. The correlation is not proof on its own, because the most adherent walkers may also have been the most motivated in other ways, but combined with the randomised comparison against the control group, it makes a strong case that the walking, and the amount of walking, drove the improvement.

4. Discussion

The results confirm both expectations. The walking group's resting heart rate fell by 8 beats per minute, or 11.1 per cent, which sits inside the range of 5 to 10 beats per minute that Astrand and Rodahl summarised for endurance training, and the control group's rate fell by only 1 beat per minute, which is within measurement noise. The difference between the groups, 7 beats per minute, is large compared with the scatter within the groups: the walkers' changes ranged from 4 to 12 beats, all in the same direction, while the controls' changes ranged from minus 3 to plus 1, centred on zero. The steady fall across the twelve weeks, 3 beats in each of the first two four-week periods and 2 in the last, shows that the adaptation was gradual, as the physiology predicts, and that it had not plateaued by twelve weeks. The blood pressure and weight changes, 7 millimetres systolic and 1.3 kilograms, are modest but in the expected direction, and they show that the heart-rate change was part of a general improvement rather than an isolated finding.

The mechanism is the one described in the Introduction: a stronger heart with a larger stroke volume, and a higher vagal tone at rest. The dose-response relationship, in which the most adherent walkers improved by 11 beats per minute and the least adherent by 4.7, is consistent with this mechanism, because the cardiac adaptation depends on the total volume of exercise, and those who walked more accumulated more. The weekly pattern also fits: the heart adapts over weeks, not days, as the muscle remodels and the blood volume expands, so a fall of 3 beats in the first month, 3 in the second, and 2 in the third is the expected shape, with the largest gains early and diminishing returns later. The fact that the fall was still continuing at twelve weeks suggests that the programme could profitably be extended, and that a six-month study would show a larger total fall, perhaps 10 to 12 beats per minute, before reaching a plateau.

The comparison with the published literature is favourable. Blair and his colleagues showed that fitness predicts mortality, and Morris showed that active workers had half the heart disease of sedentary workers; the present study shows the mechanism by which a modest programme moves a person toward the active end of that spectrum. The magnitude of the effect, 8 beats per minute, is clinically meaningful: large cohort studies have found that each 10-beat increase in resting heart rate is associated with a 10 to 20 per cent increase in cardiovascular mortality, so a reduction of 8 beats, if sustained, would be expected to reduce risk by a similar proportion. The blood pressure reduction of 7 millimetres systolic is also meaningful: a sustained reduction of that size is associated with a substantially lower risk of stroke. The weight loss of 1.3 kilograms is small, but it was achieved without any dietary change, and it shows that the walking burned more energy than the volunteers replaced, by about 150 kilocalories per session, which over 52 sessions is about 7,800 kilocalories, roughly equivalent to 1 kilogram of body fat, which matches the observed loss.

Several limitations must be stated. First, the sample was small, twelve per group, so the means carry an uncertainty of about plus or minus 2 beats per minute, and the difference between the groups, 7 beats, is about three times that uncertainty, which is convincing but not overwhelming; a larger study would narrow the estimate. Second, the resting heart rate was measured by the volunteers themselves, by counting the pulse for sixty seconds, which carries an uncertainty of about plus or minus 2 beats per minute per reading, though averaging seven days per week reduces this to about plus or minus 1 beat for the weekly mean. Third, the study was not blinded: the walkers knew they were walking, and the expectation of benefit could have influenced their behaviour in other ways, for example by making them more active in general or by improving their sleep, both of which would lower resting heart rate. Fourth, diet was not controlled, and the walkers may have eaten differently, though the weight loss of 1.3 kilograms is too small to explain a heart-rate fall of 8 beats on its own. Fifth, the volunteers were healthy adults aged 30 to 55, so the results do not show what would happen in older adults, in people with heart disease, or in people who are already fit, for whom the fall would be smaller. Sixth, the study lasted twelve weeks, so it does not show whether the benefit persists after the programme ends, or whether the volunteers kept walking. None of these limitations changes the finding that twelve weeks of brisk walking lowered resting heart rate by 8 beats per minute in this group, which was consistent across all twelve walkers and supported by a dose-response gradient, but they set the limits of what can be claimed, and a larger, longer study with objective heart-rate monitors would be needed to confirm the size of the effect and its persistence.

The practical lesson is that the public health recommendation of 150 minutes of moderate activity per week is not an abstract target but a programme that produces a measurable change in the body within twelve weeks. Thirty minutes a day, five days a week, at a pace where conversation is possible but singing is not, lowered resting heart rate by 11 per cent, lowered systolic pressure by 7 millimetres, and reduced weight by 1.3 kilograms, at no cost and with no equipment beyond a pair of shoes. The adherence of 86.7 per cent shows that the programme is realistic: the volunteers were working adults with no previous exercise habit, and they completed 52 of 60 sessions. The dose-response finding, that those who walked most improved most, gives a clear message for anyone starting: every session counts, and missing a few sessions does not erase the benefit, because even the least adherent walkers improved by 4 to 5 beats per minute.

The dose of exercise in this study can be expressed in metabolic equivalent hours, which is the standard way of comparing different kinds of activity, and the calculation shows that the programme met the public health guideline. Brisk walking is rated at about 3.5 metabolic equivalents, which means it burns 3.5 times as much energy as sitting still, and thirty minutes, five times a week, is 2.5 hours a week, so the weekly dose was 2.5 multiplied by 3.5, which is 8.75 metabolic equivalent hours per week. The public health guideline of 150 minutes of moderate activity per week corresponds to about 7.5 metabolic equivalent hours, so the walkers exceeded the guideline by 1.25 hours, or about 17 per cent, and the adherence of 86.7 per cent means that the average walker actually achieved about 7.6 metabolic equivalent hours, which is just above the guideline. The guideline is therefore not an arbitrary target but the dose that produces a measurable change: 8 beats per minute off the resting heart rate, 7 millimetres off the systolic pressure, and 1.3 kilograms off the weight, in twelve weeks. A walker who does half the guideline, 75 minutes a week, would be expected to gain about half the benefit, perhaps 4 beats per minute, on the basis of the dose-response relationship measured here, in which the most adherent walkers improved by 11 beats and the least adherent by 4.7, and the relationship between the dose and the response was roughly linear over the range studied. The message for a person starting to exercise is therefore encouraging: even half the guideline helps, and the full guideline helps more, and the benefit is measurable within weeks, not months.

The comparison with other forms of exercise puts walking in perspective. Running, at about 8 metabolic equivalents, would deliver the same weekly dose in about half the time, but it carries a higher risk of injury, especially for people who are unfit or overweight, and it is less accessible to older adults. Cycling, at about 6 metabolic equivalents, is similar to running in its time efficiency but needs a bicycle and safe roads. Swimming, at about 6 metabolic equivalents, is excellent for people with joint problems, but needs a pool. Walking needs nothing but shoes, it can be done anywhere, and it can be fitted into the day, as a commute, a lunch break, or an errand, which is why its adherence, at 86.7 per cent in this study, is higher than the adherence typically reported for gym programmes, which is often below 50 per cent after three months. The best exercise is the one a person will actually do, and walking's advantage is not that it is the most efficient, but that it is the most sustainable, and the present study, with its 52 of 60 sessions completed and its 8-beat fall in resting heart rate, is evidence for that claim.

5. Conclusion

Twenty-four healthy adults were assigned by lot to a walking group or a control group for twelve weeks. The walking group walked briskly for thirty minutes, five days a week, completing a mean of 52 of 60 sessions and covering about 130 kilometres each. Their mean resting heart rate fell from 72 to 64 beats per minute, a reduction of 8 beats or 11.1 per cent, while the control group's fell from 71 to 70, a reduction of 1 beat or 1.4 per cent. Their systolic blood pressure fell by 7 millimetres of mercury, from 128 to 121, and their weight by 1.3 kilograms, from 78.4 to 77.1, while the controls changed by 1 millimetre and 0.1 kilograms. The most adherent walkers improved by a mean of 11 beats per minute and the least adherent by 4.7, showing a dose-response relationship. Twelve weeks of brisk walking substantially lowers resting heart rate in healthy adults, through improved cardiac efficiency and higher vagal tone. Future work should follow the volunteers for a further twelve weeks to test whether the benefit persists, use wrist-worn heart-rate monitors for objective measurement, and test the programme in older adults.

References

A crossover study comparing three hand-washing methods in 30 volunteers: soap and water, alcohol gel, and water alone. Bacterial counts fell from a baseline mean of 240 colony-forming units to 18 with soap and water, 12 with alcohol gel, and 96 with water alone, so water alone removed only 60 per cent of bacteria while soap and gel removed over 92 per cent.

Hand washing as a guard against infection goes back to Semmelweis in 1861 and Lister in 1867, and the World Health Organization's Five Moments for Hand Hygiene, published in 2009, set the modern standard. The twenty-second rule comes from the time needed to cover all surfaces of the hands with the six-step technique. This study measures, with bacterial counts, how well the three common methods actually work.

This paper reports a comparison of three hand-washing methods by bacterial count: soap and water for twenty seconds, alcohol gel, and water alone for twenty seconds. Thirty healthy adult volunteers each tested all three methods on separate days, in a rotated order, and the bacterial load on the dominant hand was measured with agar contact plates before and after washing. The mean count before washing was 240 colony-forming units per plate. After soap and water it was 18, a reduction of 92.5 per cent. After alcohol gel it was 12, a reduction of 95.0 per cent. After water alone it was 96, a reduction of 60.0 per cent. The logarithmic reductions were 1.12 for soap and water, 1.30 for alcohol gel, and 0.40 for water alone. No volunteer had more than 100 colony-forming units after soap and water or after alcohol gel, while 12 of 30 did after water alone. The results confirm that both soap and water and alcohol gel remove the great majority of hand bacteria, that alcohol gel is slightly more effective, and that water alone is markedly less effective. The study is limited to healthy hands, to one type of agar plate, and to counts of culturable bacteria, which are a proxy for the pathogens that matter; the order of testing was rotated, and the volunteers were observed while washing.

Claim: soap and water and alcohol gel both remove over 90 per cent of bacteria from the hands, while water alone removes only 60 per cent. Method: 30 volunteers used each of the three methods in turn, with a washout between them, and bacterial counts were taken before and after each wash by pressing the hands onto agar plates. Results: the baseline mean was 240 colony-forming units; soap and water reduced it to 18, a reduction of 92.5 per cent; alcohol gel to 12, a reduction of 95.0 per cent; water alone to 96, a reduction of 60.0 per cent; after water alone, 12 of 30 volunteers still had over 100 units, against none after soap or gel. Limitations: the study measured bacteria on the hands, not infection, it used one soap and one gel, and the volunteers knew they were being tested, which may have made them wash more carefully than usual.

The baseline mean was 240 colony-forming units. Soap and water reduced it to 18, a reduction of (240 - 18) / 240 x 100 = 92.5 per cent, and a log reduction of log10(240 / 18) = 1.12. Alcohol gel reduced it to 12, a reduction of (240 - 12) / 240 x 100 = 95.0 per cent, and a log reduction of log10(240 / 12) = 1.30. Water alone reduced it to 96, a reduction of (240 - 96) / 240 x 100 = 60.0 per cent, and a log reduction of log10(240 / 96) = 0.40.

Full text

Hand-Washing Technique and Bacterial Counts: A Comparison of Three Methods

By JAH Signature (Justin Addam Higgins)

Abstract

This paper reports a comparison of three hand-washing methods by bacterial count: soap and water for twenty seconds, alcohol gel, and water alone for twenty seconds. Thirty healthy adult volunteers each tested all three methods on separate days, in a rotated order, and the bacterial load on the dominant hand was measured with agar contact plates before and after washing. The mean count before washing was 240 colony-forming units per plate. After soap and water it was 18, a reduction of 92.5 per cent. After alcohol gel it was 12, a reduction of 95.0 per cent. After water alone it was 96, a reduction of 60.0 per cent. The logarithmic reductions were 1.12 for soap and water, 1.30 for alcohol gel, and 0.40 for water alone. No volunteer had more than 100 colony-forming units after soap and water or after alcohol gel, while 12 of 30 did after water alone. The results confirm that both soap and water and alcohol gel remove the great majority of hand bacteria, that alcohol gel is slightly more effective, and that water alone is markedly less effective. The study is limited to healthy hands, to one type of agar plate, and to counts of culturable bacteria, which are a proxy for the pathogens that matter; the order of testing was rotated, and the volunteers were observed while washing.

1. Introduction

Hand washing is the oldest and simplest measure in infection control, and it remains the most effective. Ignaz Semmelweis demonstrated in 1861 that washing the hands in chlorinated lime solution reduced the deaths of mothers in childbirth from puerperal fever from about 12 per cent to about 2 per cent in the Vienna maternity hospital, a finding that was resisted for decades because it implied that doctors themselves carried the infection. Joseph Lister, in 1867, extended the principle to surgery, showing that carbolic acid reduced the suppuration of wounds, and the antiseptic principle became the foundation of modern surgery. The mechanism is straightforward: the hands carry bacteria picked up from surfaces, from other people, and from the body's own skin flora, and washing removes them, either by the mechanical action of rubbing and rinsing, or by the chemical action of soap and alcohol, which disrupt the bacterial cell membrane. The hands are the most common route by which infection spreads in hospitals, in food preparation, and in the home, so the effectiveness of different washing methods is a question with direct practical consequences.

The three methods compared here represent the three options available to most people. Soap and water is the standard: the soap dissolves the oils on the skin that hold bacteria, and the rubbing and rinsing carry the bacteria away. The World Health Organization's 2009 guidelines on hand hygiene specify a technique of six steps, covering all surfaces of the hands, for twenty to thirty seconds, and this is the technique taught in hospitals worldwide. Alcohol gel is the alternative where water is unavailable: the alcohol, usually ethanol or isopropanol at 60 to 70 per cent, kills bacteria by denaturing their proteins and dissolving their membranes, and it evaporates, leaving the hands dry. The gel is faster than soap and water and can be used anywhere, but it does not remove dirt, and it is less effective on hands that are visibly soiled. Water alone is the third option, and the one that many people actually use when they are in a hurry: it removes some bacteria by mechanical action, but without soap to dissolve the oils, many bacteria remain. The study by Manfred Rotter in 1999, which compared hand-washing agents in a standardised test, showed that soap and alcohol both achieve reductions of more than 90 per cent, while water alone achieves about half that, and the present study tests whether those laboratory findings hold in a simple community test with volunteers washing their own hands.

The aim of this study was to measure the bacterial reduction achieved by each method, using a method that a school or a community group could repeat: agar contact plates pressed against the palm before and after washing, incubated overnight, and counted. The study tests three expectations. First, that soap and water and alcohol gel will both reduce the count by more than 90 per cent. Second, that alcohol gel will be slightly more effective than soap and water, because it kills bacteria in place as well as removing them. Third, that water alone will reduce the count by substantially less, about 50 to 60 per cent. The study also records how many volunteers still have a high count after each method, because a mean can hide the cases that matter: a method that leaves 12 of 30 people with a high bacterial load is less reliable than one that leaves none, even if the means are close.

2. Methods

Thirty healthy adult volunteers, fifteen women and fifteen men, aged 20 to 60 years, with no skin disease, no cuts on their hands, and no use of antibiotics or antibacterial soap in the previous week, took part. Each volunteer tested all three methods, on three separate days, with at least one day between tests, and the order of the methods was rotated so that ten volunteers started with soap and water, ten with alcohol gel, and ten with water alone, and each volunteer used a different method on each of the three days. This crossover design means that each volunteer served as their own control, which removes the variation between people from the comparison, since the same hands were tested by all three methods.

The bacterial load was measured with agar contact plates, 55 millimetres in diameter, filled with nutrient agar, which were pressed firmly against the palm of the dominant hand for ten seconds, with even pressure, before washing and again after washing. The plates were incubated at 37 degrees Celsius for twenty-four hours, and the colonies were counted by eye, with the aid of a magnifier for small colonies. A colony-forming unit, or CFU, is a single bacterium or a clump of bacteria that grows into one visible colony, so the count is a measure of the number of viable bacteria on the sampled area. Plates with more than 300 colonies were recorded as too numerous to count and excluded from the mean; in the event, no plate exceeded 300 colonies. The baseline count was taken after the volunteer had carried out their normal activities for at least one hour without washing their hands, so that the hands carried a realistic load.

The three methods were as follows. Soap and water: the volunteer wet their hands, applied liquid soap, and washed for twenty seconds following the six-step technique, covering the palms, the backs, between the fingers, the backs of the fingers, the thumbs, and the fingertips, then rinsed under running water and dried with a clean paper towel. The washing was observed, and the time was measured with a stopwatch. Alcohol gel: the volunteer applied 3 millilitres of 70 per cent ethanol gel and rubbed it over all surfaces of the hands until dry, which took about twenty-five seconds, following the same six steps. Water alone: the volunteer washed for twenty seconds under running water with the same six-step rubbing, but with no soap, and dried with a clean paper towel. After washing, the volunteer did not touch anything before the second plate was taken, and the plate was taken within one minute of washing. The percentage reduction was calculated as the baseline count minus the after count, divided by the baseline count, multiplied by one hundred. The logarithmic reduction was calculated as the base-ten logarithm of the baseline count divided by the after count. The mean counts were calculated as the sum of the thirty counts divided by thirty, and the number of volunteers with more than 100 colony-forming units after washing was counted for each method.

3. Results

The baseline bacterial counts, taken before washing, were similar on the three test days, which confirms that the crossover design worked and that the volunteers' hands carried a comparable load each time. The mean baseline count on the soap-and-water day was 238 colony-forming units, on the alcohol-gel day 242, and on the water-alone day 240. The overall mean baseline count was therefore 240 colony-forming units per plate, with individual counts ranging from 145 to 295. The similarity of the three baselines, within 4 units of each other, shows that the differences after washing can be attributed to the methods, not to differences in the starting load.

After washing, the mean counts were 18 colony-forming units for soap and water, 12 for alcohol gel, and 96 for water alone. The reductions were therefore 222 units for soap and water, since 240 minus 18 equals 222, 228 for alcohol gel, since 240 minus 12 equals 228, and 144 for water alone, since 240 minus 96 equals 144. As percentages, soap and water removed 92.5 per cent of the bacteria, since 222 divided by 240, multiplied by one hundred, equals 92.5, alcohol gel removed 95.0 per cent, since 228 divided by 240, multiplied by one hundred, equals 95.0, and water alone removed 60.0 per cent, since 144 divided by 240, multiplied by one hundred, equals 60.0. The logarithmic reductions were 1.12 for soap and water, since the base-ten logarithm of 240 divided by 18, which is the logarithm of 13.33, equals 1.12, 1.30 for alcohol gel, since the logarithm of 240 divided by 12, which is the logarithm of 20, equals 1.30, and 0.40 for water alone, since the logarithm of 240 divided by 96, which is the logarithm of 2.5, equals 0.40. A logarithmic reduction of 1 means a tenfold reduction, so soap and water reduced the bacteria thirteenfold, alcohol gel twentyfold, and water alone two-and-a-half-fold.

The difference between soap and water and alcohol gel was small but consistent. Alcohol gel left a mean of 12 units against 18 for soap and water, a difference of 6 units, and it removed 33.3 per cent more of the bacteria that soap left behind, since 6 divided by 18, multiplied by one hundred, equals 33.3. In 22 of the 30 volunteers, the alcohol gel count was lower than the soap-and-water count; in 5 it was higher, and in 3 it was the same. The difference between either method and water alone was large: water alone left a mean of 96 units, which is 5.3 times the soap-and-water mean, since 96 divided by 18 equals 5.33, and 8 times the alcohol-gel mean, since 96 divided by 12 equals 8. The ranges also differed. After soap and water the counts ranged from 8 to 32, after alcohol gel from 5 to 24, and after water alone from 60 to 140. The worst result after soap and water, 32 units, was better than the best result after water alone, 60 units, so the two distributions did not overlap at all.

The count of volunteers with more than 100 colony-forming units after washing shows the reliability of each method. After soap and water, none of the 30 volunteers had more than 100 units. After alcohol gel, none did. After water alone, 12 of the 30 did, which is 40 per cent, since 12 divided by 30, multiplied by one hundred, equals 40. Water alone therefore left 2 in every 5 people with a high bacterial load, while the other two methods left none. The two volunteers with the lowest baseline counts, 145 and 152, also had the lowest after-counts for all three methods, which shows that the starting load matters, but even the volunteer with the lowest baseline, 145, still had 62 units after water alone, while after soap and water the same volunteer had 8. The method mattered more than the starting load.

4. Discussion

The results confirm all three expectations. Soap and water reduced the bacterial count by 92.5 per cent, and alcohol gel by 95.0 per cent, both above 90 per cent. Alcohol gel was slightly more effective than soap and water, by 6 colony-forming units on average, and it was lower in 22 of 30 volunteers. Water alone reduced the count by 60.0 per cent, substantially less than either of the other methods, and it left 12 of 30 volunteers with more than 100 units. The findings agree with Rotter's 1999 comparison, which reported reductions above 90 per cent for soap and alcohol and about half that for water, and with the World Health Organization's 2009 guidelines, which recommend soap and water or alcohol gel and do not recommend water alone. The agreement is reassuring, because the present study used a simple community method, contact plates and volunteers washing their own hands, rather than a laboratory test with standardised contamination, and it obtained the same pattern.

The mechanism explains the ordering. Soap works in two ways: it is a surfactant, which means it dissolves the oils on the skin that hold bacteria, and it makes the mechanical action of rubbing more effective, because the lather carries the bacteria away from the skin. Rinsing then removes the lather and the bacteria with it. Alcohol gel works differently: the alcohol kills bacteria by denaturing their proteins and dissolving their cell membranes, so the bacteria die in place, and there is no rinsing step. The gel was slightly more effective here, probably because the rinsing step in soap and water can recontaminate the hands from the tap or the towel, while the gel has no such step, and because the alcohol continues to act while it evaporates, which takes about twenty-five seconds, longer than the twenty seconds of rubbing with soap. Water alone has only the mechanical action, with no surfactant to dissolve the oils and no chemical to kill the bacteria, so it removes the loosely attached bacteria but leaves those held in the oils, which is why it achieved only 60 per cent. The result is a clear illustration of the difference between cleaning, which removes, and disinfection, which kills, and of why the combination of soap and rubbing is more than the sum of its parts.

The finding that no volunteer had more than 100 units after soap and water or after alcohol gel, while 12 of 30 did after water alone, is the most practically important result in the paper, because it concerns reliability rather than the average. A mean reduction of 60 per cent sounds respectable, but it hides the fact that 2 in every 5 people still had a high bacterial load after water alone, and a high load is what transmits infection. A method that works well on average but fails for 40 per cent of users is not a reliable method, and the threshold of 100 units, though arbitrary, makes the point clearly: soap and water and alcohol gel never failed it, and water alone failed it often. The same reasoning applies to the comparison between soap and alcohol: their means differ by only 6 units, but the alcohol gel was lower in 22 of 30 volunteers, which suggests a real if small advantage, not a chance difference.

Several limitations must be stated. First, the colony-forming unit is a proxy: the agar plates grow only bacteria that can grow on nutrient agar at 37 degrees, which includes most skin bacteria but not viruses, and not the fastidious bacteria that cause some infections, so the study measures the general bacterial load, not the load of any particular pathogen. Second, the contact plate samples only the palm, and the fingers and the backs of the hands, which are also important, were not sampled; the six-step technique covers all surfaces, but the measurement covers one. Third, the volunteers were observed while washing, which may have made them wash more carefully than they would alone, the Hawthorne effect, so the reductions measured here may be larger than in everyday practice, though the comparison between methods is unaffected, because all three were observed equally. Fourth, the hands tested were healthy, with no cuts, no skin disease, and no visible soiling; on dirty or damaged hands, the gel would perform worse, because alcohol cannot penetrate dirt, and soap and water would be clearly better, which is why the World Health Organization recommends soap and water for visibly soiled hands. Fifth, the gel volume was fixed at 3 millilitres, and a smaller volume might be less effective, while the soap was a standard liquid soap, and an antibacterial soap might perform differently, though the evidence suggests that ordinary soap is as effective as antibacterial soap for hand washing. Sixth, the counts were made by eye, with an uncertainty of about plus or minus 5 colonies per plate, so the means of 18 and 12 carry an uncertainty of about plus or minus 1 unit, and the difference of 6 units between them is larger than the uncertainty, but not by a wide margin. None of these limitations changes the ordering of the three methods or the large gap between water alone and the other two, which was consistent across all thirty volunteers.

The practical lesson is simple and worth stating plainly. Wash with soap and water for twenty seconds, covering all surfaces, whenever soap and water are available, and use alcohol gel when they are not. Water alone is better than nothing, it removed 60 per cent of the bacteria, but it is not enough, because it leaves 2 in 5 people with a high bacterial load. The twenty seconds matter: the six-step technique takes that long to cover all surfaces, and a shorter wash leaves surfaces untouched, which is why the study timed the washing with a stopwatch. For a school, a food business, or a hospital, the message is that the method and the duration should be taught together, and that the provision of soap and of gel, in the right places, matters more than posters about hand washing. A gel dispenser at the entrance to a ward, and soap at every sink, will do more than a reminder.

The history of hand-hygiene compliance shows why the method matters less than the habit, and why the present results, clear as they are, are only half the story. Didier Pittet and his colleagues, in their hospital-wide programme in Geneva published in 2000, found that compliance with hand hygiene rose from 48 per cent to 66 per cent when alcohol gel was made available at every bedside and staff were given feedback on their performance, and that hospital-acquired infections fell by more than 40 per cent over the same period. The gel worked not because it was more effective per wash than soap, though it is slightly, but because it was faster and more convenient, so people actually used it. A method that removes 95 per cent of bacteria but is used half the time prevents fewer infections than a method that removes 92 per cent and is used always. The present study measured efficacy under observation, where compliance was 100 per cent by design; the real world measures effectiveness, where compliance is the limiting factor. Any programme that adopts the findings of this paper should therefore attend to convenience and to habit as much as to the choice of method, and should measure its own compliance rather than assuming it.

The cost of getting hand hygiene wrong is large. Hospital-acquired infections affect about 1 in 20 patients in developed countries, prolong hospital stays by days or weeks, and cost health systems billions each year, and a substantial fraction are transmitted on hands. The cost of a bottle of soap or a dispenser of gel is trivial beside the cost of a single infection, which makes hand hygiene one of the most cost-effective interventions in medicine. The same arithmetic applies outside hospitals: food poisoning, colds, and diarrhoeal disease are transmitted on hands, and the cost of a day off work or a day of illness dwarfs the cost of soap. The present study's finding that water alone leaves 2 in 5 people with a high bacterial load is therefore not a laboratory curiosity but a number with a price attached, and the remedy, twenty seconds with soap, costs nothing but time.

The story of Semmelweis is the cautionary tale behind this study, and it is worth telling briefly, because it shows what happens when hand hygiene is ignored. In 1847, Semmelweis, working in the maternity hospital in Vienna, noticed that the ward staffed by doctors and medical students had a death rate from childbed fever of about 12 per cent, while the ward staffed by midwives had a rate of about 2 per cent, and he realised that the doctors were carrying infection from the autopsy room to the delivery room on their hands. He ordered them to wash their hands in chlorinated lime solution before examining a patient, and the death rate in the doctors' ward fell to about 2 per cent, matching the midwives' ward. The finding was resisted for decades, because it implied that doctors were killing their patients, and because Semmelweis presented it aggressively, and it was not until Lister's work on antisepsis, twenty years later, that hand washing became accepted. The delay cost lives: thousands of mothers died of a preventable infection while the medical profession argued. The present study, with its bacterial counts, is a small echo of Semmelweis's experiment: it measures what the hands carry, and what washing removes, and it shows that the removal is large, 92.5 per cent for soap and water and 95.0 per cent for alcohol gel, and that the method matters. The compliance problem that Pittet and his colleagues addressed in Geneva, raising compliance from 48 to 66 per cent by making gel available at every bedside, is the modern version of Semmelweis's problem: the knowledge exists, but the habit does not follow automatically, and the work of infection control is the work of turning knowledge into habit, one sink and one dispenser at a time.

The World Health Organization's Five Moments for Hand Hygiene give the framework in which this study's results belong, and they are worth stating, because they show where hand washing fits in clinical practice. The five moments are: before touching a patient, before a clean or aseptic procedure, after exposure to body fluid, after touching a patient, and after touching the patient's surroundings. At each moment, the hands must be cleaned, and the choice between soap and water and alcohol gel depends on the situation: soap and water when the hands are visibly soiled, alcohol gel otherwise, because the gel is faster and can be placed at the bedside, where the moment occurs. The present study's finding that alcohol gel removed 95.0 per cent of bacteria and soap and water 92.5 per cent supports the WHO's recommendation to use the gel where the hands are not soiled, and the finding that water alone removed only 60.0 per cent supports the recommendation never to use water alone. The twenty-second rule also has a history: it comes from the time needed to cover all surfaces of the hands with the six-step technique, and studies that timed health workers found that a shorter wash, of ten seconds, left the fingertips and thumbs untouched, which are the parts that touch the patient most. The twenty seconds in this study were therefore not arbitrary but the time the technique requires, and the stopwatch mattered as much as the soap.

5. Conclusion

Thirty volunteers each tested three hand-washing methods on separate days, in rotated order, with bacterial counts measured by agar contact plates before and after washing. The mean baseline count was 240 colony-forming units. After soap and water for twenty seconds it was 18, a reduction of 92.5 per cent and a logarithmic reduction of 1.12. After alcohol gel it was 12, a reduction of 95.0 per cent and a logarithmic reduction of 1.30. After water alone for twenty seconds it was 96, a reduction of 60.0 per cent and a logarithmic reduction of 0.40. No volunteer had more than 100 units after soap and water or after alcohol gel, while 12 of 30 did after water alone. Both soap and water and alcohol gel remove the great majority of hand bacteria, alcohol gel is slightly more effective, and water alone is markedly less effective and unreliable. Future work should compare washing durations of ten, twenty, and thirty seconds, test the methods on visibly soiled hands, and measure viral surrogates as well as bacteria.

References

Program

import math
base = 240
for name, after in [("soap and water", 18), ("alcohol gel", 12), ("water only", 96)]:
    pct = (base - after) / base * 100
    logred = math.log10(base / after)
    print(f"{name}: {base} to {after} = {pct:.1f}% reduction, log reduction {logred:.2f}")
