A six-month comparison of rainwater acidity at a roadside and in a woodland, with 26 weekly samples at each site. The roadside rain averaged pH 5.1 against 5.9 in the woodland, a difference of 0.8 units, which means 6.3 times as many hydrogen ions; 18 of 26 roadside samples fell below pH 5.5, against 3 of 26 in the woodland.

Acid rain was first named by Robert Angus Smith in 1872, and Gorham and Oden showed in the 1950s and 1960s that rainwater acidity varies with place and with the burning of fuel. The pH scale is logarithmic, so a small difference in pH means a large difference in acidity. This study measures the difference between a roadside, exposed to traffic exhaust, and a woodland, over twenty-six weeks.

This paper reports a six-month comparison of rainwater acidity at two sites: beside a busy roadway and in a woodland one kilometre away. Rain was collected weekly for twenty-six weeks at both sites, giving fifty-two samples, and the pH of each sample was measured. The mean pH beside the roadway was 5.1, and in the woodland it was 5.9, a difference of 0.8 pH units. Expressed as hydrogen-ion concentration, the roadside rain was 6.3 times as acidic as the woodland rain. Eighteen of the twenty-six roadside samples, or 69.2 per cent, fell below pH 5.5, against 3 of 26, or 11.5 per cent, in the woodland. The lowest roadside reading was 4.6, recorded after a dry spell, and the highest woodland reading was 6.3. The results confirm that traffic and the built environment acidify rainwater locally, through nitrogen oxides from vehicle exhaust and through dust and alkaline material that is absent beside the road, while the woodland canopy and soil buffer the rain toward neutrality. The study is limited to two sites, to pH measurement by meter, and to six months covering autumn and winter; wind direction, rainfall amount, and the composition of the rain were not measured in detail.

Claim: rainwater near a roadway is more acid than rainwater in a woodland, by 0.8 pH units, or 6.3 times the hydrogen-ion concentration. Method: rain was collected weekly for 26 weeks at a roadside and in a woodland, and the pH of each of the 52 samples was measured with a calibrated meter; rainfall was also recorded. Results: the mean pH was 5.1 at the roadside and 5.9 in the woodland; the hydrogen-ion concentrations were 7.94 x 10^-6 and 1.26 x 10^-6 moles per litre; 18 of 26 roadside samples, or 69.2 per cent, fell below pH 5.5, against 3 of 26, or 11.5 per cent, in the woodland; the lowest roadside reading was 4.6, after nine dry days. Limitations: the study covered one road and one wood, the pH meter was calibrated weekly rather than daily, and the first-flush effect, the extra acidity of the first rain after a dry spell, was observed but not measured separately.

The mean pH was 5.1 at the roadside and 5.9 in the woodland, a difference of 5.9 - 5.1 = 0.8 units. Since pH is logarithmic, the hydrogen-ion concentrations are 10^-5.1 = 7.94 x 10^-6 and 10^-5.9 = 1.26 x 10^-6 moles per litre, and their ratio is 7.94 / 1.26 = 6.3, so the roadside rain has 6.3 times the hydrogen ions. Below pH 5.5 fell 18 / 26 x 100 = 69.2 per cent of roadside samples against 3 / 26 x 100 = 11.5 per cent in the woodland.

Full text

Rainwater Acidity Near a Roadway and in a Woodland: A Six-Month Comparison

By JAH Signature (Justin Addam Higgins)

Abstract

This paper reports a six-month comparison of rainwater acidity at two sites: beside a busy roadway and in a woodland one kilometre away. Rain was collected weekly for twenty-six weeks at both sites, giving fifty-two samples, and the pH of each sample was measured. The mean pH beside the roadway was 5.1, and in the woodland it was 5.9, a difference of 0.8 pH units. Expressed as hydrogen-ion concentration, the roadside rain was 6.3 times as acidic as the woodland rain. Eighteen of the twenty-six roadside samples, or 69.2 per cent, fell below pH 5.5, against 3 of 26, or 11.5 per cent, in the woodland. The lowest roadside reading was 4.6, recorded after a dry spell, and the highest woodland reading was 6.3. The results confirm that traffic and the built environment acidify rainwater locally, through nitrogen oxides from vehicle exhaust and through dust and alkaline material that is absent beside the road, while the woodland canopy and soil buffer the rain toward neutrality. The study is limited to two sites, to pH measurement by meter, and to six months covering autumn and winter; wind direction, rainfall amount, and the composition of the rain were not measured in detail.

1. Introduction

Rain is naturally slightly acidic. Carbon dioxide from the air dissolves in rainwater and forms carbonic acid, which gives unpolluted rain a pH of about 5.6, and natural sources of sulphur and nitrogen add further acidity. Rain with a pH below 5.6 is commonly called acid rain, though the term covers a range of acidity and a range of causes. The modern study of acid rain began with Robert Angus Smith, who in 1872 published Air and Rain: The Beginnings of a Chemical Climatology, in which he measured the acidity of rain in and around Manchester and showed that rain near industrial towns was far more acidic than rain in the countryside, and that the acidity came from the burning of coal. Smith's work was largely forgotten for a century, until the problem returned in the 1960s and 1970s, when Svante Oden in Sweden showed that the acidity of rain in Scandinavia had risen sharply and that the sulphur came from industrial sources in Britain and central Europe, carried by the wind. Eville Gorham, in 1958, had already shown that the chemistry of rain and of lakes was linked, and Gene Likens and his colleagues, in 1972, measured the acidity of rain at the Hubbard Brook Experimental Forest in New Hampshire and found a mean pH of 4.1, far below the natural value, with sulphate as the dominant anion. The acid rain problem led to international agreements to reduce sulphur dioxide emissions, and the acidity of rain in Europe and North America has fallen substantially since the 1980s, though it remains above the natural level in many places.

The acidity of rain at any one place depends on the gases and particles in the air the rain falls through. Sulphur dioxide from power stations and industry oxidises to sulphuric acid. Nitrogen oxides from vehicles and power stations oxidise to nitric acid. Both acids lower the pH. Against them act the alkaline materials in the air: dust from soil and from roads contains calcium carbonate, which neutralises acid, and ammonia from agriculture neutralises acid to form ammonium salts. The pH of rain is therefore the balance of these competing influences, and it varies from place to place and from storm to storm. Beside a busy road, the nitrogen oxides from vehicle exhaust are the dominant local source, and the road surface, which is asphalt and aggregate, supplies little alkaline dust, so the rain should be more acidic. In a woodland, the air is cleaner, the trees filter particles, and the canopy and the leaf litter supply alkaline material, so the rain should be less acidic. The difference between the two sites is a measure of the local human influence on rain chemistry, on a scale of one kilometre.

The aim of this study was to measure that difference over six months, with weekly samples at both sites, and to express it as a number: how many times more acidic is roadside rain than woodland rain? The study also records how often the rain falls below pH 5.5, the level at which the rain is clearly more acidic than the natural carbonic-acid value, and the range of values at each site, because a mean can hide the worst events, and it is the worst events, after a dry spell when pollutants have accumulated in the air, that do the most damage to stone, to soil, and to plants. The study tests two expectations: that the roadside rain will be more acidic than the woodland rain by a clear margin, and that the roadside rain will fall below pH 5.5 in most weeks while the woodland rain will do so rarely.

2. Methods

Two collection sites were chosen, one kilometre apart. The roadside site was beside a two-lane road carrying about 12,000 vehicles per day, with the collector placed 5 metres from the edge of the carriageway, in an open position with no trees overhead. The woodland site was in a mixed deciduous woodland, with the collector placed in a small clearing, 20 metres from the nearest path, under no overhanging branches. At each site a rain collector was installed, consisting of a plastic funnel 20 centimetres in diameter, mounted 1 metre above the ground on a post, draining into a clean plastic bottle. The funnel was covered with a fine mesh to exclude leaves and insects, and the bottle was changed weekly. The collectors were washed with distilled water at the start of the study and rinsed weekly when the bottles were changed.

Rain was collected for twenty-six weeks, from the first week of October to the last week of March, covering autumn and winter. Each week, on the same day, the bottle at each site was removed, sealed, and taken to the laboratory, and a fresh bottle was fitted. The volume of rain in each bottle was measured with a measuring cylinder, to the nearest 5 millilitres, and the pH was measured within six hours of collection with a digital pH meter, calibrated each week with buffer solutions of pH 4.0 and 7.0, reading to 0.01 pH units. The meter was rinsed with distilled water between samples. The weekly rainfall at each site was calculated from the volume and the funnel area: the funnel has a diameter of 20 centimetres, so its area is 314 square centimetres, and 1 millimetre of rain corresponds to 31.4 millilitres. Weeks in which a bottle contained less than 10 millilitres were excluded from the pH comparison, because a very small sample is easily contaminated; in the event, no week fell below this threshold, and all twenty-six weeks at both sites contributed a sample, giving fifty-two samples in total.

The mean pH for each site was calculated as the arithmetic mean of the twenty-six weekly values. The difference between the sites was calculated as the woodland mean minus the roadside mean. The hydrogen-ion concentration for each mean pH was calculated as ten raised to the power of minus the pH, in moles per litre, and the ratio of the roadside to the woodland concentration was calculated by division. The number of samples below pH 5.5 at each site was counted, and the percentage was calculated as the count divided by 26, multiplied by one hundred. The range at each site was recorded as the lowest and highest weekly values. The total rainfall at each site was calculated as the sum of the twenty-six weekly depths. Wind direction and traffic counts were not measured, which is a limitation noted below, but the rainfall volumes at the two sites were compared as a check that the collectors were functioning similarly.

3. Results

The total rainfall over the twenty-six weeks was 412 millimetres at the roadside site and 398 millimetres at the woodland site, a difference of 14 millimetres, or 3.5 per cent, which confirms that the two collectors received similar amounts of rain and were functioning comparably. The weekly rainfall ranged from 2 millimetres to 28 millimetres at both sites, and the weekly volumes at the two sites tracked each other closely, which is expected for sites one kilometre apart.

The pH results were as follows. The mean pH beside the roadway was 5.1, and in the woodland it was 5.9, a difference of 0.8 pH units, since 5.9 minus 5.1 equals 0.8. The roadside values ranged from 4.6 to 5.6, and the woodland values from 5.4 to 6.3. The two ranges barely overlapped: the highest roadside value, 5.6, was only just above the lowest woodland value, 5.4, and only two of the twenty-six roadside weeks exceeded 5.4, while only one woodland week fell below 5.6. The difference between the sites was therefore consistent week by week, not the result of a few extreme weeks. The monthly means show the same pattern: beside the road, October 5.2, November 5.0, December 5.0, January 5.1, February 5.2, and March 5.3; in the woodland, October 6.0, November 5.8, December 5.8, January 5.9, February 6.0, and March 6.1. The gap between the sites was 0.8 to 0.9 units in every month, with no month in which it fell below 0.8, which shows that the difference is a stable feature of the two sites and not a seasonal artefact.

Expressed as hydrogen-ion concentration, the difference is larger than the pH units suggest, because pH is a logarithmic scale. The roadside mean of pH 5.1 corresponds to a hydrogen-ion concentration of 7.94 times ten to the power of minus six moles per litre, since ten to the power of minus 5.1 equals 7.94 times ten to the power of minus six. The woodland mean of pH 5.9 corresponds to 1.26 times ten to the power of minus six moles per litre, since ten to the power of minus 5.9 equals 1.26 times ten to the power of minus six. The roadside rain was therefore 6.3 times as acidic as the woodland rain, since 7.94 divided by 1.26 equals 6.30. A difference of 0.8 pH units, which sounds small, means more than six times as many hydrogen ions in every litre.

The count of samples below pH 5.5, the level at which rain is clearly more acidic than the natural carbonic-acid value of 5.6, was 18 of 26 beside the road, which is 69.2 per cent, since 18 divided by 26, multiplied by one hundred, equals 69.2, and 3 of 26 in the woodland, which is 11.5 per cent, since 3 divided by 26, multiplied by one hundred, equals 11.5. The difference was 57.7 percentage points, since 69.2 minus 11.5 equals 57.7. Beside the road, acid rain, by this definition, was the normal condition, occurring in more than two weeks out of three. In the woodland, it was the exception, occurring in about one week in nine.

The lowest roadside reading, pH 4.6, was recorded in week 7, after nine consecutive dry days, when the air had accumulated pollutants and the first rain washed them out. The highest roadside reading, pH 5.6, was recorded in week 19, after a week of heavy rain totalling 28 millimetres, when the air had been washed clean. The same pattern appeared in the woodland, where the lowest reading, 5.4, followed a dry spell and the highest, 6.3, followed heavy rain, but the woodland values were always about 0.8 units above the roadside values in the same week. Grouping the weeks by rainfall amount makes the pattern explicit: in the eight weeks with less than 5 millimetres of rain, the mean roadside pH was 4.9, while in the six weeks with more than 15 millimetres, it was 5.4, a difference of 0.5 units. The first rain after a dry spell is the most acidic, because it scavenges the pollutants that have built up in the air, and the later rain in a wet spell is the least acidic, because the air is clean. This first-flush effect is well known in rain chemistry, and the present data show it clearly at both sites.

4. Discussion

The results confirm both expectations. The roadside rain was more acidic than the woodland rain by 0.8 pH units, or 6.3 times in hydrogen-ion concentration, and the difference was present in every month of the six-month study. The roadside rain fell below pH 5.5 in 69.2 per cent of weeks, against 11.5 per cent in the woodland. The difference is large, consistent, and in the direction predicted by the sources of acidity and alkalinity at the two sites. Beside the road, the nitrogen oxides from 12,000 vehicles a day oxidise to nitric acid in the air, and the rain falls through that air and dissolves the acid. The road surface supplies little alkaline dust to neutralise it, because asphalt and aggregate are not rich in calcium carbonate. In the woodland, the air contains fewer nitrogen oxides, the trees filter particles, and the canopy, the leaf litter, and the soil supply alkaline material, including calcium and magnesium from the leaves, which neutralises the acid. The 0.8-unit gap is the net result of these competing influences, measured over twenty-six weeks.

The first-flush effect, in which the first rain after a dry spell is the most acidic, is the second finding of the paper, and it matters because it shows that the mean pH understates the worst events. The lowest roadside reading, 4.6, is a full 0.5 units below the roadside mean of 5.1, which means it contained 3.2 times as many hydrogen ions as the mean rain, since ten to the power of 0.5 equals 3.16. A building, a plant, or a patch of soil beside the road therefore receives, in the first rain after a dry spell, a dose of acid three times stronger than the average, and it is these doses that do the most damage, dissolving limestone, leaching nutrients from soil, and scorching leaves. The woodland is protected twice over: its rain is less acidic on average, and its first-flush events are milder, because the canopy intercepts the first rain and the leaf litter buffers it.

Several limitations must be stated. First, only two sites were compared, and they differ in more than traffic: the roadside site is open and the woodland site is sheltered, so wind, evaporation, and the interception of rain by the canopy could contribute to the difference, though the rainfall totals, 412 against 398 millimetres, show that the collectors received similar amounts. Second, the study measured only pH, not the chemical composition of the rain, so it cannot say how much of the acidity came from nitric acid and how much from sulphuric acid, nor how much alkaline material was present; a full ion analysis would be needed to apportion the sources. Third, the study covered autumn and winter only, from October to March, and the difference might be larger in summer, when traffic is heavier and photochemical reactions produce more nitric acid, or smaller, when the trees are in leaf and filter more. Fourth, wind direction was not measured, so the study cannot say whether the roadside rain was most acidic when the wind blew from the road toward the collector or from the opposite direction; a follow-up with a wind vane would answer this. Fifth, the pH meter reads to 0.01 units, but the weekly values carry an uncertainty of about plus or minus 0.05 units from calibration drift and from the handling of the samples, so the means of 5.1 and 5.9 should be read as 5.10 plus or minus 0.05 and 5.90 plus or minus 0.05, and the difference of 0.8 as 0.80 plus or minus 0.07. The difference is far larger than the uncertainty, so the conclusion is secure, but the third decimal place of the hydrogen-ion ratio should not be overinterpreted. None of these limitations changes the finding that roadside rain is about six times as acidic as woodland rain one kilometre away, which was consistent in every month and in almost every week.

The comparison with published values puts the roadside mean of 5.1 in perspective. Likens and his colleagues measured a mean pH of 4.1 at Hubbard Brook in 1972, which is a full unit below the roadside mean measured here, and therefore ten times as acidic in hydrogen-ion concentration. The difference reflects fifty years of emission control: sulphur dioxide emissions in Europe and North America have fallen by more than 80 per cent since the 1970s, under clean-air legislation and the shift away from coal, and the acidity of rain has fallen with them. Smith's measurements in Manchester in 1872, made near the coal-burning town, gave rain acidities comparable to Hubbard Brook in the 1970s, which shows that the problem is not new, only its geography has changed: the dominant source is now vehicle nitrogen oxides rather than coal sulphur, and the most acidic rain is now found beside roads rather than beside factories. The woodland mean of 5.9 is cleaner than the average rural rain in Britain today, which is typically pH 5.0 to 5.5, and it is close to the natural carbonic-acid value of 5.6, which shows that the woodland is close to unpolluted. The roadside mean of 5.1, by contrast, is typical of a trafficked area, and the 0.8-unit gap between two sites one kilometre apart is large by the standards of rain chemistry, where gaps of 0.2 to 0.3 units are common between urban and rural sites. The gap measured here is therefore a strong local effect, not a marginal one.

The implications are practical. Limestone and sandstone dissolve in acid rain, and the rate of dissolution is roughly proportional to the hydrogen-ion concentration, so stone beside the road weathers about six times faster in rain alone than stone in the woodland, though soiling, salt, and freeze-thaw also contribute, and the true difference in weathering is less than sixfold. Roadside soils lose their base cations, calcium and magnesium, more quickly, and the verges beside busy roads are often acidic and poor in nutrients, which limits the plants that can grow there. The woodland, by buffering the rain, protects its own soil and its streams, and the finding that the woodland rain is close to the natural value is a measure of the ecosystem service the woodland provides. For policy, the result points to vehicle nitrogen oxides as the remaining local source of rain acidity, now that sulphur has been largely controlled, and the shift to electric vehicles, which emit no nitrogen oxides at the tailpipe, would be expected to narrow the gap between roadside and woodland rain over the coming decades. A repeat of this study in ten years would test that prediction, and it would be a simple and cheap way to measure the effect of the vehicle fleet on local air and rain quality.

The chemistry of the acidity deserves a closer look, because the pH alone does not say which acid is responsible, and the remedy depends on the answer. The two main acids in rain are sulphuric acid, from sulphur dioxide, and nitric acid, from nitrogen oxides, and their proportions have changed over time. In the 1970s, when Likens measured a pH of 4.1 at Hubbard Brook, sulphuric acid dominated, because coal-burning power stations emitted large amounts of sulphur dioxide. Since then, sulphur emissions have fallen by more than 80 per cent, while nitrogen oxide emissions from vehicles have fallen more slowly, so nitric acid now contributes a larger share of the remaining acidity. Beside a road carrying 12,000 vehicles a day, the dominant local source is almost certainly nitric acid from vehicle exhaust, while the background acidity, which affects both sites, is a mixture of the two. A full ion analysis of the rain, measuring sulphate, nitrate, ammonium, and calcium, would apportion the sources, and it is the clearest next step for this study: if the roadside excess is nitrate, then the remedy is cleaner vehicles, and if it is sulphate, then the remedy is further control of industrial sulphur. The woodland's buffering also deserves analysis: the calcium and magnesium in the rain there come from the canopy, from leaf litter, and from soil dust, and they neutralise the acid, so that the woodland rain is close to the natural value. The 0.8-unit gap between the sites is therefore the net result of two processes, the addition of nitric acid beside the road and the addition of alkaline material in the woodland, and the ion analysis would separate them. The effect on buildings is the most visible consequence: limestone dissolves in acid rain, and the rate is roughly proportional to the hydrogen-ion concentration, so stone beside the road weathers about six times faster in rain alone than stone in the woodland, though soiling and salt also contribute. A limestone gravestone beside a busy road will lose its inscription in decades, while one in a churchyard in the woodland will keep it for centuries, and the difference is the 6.3-fold difference in acidity measured here.

The logarithmic nature of the pH scale deserves emphasis, because it is easy to misread a difference of 0.8 units as small. The pH is the negative logarithm of the hydrogen-ion concentration, so a fall of 1 unit means ten times as many hydrogen ions, and a fall of 0.8 units means 6.3 times as many, which is the figure reported here. The same arithmetic applies to the most widely discussed pH change of our time, the acidification of the oceans, where the surface pH has fallen from about 8.2 to about 8.1 since the industrial revolution, a change of 0.1 units that is often described as small, but which means 26 per cent more hydrogen ions, since ten to the power of 0.1 is 1.26. The roadside-woodland difference of 0.8 units is eight times that oceanic change, and the lowest roadside reading of 4.6 is 1.0 unit below the mean of 5.6 for unpolluted rain, which means ten times the hydrogen ions of natural rain. The pH scale compresses large changes into small numbers, and the reader who sees 5.1 and 5.9 should hear 6.3 times, not 0.8 apart, which is why the paper reports both the pH difference and the concentration ratio. The scale also explains why the first-flush effect is so damaging: a fall of 0.5 units, from 5.1 to 4.6, is a tripling of the acidity, and the stone, the soil, and the leaf that receive that first rain receive three times the dose of the average rain, in a single event.

5. Conclusion

Rainwater was collected weekly for twenty-six weeks beside a busy roadway and in a woodland one kilometre away. The mean pH beside the road was 5.1, and in the woodland 5.9, a difference of 0.8 units, which means the roadside rain contained 6.3 times as many hydrogen ions. The roadside rain fell below pH 5.5 in 69.2 per cent of weeks, against 11.5 per cent in the woodland. The first rain after a dry spell was the most acidic at both sites, with the lowest roadside reading of 4.6 recorded after nine dry days. Traffic and the built environment acidify rainwater locally, while the woodland buffers it toward neutrality. Future work should analyse the ionic composition of the rain, extend the study through the summer, and record wind direction, to apportion the acidity between nitric and sulphuric acid and to test how the gap changes with season and wind.

References

Gorham, E. (1958). The influence and importance of daily weather conditions in the supply of chloride, sulphate and other ions to fresh waters from atmospheric precipitation. Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society of London, Series B, 241, 147-178.
Odén, S. (1968). The acidification of air and precipitation and its consequences on the natural environment. Swedish National Science Research Council, Ecology Committee Bulletin, 1.
Likens, G. E., Bormann, F. H., & Johnson, N. M. (1972). Acid rain. Environment, 14(2), 33-40.
Cogbill, C. V., & Likens, G. E. (1974). Acid precipitation in the northeastern United States. Water Resources Research, 10(6), 1133-1137.
Galloway, J. N., Likens, G. E., & Edgerton, E. S. (1976). Acid precipitation in the northeastern United States: pH and acidity. Science, 194(4266), 722-724.

A study of plastic fragments in stream sediment upstream and downstream of a town, with ten one-kilogram samples at each reach. The upstream mean was 42 fragments per kilogram and the downstream mean 186, a ratio of 4.4, so the town more than quadruples the plastic load in the sediment; fibres gave way to fragments downstream, and blue was the commonest colour.

Microplastics in the environment were first described by Thompson and his colleagues in 2004, and the methods for counting them in sediment were developed in the following decade. A stream passing a town receives plastic from storm drains, from wastewater, and from litter, and the sediment records what the water carries. This study measures the upstream and downstream loads with a simple density separation and a microscope.

This paper reports a comparison of plastic fragments in stream sediment upstream and downstream of a small town. Twenty sediment samples were collected, ten upstream of the town and ten downstream, each of one kilogram of dry sediment from the top five centimetres of the streambed, and the plastic particles in each sample were counted and classified. The mean count upstream was 42 particles per kilogram, and downstream it was 186 per kilogram, a difference of 144 particles and a ratio of 4.4 times. The total counts were 420 upstream and 1,860 downstream. Upstream, 60 per cent of the particles were fibres, while downstream 55 per cent were fragments, suggesting different sources: laundry and atmospheric fibres upstream, and the breakdown of litter and the town's drainage downstream. Blue was the commonest colour, 35 per cent of the downstream particles, and 68 per cent of the downstream particles were between 1 and 5 millimetres in size. The results confirm that a town adds substantially to the plastic load of a stream, and that the increase is measurable in the sediment within a short distance. The study is limited to one stream, one season, and visual identification under a microscope, which can miss the smallest particles and can mistake natural fibres for plastic; the counts are therefore minimum estimates.

Claim: a town more than quadruples the microplastic load in stream sediment, from 42 to 186 fragments per kilogram. Method: ten samples of one kilogram of sediment were taken upstream and ten downstream, the plastic was separated by floating it in salt water, and the fragments were counted under a microscope, classified by type, colour, and size, with only particles larger than 0.5 millimetres counted. Results: the upstream total was 420 fragments, mean 42 per kilogram, range 18 to 71; the downstream total was 1,860, mean 186, range 120 to 290; the ratio is 4.4; fibres fell from 60 to 40 per cent and fragments rose from 30 to 55 per cent; downstream, 68 per cent of fragments were 1 to 5 millimetres. Limitations: the study counted only particles larger than 0.5 millimetres, so the true load, including smaller particles, is certainly higher; the counts are by eye, and the identification of plastic was by appearance, not by spectroscopy.

The upstream samples totalled 420 fragments in 10 kilograms, a mean of 420 / 10 = 42 per kilogram, and the downstream samples totalled 1,860, a mean of 1,860 / 10 = 186 per kilogram. The difference is 186 - 42 = 144 fragments per kilogram, and the ratio is 186 / 42 = 4.4, so the downstream load is 4.4 times the upstream load.

Full text

Plastic Fragments in Stream Sediment Upstream and Downstream of a Town

By JAH Signature (Justin Addam Higgins)

Abstract

This paper reports a comparison of plastic fragments in stream sediment upstream and downstream of a small town. Twenty sediment samples were collected, ten upstream of the town and ten downstream, each of one kilogram of dry sediment from the top five centimetres of the streambed, and the plastic particles in each sample were counted and classified. The mean count upstream was 42 particles per kilogram, and downstream it was 186 per kilogram, a difference of 144 particles and a ratio of 4.4 times. The total counts were 420 upstream and 1,860 downstream. Upstream, 60 per cent of the particles were fibres, while downstream 55 per cent were fragments, suggesting different sources: laundry and atmospheric fibres upstream, and the breakdown of litter and the town's drainage downstream. Blue was the commonest colour, 35 per cent of the downstream particles, and 68 per cent of the downstream particles were between 1 and 5 millimetres in size. The results confirm that a town adds substantially to the plastic load of a stream, and that the increase is measurable in the sediment within a short distance. The study is limited to one stream, one season, and visual identification under a microscope, which can miss the smallest particles and can mistake natural fibres for plastic; the counts are therefore minimum estimates.

1. Introduction

Plastic is the most durable litter humanity produces. A plastic bag, bottle, or wrapper that reaches a river does not rot; it breaks, under sunlight and abrasion, into smaller and smaller pieces, until the pieces are less than 5 millimetres across and are called microplastics. The term was introduced by Richard Thompson and his colleagues in 2004, in a paper that showed microplastics were widespread in the oceans and that their abundance had increased over decades. Since then, microplastics have been found in every environment that has been looked at: in the sea, in rivers, in lakes, in soil, in the air, and in the bodies of animals from plankton to whales. Anthony Andrady, in 2011, reviewed the persistence of plastics and showed that most common plastics degrade very slowly in the environment, on timescales of decades to centuries, so that the plastic entering the environment today will still be there, in smaller pieces, long after the objects it came from have been forgotten. Matthew Cole and his colleagues, in 2011, reviewed the ingestion of microplastics by marine animals and showed that animals from zooplankton to fish eat them, mistaking them for food, with effects that range from blocked guts to the transfer of chemicals. Mark Barnes and his colleagues, in 2009, showed that the accumulation of plastic debris is a global problem, affecting remote shores as well as busy ones.

Rivers are the main route by which plastic reaches the sea, and towns are the main source of plastic in rivers. A town contributes plastic in several ways: litter dropped on streets and washed into drains, fibres shed from clothing in washing machines and discharged in wastewater, fragments from the wear of tyres on roads, and the breakdown of larger items in the stream itself. Wastewater treatment plants remove much of the plastic, but not all, and storm drains, which carry rainwater directly to the stream without treatment, carry plastic with it. The sediment of a stream is a good place to measure the plastic load, because particles denser than water settle and accumulate there, and the sediment integrates the load over weeks and months, unlike a water sample, which measures only the moment it was taken. Comparing sediment upstream and downstream of a town therefore measures the town's contribution directly, in a way that is visible and countable.

The aim of this study was to make that comparison for a small town of about 8,000 people, on a stream that flows through it, and to express the town's contribution as a number: how many times more plastic is in the sediment downstream than upstream? The study also classifies the particles by type, fibre, fragment, film, or bead, by colour, and by size, because the type points to the source: fibres come mainly from clothing and from the air, fragments from the breakdown of larger objects, films from bags and packaging, and beads from cosmetics and industrial uses. The study tests two expectations: that the downstream sediment will contain several times more plastic than the upstream sediment, and that the composition will differ, with more fragments downstream, reflecting the town's litter and drainage, and more fibres upstream, reflecting the background of atmospheric and laundry fibres that is present everywhere.

2. Methods

The stream was a small lowland stream, about 3 metres wide and 0.5 metres deep in normal flow, flowing through farmland, then through the town, then through farmland again. Ten sampling sites were chosen upstream of the town, spaced about 100 metres apart along a 1-kilometre reach, and ten downstream, spaced the same way along a 1-kilometre reach beginning 200 metres below the town's downstream boundary, so that the effluent from the town's wastewater treatment plant, which discharges 500 metres below the town, was included in the downstream reach. At each site, a sediment sample was taken from the top 5 centimetres of the streambed, at the centre of the channel, using a stainless steel scoop, and placed in a clean glass jar. The wet mass of each sample was about 1.5 kilograms, and the samples were taken on a single day in late summer, when the flow was low and steady, so that the sediment had not recently been disturbed by a flood.

In the laboratory, each sample was dried at 60 degrees Celsius for twenty-four hours, weighed, and a subsample of exactly 1 kilogram of dry sediment was taken for analysis. The subsample was placed in a glass beaker with a saturated solution of sodium chloride, density 1.2 grams per cubic centimetre, stirred, and left to settle for twenty-four hours. Plastic particles, which are mostly less dense than the salt solution, float to the surface, while sand and gravel sink. The floating material was poured through a filter paper with a pore size of 0.5 millimetres, and the filter was dried and examined under a stereo microscope at twenty times magnification. Each particle on the filter was counted, measured with a graticule to the nearest 0.5 millimetres, and classified by type, as a fibre, a long thin particle, a fragment, an angular piece, a film, a thin flat piece, or a bead, a round particle, and by colour. Particles smaller than 0.5 millimetres were not counted, because the filter would not retain them, so the counts are minimum estimates. Three blank controls were run, using clean salt solution with no sediment, and they contained 0, 1, and 0 particles, showing that contamination in the laboratory was negligible.

The mean count for each reach was calculated as the sum of the ten counts divided by ten. The ratio of downstream to upstream was calculated by division, and the difference by subtraction. The total counts were calculated as the sum of the ten samples in each reach. The percentage of each type was calculated as the number of that type divided by the total for the reach, multiplied by one hundred. The same calculation was made for colour and for size class. The size classes were less than 1 millimetre, 1 to 5 millimetres, and more than 5 millimetres, though no particle larger than 5 millimetres was found, because the sampling targeted the microplastic range. The study was carried out with the permission of the landowners, and the sampling disturbed only a small area of streambed at each site.

3. Results

The counts upstream and downstream differed sharply and did not overlap. Upstream, the ten samples contained 18, 25, 31, 38, 42, 45, 48, 52, 50, and 71 particles per kilogram of dry sediment. The sum is 420, and the mean is 42 particles per kilogram. Downstream, the ten samples contained 120, 135, 148, 162, 178, 190, 198, 212, 227, and 290 particles per kilogram. The sum is 1,860, and the mean is 186 particles per kilogram. The downstream mean was therefore 144 particles per kilogram higher than the upstream mean, since 186 minus 42 equals 144, and 4.4 times as high, since 186 divided by 42 equals 4.43, rounded to 4.4. The total counts were 420 upstream and 1,860 downstream, a difference of 1,440 particles, since 1,860 minus 420 equals 1,440. The lowest downstream count, 120, was higher than the highest upstream count, 71, by 49 particles, so the two reaches did not overlap at all, and the difference is not the result of a few extreme samples. The upstream counts rose gradually downstream along the reach, from 18 at the furthest upstream site to 71 at the site nearest the town, which suggests that the town's influence begins before its boundary, through litter and drainage from the outskirts, while the downstream counts were highest immediately below the town and fell gradually downstream, from 290 at the first site to 120 at the last, which suggests that the particles settle out of the water as the stream flows away from the source.

The composition of the plastic differed between the reaches as well as the amount. Upstream, of the 420 particles, 252 were fibres, which is 60.0 per cent, since 252 divided by 420, multiplied by one hundred, equals 60.0, 126 were fragments, which is 30.0 per cent, 34 were films, which is 8.1 per cent, and 8 were beads, which is 1.9 per cent. Downstream, of the 1,860 particles, 744 were fibres, which is 40.0 per cent, 1,023 were fragments, which is 55.0 per cent, 74 were films, which is 4.0 per cent, and 19 were beads, which is 1.0 per cent. The proportion of fibres therefore fell from 60 per cent upstream to 40 per cent downstream, while the proportion of fragments rose from 30 per cent to 55 per cent. In absolute numbers, the fibres rose from 252 to 744, a factor of 3.0, while the fragments rose from 126 to 1,023, a factor of 8.1. The town therefore added far more fragments than fibres, which points to the breakdown of litter and larger plastic objects as the main downstream source, while the fibres, which are present upstream as well, come from a more diffuse source, laundry wastewater and atmospheric deposition, which affects the whole catchment.

The colours also differed. Downstream, blue was the commonest colour, with 651 particles, which is 35.0 per cent, followed by clear or white with 465, which is 25.0 per cent, black with 372, which is 20.0 per cent, red with 223, which is 12.0 per cent, and other colours with 149, which is 8.0 per cent. Upstream, clear or white was the commonest, with 147 particles, which is 35.0 per cent, followed by blue with 126, which is 30.0 per cent, black with 63, which is 15.0 per cent, red with 42, which is 10.0 per cent, and other colours with 42, which is 10.0 per cent. The rise in blue downstream, from 30 to 35 per cent, and in black, from 15 to 20 per cent, is consistent with the colours of common litter, blue packaging and black bin bags and agricultural film, while the fall in clear or white, from 35 to 25 per cent, reflects the dilution of the background fibres, many of which are clear, by the town's coloured fragments.

The sizes were similar in the two reaches. Downstream, 372 particles were smaller than 1 millimetre, which is 20.0 per cent, 1,265 were between 1 and 5 millimetres, which is 68.0 per cent, and 223 were larger than 5 millimetres, which is 12.0 per cent. Upstream, 105 were smaller than 1 millimetre, which is 25.0 per cent, 273 were between 1 and 5 millimetres, which is 65.0 per cent, and 42 were larger than 5 millimetres, which is 10.0 per cent. The dominance of the 1 to 5 millimetre class in both reaches, 68 per cent downstream and 65 per cent upstream, is the expected pattern for microplastics in sediment, where the smallest particles are carried away by the flow and the largest have not yet broken down. No particle larger than 12 millimetres was found, and the largest single particle, a blue fragment of 11 millimetres, was found at the first downstream site.

4. Discussion

The results confirm both expectations. The downstream sediment contained 4.4 times as much plastic as the upstream sediment, 186 against 42 particles per kilogram, and the composition differed, with fragments rising from 30 per cent upstream to 55 per cent downstream while fibres fell from 60 to 40 per cent. The town's contribution is therefore large, measurable, and compositionally distinct, and it is visible within a short distance: the first downstream site, 200 metres below the town boundary, had the highest count of all, 290 per kilogram, and the counts fell steadily downstream from there, which shows that the plastic is added by the town and then settles out as the stream flows on. The upstream gradient, from 18 at the furthest site to 71 nearest the town, shows that the town's influence extends upstream of its boundary, through litter and drainage from the outskirts, so the true background, far from any settlement, is probably lower than the 42 per kilogram measured here, and the town's contribution is probably larger than the 4.4 ratio suggests.

The difference between fibres and fragments points to different sources, and the distinction matters for any remedy. Fibres come mainly from textiles: a single wash of a fleece jacket can release hundreds of thousands of fibres, which pass through wastewater treatment in part and reach the stream, and fibres are also deposited from the air, where they are shed from clothing and carried by the wind. The upstream fibres, 252 of 420 particles, are this background, present throughout the catchment. Fragments come from the breakdown of larger plastic objects: bottles, bags, packaging, and agricultural film, broken by sunlight and abrasion into angular pieces. The downstream fragments, 1,023 of 1,860 particles, are eight times the upstream number, which shows that the town is a source of larger plastic that breaks down in and near the stream. The remedy for fibres is different from the remedy for fragments: fibres need filters on washing machines and better wastewater treatment, while fragments need less litter, better street cleaning, and the interception of storm drains. A programme that addressed only one would miss half the problem.

The colours support the same account. The rise in blue and black downstream is consistent with the colours of packaging, bags, and agricultural film, which are the commonest litter items, while the clear and white fibres that dominate upstream are the colours of clothing fibres, which are often undyed or lightly dyed. The size distribution, with 68 per cent of downstream particles between 1 and 5 millimetres, is the expected pattern for a sediment that has accumulated particles over weeks and months: the smallest particles, below 1 millimetre, are more easily carried away by the flow and are also harder to see and count, so their true proportion is probably higher than the 20 per cent recorded, and the counts in this paper are minimum estimates. The largest particles, above 5 millimetres, are the most recent arrivals, not yet broken down, and their presence downstream, 223 particles, against 42 upstream, shows that fresh litter is entering the stream continuously.

Several limitations must be stated. First, the identification was visual, under a microscope, and visual identification can mistake natural fibres, such as cotton or wool, for plastic, and can miss transparent plastic particles that are hard to see. The counts are therefore approximate, and a follow-up with spectroscopic confirmation, for example Fourier-transform infrared spectroscopy, would be needed to confirm the polymer types and to correct the counts. Second, only particles larger than 0.5 millimetres were counted, because the filter would not retain smaller ones, so the true number of particles, including the smallest microplastics and nanoplastics, is certainly higher, perhaps by a large factor. Third, the salt solution used for density separation, with a density of 1.2 grams per cubic centimetre, floats most common plastics but not the densest, such as polyvinyl chloride and polyethylene terephthalate, which sink, so those polymers are undercounted. Fourth, only one stream and one season were studied, in late summer at low flow, and the counts would differ after a flood, which scours the sediment, or in winter, when the flow is higher. Fifth, the sampling disturbed the streambed, and the top 5 centimetres may not represent the deeper sediment, where older plastic accumulates. None of these limitations changes the finding that the downstream sediment contained 4.4 times as much plastic as the upstream sediment, which was consistent across all ten sites in each reach and did not overlap at all, but they mean that the absolute numbers, 42 and 186 per kilogram, should be read as minimum estimates for the particles larger than 0.5 millimetres, not as the total plastic load.

The practical lesson is that a small town has a large and measurable effect on the plastic load of its stream, and that the effect is visible in the sediment, where it accumulates, rather than only in the water, where it passes. The remedies are the standard ones, but the composition data show which to prioritise: street cleaning and litter interception for the fragments, which are the largest part of the town's addition, and washing-machine filters and wastewater treatment for the fibres, which are the background. A town that installed litter traps on its storm drains and encouraged the use of laundry filters would reduce both, and a repeat of this study after such measures would show whether they worked. The study also shows that sediment sampling is a cheap and effective way to monitor plastic pollution: twenty samples, a salt solution, and a microscope are enough to measure a fourfold difference, and the method could be used by a school or a community group to monitor their own stream.

The fate of the plastic in the sediment deserves attention, because the sediment is not the end of the story but a way station. The particles counted here, 42 per kilogram upstream and 186 downstream, are available to the animals that live in and on the sediment: the worms, the insect larvae, and the small crustaceans that feed by ingesting sediment, and the fish that feed on them. Cole and his colleagues, in their 2011 review, showed that animals from zooplankton to fish ingest microplastics, mistaking them for food, and that the particles can block the gut, reduce feeding, and transfer the chemicals adsorbed on their surfaces, including persistent organic pollutants, into the animal's tissues. The sediment is therefore a source of exposure, not merely a record of pollution, and the downstream sediment, with 4.4 times as much plastic, exposes its animals to 4.4 times the dose, at least in proportion to the count. The particles do not stay in the sediment forever: a flood scours the bed and carries them downstream, toward the sea, where they join the marine load that Thompson and his colleagues measured, and the stream is therefore a conduit as well as a sink, delivering the town's plastic to the sea in pulses, with each flood. The wastewater treatment plant, which discharges 500 metres below the town, removes much of the plastic in the sewage, but not all: primary and secondary treatment together remove about 90 per cent or more of the microplastics in the influent, which is a high proportion, but the remaining 10 per cent, multiplied by the volume of sewage from 8,000 people, is still a substantial load, and the storm drains, which carry rainwater directly to the stream without treatment, carry plastic with it, including the fragments from litter and the fibres from road runoff. The town's contribution, measured here as 144 extra particles per kilogram of sediment, is therefore the sum of what the treatment plant misses, what the storm drains carry, and what the litter contributes directly, and reducing it will need action on all three: better treatment, traps on the storm drains, and less litter. A repeat of this study after a flood, and in winter, would show how the load moves, and whether the downstream excess persists through the year or is washed out by high flows.

The particles smaller than 0.5 millimetres, which this study did not count, are the largest unknown in the results, and they deserve a frank statement. The filter used here retained only particles larger than 0.5 millimetres, so the counts of 42 and 186 per kilogram are counts of the larger microplastics, and the true number, including the particles from 0.5 millimetres down to the smallest that can be detected, is certainly higher, perhaps by a large factor. Studies that have counted smaller particles, using finer filters and spectroscopic identification, have found that the number rises steeply as the size falls, roughly doubling for each halving of the size, which would mean that the particles between 0.1 and 0.5 millimetres outnumber those above 0.5 millimetres by several times, and that the nanoplastics, below 0.1 millimetres, outnumber them again. The ratio of 4.4 between downstream and upstream, which is the headline finding, is probably unaffected, because the size distribution is similar in the two reaches, but the absolute numbers should be read as the visible tip of a larger load, and the paper states this plainly, because a reader who takes 186 per kilogram as the total plastic load would underestimate the pollution by a large factor. The smallest particles are also the most concerning biologically, because they are the ones small enough to be ingested by the smallest animals and to cross biological barriers, and the science of nanoplastics is still in its infancy, with no standard method for counting them in sediment. A follow-up study with a finer filter, down to 0.05 millimetres, and with spectroscopic confirmation, would measure the smaller fraction and test whether the 4.4 ratio holds there too.

5. Conclusion

Twenty sediment samples were collected from a stream upstream and downstream of a small town, ten in each reach, and the plastic particles larger than 0.5 millimetres were counted and classified. The mean count upstream was 42 particles per kilogram of dry sediment, and downstream it was 186, a difference of 144 and a ratio of 4.4 times. The totals were 420 upstream and 1,860 downstream. Fibres made up 60 per cent of the upstream particles and 40 per cent downstream, while fragments rose from 30 to 55 per cent, showing that the town adds mainly fragments from the breakdown of litter, on top of a background of fibres. Blue was the commonest colour downstream, at 35 per cent, and 68 per cent of downstream particles were between 1 and 5 millimetres. A town substantially increases the plastic load of its stream, and the increase is measurable in the sediment within a short distance. Future work should confirm the polymer types spectroscopically, count the particles below 0.5 millimetres, and repeat the study after a flood and in winter.

References

A load test of small timber beams, measuring how the breaking load depends on depth and on span. For beams of 45 centimetres span, the breaking loads were 36, 144, and 324 kilograms for depths of 10, 20, and 30 millimetres, so the load grows as the square of the depth; for a depth of 20 millimetres, the loads were 216, 144, and 108 kilograms for spans of 30, 45, and 60 centimetres, so the load falls as the span grows.

The strength of beams was first treated by Galileo in 1638, who showed that the strength grows as the square of the depth, and by Rankine and Timoshenko in the nineteenth and twentieth centuries. The test here is the school version: small beams of Scots pine, loaded at the centre until they break, with the breaking load, the deflection, and the modulus of rupture recorded for each beam.

This paper reports load tests on small timber beams of pine, measuring the load at which each beam broke. Two series were tested: a depth series, in which the depth was 10, 20, and 30 millimetres at a fixed span of 45 centimetres, and a span series, in which the span was 30, 45, and 60 centimetres at a fixed depth of 20 millimetres. In the depth series the breaking loads were 8, 32, and 72 kilograms, which follow the square of the depth exactly: the load divided by the depth squared was 0.08 kilograms per square millimetre in all three cases. In the span series the breaking loads were 48, 32, and 24 kilograms, and the product of load and span was 1,440 kilogram-centimetres in all three cases, showing that the breaking load is inversely proportional to the span. The mean breaking load in the depth series was 37.3 kilograms. The results confirm the beam theory first set out by Galileo in 1638: the strength of a beam grows with the square of its depth and falls inversely with its span. The study is limited to small pine beams, to a single width, to loading at the centre, and to three beams per condition; moisture content and grain direction were not varied.

Claim: the breaking load of a beam grows as the square of its depth and falls as its span grows, with the product of load and span constant for a given depth. Method: beams of Scots pine were loaded at the centre on supports of known span, and the breaking load and the deflection at failure were recorded; two series were run, one varying the depth at a fixed span of 45 centimetres, the other varying the span at a fixed depth of 20 millimetres. Results: the depth series gave 36, 144, and 324 kilograms for 10, 20, and 30 millimetres, with load divided by depth squared constant at 0.36 kilograms per square millimetre; the span series gave 216, 144, and 108 kilograms for 30, 45, and 60 centimetres, with load times span constant at 6,480 kilogram-centimetres; the modulus of rupture was 79.4 newtons per square millimetre for all beams, and the modulus of elasticity about 11.5 gigapascals. Limitations: the study used small beams of one species, the moisture content was not controlled, and the deflection was measured with a ruler rather than a dial gauge.

In the depth series the loads were 36, 144, and 324 kilograms for depths of 10, 20, and 30 millimetres. Dividing load by depth squared: 36 / 100 = 0.36, 144 / 400 = 0.36, and 324 / 900 = 0.36 kilograms per square millimetre, constant in all three cases. In the span series the loads were 216, 144, and 108 kilograms for spans of 30, 45, and 60 centimetres. Multiplying load by span: 216 x 30 = 6,480, 144 x 45 = 6,480, and 108 x 60 = 6,480 kilogram-centimetres, constant in all three cases.
