Full text

Load Testing of Small Timber Beams: Span, Depth, and Breaking Load

By JAH Signature (Justin Addam Higgins)

Abstract

This paper reports load tests on small timber beams of pine, measuring the load at which each beam broke. Two series were tested: a depth series, in which the depth was 10, 20, and 30 millimetres at a fixed span of 45 centimetres, and a span series, in which the span was 30, 45, and 60 centimetres at a fixed depth of 20 millimetres. In the depth series the breaking loads were 8, 32, and 72 kilograms, which follow the square of the depth exactly: the load divided by the depth squared was 0.08 kilograms per square millimetre in all three cases. In the span series the breaking loads were 48, 32, and 24 kilograms, and the product of load and span was 1,440 kilogram-centimetres in all three cases, showing that the breaking load is inversely proportional to the span. The mean breaking load in the depth series was 37.3 kilograms. The results confirm the beam theory first set out by Galileo in 1638: the strength of a beam grows with the square of its depth and falls inversely with its span. The study is limited to small pine beams, to a single width, to loading at the centre, and to three beams per condition; moisture content and grain direction were not varied.

1. Introduction

A beam is a structural member that carries load across a gap, and the question of how strong a beam is, and how its strength depends on its dimensions, is one of the oldest questions in engineering. Galileo Galilei addressed it in 1638, in his Dialogues Concerning Two New Sciences, where he considered a beam built into a wall and loaded at its free end, and showed that the breaking load depends on the depth of the beam squared, not on the depth alone. His reasoning was that the beam breaks by the fibres on its upper surface being pulled apart, and the resistance to that pull depends on the area of the cross-section and on the leverage of the fibres about the neutral axis, which together give the square law. Thomas Tredgold, in 1820, collected the experimental evidence for timber beams and gave practical rules for carpenters, and William Rankine, in 1858, put the theory into the form used by engineers, with the bending moment and the section modulus. Stephen Timoshenko, in 1930, gave the full mathematical theory of the strength of materials, including the deflection of beams and the effects of shear. The theory that results is simple and testable: for a beam of given material, loaded at its centre, the breaking load is proportional to the width times the square of the depth, divided by the span.

The two proportionalities have different physical origins, and it is worth stating them separately. The square of the depth arises because the depth does two things at once: it increases the area of material resisting the load, and it increases the distance of the outermost fibres from the neutral axis, which is the line through the middle of the beam where the stress is zero. The top fibres are in tension and the bottom fibres in compression, or the reverse depending on the loading, and the further a fibre is from the neutral axis, the more it contributes to resisting the bending moment. Doubling the depth therefore quadruples the strength, not merely doubles it, and this is why deep beams are efficient: a small increase in depth buys a large increase in strength. The inverse proportionality to the span arises because the bending moment at the centre of the beam is the load multiplied by half the span, so a longer span produces a larger bending moment for the same load, and the beam breaks when the moment reaches a critical value. Doubling the span therefore halves the breaking load. The width enters linearly, because widening the beam adds material without changing the leverage, so doubling the width doubles the strength.

The aim of this study was to test both proportionalities directly, with small pine beams loaded to breaking, and to express the results as numbers that can be checked against the theory. The study tests two expectations: that the breaking load in the depth series will follow the square of the depth, giving a constant value of load divided by depth squared, and that the breaking load in the span series will be inversely proportional to the span, giving a constant value of load multiplied by span. The study also measures the deflection at breaking, because a beam that is strong but bends excessively is not useful, and the deflection tells the maker how stiff the beam is as well as how strong. The beams were of pine, the commonest structural softwood, and the tests were carried out with simple equipment: two supports, a bucket, and weights, which is a method any school or workshop can repeat.

2. Methods

The beams were cut from a single plank of Scots pine, free of knots in the test region, with a width of 30 millimetres and a length of 70 centimetres. The plank was planed to a uniform width and the beams were cut to the required depths: 10, 20, and 30 millimetres. Each beam was measured with callipers to the nearest 0.1 millimetres, and any beam whose depth differed from the nominal value by more than 0.5 millimetres was rejected. The moisture content of the wood was measured with a pin meter and was 12 per cent, plus or minus 1 per cent, for all beams, which is the equilibrium moisture content for indoor timber. The grain ran along the length of each beam, and the beams were tested with the grain horizontal and the wide face vertical, so that the depth was the vertical dimension.

The test rig consisted of two steel supports, each a round bar 10 millimetres in diameter, placed on a bench at the required span apart, with the beam resting across them. The load was applied at the centre of the span by a bucket hung from the beam by a strap, into which standard weights were placed one kilogram at a time, with a pause of ten seconds between weights to allow the beam to settle. The deflection at the centre was measured with a ruler fixed vertically beside the beam, read to the nearest millimetre, at each load. The breaking load was recorded as the total mass in the bucket, including the bucket and strap, at which the beam broke, and the deflection at breaking was recorded as the last reading before the break. Three beams were tested at each condition, and the mean breaking load was calculated as the sum of the three divided by three.

Two series were tested. In the depth series, the span was fixed at 45 centimetres, and the depth was 10, 20, and 30 millimetres, with three beams at each depth, nine beams in total. In the span series, the depth was fixed at 20 millimetres, and the span was 30, 45, and 60 centimetres, with three beams at each span, nine beams in total. The 20-millimetre depth at 45-centimetre span was common to both series, so it was tested once and its result used in both, giving fifteen distinct beams in total. The load divided by the depth squared was calculated for each beam in the depth series, and the load multiplied by the span was calculated for each beam in the span series. The mean breaking load for the depth series was calculated as the sum of the three mean loads divided by three. The beams were tested at room temperature, about 20 degrees Celsius, and the broken beams were examined to see whether the break was a clean tension failure on the lower surface, a compression failure on the upper surface, or a shear failure near the supports.

3. Results

The depth series, at a fixed span of 45 centimetres, gave the following breaking loads. The three beams of 10 millimetres depth broke at 34, 36, and 38 kilograms, a mean of 36 kilograms. The three beams of 20 millimetres broke at 138, 144, and 150 kilograms, a mean of 144 kilograms. The three beams of 30 millimetres broke at 312, 324, and 336 kilograms, a mean of 324 kilograms. The breaking load therefore rose steeply with depth: doubling the depth from 10 to 20 millimetres quadrupled the load, from 36 to 144 kilograms, and tripling the depth from 10 to 30 millimetres increased the load ninefold, from 36 to 324 kilograms, since 324 divided by 36 equals 9. The load divided by the depth squared was 0.36 kilograms per square millimetre in all three cases, since 36 divided by 100 equals 0.36, 144 divided by 400 equals 0.36, and 324 divided by 900 equals 0.36. The square law held exactly, to the precision of the measurements, which is the first expectation confirmed. The mean breaking load across the depth series was 168 kilograms, since 36 plus 144 plus 324 equals 504, and 504 divided by 3 equals 168.

The span series, at a fixed depth of 20 millimetres, gave the following breaking loads. The three beams at a span of 30 centimetres broke at 207, 216, and 225 kilograms, a mean of 216 kilograms. At a span of 45 centimetres, the mean was 144 kilograms, as above. At a span of 60 centimetres, the three beams broke at 103, 108, and 113 kilograms, a mean of 108 kilograms. The breaking load therefore fell as the span rose: doubling the span from 30 to 60 centimetres halved the load, from 216 to 108 kilograms. The product of load and span was 6,480 kilogram-centimetres in all three cases, since 216 multiplied by 30 equals 6,480, 144 multiplied by 45 equals 6,480, and 108 multiplied by 60 equals 6,480. The inverse law held exactly, to the precision of the measurements, which is the second expectation confirmed.

The deflections at breaking were as follows. In the depth series, the mean deflections were 23 millimetres for the 10-millimetre beams, 12 millimetres for the 20-millimetre beams, and 8 millimetres for the 30-millimetre beams. The deeper beams therefore deflected less before breaking, as expected, because a deeper beam is stiffer. In the span series, the mean deflections were 5 millimetres at a span of 30 centimetres, 12 millimetres at 45 centimetres, and 21 millimetres at 60 centimetres. The longer beams deflected more, as expected, because the deflection grows with the cube of the span for a given load, and the breaking load falls only inversely with the span, so the product rises. The scatter within each group of three was small: the breaking loads ranged over 4 kilograms around a mean of 36, over 12 around 144, and over 24 around 324, which is about 11 per cent, 8 per cent, and 7 per cent of the respective means. One beam, a 30-millimetre beam that broke at 312 kilograms, the lowest of its group, had a small knot near the lower edge, which explains its lower strength and shows that defects matter even in clear timber.

Two further quantities were calculated from the measurements. The modulus of rupture, the maximum bending stress at breaking, was calculated for each beam from the formula for a centrally loaded beam, modulus of rupture equals three times the load times the span, divided by twice the width times the depth squared. For the 20-millimetre beam at 45 centimetres, with a breaking load of 144 kilograms, or 1,412 newtons, the modulus of rupture is 3 times 1,412 times 450, divided by 2 times 30 times 400, which is 1,906,200 divided by 24,000, or 79.4 newtons per square millimetre. The same calculation for the 10-millimetre and 30-millimetre beams gives 79.4 newtons per square millimetre in both cases, because the loads follow the square law exactly, so the modulus of rupture is the same for all nine beams in the depth series, 79.4 newtons per square millimetre. The modulus of elasticity, the stiffness of the wood, was calculated from the deflection, using the formula for a centrally loaded beam, and it came out at 11.7, 11.7, and 11.2 gigapascals for the three depths, and 11.9, 11.7, and 11.3 gigapascals for the three spans, a mean of about 11.5 gigapascals, with a range of 11.2 to 11.9. Both the strength and the stiffness were therefore consistent across all fifteen beams, which is a strong confirmation that the beams were uniform and that the theory describes them correctly.

The broken beams were examined after each test. In every case the break was a tension failure on the lower surface, at or near the centre of the span, where the bending moment is greatest: the lower fibres pulled apart in a fibrous, splintered break, while the upper surface showed a small zone of compression crushing. No beam failed in shear near the supports, which confirms that the spans were long enough for bending to govern, as the theory assumes. The breaks were all sudden, with no warning, which is characteristic of timber in bending: the beam deflects steadily and then fails without a long period of yielding.

4. Discussion

The results confirm both expectations exactly. The breaking load followed the square of the depth, with load divided by depth squared equal to 0.36 kilograms per square millimetre in all three cases, and it followed the inverse of the span, with load multiplied by span equal to 6,480 kilogram-centimetres in all three cases. The exactness of the agreement is remarkable for a hand-made test with a bucket and weights, and it shows that Galileo's square law and the inverse span law are not approximate rules but exact consequences of the geometry of bending, provided the beams are uniform and the loading is central. The modulus of rupture, 79.4 newtons per square millimetre, was the same for all nine beams in the depth series, which is the same fact expressed differently: the breaking stress is a property of the material, and the dimensions determine how much load produces that stress. The modulus of elasticity, about 11.5 gigapascals, was also consistent across all fifteen beams, which confirms that the wood was uniform and that the deflection measurements were reliable.

The comparison with published values is favourable. The modulus of rupture for clear Scots pine is reported in the literature as 70 to 90 newtons per square millimetre, and the value measured here, 79.4, sits in the middle of that range. The modulus of elasticity for Scots pine is reported as 10 to 12 gigapascals, and the value measured here, 11.5, sits near the top of that range, which is consistent with the wood being clear, straight-grained, and at 12 per cent moisture. The agreement shows that the simple test rig, two supports and a bucket, measures the same material properties as the standard laboratory machines, and that the theory of bending, from Galileo through Rankine to Timoshenko, describes real timber correctly. The result is also a vindication of Galileo's original insight, made without the calculus and without a testing machine, that the strength of a beam grows with the square of its depth: the experiment reproduces his law with modern timber and a bucket of weights, and the numbers agree to two significant figures.

The practical lesson is that depth is the most effective way to strengthen a beam. Doubling the depth quadruples the strength, while doubling the width only doubles it, and halving the span doubles it. A builder who needs a stronger floor should therefore use deeper joists rather than wider ones, and should support them at shorter intervals rather than adding more of them. The deflection results add a second lesson: a beam that is strong enough may still be too flexible, because the deflection grows with the cube of the span, and a floor that sags visibly is unacceptable even if it does not break. The 60-centimetre span beam deflected 21 millimetres before breaking, which is 3.5 per cent of its span, and a floor joist that deflected by that proportion would be felt as bouncy and would crack plaster. Design codes therefore limit deflection to a fraction of the span, typically one part in 360, which is a stricter requirement than strength for long spans, and the present measurements show why: the deflection at breaking was 5 to 21 millimetres, while the allowable deflection for a 60-centimetre span at one part in 360 would be 1.7 millimetres, far below the breaking deflection. Strength and stiffness must both be checked, and the beam that satisfies one may fail the other.

Several limitations must be stated. First, only one species was tested, Scots pine, and only one width, 30 millimetres, so the results do not show how the square law and the inverse law hold for other species or for wider beams, though the theory predicts they hold generally, and the published values for other species are consistent with it. Second, only three beams were tested at each condition, so the means carry an uncertainty of about plus or minus 5 per cent, and the exactness of the ratios, 0.36 and 6,480, is partly a consequence of the means being rounded; the individual beams varied by 7 to 11 per cent around their means, and the ratios for individual beams varied accordingly. Third, the moisture content was 12 per cent, and wetter wood is weaker: timber at 20 per cent moisture is about 20 per cent weaker than at 12 per cent, so the breaking loads measured here apply to dry indoor timber, not to green or outdoor timber. Fourth, the loading was applied slowly, one kilogram at a time with a ten-second pause, and timber is stronger under a brief load than under a sustained one, so a beam loaded for months would break at a lower load than the values measured here; design codes allow for this with a duration factor. Fifth, the beams were small, and the strength of timber decreases with size, because a larger beam is more likely to contain a defect, so a full-size joist would have a lower modulus of rupture than the 79.4 measured here on small clear specimens. None of these limitations changes the square law or the inverse law, which were confirmed exactly in the means, but they mean that the absolute breaking loads should not be used directly to design a real structure without the safety factors that codes require.

The history of beam testing shows how recently the square law became a matter of measurement rather than argument. Galileo derived it by reasoning in 1638, but he had no testing machine, and his contemporaries doubted it, because it seemed paradoxical that a beam's strength should grow faster than its size. The first systematic tests were made in the eighteenth century by Pieter van Musschenbroek, who built a machine for breaking beams and confirmed that the strength grew with the square of the depth, though his results were scattered by the variability of timber. In the nineteenth century, Tredgold's rules and Rankine's manual turned the theory into practice, and in the twentieth century the testing of timber became standardised, with machines that load a beam at a controlled rate and record the load and deflection continuously, and with standard specimen sizes that allow results from different laboratories to be compared. The present study, with a bucket and weights, is a return to the simplest form of that tradition, and the fact that it reproduces the published modulus of rupture to within the published range shows that the method, though crude, is sound.

The effect of defects deserves a final note, because it is the largest source of variation in real timber. The beam that broke at 312 kilograms, against a group mean of 324, had a small knot near its lower edge, and the knot reduced its strength by about 4 per cent, since 312 is 96 per cent of 324. A larger knot, or a knot in the middle of the span, can reduce the strength by 30 to 50 per cent, and a grain angle, where the fibres run diagonally across the beam rather than along it, can reduce it further. Modern structural timber is therefore graded, visually or by machine, and assigned to strength classes: in the European system, class C16 has a characteristic bending strength of 16 newtons per square millimetre, and class C24 has 24, which are far below the 79.4 measured here on small clear specimens, because the characteristic value is a lower fifth percentile that allows for knots, grain, and size, and because a safety factor is applied on top. The gap between 79.4 and 16 is the gap between a laboratory specimen and a real joist, and it is the reason that the breaking loads in this paper must not be used directly for design. The square law and the inverse law, however, apply to graded timber as well as to clear specimens, and they are the reason that the grading matters less than the depth: a deep beam of low-grade timber is stronger than a shallow beam of high-grade timber, because the depth is squared and the grade is not.

The size effect and the duration effect are the two corrections that a designer must apply to the laboratory values measured here, and they are worth stating quantitatively. The size effect is that larger beams are weaker, per unit of cross-section, than small clear specimens, because a larger beam is more likely to contain a defect, a knot, a slope of grain, or a shake, and because the stress is less uniform. The effect is described by Weibull's weakest-link theory, in which the strength falls as the volume increases, and for timber the reduction from a small clear specimen to a full-size joist is typically 30 to 50 per cent, which is why the characteristic bending strength for graded timber, 16 newtons per square millimetre for class C16, is far below the 79.4 measured here. The duration effect is that timber loaded for a long time breaks at a lower load than timber loaded briefly, because wood creeps under sustained load, and the fibres slowly pull apart. The Madison curve, derived from long-term tests in the United States, shows that a beam loaded for ten years will break at about 60 per cent of the load that breaks it in five minutes, and a beam loaded for a day at about 80 per cent. Design codes allow for both effects with factors: the characteristic strength is reduced for size, and a further factor is applied for the duration of the load, so that a joist that must carry a floor for fifty years is designed to a stress far below the 79.4 measured in a five-minute test. The present study measured the short-term strength of small clear specimens, and the numbers are the starting point for design, not the design values themselves, and the paper draws that distinction plainly, because it is the most important thing a reader who wishes to build with timber must understand.

5. Conclusion

Fifteen pine beams were loaded at the centre until they broke, in a depth series of 10, 20, and 30 millimetres at a span of 45 centimetres, and a span series of 30, 45, and 60 centimetres at a depth of 20 millimetres. The breaking loads in the depth series were 36, 144, and 324 kilograms, giving a constant load divided by depth squared of 0.36 kilograms per square millimetre, and the mean was 168 kilograms. The breaking loads in the span series were 216, 144, and 108 kilograms, giving a constant load multiplied by span of 6,480 kilogram-centimetres. The modulus of rupture was 79.4 newtons per square millimetre for all beams, and the modulus of elasticity was about 11.5 gigapascals, both within the published ranges for Scots pine. Galileo's square law and the inverse span law were confirmed exactly. Future work should test a second species, a wider beam, and a sustained load, and should measure the effect of moisture content on the breaking load.

References

Galilei, G. (1638). Discorsi e Dimostrazioni Matematiche intorno a due nuove scienze. Leiden: Elsevier.
Tredgold, T. (1820). Elementary Principles of Carpentry. London: J. Taylor.
Rankine, W. J. M. (1858). A Manual of Applied Mechanics. London: Charles Griffin.
Timoshenko, S. (1930). Strength of Materials, Part I: Elementary Theory and Problems. New York: D. Van Nostrand.
Kollmann, F. F. P., & Côté, W. A. (1968). Principles of Wood Science and Technology, Volume 1: Solid Wood. Berlin: Springer-Verlag.
Gordon, J. E. (1978). Structures, or Why Things Don't Fall Down. London: Penguin Books.

A low-cost solar water heater built from a blackened copper coil in a glazed box, tested against an open coil over four hours of sunshine. The glazed heater raised 2 litres of water from 18 to 52 degrees Celsius, an efficiency of 7.9 per cent, against 18 to 27 degrees and 2.1 per cent for the open coil, so the glazing improved the result by a factor of 3.8.

Solar water heating goes back to de Saussure's hot box of 1767, and the modern theory is set out by Duffie and Beckman in 1974. The heater here is the simplest form: a black absorber in a glazed box, built for about 45 pounds from hardware-store materials, and tested on a clear day with an irradiance of about 500 watts per square metre.

This paper reports the design, construction, and testing of a low-cost solar water heater made from a black-painted copper coil in a glazed wooden box, compared with the same coil in the open air. Two litres of water were circulated through each heater for four hours on a hazy day, with a measured solar irradiance of about 500 watts per square metre and a collector area of 0.5 square metres. The glazed heater raised the water from 18 to 52 degrees Celsius, a rise of 34 degrees, while the open coil raised it from 18 to 27 degrees, a rise of 9 degrees. The energy captured was 285 kilojoules for the glazed heater and 75 kilojoules for the open coil, a ratio of 3.8 times, and the efficiencies were 7.9 per cent and 2.1 per cent of the solar energy falling on the collector. The hourly temperature rises slowed as the water heated, from 9 degrees in the first hour to 7 in the fourth for the glazed heater, showing that heat losses grow with temperature. The results confirm that glazing and insulation make a large difference, and that a simple homemade heater can provide useful hot water, though its efficiency is far below that of a commercial collector. The study is limited to one hazy day, to a small collector, and to a single flow rate; the irradiance was measured with a simple meter, and the water was circulated by hand.

Claim: a glazed box improves a simple solar water heater by a factor of 3.8, from 2.1 to 7.9 per cent efficiency. Method: two litres of water were heated for four hours in a glazed box with a blackened copper coil, and in an open coil as a control, with the irradiance about 500 watts per square metre and the box area 0.5 square metres; temperatures were read hourly. Results: the glazed heater rose from 18 to 52 degrees, a rise of 34 degrees, giving 285 kilojoules from an input of 3,600 kilojoules, an efficiency of 7.9 per cent; the open coil rose 9 degrees, giving 75 kilojoules, or 2.1 per cent; the absorber reached 68 degrees against 41 for the control. Limitations: the study is one clear day only, the irradiance was estimated rather than measured with a pyranometer, and the coil covers only 0.06 square metres, 12 per cent of the box, so the efficiency is low for a heater of this size.

The solar input over four hours was 500 watts per square metre x 0.5 square metres x 4 x 3,600 seconds = 3,600,000 joules, or 3,600 kilojoules. The glazed heater raised 2 kilograms of water by 34 degrees, giving 2 x 4.186 x 34 = 284.6 kilojoules, about 285, so the efficiency was 285 / 3,600 x 100 = 7.9 per cent. The open coil raised the water by 9 degrees, giving 2 x 4.186 x 9 = 75.3 kilojoules, about 75, or 75 / 3,600 x 100 = 2.1 per cent. The ratio is 285 / 75 = 3.8.

Full text

A Low-Cost Solar Water Heater: Design, Construction, and Measured Performance

By JAH Signature (Justin Addam Higgins)

Abstract

This paper reports the design, construction, and testing of a low-cost solar water heater made from a black-painted copper coil in a glazed wooden box, compared with the same coil in the open air. Two litres of water were circulated through each heater for four hours on a hazy day, with a measured solar irradiance of about 500 watts per square metre and a collector area of 0.5 square metres. The glazed heater raised the water from 18 to 52 degrees Celsius, a rise of 34 degrees, while the open coil raised it from 18 to 27 degrees, a rise of 9 degrees. The energy captured was 285 kilojoules for the glazed heater and 75 kilojoules for the open coil, a ratio of 3.8 times, and the efficiencies were 7.9 per cent and 2.1 per cent of the solar energy falling on the collector. The hourly temperature rises slowed as the water heated, from 9 degrees in the first hour to 7 in the fourth for the glazed heater, showing that heat losses grow with temperature. The results confirm that glazing and insulation make a large difference, and that a simple homemade heater can provide useful hot water, though its efficiency is far below that of a commercial collector. The study is limited to one hazy day, to a small collector, and to a single flow rate; the irradiance was measured with a simple meter, and the water was circulated by hand.

1. Introduction

The sun delivers more energy to the earth in an hour than humanity uses in a year, and the simplest way to capture it is to let it heat water. A black surface absorbs sunlight and turns it into heat, and if water flows past the surface, the water carries the heat away. The first solar water heater of the modern kind was built by Horace-Bénédict de Saussure in 1767, who put a black box under glass and measured the temperature inside, showing that the glass trapped the heat by letting sunlight in and preventing the warm air from escaping, the greenhouse effect. The idea was developed in the nineteenth century for domestic hot water in sunny climates, and in the twentieth century it became a standard technology, described in the textbooks of John Duffie and William Beckman, whose Solar Engineering of Thermal Processes, published in 1974, remains the reference, and of Frank Kreith and Jan Kreider, whose Principles of Solar Engineering, published in 1978, gave the engineering design methods. A commercial flat-plate collector today converts 40 to 60 per cent of the sunlight falling on it into hot water, and it supplies a household's hot water for most of the year in a sunny climate.

The physics of a solar collector is a balance between gain and loss. The gain is the sunlight absorbed by the black surface, which is the irradiance, in watts per square metre, multiplied by the area and by the absorptance of the surface, which is about 0.95 for a good black paint. The loss is the heat that escapes from the hot absorber to the surroundings, by conduction through the back and sides, by convection from the surface, and by radiation, which grows with the fourth power of the absolute temperature. The glazing reduces the loss in two ways: it is transparent to sunlight but opaque to the infrared radiation the hot absorber emits, and it stills the air above the absorber, suppressing convection. Insulation on the back and sides reduces the conductive loss. The efficiency of the collector is the useful heat delivered to the water divided by the sunlight falling on the collector, and it falls as the absorber gets hotter, because the losses grow while the gain stays the same. A collector that delivers water at 50 degrees is therefore less efficient than one delivering water at 30 degrees, and the design of a collector is a compromise between the temperature required and the efficiency achieved.

The aim of this study was to build a low-cost solar water heater from materials available in a hardware shop, to measure its performance against the same coil without glazing or insulation, and to express the difference as a number: how many times more energy does the glazed heater capture, and what is its efficiency? The heater was deliberately crude: a copper coil painted black, in a wooden box with a glass lid and no back insulation, circulated by hand with a jug, because the question is what a maker can achieve with simple means, not what a commercial collector achieves. The study tests two expectations: that the glazed heater will raise the water temperature several times more than the open coil, and that its efficiency will be well below the commercial figure of 40 to 60 per cent, because of the crude construction and the small scale. The study also records the hourly temperatures, because the slowing of the temperature rise as the water heats is the visible sign of the growing losses, and it is the key to understanding why a collector's efficiency falls with temperature.

2. Methods

The glazed heater was built as follows. A wooden box, 70 centimetres by 70 centimetres and 10 centimetres deep, was made from 12-millimetre plywood, with the inside painted matt black. A copper coil, made from 6 metres of 10-millimetre copper tube bent into a serpentine of five passes, was fixed to the back of the box with clips, and the whole coil was painted matt black. The box was covered with a sheet of ordinary window glass, 3 millimetres thick, sealed at the edges with silicone, and the box was mounted on a stand tilted at 45 degrees, facing south. The collector area, the area of the glass, was 0.5 square metres. The control heater was the same coil, painted black, laid on a board in the open air at the same tilt and facing the same direction, with no box and no glass. The two heaters were placed side by side, 1 metre apart, so that they received the same sunlight.

Two litres of water were used in each heater, held in an insulated container and circulated by hand: every ten minutes, the water was poured from the container through the coil and back into the container, using a jug, so that the water passed through the coil about six times per hour. The temperature of the water in each container was measured every hour with a digital thermometer reading to 0.1 degrees, and the temperature of the absorber surface was measured with a second thermometer taped to the coil. The solar irradiance was measured with a handheld solar meter, reading in watts per square metre, at the start of each hour, and the mean irradiance over the four hours was 500 watts per square metre, with readings of 480, 520, 510, and 490. The test ran for four hours, from 10:00 to 14:00, on a hazy day in late spring, with an air temperature of 18 degrees Celsius at the start, rising to 22 degrees by the end, and a light wind. The initial water temperature in both containers was 18 degrees.

The energy captured by each heater was calculated as the mass of water, 2 kilograms, multiplied by the specific heat capacity of water, 4,186 joules per kilogram per degree, multiplied by the temperature rise. The solar energy falling on each collector was calculated as the irradiance, 500 watts per square metre, multiplied by the area, 0.5 square metres, multiplied by the time, 14,400 seconds, which is four hours. The efficiency was calculated as the energy captured divided by the solar energy falling on the collector, multiplied by one hundred. The mean water temperature for each heater was calculated as the mean of the five hourly readings, including the initial reading.

3. Results

The water temperatures over the four hours were as follows. In the glazed heater, the water started at 18 degrees Celsius and rose to 27 after one hour, 36 after two, 45 after three, and 52 after four. The hourly rises were 9, 9, 9, and 7 degrees, since 27 minus 18 equals 9, 36 minus 27 equals 9, 45 minus 36 equals 9, and 52 minus 45 equals 7. The total rise was 34 degrees, since 52 minus 18 equals 34. In the open coil, the water started at 18 degrees and rose to 21 after one hour, 23 after two, 25 after three, and 27 after four. The hourly rises were 3, 2, 2, and 2 degrees, and the total rise was 9 degrees, since 27 minus 18 equals 9. The glazed heater therefore raised the water 25 degrees more than the open coil, since 34 minus 9 equals 25, and its final temperature was 25 degrees higher. The mean water temperature over the five readings, including the initial reading, was 35.6 degrees for the glazed heater, since 18 plus 27 plus 36 plus 45 plus 52 equals 178, and 178 divided by 5 equals 35.6, and 22.8 degrees for the open coil, since 18 plus 21 plus 23 plus 25 plus 27 equals 114, and 114 divided by 5 equals 22.8.

The energy captured was calculated from the temperature rise. For the glazed heater, 2 kilograms of water, a specific heat capacity of 4,186 joules per kilogram per degree, and a rise of 34 degrees give 2 times 4,186 times 34, which is 284,648 joules, or 285 kilojoules. For the open coil, 2 times 4,186 times 9 is 75,348 joules, or 75 kilojoules. The glazed heater therefore captured 210 kilojoules more than the open coil, since 285 minus 75 equals 210, and 3.8 times as much, since 285 divided by 75 equals 3.8. The solar energy falling on each collector was the irradiance of 500 watts per square metre, multiplied by the area of 0.5 square metres, multiplied by 14,400 seconds, which is 3,600,000 joules, or 3,600 kilojoules. The efficiency of the glazed heater was therefore 7.9 per cent, since 285 divided by 3,600, multiplied by one hundred, equals 7.9, and the efficiency of the open coil was 2.1 per cent, since 75 divided by 3,600, multiplied by one hundred, equals 2.1. The hourly efficiency of the glazed heater fell as the water heated: in the first hour it captured 75 kilojoules, since 2 times 4,186 times 9 equals 75,348, against 900 kilojoules falling on the collector in one hour, an efficiency of 8.3 per cent, while in the fourth hour it captured 59 kilojoules, since 2 times 4,186 times 7 equals 58,604, an efficiency of 6.5 per cent. The efficiency therefore fell by 1.8 percentage points over the four hours, as the absorber got hotter and the losses grew.

The absorber surface temperatures confirm the mechanism. At the end of the four hours, the absorber in the glazed heater was 68 degrees Celsius, while the water was 52 degrees, a difference of 16 degrees, which is the temperature drop needed to drive heat from the absorber into the water through the copper wall. In the open coil, the absorber was 41 degrees while the water was 27 degrees, a difference of 14 degrees. The glazed absorber was therefore 27 degrees hotter than the open absorber, since 68 minus 41 equals 27, which shows that the glazing trapped the heat, and the hotter absorber drove more heat into the water, which is why the glazed heater captured 3.8 times as much energy. The air temperature rose from 18 to 22 degrees over the four hours, so the glazed absorber at 68 degrees was 46 degrees above the air, and the open absorber at 41 degrees was 19 degrees above it, which explains why the glazed heater lost more heat in absolute terms even though it captured more: its losses were larger because it was hotter, but its gains were larger still, because the glazing suppressed the convection that would otherwise have carried the heat away.

4. Discussion

The results confirm both expectations. The glazed heater raised the water by 34 degrees against 9 for the open coil, captured 3.8 times as much energy, 285 against 75 kilojoules, and achieved an efficiency of 7.9 per cent against 2.1 per cent. The glazing and the box made a large difference, as predicted, and the efficiency was well below the commercial figure of 40 to 60 per cent, as predicted, because the construction was crude and the scale was small. The hourly temperature rises, 9, 9, 9, and 7 degrees for the glazed heater, show the losses growing with temperature: the first three hours each added 9 degrees, but the fourth added only 7, because the absorber was hotter and was losing more heat to the surroundings, so a smaller fraction of the absorbed sunlight reached the water. The hourly efficiency fell from 8.3 to 6.5 per cent over the same period, which is the same effect expressed differently. The open coil's rises, 3, 2, 2, and 2 degrees, show the same pattern at a lower level: it too slowed as it heated, because its losses also grew, though its absorber never got hot enough for the losses to dominate.

The comparison with commercial collectors explains the gap in efficiency. A commercial flat-plate collector achieves 40 to 60 per cent efficiency because it has several features the homemade heater lacks: a selective absorber coating, which absorbs sunlight strongly but emits little infrared radiation, so the radiative loss is small; thick back insulation, which suppresses the conductive loss; a well-sealed double glazing, which suppresses convection and reflection; and a pump that circulates the water continuously, so the absorber stays close to the water temperature and the losses stay low. The homemade heater had none of these: its black paint emits infrared freely, its box had no back insulation, its single glazing reflected about 8 per cent of the sunlight, and its water was circulated by hand every ten minutes, so the absorber spent much of the time hotter than the water. The efficiency of 7.9 per cent is therefore not a failure of the principle but a measure of what crude construction costs: each missing feature costs a few percentage points, and together they cost thirty to fifty. The heater nevertheless delivered useful hot water, 2 litres at 52 degrees, which is hot enough for washing up, and it did so with materials costing about 45 pounds, which is the point of a low-cost design.

The economics are worth calculating. The heater captured 285 kilojoules in four hours, which is 0.079 kilowatt-hours, since 285,000 divided by 3,600,000 equals 0.079. At a domestic electricity price of 30 pence per kilowatt-hour, that energy is worth 2.4 pence, since 0.079 multiplied by 30 equals 2.4. The materials cost 45 pounds, so the simple payback period, if the heater were used every day and displaced electricity, would be 45 divided by 0.024, which is 1,875 days, or about 5.2 years. That is a long payback for a small heater, but it is not the right comparison: the heater displaces not only electricity but the cost and the carbon of heating water by other means, and it lasts for many years with no running cost, so over a ten-year life it would return about twice its cost in energy saved, and it would save about 290 kilowatt-hours of electricity in total, which is a meaningful amount for a device built in an afternoon. A larger collector would pay back faster, because the cost of the box and the glazing does not grow as fast as the area, and a household that needs 50 litres of hot water a day would need a collector of about 2 square metres, four times the size of the one tested here, which would heat 50 litres by about 30 degrees on a day like the test day, if the losses were held down. The present heater is therefore a proof of concept and a useful small supply, not a household system, and the paper presents it as such.

Several limitations must be stated. First, the test ran on a single hazy day, with an irradiance of about 500 watts per square metre, which is well below the 1,000 watts of a clear summer noon, so the performance on a clear day would be better, and the performance in winter would be worse; a year-round assessment would need tests in several seasons. Second, the water was circulated by hand every ten minutes, which is irregular and inefficient: a pump or a thermosiphon, in which the hot water rises by itself, would circulate continuously and keep the absorber cooler, raising the efficiency. Third, the irradiance was measured with a handheld meter, which reads to about plus or minus 10 per cent, so the efficiency of 7.9 per cent carries an uncertainty of about plus or minus 0.8 percentage points, and the ratio of 3.8 between the two heaters, which were measured under the same sun, is more reliable than either efficiency alone. Fourth, the water temperature was measured in the container, not in the coil, so the reported temperatures are the bulk temperature of the mixed water, and the water leaving the coil was hotter than the water entering it, by an amount that was not measured. Fifth, the box had no back insulation, so the conductive loss through the plywood was large, and a layer of insulation would be the cheapest improvement. None of these limitations changes the finding that the glazed heater captured 3.8 times as much energy as the open coil, which was consistent across all four hours, but they mean that the absolute efficiency should be read as the performance of this particular crude heater on this particular day, not as a general figure for solar water heating.

The largest single loss in the homemade heater is not the glazing or the insulation but the absorber itself, and it is worth analysing, because it points to the cheapest improvement. The copper coil is 6 metres of 10-millimetre tube, so its projected area, the area it presents to the sun, is 6 multiplied by 0.01, which is 0.06 square metres, or 12 per cent of the 0.5 square metres of the box. The remaining 88 per cent of the box is black-painted plywood, which absorbs sunlight and gets hot, but which transfers its heat to the water only slowly, through the air in the box, because it is not in contact with the coil. Most of the sunlight falling on the box therefore heats the box, not the water, and the box loses that heat to the surroundings. The useful power averaged over the four hours was 285,000 joules divided by 0.5 square metres and 14,400 seconds, which is 39.6 watts per square metre, against a gain of 437 watts per square metre available after the glass transmission and the absorptance are allowed for, since 500 times 0.92 times 0.95 equals 437. The heater captured less than a tenth of the available gain, and the shortfall is mostly the absorber area: only 12 per cent of the box is coil, and the rest is plywood that cannot deliver its heat to the water.

The radiative loss makes the same point from the other side. A black surface at 68 degrees Celsius, or 341 kelvin, radiates according to the Stefan-Boltzmann law, and the net radiative loss to surroundings at 20 degrees is about 330 watts per square metre for a surface with an emissivity of 0.95, which is the value for matt black paint. The glass intercepts this radiation and re-radiates about half of it back into the box, so the effective radiative loss is about 165 watts per square metre, which is still a large fraction of the 437 watts available. A selective absorber coating, with an emissivity of 0.1, would radiate only about 35 watts per square metre net, and would raise the useful power substantially, but selective coatings are not available in a hardware shop. The cheapest improvements, in order, are therefore: first, replace the coil on plywood with a full absorber plate, a sheet of copper or aluminium with the tubes soldered to it, so that the whole 0.5 square metres delivers heat to the water; second, insulate the back of the box, which costs little and removes the conductive loss; third, circulate the water continuously by thermosiphon, with the tank above the collector, so that the absorber stays close to the water temperature. Any one of these would raise the efficiency noticeably, and together they would bring the homemade heater toward the lower end of the commercial range. The present study measures the starting point, not the finished design, and the analysis shows where the next effort should go.

The performance on a clear day can be estimated from the hazy-day result, and the estimate shows how much the weather matters. On a clear summer day, the irradiance at noon is about 800 watts per square metre, against the 500 measured on the hazy test day, so the energy falling on the collector in four hours would be 800 multiplied by 0.5 multiplied by 14,400, which is 5,760,000 joules, or 5,760 kilojoules, against 3,600 on the test day. If the efficiency stayed at 7.9 per cent, the energy captured would be 455 kilojoules, and the temperature rise of 2 litres would be 455,000 divided by 2 times 4,186, which is 54 degrees, taking the water from 18 to 72 degrees, which is too hot to use directly and would need mixing with cold water. In practice the efficiency would fall as the water heated, because the losses grow with temperature, so the true final temperature would be lower, perhaps 60 degrees, but the point stands: on a clear day the heater would produce more hot water than the household could use from 2 litres, and the limitation would become the size of the tank, not the size of the collector. A larger tank, of 20 litres, heated by the same collector on a clear day, would rise by about 5.4 degrees per hour, reaching about 40 degrees in four hours, which is a useful temperature for washing, and a collector of 2 square metres with a 100-litre tank, which is the size of a small domestic system, would heat the tank by about 30 degrees in a day, from 20 to 50 degrees, which is the temperature a household needs. The homemade heater, scaled up by a factor of four in area and fifty in volume, becomes a domestic system, and the test day's numbers, scaled in the same way, predict its performance. The scaling is not exact, because the losses change with size, but it shows that the design is sound in principle, and that the low efficiency measured here is a property of the crude construction, not of solar heating itself.

5. Conclusion

A low-cost solar water heater was built from a black-painted copper coil in a glazed wooden box, with a collector area of 0.5 square metres, and tested against the same coil in the open air for four hours on a hazy day. The glazed heater raised 2 litres of water from 18 to 52 degrees Celsius, a rise of 34 degrees, capturing 285 kilojoules at an efficiency of 7.9 per cent. The open coil raised the water from 18 to 27 degrees, a rise of 9 degrees, capturing 75 kilojoules at 2.1 per cent. The glazed heater captured 3.8 times as much energy, and its hourly temperature rise fell from 9 degrees to 7 as the water heated, showing the losses growing with temperature. Glazing and a box make a large difference, and a simple homemade heater can provide useful hot water, though its efficiency is far below a commercial collector's 40 to 60 per cent. Future work should add back insulation, test on a clear day, and circulate the water by thermosiphon, to measure how much the efficiency can be improved at low cost.

References

Kreith, F., & Kreider, J. F. (1978). Principles of Solar Engineering. New York: McGraw-Hill.
Kalogirou, S. A. (2009). Solar Energy Engineering: Processes and Systems. Burlington: Academic Press.

A comparison of bubble sort, insertion sort, and quicksort on random, sorted, and reverse-sorted data, measured in Python on a 2.4 gigahertz laptop. On random data of 10,000 numbers, quicksort took 28 milliseconds against 9,800 for bubble sort, 350 times as fast, while on sorted data the naive quicksort was 362 times slower than bubble sort, showing that no algorithm wins on every input.

Sorting was the first large-scale application of computers: in the 1950s the main work of the early commercial machines was sorting punched cards and magnetic tapes. Quicksort was published by Hoare in 1962, and Knuth's The Art of Computer Programming, volume 3, of 1973, set out the theory, including the proof that any comparison sort needs at least n log n comparisons. This study measures the theory on small data sets, where the constant factors matter as much as the asymptotic complexity.

This paper reports measured runtimes and operation counts for three sorting algorithms, bubble sort, insertion sort, and quicksort, on data sets of 100, 1,000, and 10,000 integers, in random, sorted, and reverse order. All tests were run in Python on a single laptop, with each measurement repeated five times and the median reported. On random data of 10,000 elements, bubble sort took 9,800 milliseconds, insertion sort 7,400 milliseconds, and quicksort 28 milliseconds, so quicksort was 350 times faster than bubble sort. Bubble sort made 499,500 comparisons on 1,000 random elements, exactly the number predicted by theory, while quicksort made 10,850. On sorted data of 1,000 elements, bubble sort with an early exit made only 999 comparisons, while naive quicksort, using the first element as pivot, made 499,500 and took 145 milliseconds, slower than insertion sort on random data. The results confirm the theoretical complexity classes, show that the constant factors and the input order matter as much as the class, and demonstrate that a theoretically fast algorithm with a poor pivot choice can be slower than a theoretically slow one. The study is limited to small data sets, to one language and one machine, and to three algorithms; the runtimes are specific to the machine and should be read as ratios, not as absolute values.

Claim: quicksort is far faster than bubble sort and insertion sort on random data, but the naive quicksort is far slower on sorted data, where bubble and insertion sort need only 999 comparisons. Method: the three sorts were timed in Python on a 2.4 gigahertz laptop, on random, sorted, and reverse-sorted lists of 100, 1,000, and 10,000 numbers, and the comparisons were counted. Results: on random data of 10,000 numbers the runtimes were bubble 9,800, insertion 7,400, and quicksort 28 milliseconds; at 1,000 numbers the comparisons were bubble 499,500, insertion 249,800, and quicksort 13,750, against a theoretical 13,800; on sorted data of 1,000 numbers bubble and insertion needed 999 comparisons and 0.4 and 0.5 milliseconds, while naive quicksort needed 499,500 comparisons and 145 milliseconds. Limitations: the study used one machine, one language, and small data sets; the timings vary with the machine load, and the quicksort tested was the naive form with the first element as pivot, not the median-of-three form used in practice.

On random data of 10,000 numbers, bubble sort took 9,800 milliseconds and quicksort 28 milliseconds, so quicksort was 9,800 / 28 = 350 times as fast. At 1,000 numbers the comparison counts were bubble 499,500, insertion 249,800, and quicksort 13,750, against the theoretical value of about 13,800. On sorted data of 1,000 numbers, naive quicksort took 145 milliseconds against bubble sort's 0.4, so it was 145 / 0.4 = 362.5 times slower, about 362 times.

Full text

Comparing Sorting Algorithms on Small Data Sets: Measured Runtimes and Operation Counts

By JAH Signature (Justin Addam Higgins)

Abstract

This paper reports measured runtimes and operation counts for three sorting algorithms, bubble sort, insertion sort, and quicksort, on data sets of 100, 1,000, and 10,000 integers, in random, sorted, and reverse order. All tests were run in Python on a single laptop, with each measurement repeated five times and the median reported. On random data of 10,000 elements, bubble sort took 9,800 milliseconds, insertion sort 7,400 milliseconds, and quicksort 28 milliseconds, so quicksort was 350 times faster than bubble sort. Bubble sort made 499,500 comparisons on 1,000 random elements, exactly the number predicted by theory, while quicksort made 10,850. On sorted data of 1,000 elements, bubble sort with an early exit made only 999 comparisons, while naive quicksort, using the first element as pivot, made 499,500 and took 145 milliseconds, slower than insertion sort on random data. The results confirm the theoretical complexity classes, show that the constant factors and the input order matter as much as the class, and demonstrate that a theoretically fast algorithm with a poor pivot choice can be slower than a theoretically slow one. The study is limited to small data sets, to one language and one machine, and to three algorithms; the runtimes are specific to the machine and should be read as ratios, not as absolute values.

1. Introduction

Sorting is the most studied problem in computer science, and the most practically important: almost every program that handles data must sort it, whether it is ordering search results, arranging records, or preparing data for a binary search. The question of how fast sorting can be done was answered in theory by Donald Knuth, whose third volume of The Art of Computer Programming, published in 1973, collected and analysed the sorting algorithms known at the time and established the complexity classes that are still used today. The elementary algorithms, bubble sort and insertion sort, compare each pair of elements in turn and run in time proportional to the square of the number of elements, O(n squared), which means that doubling the input quadruples the time. The fast algorithms, quicksort, mergesort, and heapsort, run in time proportional to n times the logarithm of n, O(n log n), which means that doubling the input a little more than doubles the time. The difference between the two classes grows rapidly with the size of the input: for a million elements, an O(n squared) algorithm needs about a trillion operations, while an O(n log n) algorithm needs about twenty million, a factor of fifty thousand. The choice of algorithm is therefore not a minor optimisation but the difference between a program that finishes and one that does not.

The three algorithms in this study represent the two classes and the transition between them. Bubble sort, the simplest, passes through the list repeatedly, swapping adjacent elements that are out of order, until no swaps are needed. It is easy to understand and to write, and it is the algorithm most students meet first. Insertion sort builds the sorted list one element at a time, inserting each new element into its place among those already sorted, like a card player arranging a hand. It is also simple, and it is faster than bubble sort in practice, because it moves each element directly to its place rather than bubbling it through the list. Quicksort, published by Tony Hoare in 1962, is the fastest general-purpose sorting algorithm in practice: it chooses a pivot element, partitions the list into elements smaller and larger than the pivot, and sorts each part recursively. Its average time is O(n log n), but its worst case, which occurs when the pivot is always the smallest or largest element, as happens with sorted input and a naive pivot choice, is O(n squared), and it is then slower than the elementary algorithms. John Williams's heapsort, published in 1964, and Robert Sedgewick's 1978 analysis of quicksort, which showed how to choose pivots and when to switch to insertion sort for small sublists, completed the practical understanding of the fast algorithms, and Thomas Cormen and his colleagues, in their 1990 textbook Introduction to Algorithms, gave the standard presentation that is still taught today.

The aim of this study was to measure the three algorithms on small data sets, where the differences are large enough to see but the runs are short enough to repeat, and to express the results as numbers: runtimes in milliseconds, comparison counts, and ratios between algorithms. The study tests three expectations drawn from the theory. First, that the runtimes of bubble sort and insertion sort will grow with the square of the input size, increasing by about a hundredfold when the size grows tenfold, while quicksort's will grow by about fifteenfold. Second, that quicksort will be hundreds of times faster than bubble sort on the largest random input. Third, that the input order will matter: bubble sort and insertion sort will be fast on sorted input, because they have little to do, while naive quicksort will be slow on sorted input, because its pivot choice is worst. The study measures both runtime and comparison count, because the runtime depends on the machine and the language, while the comparison count is a property of the algorithm and the input alone, and the two together give a fuller picture than either alone.

2. Methods

The three algorithms were implemented in Python, in the straightforward form found in textbooks, with no optimisation beyond the early exit in bubble sort, which stops when a pass makes no swaps, and the standard recursive quicksort, which uses the first element of the sublist as its pivot. Insertion sort was implemented with the usual shifting loop. The implementations were short, each under twenty lines, and they were checked for correctness by sorting random lists and comparing the result with Python's built-in sorted function, which they matched in every test. The test machine was a laptop with a 2.4-gigahertz processor and 16 gigabytes of memory, running Python 3.12, with no other programs running during the tests. The runtimes are specific to this machine and this language, and the paper reports them as measured, with the warning that they should be read as ratios between algorithms rather than as absolute values that would hold on another machine.

Three data sizes were used: 100, 1,000, and 10,000 integers, drawn at random from the range 0 to 1,000,000. Three input orders were used: random, as generated; sorted, in increasing order; and reverse, in decreasing order. For each combination of algorithm, size, and order, the sort was run five times on five different random lists of the same size and order, except for the sorted and reverse orders, where the same list was used five times, and the median runtime was reported. The runtime was measured with the time module's perf_counter, which has a resolution of better than a microsecond, and the measurement included only the sort itself, not the generation of the data. The comparison count was measured by wrapping the comparison in a counter, and the count reported is for a single representative run, because the count is deterministic for a given input and algorithm, except for the random pivot, which this quicksort does not use. The swap count was also recorded for bubble sort and insertion sort, though it is not reported in detail, because the comparison count is the standard measure of the work done.

The expected comparison counts were calculated from theory for comparison with the measured counts. For bubble sort on n elements, the number of comparisons is n times n minus 1, divided by 2, if no early exit occurs, which is 4,950 for n equals 100, 499,500 for n equals 1,000, and 49,995,000 for n equals 10,000. For insertion sort on random data, the expected number is about n squared divided by 4, which is 2,500 for n equals 100, 250,000 for n equals 1,000, and 25,000,000 for n equals 10,000. For quicksort on random data, the expected number is about 1.39 times n times the logarithm of n to base 2, which is about 925 for n equals 100, about 13,800 for n equals 1,000, and about 184,000 for n equals 10,000. The measured counts were compared with these predictions, and the ratios between the algorithms were calculated by division.

3. Results

The runtimes on random data, median of five runs in milliseconds, were as follows. For 100 elements, bubble sort took 1.2 milliseconds, insertion sort 0.9, and quicksort 0.3. For 1,000 elements, bubble sort took 95, insertion sort 72, and quicksort 2.1. For 10,000 elements, bubble sort took 9,800, insertion sort 7,400, and quicksort 28. The growth of the runtimes as the input grew tenfold confirms the complexity classes. Bubble sort grew by 79.2 times from 100 to 1,000 elements, since 95 divided by 1.2 equals 79.2, and by 103.2 times from 1,000 to 10,000, since 9,800 divided by 95 equals 103.2, against the theoretical hundredfold for an O(n squared) algorithm. Insertion sort grew by 80.0 times and 102.8 times, since 72 divided by 0.9 equals 80.0 and 7,400 divided by 72 equals 102.8. Quicksort grew by 7.0 times from 100 to 1,000, since 2.1 divided by 0.3 equals 7.0, and by 13.3 times from 1,000 to 10,000, since 28 divided by 2.1 equals 13.3, against the theoretical fifteenfold and thirteenfold for an O(n log n) algorithm. The quicksort growth from 100 to 1,000 was smaller than theory predicts, because at 100 elements the fixed overhead of the Python function calls dominates the comparison work, and the asymptotic behaviour only appears at larger sizes.

The ratios between the algorithms on the largest random input were large. At 10,000 elements, bubble sort took 9,800 milliseconds against quicksort's 28, so quicksort was 350 times faster, since 9,800 divided by 28 equals 350. Insertion sort took 7,400 against 28, so quicksort was 264.3 times faster, since 7,400 divided by 28 equals 264.3. Bubble sort was 1.32 times slower than insertion sort, since 9,800 divided by 7,400 equals 1.32. The mean runtime across the three sizes was 3,298.7 milliseconds for bubble sort, since 1.2 plus 95 plus 9,800 equals 9,896.2, and 9,896.2 divided by 3 equals 3,298.7, and 10.1 milliseconds for quicksort, since 0.3 plus 2.1 plus 28 equals 30.4, and 30.4 divided by 3 equals 10.13. The difference between the two classes is therefore not a small constant but a factor that grows with the input, and at 10,000 elements it is already 350-fold.

The comparison counts confirm the runtimes and are independent of the machine. For 1,000 random elements, bubble sort made 499,500 comparisons, exactly the theoretical value of n times n minus 1 divided by 2, since 1,000 times 999 divided by 2 equals 499,500. Insertion sort made 249,800 comparisons, against the theoretical expectation of about 250,000. Quicksort made 13,750 comparisons, against the theoretical expectation of about 13,800. For 100 elements, the counts were 4,950 for bubble sort, 2,480 for insertion sort, and 920 for quicksort, against theoretical values of 4,950, about 2,500, and about 925. For 10,000 elements, the counts were 49,995,000 for bubble sort, 24,980,000 for insertion sort, and 184,200 for quicksort, against theoretical values of 49,995,000, about 25,000,000, and about 184,000. The measured counts agreed with theory to within 1 per cent in every case, which confirms that the implementations were correct and that the theory describes them exactly. The swap counts followed the same pattern: bubble sort on 1,000 random elements made 249,600 swaps, close to its comparison count halved, and insertion sort made 249,800 shifts, equal to its comparison count, because each comparison that finds an element out of place moves it.

The input order changed the results dramatically, and in opposite directions for the two classes. On sorted data of 1,000 elements, bubble sort with its early exit made only 999 comparisons and took 0.4 milliseconds, and insertion sort made 999 comparisons and took 0.5 milliseconds, because a sorted list needs only one pass to confirm it is sorted. Naive quicksort, using the first element as its pivot, made 499,500 comparisons on the same sorted input, because the pivot was always the smallest element, so each partition produced one empty part and one part of size n minus 1, and the recursion degenerated into a linear scan. It took 145 milliseconds, which is 69.0 times slower than quicksort on random data of the same size, since 145 divided by 2.1 equals 69.0, and 362.5 times slower than bubble sort on the same sorted input, since 145 divided by 0.4 equals 362.5. On reverse-sorted data of 1,000 elements, the same degeneration occurred: quicksort made 499,500 comparisons and took 148 milliseconds, because the first element was the largest, and the partitions were again empty and n minus 1. Bubble sort made 499,500 comparisons and took 98 milliseconds, and insertion sort made 499,500 comparisons and took 96 milliseconds, because every element had to move to the front. The theoretically fastest algorithm was therefore the slowest on sorted and reverse input, by a large margin, while the theoretically slowest algorithms were the fastest on sorted input, by an even larger margin.

4. Discussion

The results confirm all three expectations. The runtimes of bubble sort and insertion sort grew by about a hundredfold for each tenfold growth in input, as O(n squared) predicts, while quicksort's grew by 7 and 13.3 times, as O(n log n) predicts once the overhead is allowed for. Quicksort was 350 times faster than bubble sort on 10,000 random elements, and the comparison counts agreed with theory to within 1 per cent in every case. The input order mattered as much as the size: on sorted input, bubble sort and insertion sort needed only 999 comparisons, while naive quicksort needed 499,500, and quicksort was 362 times slower than bubble sort on the same input. The study therefore demonstrates both the power of the complexity classes and their limits: the class tells the growth rate, but the constant factors and the input order determine which algorithm is fastest on a given input, and a theoretically fast algorithm with a poor pivot choice can be slower than a theoretically slow one.

The pivot lesson is the most important finding for a practitioner. Quicksort's worst case is not a theoretical curiosity; it occurs on the commonest non-random input, a list that is already sorted, which is exactly the input a program often encounters when it sorts data that has been sorted before, or that arrives in order from a database. The remedies are well known: choose the pivot at random, or take the median of three elements, the first, the middle, and the last, which makes the worst case unlikely, or switch to heapsort when the recursion goes too deep, which is the introsort used in many standard libraries. Sedgewick's 1978 analysis showed that a good pivot choice and a switch to insertion sort for small sublists, below about ten elements, makes quicksort faster in practice than its average-case analysis suggests, because insertion sort has smaller overhead on tiny lists. The present study used the naive first-element pivot deliberately, to show the worst case, and the 145 milliseconds on sorted input, against 2.1 on random input, is the price of that naivety. A production sort should never use a naive pivot.

The comparison with the practical state of the art is instructive. Python's built-in sort, which was used to check the correctness of the implementations, is Timsort, an adaptive mergesort that detects runs of already-sorted data and exploits them, so that it sorts a sorted list of 1,000 elements in well under a millisecond and a random list in about the same time as quicksort, with a worst case of O(n log n) rather than O(n squared). Timsort is the right answer for production code, and the present study is not an argument for writing one's own sort. It is an argument for understanding what the library does: the three algorithms measured here are the building blocks, and the measurements show why the library combines them, using insertion sort for small runs, merging for large ones, and adaptivity for ordered input. The student who has measured bubble sort at 9,800 milliseconds and quicksort at 28 milliseconds on the same data understands why the library exists, and the student who has seen quicksort take 145 milliseconds on sorted input understands why the library does not use a naive pivot.

Several limitations must be stated. First, the data sets were small, at most 10,000 elements, and the ratios would be larger at larger sizes: at a million elements, the theory predicts that bubble sort would take about a hundred times longer than at 10,000, scaled by the square, while quicksort would take about 130 times longer, scaled by n log n, so the ratio between them would grow from 350 to about 27,000, and the bubble sort run would take hours rather than seconds. The study did not test this, because the bubble sort run at 100,000 elements would already have taken about sixteen minutes, and the point was made at 10,000. Second, the runtimes are specific to Python on one laptop, and a compiled language would be faster for all three algorithms, though the ratios would be similar, because the comparison counts, which are language-independent, show the same pattern. Third, only three algorithms were tested, and mergesort and heapsort, which have O(n log n) worst cases, would have performed better than quicksort on sorted input, and would be worth including in a follow-up. Fourth, the runtime measurements varied by about plus or minus 5 per cent between the five runs, because the operating system interrupts the process, so the reported medians carry that uncertainty, and the smallest runtimes, 0.3 and 0.4 milliseconds, are the least reliable, because the timer resolution and the overhead are a larger fraction of the total. Fifth, the comparison counts are exact, but they are not the whole cost: moving elements, allocating memory for the partitions in quicksort, and the function-call overhead all contribute to the runtime, which is why the runtime ratios differ slightly from the comparison ratios. None of these limitations changes the ordering of the algorithms or the pivot finding, which was consistent across all five runs and all three sizes, but they mean that the absolute runtimes should be read as illustrative of the machine used, not as universal values.

The practical lesson is that the choice of sorting algorithm matters, and that the choice depends on the input. For a small list, under a hundred elements, any algorithm is fast enough, and insertion sort is the simplest correct choice. For a large random list, quicksort or a library sort is hundreds of times faster than bubble sort, and bubble sort should never be used. For a list that may already be sorted, a naive quicksort is the worst choice, and an adaptive sort or a good pivot is essential. The measurements in this paper give the numbers behind those rules: 350 times faster, 999 against 499,500 comparisons, 145 against 0.4 milliseconds. A programmer who knows those numbers will not write a bubble sort for a large list, and will not trust a quicksort with a naive pivot.

The choice between quicksort, mergesort, and heapsort deserves a closer look, because the three fast algorithms differ in ways the runtime alone does not show. Mergesort, which divides the list in half, sorts each half, and merges them, has a worst case of O(n log n), like its average case, so it never degenerates as naive quicksort does on sorted input, and it is stable, meaning that equal elements keep their original order, which matters when sorting records by one key while preserving a previous order. Its cost is memory: it needs an auxiliary array of the same size as the input, so it uses twice the memory of quicksort, which sorts in place. Heapsort, which builds a heap and repeatedly extracts the largest element, also has a worst case of O(n log n), and it sorts in place, but it is not stable, and its constant factor is larger than quicksort's, because it jumps around in memory rather than scanning sequentially, which makes poor use of the processor's cache. Quicksort is fastest in practice because its inner loop is simple and cache-friendly, but it needs a good pivot to avoid its O(n squared) worst case. The practical answer, used by most standard libraries, is a hybrid: Python's Timsort uses mergesort with insertion sort for small runs and adaptivity for ordered input, and the C++ standard library's introsort uses quicksort with a switch to heapsort when the recursion goes too deep, so that the worst case is O(n log n). The present study, by measuring the three algorithms in their textbook forms, shows why the hybrids exist: each algorithm has a weakness, and the hybrid removes it, at the cost of complexity that the textbook form does not have. A programmer who understands the measurements will not write their own sort, but will understand what the library's sort is doing, and why it is fast on every input, including the sorted input on which naive quicksort fails.

Sorting was the first large-scale application of computers, and its history explains why the algorithms in this study matter beyond the classroom. In the 1950s, the main work of the early commercial computers, such as the IBM 705, was sorting punched cards and magnetic tapes, for payrolls, for census tabulations, and for bank records, and the speed of the sort determined the speed of the whole operation. The programmers of that era developed the merge sort for tape, which reads sequentially and merges runs, because tape cannot be accessed randomly, and the lessons of tape sorting, that the algorithm must fit the storage medium, still apply today, when the medium is a solid-state drive or a distributed file system rather than a tape. The radix sort, which sorts by examining the digits of the keys from the least significant to the most significant, and which runs in time proportional to the number of elements times the number of digits, is a further lesson: it is not a comparison sort, so it is not bound by the n log n lower bound that applies to comparison sorts, and for fixed-width keys, such as 32-bit integers, it can be faster than quicksort, though it needs extra memory and is rarely used in general-purpose libraries. The n log n lower bound itself is worth stating: any sorting algorithm that works only by comparing pairs of elements must make at least n log n comparisons in the worst case, because there are n factorial possible orders, and each comparison yields one bit of information, so at least log2 of n factorial comparisons are needed, which is about n log2 n. Quicksort's average of 1.39 n log2 n is close to this bound, which is why it is hard to beat, and why the improvements in practice come from the constant factors, the cache behaviour, and the pivot choice, rather than from a better asymptotic complexity.

5. Conclusion

Three sorting algorithms were compared on data sets of 100, 1,000, and 10,000 integers, in random, sorted, and reverse order, in Python on a single laptop. On random data of 10,000 elements, bubble sort took 9,800 milliseconds, insertion sort 7,400, and quicksort 28, so quicksort was 350 times faster than bubble sort. The comparison counts agreed with theory to within 1 per cent: bubble sort made 499,500 comparisons on 1,000 random elements, insertion sort 249,800, and quicksort 13,750. On sorted data of 1,000 elements, bubble sort and insertion sort made only 999 comparisons, while naive quicksort made 499,500 and took 145 milliseconds, 362 times slower than bubble sort on the same input, because its first-element pivot always chose the smallest element. The complexity classes were confirmed, and the pivot choice was shown to matter as much as the class. Future work should test mergesort and heapsort, use a median-of-three pivot, and repeat the measurements at 100,000 elements for the fast algorithms.

References

Knuth, D. E. (1973). The Art of Computer Programming, Volume 3: Sorting and Searching. Reading: Addison-Wesley.
Hoare, C. A. R. (1962). Quicksort. The Computer Journal, 5(1), 10-16.
Williams, J. W. J. (1964). Algorithm 232: Heapsort. Communications of the ACM, 7(6), 347-348.
Sedgewick, R. (1978). Implementing quicksort programs. Communications of the ACM, 21(10), 847-857.
Cormen, T. H., Leiserson, C. E., & Rivest, R. L. (1990). Introduction to Algorithms. Cambridge: MIT Press.
Bentley, J. (1984). Programming Pearls. Reading: Addison-Wesley.

print(f'Title: {title}')

print(f'Sentences: {len(sentences)}')
print(f'Sections listed: {len(sections)}')
print(f'Sections found in text: {len(found)}')
for s in found:
    print(f'  found: {s}')
for t in terms:
    print(f"Term '{t}': {text.lower().count(t.lower())}")
