A rule-based spam filter with six hand-written rules and a threshold of three points, tested on 200 emails, 100 spam and 100 legitimate. It classified 179 correctly, an accuracy of 89.5 per cent, with 87 of 100 spam caught and 92 of 100 legitimate emails passed, so its false positive rate was 8.0 per cent, about one legitimate email in twelve sent to the spam folder.

Rule-based filtering was the first defence against spam, and the methods it uses, scoring words, counting exclamation marks, and checking the sender, go back to the earliest filters of the 1990s. Machine-learning filters, from Graham's Bayesian filter of 2002 onwards, have largely replaced hand-written rules, because spammers adapt to any fixed rule. This study measures what a transparent rule-based filter can and cannot do, as a baseline and as a teaching tool.

This paper reports the design and testing of a rule-based spam filter, built from six simple rules and tested on 200 emails, 100 spam and 100 legitimate mail. Each email was scored by counting how many of the six rules it triggered, and an email scoring 3 or more was classified as spam. The filter correctly identified 87 of the 100 spam emails and 92 of the 100 legitimate emails, giving an accuracy of 89.5 per cent, a precision of 91.6 per cent, a recall of 87.0 per cent, and an F1 score of 0.892. A single-keyword baseline, using the word FREE alone, achieved a recall of only 54.0 per cent. Six of the eight false positives were legitimate newsletters from senders not in the contacts list, and nine of the thirteen false negatives scored exactly 2, just below the threshold. The results confirm that a small set of transparent rules can filter spam with useful accuracy, and that the threshold is the key design choice. The study is limited to a small, curated test set, to rules chosen by hand, and to English-language email; the filter was not tested against adversarial spam designed to evade it.

Claim: six hand-written rules with a threshold of three points classify 200 test emails with 89.5 per cent accuracy. Method: 200 emails, 100 spam and 100 legitimate, collected over six months, were scored by six rules, and the score of three or more was called spam; the results were tabulated as true positives, false negatives, true negatives, and false positives. Results: true positives 87, false negatives 13, true negatives 92, false positives 8; accuracy 89.5 per cent, precision 91.6 per cent, recall 87.0 per cent, specificity 92.0 per cent, and F1 0.892; the mean score was 4.04 for spam and 1.00 for legitimate mail; a threshold of 2 gave 83.0 per cent and a threshold of 4 gave 83.5 per cent. Limitations: the filter was tested on the spam it was built from, not on spam designed to evade it; the false positive rate of 8.0 per cent is too high for a mail server; and the rules do not read images, so image spam passes through.

The confusion counts were true positives 87, false negatives 13, true negatives 92, and false positives 8, from 200 emails. Accuracy is (87 + 92) / 200 x 100 = 89.5 per cent. Precision is 87 / (87 + 8) x 100 = 91.6 per cent. Recall is 87 / (87 + 13) x 100 = 87.0 per cent. Specificity is 92 / (92 + 8) x 100 = 92.0 per cent. The F1 score is 2 x 0.916 x 0.870 / (0.916 + 0.870) = 0.892. The false positive rate is 8 / 100 = 8.0 per cent, or about 1 in 12.5 legitimate emails.

Full text

A Rule-Based Spam Filter: Design, Testing, and Measured Accuracy

By JAH Signature (Justin Addam Higgins)

Abstract

This paper reports the design and testing of a rule-based spam filter, built from six simple rules and tested on 200 emails, 100 spam and 100 legitimate mail. Each email was scored by counting how many of the six rules it triggered, and an email scoring 3 or more was classified as spam. The filter correctly identified 87 of the 100 spam emails and 92 of the 100 legitimate emails, giving an accuracy of 89.5 per cent, a precision of 91.6 per cent, a recall of 87.0 per cent, and an F1 score of 0.892. A single-keyword baseline, using the word FREE alone, achieved a recall of only 54.0 per cent. Six of the eight false positives were legitimate newsletters from senders not in the contacts list, and nine of the thirteen false negatives scored exactly 2, just below the threshold. The results confirm that a small set of transparent rules can filter spam with useful accuracy, and that the threshold is the key design choice. The study is limited to a small, curated test set, to rules chosen by hand, and to English-language email; the filter was not tested against adversarial spam designed to evade it.

1. Introduction

Spam, unsolicited bulk email, is one of the oldest problems on the internet, and one of the most persistent. The first spam message was sent in 1978, to a few hundred users of the early ARPANET, and by the 1990s spam was a substantial fraction of all email. The problem is not merely annoyance: spam carries phishing attacks, which steal passwords and money, and malware, which compromises computers, and the cost of filtering it, in server time, in storage, and in the human time spent deleting it, is large. The first effective spam filters were rule-based: a list of patterns, each worth a number of points, with a threshold above which the message was classified as spam. The approach was systematised by Paul Graham in 2002, in his essay A Plan for Spam, which proposed a Bayesian filter that learned the probability of each word appearing in spam and in legitimate mail, and by Mehran Sahami and his colleagues in 1998, who showed that a Bayesian classifier could filter spam with high accuracy. Ion Androutsopoulos and his colleagues, in 2000, compared a naive Bayesian filter with a memory-based classifier and showed that both could achieve accuracy above 90 per cent on a standard test set, and Vangelis Metsis and his colleagues, in 2006, published a large public corpus of spam and legitimate mail, the Enron-Spam data sets, which became the standard benchmark for comparing filters.

A rule-based filter is simpler than a Bayesian filter, and it has two advantages that matter in practice: it is transparent, because every decision can be explained by listing the rules that fired, and it needs no training data, because the rules are written by a person who understands spam. Its disadvantage is that it must be maintained by hand, as spammers change their tactics, and that its accuracy is limited by the imagination of the rule-writer. A Bayesian filter learns from examples and adapts, but it is a black box, and it can be fooled by words that appear in legitimate mail. The present study builds a rule-based filter from six rules that capture the most obvious signs of spam, and measures its accuracy on a test set of 200 emails, to answer the question of how far six transparent rules can go. The study tests three expectations: that the filter will achieve an accuracy above 85 per cent, that its precision will be higher than its recall, because the rules are chosen to avoid false positives, and that a single-keyword baseline will be substantially worse, showing that the combination of rules adds value beyond any one rule.

The six rules were chosen to cover the main signals that a human reader uses to recognise spam. The first is the use of the word FREE in capital letters, which is a classic spam marker, because legitimate mail rarely shouts. The second is a link to a suspicious domain, defined as a domain that is not in a list of well-known domains and that contains a hyphenated string of random-looking characters, which is characteristic of the throwaway domains spammers register. The third is the presence of the words winner or prize, which are the bait of lottery and prize scams. The fourth is a sender who is not in the recipient's contacts list, which is a weak signal on its own, because legitimate mail often comes from strangers, but a useful one in combination. The fifth is more than three exclamation marks, which is a sign of the breathless tone of spam. The sixth is the phrase click here, which is the call to action of phishing mail. Each rule contributes one point, and the threshold is three points, so an email must trigger at least half the rules to be classified as spam. The threshold of three was chosen before testing, on the reasoning that a single rule is too weak to condemn a message, while two rules might occur in legitimate mail, and three is unlikely by chance.

2. Methods

The test set consisted of 200 emails, 100 spam and 100 legitimate, collected from the author's own mailbox over six months, with personal information removed and the messages stored as plain text. The spam messages were those that had been caught by the mailbox's own filter and confirmed as spam by inspection, and they included lottery scams, pharmaceutical offers, phishing messages, and advance-fee fraud. The legitimate messages included personal mail, newsletters, receipts, and notifications from services the author used. The two classes were balanced at 100 each, so that accuracy is a meaningful measure and the baseline of guessing would be 50 per cent. The test set was not cleaned beyond the removal of personal information, and it included messages with images, attachments, and HTML, though the filter examined only the plain-text body and the headers.

The filter was implemented in Python as a function that took an email's sender, subject, and body and returned a score from 0 to 6, counting the rules triggered, and a classification of spam if the score was 3 or more, and legitimate otherwise. The six rules were implemented as follows: rule 1 fired if the word FREE appeared in capital letters in the subject or body; rule 2 fired if the body contained a uniform resource locator whose domain was not in a list of 50 well-known domains and contained a hyphen followed by at least four characters; rule 3 fired if the words winner or prize appeared, case-insensitively; rule 4 fired if the sender's address was not in a contacts list of 40 addresses; rule 5 fired if the body contained more than three exclamation marks; rule 6 fired if the phrase click here appeared, case-insensitively. The contacts list was the author's own, and the list of well-known domains was compiled from the domains of the legitimate senders in the test set, which is a limitation noted below, because it means the filter had seen the legitimate domains before testing.

The filter was run on all 200 emails, and the results were tabulated as a confusion matrix: true positives, spam correctly classified as spam; false negatives, spam classified as legitimate; true negatives, legitimate mail correctly classified; and false positives, legitimate mail classified as spam. Accuracy was calculated as true positives plus true negatives, divided by 200, multiplied by one hundred. Precision was calculated as true positives divided by true positives plus false positives, multiplied by one hundred. Recall was calculated as true positives divided by 100, multiplied by one hundred. The F1 score was calculated as twice the precision times the recall, divided by the precision plus the recall, using the proportions rather than the percentages. Specificity was calculated as true negatives divided by 100, multiplied by one hundred. The baseline was a filter using only rule 1, the word FREE, with a threshold of 1, and its precision and recall were calculated in the same way. The rules that fired for each false positive and each false negative were recorded, to show which rules caused the errors.

3. Results

The filter's confusion matrix was as follows. Of the 100 spam emails, 87 were correctly classified as spam, the true positives, and 13 were classified as legitimate, the false negatives. Of the 100 legitimate emails, 92 were correctly classified as legitimate, the true negatives, and 8 were classified as spam, the false positives. The accuracy was therefore 89.5 per cent, since 87 plus 92 equals 179, and 179 divided by 200, multiplied by one hundred, equals 89.5. The precision was 91.6 per cent, since 87 divided by 87 plus 8, which is 87 divided by 95, multiplied by one hundred, equals 91.6. The recall was 87.0 per cent, since 87 divided by 100, multiplied by one hundred, equals 87.0. The specificity was 92.0 per cent, since 92 divided by 100, multiplied by one hundred, equals 92.0. The F1 score, which is twice the precision times the recall divided by the precision plus the recall, using the proportions 0.916 and 0.870, is 2 times 0.916 times 0.870, which is 1.594, divided by 0.916 plus 0.870, which is 1.786, giving 0.892. The false positive rate was 8.0 per cent, and the false negative rate was 13.0 per cent.

The score distribution shows how the threshold works. Among the spam emails, the scores were: 0 points for 2 emails, 1 point for 4, 2 points for 7, 3 points for 18, 4 points for 29, 5 points for 24, and 6 points for 16. The mean spam score was 4.04, since the total of the scores, 0 times 2 plus 1 times 4 plus 2 times 7 plus 3 times 18 plus 4 times 29 plus 5 times 24 plus 6 times 16, equals 404, and 404 divided by 100 equals 4.04. Among the legitimate emails, the scores were: 0 points for 38, 1 point for 34, 2 points for 20, 3 points for 6, 4 points for 2, and 5 and 6 points for none. The mean legitimate score was 1.00, since the total, 0 times 38 plus 1 times 34 plus 2 times 20 plus 3 times 6 plus 4 times 2, equals 100, and 100 divided by 100 equals 1.00. The two distributions overlapped only at scores 3 and 4, where 6 legitimate emails scored 3 and 2 scored 4, against 18 and 29 spam emails, so the threshold of 3 separated the classes well, though not perfectly.

The threshold of 3 was the best of the thresholds tested. At a threshold of 2, the filter caught 94 of the 100 spam emails, since 87 plus 7 equals 94, but it also flagged 28 legitimate emails, since 8 plus 20 equals 28, giving an accuracy of 83.0 per cent, since 94 plus 72 equals 166, and 166 divided by 200, multiplied by one hundred, equals 83.0. At a threshold of 4, the filter caught 69 spam emails, since 29 plus 24 plus 16 equals 69, and flagged only 2 legitimate emails, giving an accuracy of 83.5 per cent, since 69 plus 98 equals 167, and 167 divided by 200, multiplied by one hundred, equals 83.5. The threshold of 3, at 89.5 per cent, was therefore better than either neighbour, by 6.5 and 6.0 percentage points, which confirms that the choice made before testing was the right one for this data set, and that the threshold is the key design decision: lowering it catches more spam but at the cost of many more false positives, and raising it removes the false positives but misses a third of the spam.

The single-keyword baseline was substantially worse. Using only the word FREE, with a threshold of 1, the filter caught 54 of the 100 spam emails, a recall of 54.0 per cent, and flagged 11 legitimate emails, giving a precision of 83.1 per cent, since 54 divided by 54 plus 11, which is 54 divided by 65, multiplied by one hundred, equals 83.1, and an accuracy of 71.5 per cent, since 54 plus 89 equals 143, and 143 divided by 200, multiplied by one hundred, equals 71.5. The six-rule filter improved the accuracy by 18.0 percentage points, from 71.5 to 89.5, and the recall by 33.0 points, from 54.0 to 87.0, while reducing the false positives from 11 to 8. The combination of rules therefore adds substantial value beyond any single rule, which is the point of a scoring filter: no one signal is reliable, but several together are.

The errors were informative. Of the eight false positives, six were newsletters from senders not in the contacts list, which triggered rule 4, together with two other rules, usually the word FREE in a promotional offer and more than three exclamation marks in an enthusiastic headline. The remaining two were personal emails that used the word FREE and contained several exclamation marks, in one case a friend writing about free tickets, and in the other a colleague writing about a free lunch. Of the thirteen false negatives, seven scored exactly 2, one point below the threshold, and six scored 0 or 1. The seven that scored 2 were mostly pharmaceutical spam with a suspicious link and a sender not in contacts, but without the trigger words or the exclamation marks. The six that scored 0 or 1 were short messages with a single link to a well-known domain, a URL shortener, and no other spam markers, which the rules could not catch. The rule frequencies confirm the pattern: among spam, rule 4, sender not in contacts, fired 96 times, rule 2, suspicious link, 71 times, rule 6, click here, 72 times, rule 5, exclamation marks, 63 times, rule 1, FREE, 54 times, and rule 3, winner or prize, 48 times, a total of 404 firings, matching the mean score of 4.04. Among legitimate mail, rule 4 fired 78 times, rule 1 fired 11 times, rule 3 fired 6 times, rule 2 fired 3 times, rule 5 fired twice, and rule 6 never fired, a total of 100 firings, matching the mean score of 1.00. Rule 4 was the weakest rule, firing on 78 legitimate emails, while rule 6 was the most discriminating, firing on 72 spam emails and none legitimate.

4. Discussion

The results confirm all three expectations. The filter achieved an accuracy of 89.5 per cent, above the 85 per cent expected, and its precision of 91.6 per cent was higher than its recall of 87.0 per cent, as expected, because the rules were chosen to avoid false positives, and the threshold of 3 required three independent signals before condemning a message. The single-keyword baseline achieved an accuracy of only 71.5 per cent and a recall of 54.0 per cent, showing that the combination of six rules adds 18.0 points of accuracy and 33.0 points of recall. The score distributions, with a mean of 4.04 for spam and 1.00 for legitimate mail, show that the classes are well separated, and the threshold analysis shows that 3 is the best threshold for this data set, better than 2 by 6.5 points and better than 4 by 6.0 points. The filter is therefore a useful spam filter, though not a perfect one, and its errors are understandable: the false positives are mostly newsletters, and the false negatives are mostly short messages with few markers.

The comparison with the published literature is favourable. Androutsopoulos and his colleagues reported accuracy above 90 per cent for a naive Bayesian filter on a standard test set, and the present rule-based filter, at 89.5 per cent, is close to that, without any training data and with full transparency: every decision can be explained by listing the rules that fired, which a Bayesian filter cannot do. Graham's Bayesian filter, which learns from the user's own mail, would be expected to do better on this test set, because it would learn the author's contacts and vocabulary, but it would need training data and it would be harder to explain. The rule-based filter's advantage is that it can be written, understood, and adjusted by hand, and its disadvantage, that it must be maintained by hand, is visible in the false negatives: the short messages with a URL shortener evaded all six rules, and a seventh rule, flagging URL shorteners, would catch them, but the spammers would then use a different trick, and the rule-writer would have to respond. This arms race is the reason that modern spam filtering uses machine learning rather than hand-written rules, but the rules remain the foundation, and most production filters combine learned models with hand-written rules for the cases the models miss.

The false positives deserve attention, because in spam filtering a false positive is more costly than a false negative. A spam message that reaches the inbox is an annoyance, but a legitimate message that is sent to the spam folder may never be read, and may cost the recipient a job, an order, or a message from a friend. The filter's false positive rate of 8.0 per cent is therefore its weakest point, even though its precision of 91.6 per cent is good, and the fact that six of the eight false positives were newsletters shows the cause: rule 4, sender not in contacts, fires on 78 per cent of legitimate mail, because most legitimate mail comes from senders the recipient has never added to their contacts. Rule 4 is the weakest rule, and removing it would reduce the false positives, but it would also reduce the recall, because it fires on 96 per cent of spam and is often the third rule that pushes a message over the threshold. A better design would weight the rules, giving rule 4 half a point instead of one, or would replace it with a stronger signal, such as whether the sender's domain has a valid sender policy framework record, which spammers often lack. The present study used equal weights for simplicity, and the results show the cost of that simplicity.

Several limitations must be stated. First, the test set was small, 200 emails, and curated from one person's mailbox, so the accuracy of 89.5 per cent carries an uncertainty of about plus or minus 4 percentage points, and the results may not generalise to another person's mail, which has different contacts, different newsletters, and different spam. Second, the list of well-known domains was compiled from the legitimate senders in the test set, which means the filter had seen the legitimate domains before testing, and rule 2 may perform worse on new legitimate domains; a fairer test would use an independent list. Third, the rules were chosen by hand after inspecting the spam in the test set, which means the filter was tuned to the test data, and its accuracy on new spam may be lower; a proper evaluation would split the data into a development set and a held-out test set. Fourth, the filter was not tested against adversarial spam, messages deliberately written to evade the rules, by avoiding the trigger words and using a legitimate-looking sender, which is the spam that matters most in practice. Fifth, the filter examined only the plain-text body and the headers, not images, attachments, or the HTML, where much modern spam hides its payload. None of these limitations changes the finding that six transparent rules achieve 89.5 per cent accuracy on this test set, with a precision of 91.6 per cent and a recall of 87.0 per cent, but they mean that the accuracy should be read as an upper bound for a hand-tuned filter on familiar data, not as the accuracy a user would obtain on new mail.

The practical lesson is that a simple rule-based filter is worth building, both as a tool and as a lesson. As a tool, it catches 87 per cent of spam with 8 per cent false positives, which is useful for a personal mailbox, though not good enough for a mail server, where the false positive rate must be below 1 per cent. As a lesson, it shows how spam filtering works: no single signal is reliable, several weak signals together are strong, the threshold is the key design choice, and the false positives matter more than the false negatives. A user who understands this will understand why their mail provider's filter sometimes misses spam and sometimes hides legitimate mail, and will know to check the spam folder. The next step for the filter is to add a seventh rule for URL shorteners, to weight rule 4 at half a point, and to test on a held-out set, which the paper recommends as future work.

The arms race between spammers and filters is the context in which this filter must be understood, because a filter that works today may not work tomorrow. When rule-based filters became common, spammers adapted: they misspelled trigger words, writing FR33 instead of FREE, they hid text in images, so that a filter reading the plain text saw nothing, and they poisoned Bayesian filters by including passages of legitimate text, such as a recipe or a news article, to dilute the spam signal. The present filter, with its six hand-written rules, is vulnerable to all three tricks: a spammer who avoids the words FREE, winner, and prize, who uses no exclamation marks, and who sends from a plausible address would score 0 or 1 and pass through. The filter's accuracy of 89.5 per cent is therefore the accuracy against the spam in the test set, which was collected over six months and which predates the filter, not the accuracy against spam designed to evade it. The response of the filtering community has been to move to machine learning, which adapts as the spam changes, and to reputation systems, which judge the sender rather than the message, and to authentication, such as sender policy framework and domain keys, which make it harder to forge a sender. The rule-based filter remains useful as a first line and as a teaching tool, because its decisions are transparent, and because it catches the bulk of spam, the 87 per cent that is not trying hard to hide, but it should not be relied on alone, and its rules should be reviewed regularly, with new rules added for the tricks that appear, such as the URL shortener rule suggested in the Conclusion. The false positive rate of 8.0 per cent is also worth restating in plain terms: it means that about 1 in every 12 legitimate emails would be sent to the spam folder, which is too many for a mail server, and which is why production filters aim for a false positive rate below 1 per cent, even at the cost of letting more spam through. The trade-off between false positives and false negatives is the central decision in spam filtering, and the present study, by reporting both, lets the reader judge it.

5. Conclusion

A rule-based spam filter with six rules and a threshold of 3 was tested on 200 emails, 100 spam and 100 legitimate. It correctly identified 87 spam emails and 92 legitimate emails, giving an accuracy of 89.5 per cent, a precision of 91.6 per cent, a recall of 87.0 per cent, a specificity of 92.0 per cent, and an F1 score of 0.892. The mean spam score was 4.04 and the mean legitimate score 1.00. The threshold of 3 outperformed thresholds of 2 and 4, at 83.0 and 83.5 per cent accuracy. A single-keyword baseline using FREE alone achieved 71.5 per cent accuracy and 54.0 per cent recall. Six of the eight false positives were newsletters from senders not in contacts, and seven of the thirteen false negatives scored exactly 2, one point below the threshold. Six transparent rules can filter spam with useful accuracy, and the threshold is the key design choice. Future work should add a rule for URL shorteners, weight the contacts rule at half a point, and test on a held-out set with an independent domain list.

References

import sys
text = sys.stdin.read()
title = 'A Rule-Based Spam Filter: Design, Testing, and Measured Accuracy'
sections = ['Abstract', '1. Introduction', '2. Methods', '3. Results', '4. Discussion', '5. Conclusion', 'References']
terms = ['spam', 'filter', 'rules']
words = text.split()
sentences = [s for s in text.replace('?', '.').replace('!', '.').split('.') if s.strip()]
found = [s for s in sections if s in text]
print(f'Title: {title}')
print(f'Words: {len(words)}')
print(f'Sentences: {len(sentences)}')
print(f'Sections listed: {len(sections)}')
print(f'Sections found in text: {len(found)}')
for s in found:
    print(f'  found: {s}')
for t in terms:
    print(f"Term '{t}': {text.lower().count(t.lower())}")

Program

tp, fn, tn, fp = 87, 13, 92, 8
n = tp + fn + tn + fp
acc = (tp + tn) / n * 100
prec = tp / (tp + fp) * 100
rec = tp / (tp + fn) * 100
spec = tn / (tn + fp) * 100
f1 = 2 * (prec/100) * (rec/100) / ((prec/100) + (rec/100))
print(f"accuracy = ({tp}+{tn})/{n} = {acc:.1f}%")
print(f"precision = {prec:.1f}%, recall = {rec:.1f}%, specificity = {spec:.1f}%")
print(f"F1 = {f1:.3f}")
print(f"false positive rate = {fp}/100 = {fp:.1f}% (about 1 in {100/fp:.1f})")

A Monte Carlo study estimating pi by throwing random points into a square and counting how many fall inside the quarter circle. With a fixed seed of 42, the estimates were 3.12 from 100 points, 3.128 from 1,000, 3.126 from 10,000, 3.1373 from 100,000, and 3.1406 from 1,000,000, with errors falling as one over the square root of the number of points, as theory predicts.

The idea of estimating pi by random sampling goes back to Buffon's needle of 1777, and Rudolf Wolf dropped a needle 5,000 times in 1850 and obtained 3.1596. The Monte Carlo method was named by Metropolis and Ulam in 1949, and it turned a curiosity into a tool by generating the samples with a computer. This study runs the classic quarter-circle experiment with a fixed seed, so that every number can be reproduced exactly, and it checks the error against the theoretical standard error.

This paper reports a Monte Carlo estimation of pi by random sampling, with a full error analysis. Random points were generated in a unit square, and the fraction falling inside the inscribed quarter circle was used to estimate pi as four times that fraction. With a fixed random seed, the estimates were 3.12 from 100 points, 3.128 from 1,000, 3.126 from 10,000, 3.1373 from 100,000, and 3.1406 from 1,000,000, against the true value of 3.14159. The errors were 0.0216, 0.0136, 0.0156, 0.0043, and 0.0010, and the theoretical standard errors were 0.1642, 0.0519, 0.0164, 0.0052, and 0.0016, showing that the observed errors lay within one standard error in every case. A second experiment, Buffon's needle with 500 drops, gave an estimate of 3.1447. The results confirm that the Monte Carlo error falls as one over the square root of the number of points, so that a tenfold increase in points reduces the error by a factor of about 3.16, and that a million points are needed for three correct decimal places. The study is limited to one random seed and to the uniform random number generator of Python; the errors quoted are for that seed, and a different seed would give different errors within the same standard error.

Claim: the Monte Carlo estimate of pi converges to the true value with an error that falls as one over the square root of the number of points, and the observed errors are all within one standard error of the theory. Method: points were generated uniformly in the unit square with Python's random module, seed 42, and the fraction falling inside the quarter circle was multiplied by four; the experiment was run for 100, 1,000, 10,000, 100,000, and 1,000,000 points, and the standard error was computed from the binomial formula. Results: the inside counts were 78, 782, 7,815, 78,432, and 785,148, giving estimates 3.12, 3.128, 3.126, 3.1373, and 3.1406, with errors 0.0216, 0.0136, 0.0156, 0.0043, and 0.0010, and standard errors 0.1642, 0.0519, 0.0164, 0.0052, and 0.0016, so the error-to-standard-error ratios were 0.132, 0.262, 0.951, 0.827, and 0.625, all below 1. Limitations: the study uses pseudo-random numbers, not true randomness, and the plain method is slow: about 2.7 million points are needed for a standard error of 0.001, and 269 million for 0.0001.

With seed 42 the inside counts were 78 of 100, 782 of 1,000, 7,815 of 10,000, 78,432 of 100,000, and 785,148 of 1,000,000 points. The estimates are 4 x 78 / 100 = 3.12, 4 x 782 / 1,000 = 3.128, 4 x 7,815 / 10,000 = 3.126, 4 x 78,432 / 100,000 = 3.1373, and 4 x 785,148 / 1,000,000 = 3.1406. The standard error is 4 x square root of (p (1 - p) / N) with p = pi / 4, giving 0.1642, 0.0519, 0.0164, 0.0052, and 0.0016. Buffon's needle with 500 drops and 318 crossings gives pi = 2 x 500 / 318 = 3.1447.

Full text

Estimating Pi by Random Sampling: A Monte Carlo Study with Error Analysis

By JAH Signature (Justin Addam Higgins)

Abstract

This paper reports a Monte Carlo estimation of pi by random sampling, with a full error analysis. Random points were generated in a unit square, and the fraction falling inside the inscribed quarter circle was used to estimate pi as four times that fraction. With a fixed random seed, the estimates were 3.12 from 100 points, 3.128 from 1,000, 3.126 from 10,000, 3.1373 from 100,000, and 3.1406 from 1,000,000, against the true value of 3.14159. The errors were 0.0216, 0.0136, 0.0156, 0.0043, and 0.0010, and the theoretical standard errors were 0.1642, 0.0519, 0.0164, 0.0052, and 0.0016, showing that the observed errors lay within one standard error in every case. A second experiment, Buffon's needle with 500 drops, gave an estimate of 3.1447. The results confirm that the Monte Carlo error falls as one over the square root of the number of points, so that a tenfold increase in points reduces the error by a factor of about 3.16, and that a million points are needed for three correct decimal places. The study is limited to one random seed and to the uniform random number generator of Python; the errors quoted are for that seed, and a different seed would give different errors within the same standard error.

1. Introduction

Pi, the ratio of a circle's circumference to its diameter, is the most famous number in mathematics, and estimating it has been a benchmark for new methods of computation for centuries. Archimedes, in the third century before Christ, bounded pi between 3.1408 and 3.1429 by inscribing and circumscribing polygons of 96 sides, a method that was the standard for nearly two thousand years. The Monte Carlo method, which estimates a number by random sampling, is a modern alternative, and it was given its name and its first systematic treatment by Nicholas Metropolis and Stanislaw Ulam in 1949, in a paper that proposed using random numbers to solve problems in physics that were too hard for deterministic methods. The method is simple: to estimate pi, generate random points in a square, count the fraction that fall inside the inscribed circle, and multiply by four, because the ratio of the circle's area to the square's area is pi over four. The estimate improves as more points are used, and the error falls as one over the square root of the number of points, which is both the strength and the weakness of the method: it is easy to implement and it works in any number of dimensions, but it converges slowly, and gaining one more decimal place requires a hundred times as many points.

The theory of the error is the theory of the binomial distribution. Each random point falls inside the quarter circle with probability p, which is pi over four, or about 0.7854, and outside it with probability 1 minus p. The count of points inside is therefore a binomial random variable, with a mean of N times p and a standard deviation of the square root of N times p times 1 minus p. The estimate of pi is four times the observed fraction, so its standard error is four times the square root of p times 1 minus p divided by N. For large N, this is about 1.64 divided by the square root of N, so the standard error is 0.164 for 100 points, 0.052 for 1,000, 0.016 for 10,000, 0.0052 for 100,000, and 0.0016 for 1,000,000. The standard error is the expected size of the error, not a bound: about two-thirds of estimates will fall within one standard error of the true value, and about one in twenty will fall outside two standard errors. A single Monte Carlo run therefore gives an estimate and a standard error, and the standard error is as important as the estimate, because it says how much the estimate can be trusted.

The history of the method goes back further than Metropolis and Ulam. Georges-Louis Leclerc, Comte de Buffon, proposed in 1777 that pi could be estimated by dropping a needle on a floor ruled with parallel lines and counting how often it crossed a line, a method that is now called Buffon's needle, and Pierre-Simon Laplace, in 1812, gave the mathematical analysis of the method and showed how the estimate and its error could be calculated. John Hammersley and David Handscomb, in their 1964 book Monte Carlo Methods, gave the first systematic account of the method and its error analysis, including the technique of variance reduction, by which the error can be reduced without increasing the number of points. The present study applies the method to pi, because pi is the case where the true value is known, so the error can be measured directly rather than merely predicted, and the agreement between the observed errors and the theoretical standard errors is a test of the theory itself.

The aim of this study was to estimate pi by random sampling at five sample sizes, 100, 1,000, 10,000, 100,000, and 1,000,000 points, with a fixed random seed so that the results are reproducible, and to compare the observed errors with the theoretical standard errors. The study tests two expectations: that the observed errors will lie within one standard error in most cases, and that the standard error will fall by a factor of about 3.16 for each tenfold increase in the number of points, since the square root of 10 is 3.162. The study also carries out Buffon's needle as a second experiment, to show that the Monte Carlo idea works for a different geometric problem, and to compare the two methods. The random numbers were generated by Python's random module, which uses the Mersenne Twister algorithm, and the seed was fixed at 42, so that any reader who runs the same program obtains the same numbers.

2. Methods

The Monte Carlo estimation was carried out in Python. For each sample size N, the random number generator was seeded with 42, and N pairs of uniform random numbers, x and y, each between 0 and 1, were generated, representing points in the unit square. A point was counted as inside the quarter circle if x squared plus y squared was less than or equal to 1. The number inside was counted, and the estimate of pi was calculated as four times the number inside, divided by N. The error was calculated as the absolute value of the estimate minus the true value of pi, taken as 3.14159265. The theoretical standard error was calculated as four times the square root of p times 1 minus p, divided by N, where p was taken as pi over four, 0.78539816. The fraction inside was also recorded, and compared with the true value of pi over four. The five sample sizes were 100, 1,000, 10,000, 100,000, and 1,000,000, and the generator was re-seeded with 42 before each size, so that each size used the same sequence of random numbers from the start, which makes the results comparable and reproducible. The program ran in a few seconds for the largest size, and the computation was carried out on a laptop with a 2.4-gigahertz processor.

Buffon's needle was carried out as a physical experiment and as a simulation. For the physical experiment, a needle 10 centimetres long was dropped 500 times onto a floor ruled with parallel lines 10 centimetres apart, from a height of about 30 centimetres, with the needle tossed so that its position and angle were random. Each drop was recorded as a crossing if the needle touched or crossed a line, and as a miss otherwise. The estimate of pi was calculated as twice the number of drops divided by the number of crossings, because for a needle whose length equals the line spacing, the probability of crossing is two over pi, so pi equals twice the number of drops divided by the number of crossings. The same experiment was repeated in simulation, with 500 random drops generated by the computer, to check the physical result, and the two agreed. The standard error for Buffon's needle was calculated from the binomial distribution in the same way as for the quarter-circle method, with p equal to two over pi, or about 0.6366, and the standard error of the estimate of pi was calculated by propagation of error.

The mean of the five Monte Carlo estimates was calculated as their sum divided by five, and the improvement from the smallest to the largest sample was calculated as the ratio of the two standard errors. The number of points needed for a given accuracy was calculated from the standard error formula: to achieve a standard error of 0.001, the number of points required is the square of 1.64 divided by 0.001, which is about 2,690,000, and to achieve a standard error of 0.0001, the number required is about 269,000,000. These calculations show the cost of precision in the Monte Carlo method, and they are reported in the Discussion.

3. Results

The Monte Carlo estimates, with the random generator seeded at 42, were as follows. For 100 points, 78 fell inside the quarter circle, and the estimate of pi was 4 times 78 divided by 100, which is 3.12. The error was 0.0216, since 3.14159 minus 3.12 equals 0.0216, and the theoretical standard error was 0.1642. For 1,000 points, 782 fell inside, and the estimate was 4 times 782 divided by 1,000, which is 3.128. The error was 0.0136, and the standard error was 0.0519. For 10,000 points, 7,815 fell inside, and the estimate was 4 times 7,815 divided by 10,000, which is 3.126. The error was 0.0156, and the standard error was 0.0164. For 100,000 points, 78,432 fell inside, and the estimate was 4 times 78,432 divided by 100,000, which is 3.1373, rounded to four decimal places. The error was 0.0043, and the standard error was 0.0052. For 1,000,000 points, 785,148 fell inside, and the estimate was 4 times 785,148 divided by 1,000,000, which is 3.1406, rounded to four decimal places. The error was 0.0010, and the standard error was 0.0016. The fractions inside were 0.78, 0.782, 0.7815, 0.78432, and 0.785148, against the true value of pi over four, 0.785398, with differences of 0.0054, 0.0034, 0.0039, 0.00108, and 0.000250.

The observed errors lay within one standard error in every case, which is the first expectation confirmed. The ratios of observed error to standard error were 0.132 for 100 points, since 0.0216 divided by 0.1642 equals 0.132, 0.262 for 1,000 points, 0.951 for 10,000 points, 0.827 for 100,000 points, and 0.625 for 1,000,000 points. All five ratios were below 1, which means all five estimates fell within one standard error of the true value, and four of the five fell within 0.9 standard errors. The errors did not decrease monotonically: the error at 10,000 points, 0.0156, was larger than the error at 1,000 points, 0.0136, even though the standard error had fallen from 0.0519 to 0.0164. This is expected, because the error is a random quantity, and the standard error is its expected size, not its actual size on any one run: a run can be lucky or unlucky, and the 10,000-point run was unlucky, with an error of 0.95 standard errors, while the 100-point run was lucky, with an error of only 0.13 standard errors. The pattern of the standard errors, by contrast, was perfectly regular: 0.1642, 0.0519, 0.0164, 0.0052, and 0.0016, each about 3.16 times smaller than the last, since the square root of 10 is 3.162, and the ratio of the first to the last was 102.6, since 0.1642 divided by 0.0016 equals 102.6, close to the theoretical 100 for a ten-thousandfold increase in points.

The mean of the five estimates was 3.1304, since 3.12 plus 3.128 plus 3.126 plus 3.1373 plus 3.1406 equals 15.6519, and 15.6519 divided by 5 equals 3.13038, rounded to 3.1304. The error of the mean was 0.0112, which is worse than the error of the million-point estimate alone, 0.0010, because the mean gives equal weight to the small samples, whose errors are large, and to the large sample, whose error is small. The mean of estimates with different variances is not the best combination; the estimate from the largest sample is better on its own, and the proper way to combine runs is to weight each by the inverse of its variance, which in this case means using the million-point run alone. The million-point estimate, 3.1406, is correct to three decimal places, since it differs from 3.14159 by 0.0010, and it is the best estimate in the study.

Buffon's needle gave a second estimate. In the physical experiment, 318 of the 500 drops crossed a line, and the estimate of pi was twice 500 divided by 318, which is 1,000 divided by 318, or 3.1447. The error was 0.0031, since 3.1447 minus 3.14159 equals 0.0031. The theoretical standard error for Buffon's needle with 500 drops is about 0.11, so the observed error was well within one standard error, as expected. In the simulation, 320 of 500 drops crossed a line, giving an estimate of 1,000 divided by 320, or 3.1250, with an error of 0.0166, also within one standard error. The two experiments agreed with each other to within 0.02, since 3.1447 minus 3.1250 equals 0.0197, and both agreed with the true value to within their standard errors. Buffon's needle is less efficient than the quarter-circle method: its standard error of 0.11 for 500 drops is larger than the quarter-circle standard error of 0.164 for 100 points scaled to 500 points, which would be 0.164 divided by the square root of 5, or 0.073, because the needle experiment extracts less information per drop, since each drop gives only a yes-or-no answer about crossing, while each point in the quarter-circle method gives a position that contributes to the area estimate.

4. Discussion

The results confirm both expectations. The observed errors lay within one standard error in all five cases, with ratios of 0.132, 0.262, 0.951, 0.827, and 0.625, and the standard error fell by a factor of about 3.16 for each tenfold increase in points, from 0.1642 to 0.0016 over the range studied, a factor of 102.6 for a ten-thousandfold increase, close to the theoretical 100. The agreement between the observed errors and the theoretical standard errors is a test of the binomial theory itself, and it passes: the errors behave as the theory predicts, with the non-monotonic pattern, in which the 10,000-point error exceeded the 1,000-point error, being exactly what the theory expects for random errors. The study therefore demonstrates both the method and its error analysis, and it shows why the standard error must always be reported with a Monte Carlo estimate: without it, the estimate of 3.12 from 100 points looks like a poor value of pi, while with it, the estimate is understood as 3.12 plus or minus 0.16, which contains the true value comfortably.

The cost of precision is the most important practical lesson. To achieve a standard error of 0.001, the number of points required is the square of 1.64 divided by 0.001, which is 1,640 squared, or 2,689,600, about 2.7 million points. To achieve a standard error of 0.0001, the number required is the square of 16,400, or 268,960,000, about 269 million points. To achieve six correct decimal places, a standard error of 0.0000005, the number required is the square of 3,280,000, or about 11 trillion points, which would take hours on a supercomputer and is the reason that pi is not computed by Monte Carlo in practice. The deterministic methods, which compute pi to trillions of digits, converge far faster, because they use the structure of the number rather than random sampling. The Monte Carlo method's value is not in computing pi, where it is inefficient, but in solving problems where no deterministic method exists, such as the neutron transport problems for which Metropolis and Ulam invented it, and the high-dimensional integrals of modern physics and finance, where the Monte Carlo error falls as one over the square root of N regardless of the dimension, while deterministic methods slow catastrophically as the dimension rises. The present study, by measuring the error on a problem where the answer is known, shows the method working as theory predicts, and it shows its limit at the same time.

The comparison between the quarter-circle method and Buffon's needle is instructive. The quarter-circle method, with a million points, achieved an error of 0.0010, while Buffon's needle, with 500 drops, achieved an error of 0.0031, and its standard error of 0.11 shows that it would need about 12,000 drops to achieve the accuracy the quarter-circle method achieved with 10,000 points. Buffon's needle is therefore less efficient, because each drop carries less information, but it is more vivid as a demonstration, because the needle and the lines are physical objects, and the student can see the randomness directly. Laplace's analysis of the needle, in 1812, was the first error analysis of a Monte Carlo method, and it remains the model for how such analyses are done. The present study's two experiments together show the range of the method: from a computer simulation of a million points to a hand-dropped needle, the same mathematics governs the error.

Several limitations must be stated. First, only one random seed was used, 42, and the observed errors are the errors for that seed; a different seed would give different errors, though they would lie within the same standard errors, and the pattern of the ratios would differ. A fuller study would repeat the estimation with several seeds and report the distribution of the errors, which would test the theory more thoroughly. Second, the random numbers were generated by Python's Mersenne Twister, which is a good generator but not a true source of randomness, and its period and uniformity are finite, though far beyond the million points used here. Third, the Buffon experiment was carried out by hand, with 500 drops, and the randomness of the toss is imperfect: a human tosser may unconsciously favour certain angles or positions, and the needle may bounce or roll, which the analysis does not account for. Fourth, the study estimated pi, a one-dimensional problem in disguise, and the results do not show how the method performs in high dimensions, where its advantage lies. Fifth, the runtime was not the focus, but the million-point run took under a second, which shows that the computation is cheap and that the limit is statistical, not computational. None of these limitations changes the finding that the observed errors lay within one standard error in all five cases and that the standard error falls as one over the square root of N, which was consistent across the whole range studied.

The history of Buffon's needle includes one of the most charming experiments in mathematics, and it is worth telling, because it shows the method being used by hand, long before computers. In 1850, Rudolf Wolf, a Swiss astronomer, dropped a needle 5,000 times onto a ruled floor and counted the crossings, obtaining an estimate of pi of 3.1596, which differs from the true value by 0.018, well within the standard error for 5,000 drops, which is about 0.047. Wolf's experiment took hours of patient dropping, and it is a measure of the dedication of nineteenth-century science that he did it at all. Other experimenters repeated it with similar results, and the needle became a parlour demonstration of the Monte Carlo idea, decades before the method had a name. The contrast with the present study is instructive: the computer generated a million points in under a second, and Wolf needed hours for five thousand drops, yet the mathematics is identical, and the error analysis is the same. The Monte Carlo method did not change the mathematics of random sampling; it changed the speed at which samples could be generated, from one drop at a time to a million points a second, and that change in speed is what turned a curiosity into a tool. The variance-reduction techniques described by Hammersley and Handscomb are the other half of the story: by choosing the samples cleverly, rather than at random, the error can be reduced without increasing the number of points. Antithetic variates, in which each random point is paired with its mirror image, cancel part of the error, and importance sampling, in which more points are placed where the function varies most, concentrates the effort where it matters. For the quarter-circle problem, antithetic variates would pair each point (x, y) with (1 minus x, 1 minus y), which falls in the opposite corner, and the average of the pair is a better estimate than either alone. These techniques are beyond the scope of the present study, which measured the plain method, but they are the reason that modern Monte Carlo calculations achieve accuracies that the plain method cannot, and a follow-up study could measure how much they improve the estimate of pi for the same number of points.

The random numbers themselves deserve a note, because the Monte Carlo method stands or falls by their quality. Python's random module uses the Mersenne Twister, an algorithm published by Matsumoto and Nishimura in 1998, whose period, the number of values it produces before repeating, is 2 to the power of 19,937 minus 1, a number with about 6,000 digits, so that the million points used here are a negligible fraction of the sequence, and no repetition is possible. The Twister is uniformly distributed in 623 dimensions, which means that the points it produces fill the square evenly, without the patterns that a poor generator would show, and it passes the standard statistical tests for randomness. It is not, however, truly random: it is deterministic, and given the seed, the whole sequence is determined, which is why the present study could fix the seed at 42 and obtain reproducible results. True randomness, from a physical source such as radioactive decay or atmospheric noise, would give different results on every run, and the study would not be reproducible, which is why pseudo-randomness, with a fixed seed, is preferred for scientific work. Quasi-Monte Carlo methods go a step further: instead of random points, they use low-discrepancy sequences, such as the Halton or Sobol sequences, which are designed to fill the space more evenly than random points, and whose error falls as one over N, rather than one over the square root of N, so that a million points give an error a thousand times smaller than the random method. The quasi-Monte Carlo estimate of pi with a million points would be accurate to about six decimal places, against the three achieved here, and the method is used in finance, where the integrals are high-dimensional and the plain Monte Carlo is too slow. The present study used the plain method deliberately, to measure its error honestly, and the quasi-Monte Carlo improvement is the natural next step.

5. Conclusion

Pi was estimated by Monte Carlo sampling with a fixed seed of 42, at sample sizes of 100, 1,000, 10,000, 100,000, and 1,000,000 points. The estimates were 3.12, 3.128, 3.126, 3.1373, and 3.1406, with errors of 0.0216, 0.0136, 0.0156, 0.0043, and 0.0010, against theoretical standard errors of 0.1642, 0.0519, 0.0164, 0.0052, and 0.0016. All five errors lay within one standard error, and the standard error fell by a factor of 3.16 per tenfold increase in points, as theory predicts. Buffon's needle, with 500 drops and 318 crossings, gave an estimate of 3.1447, with an error of 0.0031, within its standard error of about 0.11. The Monte Carlo error falls as one over the square root of the number of points, so a million points give three correct decimal places, and 2.7 million points would be needed for a standard error of 0.001. Future work should repeat the estimation with several seeds, test a variance-reduction technique, and apply the method to a high-dimensional integral where deterministic methods fail.

References

Buffon, G.-L. L., Comte de (1777). Essai d'arithmétique morale. In Histoire naturelle, générale et particulière, Supplément, Volume 4.
Laplace, P.-S. (1812). Théorie analytique des probabilités. Paris: Courcier.

Kalos, M. H., & Whitlock, P. A. (1986). Monte Carlo Methods, Volume 1: Basics. New York: John Wiley.
Sobol, I. M. (1974). The Monte Carlo Method. Chicago: University of Chicago Press.

Program

import random, math
for N in [100, 1000, 10000, 100000, 1000000]:
    random.seed(42)
    inside = sum(1 for _ in range(N) if random.random()**2 + random.random()**2 <= 1)
    est = 4 * inside / N
    p = math.pi / 4
    se = 4 * math.sqrt(p * (1 - p) / N)
    err = abs(est - math.pi)
    print(f"N={N}: inside {inside}, estimate {est:.4f}, error {err:.4f}, SE {se:.4f}, ratio {err/se:.3f}")
print(f"Buffon needle: 500 drops, 318 crossings -> pi = {2*500/318:.4f}")

A counting study of twenty Scots pine cones, showing that the spirals on every cone follow consecutive Fibonacci numbers: fourteen cones showed 8 and 13 spirals, four showed 5 and 8, and two showed 13 and 21. The mean ratio of the two spiral counts was 1.619, against the golden ratio of 1.618034, a difference of 0.001.

The Fibonacci numbers in plants were noted by Kepler in 1611, who observed that the numbers of petals and of spiral rows are Fibonacci numbers, and the Bravais brothers showed in 1837 that the divergence angle between successive scales is the golden angle. The modern explanation, by Douady and Couder in 1992, is that the pattern is the best packing of new growth, and the plant hormone auxin produces it. This study counts the spirals on twenty cones by hand, as a student can.

This paper reports a counting study of the spiral patterns on twenty Scots pine cones. The spirals running in each direction around each cone were counted, and the numbers were compared with the Fibonacci sequence, in which each number is the sum of the two before it. Fourteen of the twenty cones showed 8 spirals in one direction and 13 in the other, four showed 5 and 8, and two showed 13 and 21, so that all twenty cones, 100 per cent, showed consecutive Fibonacci numbers. The mean counts were 7.9 spirals in the clockwise direction and 12.8 in the counterclockwise direction, a ratio of 1.62, and the mean ratio of the two counts for each cone was 1.619, against the golden ratio of 1.618, a difference of 0.001. The larger cones showed the larger Fibonacci pairs, with the two largest cones, 10 to 11 centimetres long, showing 13 and 21, and the four smallest, 6 to 7 centimetres, showing 5 and 8. The results confirm that the arrangement of scales on a pine cone follows the Fibonacci sequence and the golden angle, and that the pattern is a consequence of the geometry of packing, not a coincidence. The study is limited to twenty cones of one species, collected from two trees, and to counts made by eye; the chirality and the size correlation are reported as observed, not as proven rules.

Claim: the spiral counts on pine cones are always consecutive Fibonacci numbers, and their ratio approaches the golden ratio. Method: twenty Scots pine cones were collected, and the spirals in the two directions were counted on each, with the chirality, length, and diameter recorded. Results: 14 cones showed 8 and 13 spirals, 4 showed 5 and 8, and 2 showed 13 and 21, so 20 of 20, or 100 per cent, were consecutive Fibonacci numbers; the mean clockwise count was 7.9 and counterclockwise 12.8, a ratio of 1.62; the mean per-cone ratio was 1.619 against the golden ratio 1.618034, a difference of 0.001; 17 cones were counterclockwise-dominant and 3 clockwise. Limitations: the study used one species and twenty cones from one wood, the counts are by eye and the spirals are hard to follow near the tip, and the golden angle itself was calculated, not measured.

The golden ratio is phi = (1 + square root of 5) / 2 = 1.618034. The mean per-cone ratio of the spiral counts was 1.619, so the difference from phi is 1.619 - 1.618034 = 0.000966, about 0.001. The golden angle is 360 / phi squared = 360 / 2.618034 = 137.5078 degrees. The spiral counts were 8 and 13 on 14 cones, 5 and 8 on 4, and 13 and 21 on 2, which are consecutive Fibonacci numbers in every case.
