Full text

Does Background Music Affect Short-Term Memory Scores? A Controlled Experiment

By JAH Signature (Justin Addam Higgins)

Abstract

This paper reports a controlled experiment on the effect of background music on short-term memory in school pupils. Forty pupils aged 12 to 14 were tested twice, once in silence and once with background music, in a rotated order, on a word-list recall test of twenty words. The mean score in silence was 13.2 words out of 20, or 66.0 per cent, and with music it was 11.4 words, or 57.0 per cent, a difference of 1.8 words or 9.0 percentage points. Twenty-eight of the forty pupils scored lower with music, seven scored higher, and five scored the same. In a subgroup comparison, the twenty pupils who heard music with lyrics scored a mean of 10.6, while the twenty who heard instrumental music scored 12.2, a difference of 1.6 words. The results confirm that background music impairs short-term memory, and that music with lyrics impairs it more than instrumental music, because the words of the song compete with the words being remembered. The study is limited to one age group, to one type of memory test, and to a single session per condition; the music was played at a fixed volume, and the pupils' musical preferences were not measured.

1. Introduction

Short-term memory is the system that holds information for seconds to minutes while it is being used, and it is the system on which school learning depends: a pupil who cannot hold a list of words, a set of instructions, or a line of working in mind cannot learn from it. The capacity of short-term memory is limited, famously to about seven items, plus or minus two, and it is vulnerable to interference, because new information displaces old. Alan Baddeley, in 1966, showed that short-term memory is particularly vulnerable to acoustic interference: lists of words that sound alike are harder to remember than lists that sound different, which showed that short-term memory codes information by sound, in what Baddeley later called the phonological loop. Pierre Salame and Alan Baddeley, in 1982, showed that irrelevant speech impairs the recall of visually presented words, even when the speech is in a language the listener does not understand, which demonstrated that it is the sound of speech, not its meaning, that interferes. The effect is called the irrelevant sound effect, and it is one of the most robust findings in the psychology of memory.

Background music is a common form of irrelevant sound, and pupils often listen to it while they study, in the belief that it helps them concentrate. The evidence suggests otherwise. Nick Perham and Joanne Vizard, in 2011, tested the effect of music with lyrics, music without lyrics, and silence on serial recall, and found that music with lyrics impaired recall, while instrumental music did not, which they attributed to the lyrics competing with the words being remembered in the phonological loop. William Thompson and his colleagues, in 2001, showed that the effect depends on the music: fast, loud music impairs performance, while slow, quiet music may not, and familiar music may be less disruptive than unfamiliar music, because it demands less attention. The popular claim that listening to Mozart makes people smarter, the Mozart effect reported by Frances Rauscher and her colleagues in 1993, was shown on re-examination to be a brief improvement in spatial reasoning after listening to music, not a lasting increase in intelligence, and it does not contradict the finding that music impairs memory while it is playing. The question addressed in this study is simple and practical: does background music, of the kind a pupil might play while revising, impair short-term memory, and does it matter whether the music has lyrics?

The aim of this study was to answer that question with a controlled experiment: forty pupils, each tested in silence and with music, on the same kind of word-list recall test, with the order rotated so that half heard music first and half heard silence first, and with the music divided into lyrical and instrumental subgroups. The study tests three expectations drawn from the literature: that the mean score will be lower with music than in silence; that more pupils will score lower with music than higher; and that music with lyrics will impair recall more than instrumental music. The study measures the number of words recalled out of twenty, which is a simple and reliable measure of short-term memory, and it records the individual differences, because the effect of music varies between people, and some pupils may genuinely work better with music, even if most do not.

2. Methods

Forty pupils from a single school, twenty boys and twenty girls, aged 12 to 14 years, with a mean age of 13.1 years, took part, with the permission of their parents and the school. No pupil had a diagnosed hearing problem or a learning difficulty affecting memory, and no pupil was taking medication that affects attention. Each pupil was tested twice, on two separate days, one week apart, once in silence and once with background music, and the order was rotated: twenty pupils were tested in silence first and with music second, and twenty in the opposite order, so that any effect of practice or of the day would affect both conditions equally. The test was a word-list recall test: the experimenter read aloud a list of twenty common nouns, one every two seconds, and the pupil then had one minute to write down as many as they could remember, in any order. Two different lists were used, matched for word length and frequency, one for the first session and one for the second, so that no pupil heard the same list twice, and the lists were counterbalanced across the conditions, so that half the pupils heard list A in silence and list B with music, and half the reverse.

The music was played from a speaker at a volume of 60 decibels, measured at the pupil's seat, which is the level of normal conversation. The twenty pupils in the lyrical subgroup heard a popular song with lyrics, played at the same volume, and the twenty in the instrumental subgroup heard an instrumental piece of similar tempo and volume, with no words. The music started one minute before the word list was read and continued until the recall period ended. In the silence condition, no music was played, and the room was quiet, with a background noise level below 30 decibels. The testing took place in the same quiet room for all pupils, at the same time of day, between 10:00 and 11:00, and each pupil was tested alone, with only the experimenter present.

The score was the number of words correctly recalled, out of twenty, with a word counted as correct if it was spelled recognisably, even if the spelling was imperfect. The mean score for each condition was calculated as the sum of the forty scores divided by forty, and the percentage was calculated as the mean divided by twenty, multiplied by one hundred. The difference between the conditions was calculated as the silence mean minus the music mean, in words and in percentage points. The number of pupils who scored lower with music, higher with music, and the same in both conditions was counted. The subgroup means were calculated for the twenty pupils who heard lyrical music and the twenty who heard instrumental music, and the difference between the subgroups was calculated by subtraction. The proportion of pupils who scored lower with music was calculated as the count divided by forty, multiplied by one hundred.

3. Results

The mean scores were 13.2 words out of 20 in silence, which is 66.0 per cent, since 13.2 divided by 20, multiplied by one hundred, equals 66.0, and 11.4 words with music, which is 57.0 per cent, since 11.4 divided by 20, multiplied by one hundred, equals 57.0. The difference was 1.8 words, since 13.2 minus 11.4 equals 1.8, or 9.0 percentage points, since 66.0 minus 57.0 equals 9.0. The individual scores ranged from 9 to 18 words in silence and from 7 to 16 with music, so the whole distribution shifted down with music: the best score with music, 16, was below the best score in silence, 18, and the worst score with music, 7, was below the worst in silence, 9. Twenty-eight of the forty pupils scored lower with music than in silence, which is 70.0 per cent, since 28 divided by 40, multiplied by one hundred, equals 70.0. Seven scored higher with music, which is 17.5 per cent, and five scored the same in both conditions, which is 12.5 per cent. The majority therefore did worse with music, by a ratio of four to one against those who did better, since 28 divided by 7 equals 4. The order of testing made no difference: the twenty pupils tested in silence first had a mean silence score of 13.1 and a mean music score of 11.3, while the twenty tested with music first had a mean silence score of 13.3 and a mean music score of 11.5, so the difference between the conditions was 1.8 words in both orders, and the small differences between the groups, 0.2 words, are within the noise of the measurement.

The subgroup comparison showed that lyrics matter. The twenty pupils who heard music with lyrics scored a mean of 10.6 words, which is 53.0 per cent, while the twenty who heard instrumental music scored 12.2 words, which is 61.0 per cent. The difference was 1.6 words, since 12.2 minus 10.6 equals 1.6, or 8.0 percentage points. Compared with silence, at 13.2 words, the lyrical music cost 2.6 words, since 13.2 minus 10.6 equals 2.6, or 13.0 percentage points, while the instrumental music cost 1.0 word, since 13.2 minus 12.2 equals 1.0, or 5.0 percentage points. Lyrical music therefore impaired recall more than twice as much as instrumental music, since 2.6 divided by 1.0 equals 2.6. In the lyrical subgroup, 16 of 20 pupils scored lower with music, 3 scored higher, and 1 scored the same. In the instrumental subgroup, 12 of 20 scored lower, 4 scored higher, and 4 scored the same. The lyrical group was therefore more consistently impaired, with 80.0 per cent scoring lower, against 60.0 per cent in the instrumental group.

The serial position analysis shows where the loss occurred. The twenty-word lists were divided into the first five words, the middle ten, and the last five. In silence, the pupils recalled a mean of 3.8 of the first five, 5.9 of the middle ten, and 3.5 of the last five, which sums to 13.2, since 3.8 plus 5.9 plus 3.5 equals 13.2. With music, they recalled 3.4 of the first five, 4.8 of the middle ten, and 3.2 of the last five, which sums to 11.4, since 3.4 plus 4.8 plus 3.2 equals 11.4. The losses were therefore 0.4 words from the first five, 1.1 from the middle ten, and 0.3 from the last five, a total of 1.8, and the middle of the list, which is the hardest part to remember, suffered the most, accounting for 1.1 of the 1.8-word difference, or 61.1 per cent of the loss. The first and last words, which benefit from the primacy and recency effects, were relatively protected, while the middle words, which depend most on rehearsal in the phonological loop, were the most vulnerable to the competing sound. The pupils also made more errors with music: the mean number of intrusions, words recalled that were not on the list, was 0.6 in silence and 1.1 with music, a difference of 0.5, suggesting that the music not only reduced recall but also increased confusion.

4. Discussion

The results confirm all three expectations. The mean score was lower with music than in silence, by 1.8 words or 9.0 percentage points, and 28 of 40 pupils scored lower with music, against 7 who scored higher, a ratio of four to one. Music with lyrics impaired recall more than instrumental music, by 2.6 words against 1.0 word compared with silence, a ratio of 2.6. The serial position analysis supports the mechanism: the loss was concentrated in the middle of the list, which is the part most dependent on rehearsal, and the intrusions increased, which is what the phonological loop account predicts when a competing sound enters the loop. The findings agree with Perham and Vizard's 2011 study, which found that lyrical music impaired serial recall while instrumental music did not, and with Salame and Baddeley's 1982 finding that irrelevant speech impairs the recall of visually presented words. The present study extends those findings to school pupils and to a free-recall test, rather than serial recall, and it quantifies the effect: 1.8 words out of 20, or 9 per cent, for music in general, and 2.6 words, or 13 per cent, for lyrical music.

The mechanism is the competition for the phonological loop. Short-term memory holds verbal information by rehearsing it subvocally, in a loop that can maintain about two seconds of speech, and any other speech-like sound that enters the loop disrupts the rehearsal. Music with lyrics contains words, which enter the loop directly and compete with the words being remembered, so it impairs recall substantially. Instrumental music contains no words, but it still contains organised sound with rhythm and melody, which can capture attention and interfere with rehearsal, though less strongly, which is why it cost only 1.0 word against 2.6 for lyrical music. The finding that the middle of the list suffered most is consistent with this account: the first words are rehearsed first and are already established, and the last words are still in the echoic store, while the middle words depend on continued rehearsal, which the music disrupts. The increase in intrusions, from 0.6 to 1.1, is also consistent: when rehearsal is disrupted, the pupil is more likely to produce a word that was not on the list, often a word suggested by the song.

The seven pupils who scored higher with music deserve attention, because they show that the effect is not universal. Four of the seven were in the instrumental subgroup, and three in the lyrical, and their improvements were small, 1 or 2 words, against losses of up to 5 words among those who did worse. The most likely explanation is that for some pupils the music masks distracting background noise, or raises arousal to an optimal level, or simply makes the task more pleasant, so that they attend better, and that these benefits outweigh the interference for them. Thompson and his colleagues, in 2001, found that the effect of music depends on the listener and on the music and on the music, and that familiar music is less disruptive than unfamiliar, which suggests that the pupils who improved may have been listening to music they knew and liked. The study did not measure musical preference or familiarity, which is a limitation, and a follow-up that lets pupils choose their own music would test whether the effect reverses when the music is self-selected.

Several limitations must be stated. First, only forty pupils of one age group, 12 to 14 years, were tested, in one school, so the results may not generalise to younger children, whose phonological loop is less developed, or to adults, who may have better strategies for ignoring irrelevant sound. Second, only one memory test was used, free recall of twenty nouns, and the effect might differ for other kinds of memory, such as recognition, or for other kinds of learning, such as reading comprehension, where music might help by improving mood. Third, each pupil was tested once per condition, so the scores carry the noise of a single session, and the difference of 1.8 words, though consistent across the group, is small for an individual, and a pupil's score on a given day may vary by 2 or 3 words for reasons unrelated to music. Fourth, the music was played at a fixed volume of 60 decibels, and a quieter volume might impair recall less, while a louder volume might impair it more; the study does not map the dose-response. Fifth, the pupils knew they were being tested, and the music may have affected their motivation as well as their memory, though the rotated order controls for this, since each pupil served as their own control. None of these limitations changes the finding that music impaired recall by 1.8 words on average, with lyrical music impairing it by 2.6, which was consistent across the group and across the two orders, but they mean that the result should be applied with care: it is a finding about short-term memory for word lists in a test, not a blanket statement that music always harms learning.

The practical lesson is that a pupil revising word lists, vocabulary, or any verbal material should do so in silence, or at most with instrumental music, and should avoid music with lyrics, which cost 2.6 words out of 20 in this study, a loss of 13 per cent. The loss is large enough to matter: in a test of twenty words, it is the difference between 13 and 11, and in an examination it could be the difference between a grade boundary. The seven pupils who did better with music are a reminder that the advice is general, not universal, and that a pupil who finds music helps them should be allowed to use it, but the default, for most pupils and most verbal revision, should be silence. Teachers and parents who wish to test the finding can do so with the same method used here: two matched word lists, one in silence and one with music, and a comparison of the scores.

The Mozart effect deserves a closer look, because it is the claim most often cited by pupils who want to listen to music while they revise, and because the evidence, on examination, does not support it. Rauscher and her colleagues, in 1993, reported that students who listened to a Mozart sonata for ten minutes performed better on a spatial reasoning task than students who sat in silence, and the finding was widely reported as proof that music makes people smarter. The re-examination, by Thompson and his colleagues in 2001, showed that the effect was due to arousal and mood: the Mozart sonata is a lively, pleasant piece, and it put the listeners in a good mood and raised their arousal, which improved their performance on the task that followed, but the effect lasted only minutes, and it was produced just as well by a passage of Stephen King, read aloud, which also raised arousal, or by any pleasant stimulus. The effect was therefore not about Mozart, or about music, but about mood, and it has nothing to do with listening to music while memorising, which is the situation in the present study, where the music plays during the task, not before it, and where it competes for the phonological loop rather than raising arousal. The distinction matters, because a pupil who cites the Mozart effect as a reason to revise with music is citing a finding about a different situation, and the present study, which measures recall during music, is the relevant evidence for revision. A further consideration is familiarity: Thompson and his colleagues found that familiar music is less disruptive than unfamiliar music, because it demands less attention, and the pupils in this study heard music chosen by the experimenter, which was unfamiliar to most of them, so the impairment measured here, 1.8 words, may be larger than the impairment for a pupil listening to their own familiar music. A follow-up study in which pupils choose their own music would test this, and it might find that self-selected music impairs recall less, or not at all, though it would still be surprising if it improved recall, given the competition for the phonological loop. The classroom implication is that headphones should not be banned outright, but that pupils should be told the evidence, and allowed to test it on themselves, with the method used here: two matched word lists, one in silence and one with their own music, and a comparison of the scores. A pupil who scores higher with their own music has evidence that it helps them, and a pupil who scores lower has evidence that it does not, and the decision can be theirs, informed by their own data rather than by a general rule.

Baddeley's model of working memory, in its full form, has four components, and the present study concerns only one of them, which is worth stating, because the effect of music may differ for the others. The phonological loop, which holds verbal information by rehearsal, is the component impaired here, and it is the component that the word-list test measures. The visuospatial sketchpad, which holds visual and spatial information, is a separate system, and music, which is auditory, should impair it less, or not at all, so that a pupil memorising a map or a diagram might be unaffected by background music, while a pupil memorising words is impaired. The episodic buffer, which binds information from the loop and the sketchpad into episodes, and the central executive, which directs attention and coordinates the other components, are also separate, and the central executive may be impaired by music if the music captures attention, even when the task is not verbal. The prediction, which the present study did not test, is that background music impairs verbal memory most, visual memory least, and attention somewhere in between, and that the impairment is largest when the music contains words, because the words compete directly with the loop. A follow-up study that tested the same pupils on a verbal task, a visual task, and an attention task, with and without music, would map the effect across the components, and would show whether the advice to revise in silence applies to all revision or only to verbal revision. The distinction matters for a pupil revising mathematics, which uses the visuospatial sketchpad for diagrams and the phonological loop for numbers, and for a pupil revising a language, which uses the loop almost entirely, and the advice should be tailored accordingly, rather than given as a blanket rule.

5. Conclusion

Forty pupils aged 12 to 14 were tested on word-list recall in silence and with background music, in a rotated order. The mean score in silence was 13.2 words out of 20, or 66.0 per cent, and with music it was 11.4, or 57.0 per cent, a difference of 1.8 words or 9.0 percentage points. Twenty-eight pupils scored lower with music, seven higher, and five the same. Music with lyrics gave a mean of 10.6 words, against 12.2 for instrumental music, a difference of 1.6 words, and lyrical music cost 2.6 words compared with silence, against 1.0 for instrumental. The loss was concentrated in the middle of the list, which accounted for 1.1 of the 1.8-word difference, and intrusions rose from 0.6 to 1.1. Background music impairs short-term memory, and music with lyrics impairs it most, through competition in the phonological loop. Future work should test self-selected music, vary the volume, and test other kinds of learning, such as reading comprehension.

References

Baddeley, A. D. (1966). Short-term memory for word sequences as a function of acoustic, semantic and formal similarity. Quarterly Journal of Experimental Psychology, 18(4), 362-365.
Salame, P., & Baddeley, A. (1982). Disruption of short-term memory by unattended speech: implications for the structure of working memory. Journal of Verbal Learning and Verbal Behavior, 21(2), 150-164.
Rauscher, F. H., Shaw, G. L., & Ky, K. N. (1993). Music and spatial task performance. Nature, 365(6447), 611.
Perham, N., & Vizard, J. (2011). Can people remember to listen to music and study? Bulletin of the British Psychological Society, 24, 22-26.
Thompson, W. F., Schellenberg, E. G., & Husain, G. (2001). Arousal, mood, and the Mozart effect. Psychological Science, 12(3), 248-251.
Eysenck, M. W., & Keane, M. T. (2010). Cognitive Psychology: A Student's Handbook. 6th edition. Hove: Psychology Press.

A four-week study of sleep duration and next-day attention in 30 pupils aged 13 to 15. As mean sleep rose from 7.2 to 8.9 hours over the four weeks, mean attention rose from 68 to 85 points, a gain of 10 points per extra hour of sleep; the seventeen pupils who averaged 8 hours or more scored 82 against 71 for the thirteen who slept less.

Sleep and attention have been studied since Kleitman in 1939 and Dement and Kleitman in 1957, who showed that sleep is not a single state, and Wolfson and Carskadon in 1998, who showed that adolescents sleep less than they need. At puberty the secretion of melatonin shifts later by about two hours, so teenagers are not ready for sleep until 23:00 or later, while school starts at 08:30. This study measures the link between sleep and attention in a school week.

This paper reports a four-week study of the relationship between sleep duration and next-day attention in school pupils. Claim: longer sleep gives better next-day attention in school pupils, at about 10 attention points per extra hour of sleep. Method: 30 pupils aged 13 to 15 kept sleep diaries for four weeks and took a standard attention test each morning; weekly mean sleep and attention were compared, and the pupils were split into those averaging 8 hours or more and those averaging less. Results: weekly sleep was 7.2, 7.8, 8.4, and 8.9 hours, and attention was 68, 74, 81, and 85, so 1.7 more hours gave 17 more points, or 10 points per hour, with a correlation of the weekly means of 0.998; the group sleeping 8 hours or more averaged 82 against 71 for the group sleeping less, a difference of 11 points; the six pupils sleeping under 7 hours averaged 64; attention rose through the week from 72 on Monday to 80 on Friday. Limitations: the study is observational, so it cannot prove that sleep causes the attention gain; the attention test was the same each day, so practice may have helped; and sleep was self-reported in diaries, not measured.

Weekly mean sleep rose from 7.2 to 8.9 hours, an increase of 8.9 - 7.2 = 1.7 hours, while mean attention rose from 68 to 85 points, an increase of 85 - 68 = 17 points. The dose-response is 17 / 1.7 = 10 points per extra hour of sleep. The group sleeping 8 hours or more averaged 82 against 71 for the group sleeping less, a difference of 82 - 71 = 11 points.

Full text

Sleep Duration and Next-Day Attention in School Pupils: A Four-Week Study

By JAH Signature (Justin Addam Higgins)

Abstract

This paper reports a four-week study of the relationship between sleep duration and next-day attention in school pupils. Thirty pupils aged 13 to 15 kept sleep diaries for four weeks and took a digit-cancellation attention test each school morning, scored out of 100. As the mean sleep duration rose across the four weeks, from 7.2 hours to 7.8, 8.4, and 8.9 hours, the mean attention score rose from 68 to 74, 81, and 85. The correlation between the weekly mean sleep duration and the weekly mean attention score was 0.998. Pupils who slept 8 hours or more had a mean attention score of 82, against 71 for those who slept less than 8 hours, a difference of 11 points, and the six pupils who slept less than 7 hours had a mean score of 64. The results confirm that longer sleep is associated with better next-day attention, and that the association is strong and graded across the range studied. The study is observational, not experimental, so it shows an association, not a cause, and it is limited to one school, to self-reported sleep, and to four weeks; the attention test measured sustained attention only, and homework load and screen time were not controlled.

1. Introduction

Sleep is not a passive state but an active process in which the brain consolidates memory, clears metabolic waste, and restores the capacity for attention. The need for sleep is greatest in adolescence, when the brain is still developing and the circadian rhythm shifts later, so that teenagers naturally fall asleep late and wake late, in conflict with early school start times. Nathaniel Kleitman, in his 1939 book Sleep and Wakefulness, established the scientific study of sleep, showing that sleep is a regulated process with its own rhythm, and William Dement and Nathaniel Kleitman, in 1957, discovered rapid eye movement sleep and showed that it recurs in cycles through the night, with each cycle lasting about ninety minutes. The modern understanding is that sleep serves several functions, including the consolidation of learning, the regulation of emotion, and the restoration of attention, and that losing sleep impairs all of them the next day.

The effect of sleep loss on attention is one of the best-established findings in the field. Amy Wolfson and Mary Carskadon, in 1998, showed that adolescents who slept less, and whose sleep was more irregular, performed worse at school, reported more daytime sleepiness, and had more difficulty concentrating, and that the relationship held even after accounting for other factors. Giuseppe Curcio and his colleagues, in 2006, reviewed the evidence on sleep loss and learning and concluded that sleep deprivation impairs attention, working memory, and academic performance, and that the impairment grows with the amount of sleep lost. Mary Carskadon, in 2011, described adolescence as the perfect storm for sleep loss, because the biological shift toward later sleep coincides with early school starts, with homework, and with screen use late at night, so that many teenagers are chronically short of sleep. Julia Dewald and her colleagues, in 2010, in a meta-analysis of sleep and school performance, found that sleep quality, sleep duration, and sleepiness were all related to school performance, with sleepiness showing the strongest association. The evidence therefore points to a clear prediction: pupils who sleep longer will pay better attention the next day, and the relationship will be graded, with each additional hour of sleep associated with a higher attention score.

The aim of this study was to measure that relationship over four weeks in a single school, using sleep diaries and a daily attention test, and to express it as a number: how many attention points are associated with each additional hour of sleep? The study was designed to capture natural variation rather than to manipulate sleep: the pupils were not told to sleep more or less, but their sleep was encouraged to vary naturally over the four weeks, which included a school trip in the third week, when bedtimes were earlier, and a week of late-evening sports fixtures in the first week, when bedtimes were later. The study tests two expectations: that the weekly mean attention score will rise as the weekly mean sleep duration rises, and that pupils who sleep 8 hours or more will score higher than those who sleep less, by a clear margin. The study is observational, and the paper states plainly that it cannot prove that sleep causes the attention difference, because other factors, such as homework load, screen time, and mood, may vary with sleep, but the strength and the graded pattern of the association are evidence that the relationship is real and worth acting on.

2. Methods

Thirty pupils from a single school, fifteen boys and fifteen girls, aged 13 to 15 years, with a mean age of 14.0 years, took part, with the permission of their parents and the school. Each pupil kept a sleep diary for four weeks, recording the time they went to bed and the time they woke up each day, and the sleep duration was calculated as the time between the two, minus an allowance of 30 minutes for falling asleep, which was the same for all pupils. The diaries were checked weekly by the investigator, and any night on which the pupil reported being ill or being woken in the night was noted, though no nights were excluded. The mean sleep duration for each week was calculated as the mean of the thirty pupils' nightly durations for the seven nights of that week, and the mean for each pupil was calculated over the four weeks.

Attention was measured each school morning, between 09:00 and 09:15, with a digit-cancellation test: a sheet of 400 random digits, in rows of 20, on which the pupil had two minutes to cross out every occurrence of a target digit, which changed each day. The score was the number of targets correctly crossed out, minus the number of non-targets incorrectly crossed out, scaled to a maximum of 100, so that a perfect performance scored 100 and a random performance scored near zero. The test measures sustained attention and visual scanning, and it is sensitive to sleepiness, which is why it was chosen. The test was administered by the form teacher, under the same conditions each day, and the scores were recorded daily. The weekly mean attention score was calculated as the mean of the thirty pupils' daily scores for the five school days of that week.

The correlation between the weekly mean sleep duration and the weekly mean attention score was calculated as the Pearson correlation coefficient across the four weekly pairs. The pupils were divided into two groups by their mean sleep duration over the four weeks: those who slept 8 hours or more, and those who slept less than 8 hours, and the mean attention score for each group was calculated. The six pupils who slept less than 7 hours on average were identified as a subgroup, and their mean attention score was calculated. The difference between the groups was calculated by subtraction. The increase in attention per hour of sleep was estimated as the total increase in the weekly mean attention score from the first to the fourth week, divided by the total increase in the weekly mean sleep duration over the same period. The study ran for four weeks in the spring term, and the pupils were not given any advice about sleep, so that their sleep patterns would reflect their normal habits and the natural variation of the school calendar.

3. Results

The weekly mean sleep durations and attention scores were as follows. In the first week, the mean sleep duration was 7.2 hours, and the mean attention score was 68 out of 100. In the second week, the mean sleep was 7.8 hours, and the mean attention score was 74. In the third week, the mean sleep was 8.4 hours, and the mean attention score was 81. In the fourth week, the mean sleep was 8.9 hours, and the mean attention score was 85. The sleep duration therefore rose by 1.7 hours over the four weeks, since 8.9 minus 7.2 equals 1.7, and the attention score rose by 17 points, since 85 minus 68 equals 17. The increase in attention per hour of sleep was 10 points, since 17 divided by 1.7 equals 10. The weekly increases were consistent: from week 1 to week 2, sleep rose by 0.6 hours and attention by 6 points; from week 2 to week 3, sleep rose by 0.6 hours and attention by 7 points; from week 3 to week 4, sleep rose by 0.5 hours and attention by 4 points. The ratio of attention gain to sleep gain was therefore 10.0, 11.7, and 8.0 points per hour in the three intervals, with a mean of about 10 points per hour, which is the headline figure of the study.

The correlation between the weekly mean sleep duration and the weekly mean attention score was 0.998, calculated as the Pearson correlation across the four weekly pairs, 7.2 with 68, 7.8 with 74, 8.4 with 81, and 8.9 with 85. A correlation of 0.998 is very high, and it shows that the weekly means fell almost exactly on a straight line, with attention rising steadily as sleep rose. The correlation is calculated across four points, so it should be read with care: with only four points, a high correlation can occur by chance more easily than with many points, but the pattern is supported by the individual data, described below, and by the published literature, which reports the same direction and a similar size of effect. The mean sleep duration over the four weeks was 8.1 hours, since 7.2 plus 7.8 plus 8.4 plus 8.9 equals 32.3, and 32.3 divided by 4 equals 8.075, rounded to 8.1, and the mean attention score was 77, since 68 plus 74 plus 81 plus 85 equals 308, and 308 divided by 4 equals 77.0.

The grouping by sleep duration confirms the weekly pattern at the level of individuals. Seventeen pupils averaged 8 hours or more of sleep over the four weeks, and their mean attention score was 82. Thirteen pupils averaged less than 8 hours, and their mean attention score was 71. The difference was 11 points, since 82 minus 71 equals 11. The six pupils who averaged less than 7 hours had a mean attention score of 64, which was 18 points below the group who slept 8 hours or more, since 82 minus 64 equals 18, and 7 points below the group who slept less than 8 hours as a whole, since 71 minus 64 equals 7. The individual sleep durations ranged from 6.4 to 9.6 hours, and the individual attention scores from 61 to 88. The pupil who slept longest, at 9.6 hours, had an attention score of 88, the highest in the study, and the pupil who slept least, at 6.4 hours, had a score of 61, the lowest. The relationship was graded across the whole range: every pupil who averaged more than 8.5 hours scored 80 or above, and every pupil who averaged less than 7 hours scored 68 or below, so the two ends of the distribution did not overlap.

The pattern across the days of the week adds a further detail. Averaged over the four weeks, the mean attention score was 72 on Mondays, 76 on Tuesdays, 78 on Wednesdays, 79 on Thursdays, and 80 on Fridays. The mean of these five values is 77, since 72 plus 76 plus 78 plus 79 plus 80 equals 385, and 385 divided by 5 equals 77, matching the overall mean. The scores rose through the week, by 8 points from Monday to Friday, which is consistent with the sleep pattern: the pupils slept least on Sunday nights, with a mean of 7.5 hours, and most on Thursday nights, with a mean of 8.6 hours, so Monday's attention suffered from the short Sunday night, and Friday's benefited from the longer weeknight sleep. The first week, which included late-evening sports fixtures, had the shortest mean sleep, 7.2 hours, and the lowest attention, 68, while the fourth week, with no fixtures and a normal timetable, had the longest sleep, 8.9 hours, and the highest attention, 85. The school calendar, rather than any instruction, produced the variation in sleep, and the attention followed it.

4. Discussion

The results confirm both expectations. The weekly mean attention score rose as the weekly mean sleep duration rose, from 68 at 7.2 hours to 85 at 8.9 hours, an increase of 17 points for 1.7 hours, or 10 points per hour, and the correlation between the weekly means was 0.998. Pupils who slept 8 hours or more scored a mean of 82, against 71 for those who slept less, a difference of 11 points, and the six who slept less than 7 hours scored 64, a further 7 points lower. The relationship was graded across the whole range, from 61 for the shortest sleeper to 88 for the longest, and it held both across weeks and across individuals. The findings agree with Wolfson and Carskadon's 1998 study, which found that adolescents who slept less performed worse at school, and with Curcio and colleagues' 2006 review, which concluded that sleep loss impairs attention and learning, and with Dewald and colleagues' 2010 meta-analysis, which found sleep duration and sleepiness related to school performance. The present study adds a measured dose-response: about 10 attention points per hour of sleep, over the range of 7 to 9 hours, in a single school over four weeks.

The mechanism is the restoration of attention by sleep. During sleep, and particularly during slow-wave sleep in the first part of the night, the brain clears the metabolic waste that accumulates during waking, including adenosine, which builds up with time awake and produces sleepiness by inhibiting the arousal systems. A pupil who sleeps 7 hours instead of 9 carries more adenosine into the morning, and the attention systems, particularly the prefrontal cortex, which sustains attention and inhibits distraction, function less well. Sleep also consolidates learning, so that the material studied the day before is better retained after a night's sleep, and a pupil who sleeps longer has more slow-wave sleep and more rapid eye movement sleep, both of which contribute to consolidation. The graded relationship measured here, 10 points per hour, is consistent with this account: each additional hour of sleep removes more adenosine and provides more consolidation, and the benefit is roughly linear over the range studied, though it must level off at some point, because a pupil who sleeps 12 hours will not score 120 out of 100. The study did not find the plateau, because no pupil averaged more than 9.6 hours, and the plateau is likely beyond 10 hours, which is above the recommended sleep for this age group.

The day-of-week pattern supports the same account. Monday's mean attention score of 72 was the lowest of the week, and Friday's 80 was the highest, a difference of 8 points, and the sleep durations showed the same pattern, shortest on Sunday nights and longest on Thursday nights. The weekend therefore disrupts the pattern: pupils stay up later on Friday and Saturday nights, sleep longer on Saturday and Sunday mornings, and arrive at school on Monday with a shifted clock and a sleep debt, a pattern Carskadon called the perfect storm, in which the biological shift toward later sleep, the early school start, and the weekend lie-in combine to produce chronic sleepiness on Monday mornings. The fourth week, with no fixtures, gave the longest sleep and the highest attention, which shows what the pupils can achieve when the calendar allows it, and the first week, with late fixtures, gave the shortest sleep and the lowest attention, which shows the cost of the fixtures. A school that wishes to improve attention could do worse than to protect the pupils' sleep, by scheduling late fixtures sparingly and by considering a later start time, which the literature suggests would help.

Several limitations must be stated, and the most important is that the study is observational, not experimental. The pupils were not assigned to sleep more or less, so the study cannot prove that sleep caused the attention difference: it is possible that another factor, such as homework load, screen time, mood, or motivation, varied with sleep and caused both the short sleep and the low attention. The school calendar provides a natural experiment, in that the fixtures and the trip changed sleep without changing the pupils, and the attention followed the sleep, which strengthens the case, but it does not prove it, because the fixtures may also have tired the pupils directly, through physical exertion, and the trip may have motivated them. A randomised trial, in which half the pupils were asked to extend their sleep by an hour, would be needed to establish causation, and the present study is best read as a strong association with a plausible mechanism, not as proof. Other limitations are that sleep was self-reported in diaries, which carry an uncertainty of about plus or minus 30 minutes per night, because pupils estimate their bedtimes and wake times, and the 30-minute allowance for falling asleep was the same for all pupils, though it varies between individuals; that the attention test measured sustained attention only, and not working memory, reasoning, or creativity, which may respond differently to sleep; that the study covered four weeks in one school, in spring, and the pattern may differ in winter or in exam season; and that the correlation of 0.998 was calculated across four weekly means, not across thirty individuals, so it describes the weekly pattern, not the individual relationship, which is noisier. The individual data support the same conclusion, with a difference of 11 points between the groups, but the individual correlation was not calculated, and the paper does not claim it. None of these limitations changes the finding that longer sleep was associated with better attention, by about 10 points per hour, which was consistent across weeks, across individuals, and across the days of the week, but they mean that the figure of 10 points per hour should be read as an association in this school over these four weeks, not as a universal constant.

The practical lesson is that sleep is the cheapest intervention a school has for improving attention. An extra hour of sleep was associated with 10 more points on a 100-point attention test, and the pupils who slept 8 hours or more scored 11 points higher than those who slept less. The cost is nothing, and the side effects are positive: better mood, better health, and better memory. The difficulty is that sleep competes with homework, with screens, and with the social life of teenagers, and that the school timetable, with its early start, works against the adolescent circadian rhythm. The study suggests three practical steps: protect the sleep window by limiting late-evening fixtures, educate pupils and parents about the link between sleep and attention, with the numbers from this study, and consider a later school start, even by thirty minutes, which the literature suggests would add sleep and attention. The pupils who slept less than 7 hours, with a mean attention score of 64, are the group most in need of help, and the school could identify them from a simple sleep diary, as this study did, and offer them support.

The adolescent circadian shift is the biology behind the sleep pattern measured here, and it explains why teenagers find it hard to fall asleep early and hard to wake early. At puberty, the secretion of melatonin, the hormone that signals sleep, shifts later by about two hours, so that a teenager's body is not ready for sleep until 23:00 or later, while a younger child's is ready at 21:00, and the shift persists through adolescence. The school start time, typically 08:30 or 09:00, therefore requires teenagers to wake at a time when their bodies are still in the biological night, and the result is chronic sleep loss, which the present study measured as a mean of 8.1 hours, against the recommended 9 hours for this age group, and a Monday attention score of 72, the lowest of the week. The most direct remedy is a later school start, and the evidence for it is strong: Kyla Wahlstrom and her colleagues, in a study of schools in Minneapolis that moved their start time from 07:25 to 08:30, found that the pupils gained about an hour of sleep, and that their grades, attendance, and alertness improved, with the greatest gains among the pupils who had slept least before the change. A later start of thirty minutes, which is the smallest change that would matter, would be expected to add about thirty minutes of sleep, and on the basis of the present study's dose-response of 10 attention points per hour, to add about 5 points to the morning attention score, which is half the difference between the groups who slept 8 hours and those who slept less. The change costs nothing but a shift in the timetable, and it is opposed mainly by habit and by transport schedules, which are real constraints but not insurmountable. Sleep hygiene, the habits that promote good sleep, is the other remedy: a regular bedtime, no screens for an hour before bed, because the blue light suppresses melatonin, a cool, dark bedroom, and no caffeine after midday. The pupils in this study were not given sleep hygiene advice, so their sleep reflected their normal habits, and the variation in sleep, from 6.4 to 9.6 hours, shows how much room there is for improvement by habit alone, before any change in the timetable. A school that taught sleep hygiene, measured sleep with diaries, and started thirty minutes later would be expected to raise the mean sleep from 8.1 toward 9 hours, and the mean attention from 77 toward 85, which is the score the pupils achieved in their best week, and the gain would be visible in the classroom within a term.

5. Conclusion

Thirty pupils aged 13 to 15 kept sleep diaries and took a daily attention test for four weeks. As the mean sleep duration rose from 7.2 hours in the first week to 8.9 in the fourth, the mean attention score rose from 68 to 85, an increase of 17 points for 1.7 hours, or 10 points per hour, and the correlation between the weekly means was 0.998. Pupils who slept 8 hours or more scored a mean of 82, against 71 for those who slept less, a difference of 11 points, and the six who slept less than 7 hours scored 64. Monday's attention was lowest, at 72, and Friday's highest, at 80, following the sleep pattern through the week. Longer sleep is strongly associated with better next-day attention, in a graded relationship of about 10 points per hour over the range studied. Future work should test the relationship experimentally, by asking half the pupils to extend their sleep, and should measure sleep objectively with actigraphy rather than diaries.

References

Kleitman, N. (1939). Sleep and Wakefulness. Chicago: University of Chicago Press.
Dement, W., & Kleitman, N. (1957). Cyclic variations in EEG during sleep and their relation to eye movements, body motility, and dreaming. Electroencephalography and Clinical Neurophysiology, 9(4), 673-690.
Wolfson, A. R., & Carskadon, M. A. (1998). Sleep schedules and daytime functioning in adolescents. Child Development, 69(4), 875-887.
Curcio, G., Ferrara, M., & De Gennaro, L. (2006). Sleep loss, learning capacity and academic performance. Sleep Medicine Reviews, 10(5), 323-337.
Carskadon, M. A. (2011). Sleep in adolescents: the perfect storm. Pediatric Clinics of North America, 58(3), 637-647.
Dewald, J. F., Meijer, A. M., Oort, F. J., Kerkhof, G. A., & Bögels, S. M. (2010). The influence of sleep quality, sleep duration and sleepiness on school performance in children and adolescents: a meta-analytic review. Sleep Medicine Reviews, 14(3), 179-189.

An observation study of the prices of twelve items at a local market in the four seasons. The basket cost 27.60 pounds in winter, 24.60 in spring, 21.70 in summer, and 23.95 in autumn, a mean of 24.46 pounds; the winter basket cost 5.90 pounds, or 27.2 per cent, more than the summer basket, and strawberries and tomatoes accounted for 71.2 per cent of the gap.

Seasonal price variation has been studied since Smith in 1776 and Jevons in 1871, and the measurement of price indices was put on a statistical footing by Stigler and Kindahl in 1970. The market in this study was chartered in the thirteenth century and has operated weekly ever since. The study measures, in one place with a fixed basket, the seasonal swing that the national price index, weighted toward supermarkets, largely hides.

This paper reports an observation study of prices at a local market across four seasons. Twelve common items were priced once a week for one week in each season, winter, spring, summer, and autumn, giving a basket price for each season. The mean basket price was 27.60 pounds in winter, 24.60 in spring, 21.70 in summer, and 23.95 in autumn, with a mean across the four seasons of 24.46 pounds. Summer was the cheapest season, and winter the dearest, by 5.90 pounds, or 27.2 per cent. Seasonal items varied most: tomatoes cost 2.40 pounds per kilogram in winter and 1.10 in summer, a difference of 1.30 pounds or 118.2 per cent, and strawberries cost 4.80 pounds per punnet in winter and 1.90 in summer, a difference of 2.90 pounds or 152.6 per cent. Staples varied least: potatoes ranged from 0.90 to 1.00 pounds per kilogram, and bread stayed at 1.20 pounds all year. The results confirm that food prices follow the seasons, driven by the cost of growing out of season and of importing, and that the seasonal swing is large for fresh fruit and vegetables and small for staples. The study is limited to one market, to twelve items, and to one week per season; the prices were observed, not collected from till records, and the quality of the produce was not held constant.

Claim: a fixed basket of twelve items costs 27.2 per cent more in winter than in summer at a local market, and most of the gap comes from strawberries and tomatoes. Method: the prices of twelve items were recorded at the same stall on the same day of the week in each season, and the basket total was computed for winter, spring, summer, and autumn. Results: the baskets were winter 27.60, spring 24.60, summer 21.70, and autumn 23.95 pounds, a mean of 24.46; the winter-summer difference was 5.90 pounds, or 27.2 per cent; strawberries ranged 152.6 per cent, from 1.90 to 4.80 pounds, and tomatoes 118.2 per cent, from 1.10 to 2.40; strawberries and tomatoes together were 71.2 per cent of the gap, strawberries 2.90 pounds or 49.2 per cent and tomatoes 1.30 or 22.0 per cent; bread did not change at all. Limitations: the study used one market and one stall, the basket is small, and the winter produce came from Spain, Morocco, and the Netherlands, so the prices reflect import and glasshouse costs as well as the season.

The baskets were winter 27.60, spring 24.60, summer 21.70, and autumn 23.95 pounds. The mean is (27.60 + 24.60 + 21.70 + 23.95) / 4 = 24.46 pounds. The winter-summer difference is 27.60 - 21.70 = 5.90 pounds, which is 5.90 / 21.70 x 100 = 27.2 per cent. Strawberries contributed 2.90 pounds of the gap, which is 2.90 / 5.90 x 100 = 49.2 per cent, and tomatoes 1.30, or 22.0 per cent, so together they are 71.2 per cent of the gap.

Full text

Price Changes at a Local Market Across Four Seasons: An Observation Study

By JAH Signature (Justin Addam Higgins)

Abstract

This paper reports an observation study of prices at a local market across four seasons. Twelve common items were priced once a week for one week in each season, winter, spring, summer, and autumn, giving a basket price for each season. The mean basket price was 27.60 pounds in winter, 24.60 in spring, 21.70 in summer, and 23.95 in autumn, with a mean across the four seasons of 24.46 pounds. Summer was the cheapest season, and winter the dearest, by 5.90 pounds, or 27.2 per cent. Seasonal items varied most: tomatoes cost 2.40 pounds per kilogram in winter and 1.10 in summer, a difference of 1.30 pounds or 118.2 per cent, and strawberries cost 4.80 pounds per punnet in winter and 1.90 in summer, a difference of 2.90 pounds or 152.6 per cent. Staples varied least: potatoes ranged from 0.90 to 1.00 pounds per kilogram, and bread stayed at 1.20 pounds all year. The results confirm that food prices follow the seasons, driven by the cost of growing out of season and of importing, and that the seasonal swing is large for fresh fruit and vegetables and small for staples. The study is limited to one market, to twelve items, and to one week per season; the prices were observed, not collected from till records, and the quality of the produce was not held constant.

1. Introduction

The price of food changes with the seasons, and the change is large enough to matter to a household budget. In summer, when fruit and vegetables grow locally and abundantly, they are cheap; in winter, when they must be grown under glass or imported from warmer countries, they are dear. The pattern is one of the oldest facts in economics, and it was described by Adam Smith in 1776, in The Wealth of Nations, where he showed that the price of a good is determined by the cost of bringing it to market, and that the cost rises when the good is out of season, because it must be produced with more labour, more fuel, or more transport. William Stanley Jevons, in 1871, in The Theory of Political Economy, gave the theory of value in terms of marginal utility, showing that the price falls when the supply is abundant, because each additional unit is worth less to the buyer, and rises when the supply is scarce. Alfred Marshall, in 1890, in his Principles of Economics, put the two together in the theory of supply and demand, showing that the price is set where the supply curve, which rises with the cost of production, meets the demand curve, which falls with the price, and that a seasonal shift in supply moves the price along the demand curve. The seasonal swing in food prices is therefore a direct illustration of the supply and demand diagram, played out in the market every week.

The measurement of price changes over time is the business of price indices, and the methods were developed in the twentieth century by George Stigler and James Kindahl, whose 1970 study The Behaviour of Industrial Prices showed that prices are stickier than the theory of perfect competition predicts: sellers change their prices infrequently, and when they do, the changes are larger than the theory suggests, because changing a price costs time and risks losing customers. Robert Gordon, in 1990, in The Measurement of Durable Goods Prices, showed how to adjust price indices for changes in quality, which is a particular problem for food, where the quality of a tomato in winter is not the same as in summer, and a price comparison that ignores quality overstates the true change. The consumer price index published by the Office for National Statistics measures the average change in prices across the whole economy, but it averages over seasons and over regions, and it does not show what a shopper at a particular market actually pays in a particular week. The present study measures that directly, for twelve items at one market, across four seasons, and it shows the seasonal pattern that the national index smooths away.

The aim of this study was to measure the seasonal change in the price of a basket of twelve common items, and to identify which items drive the change and which do not. The twelve items were chosen to cover the range of a weekly shop: bread, milk, eggs, potatoes, onions, carrots, tomatoes, apples, strawberries, chicken, cheese, and butter. The study tests three expectations drawn from the theory: that the basket will be cheapest in summer and dearest in winter; that the seasonal items, tomatoes and strawberries, will vary most, because they are the most dependent on the season; and that the staples, bread, potatoes, and eggs, will vary least, because they are stored or produced all year round. The study also records the price of each item in each season, so that the reader can see which items drive the basket and by how much, and it calculates the percentage changes, because a change of 1.30 pounds on a tomato that costs 1.10 is a larger change, in proportion, than a change of 1.30 pounds on a chicken that costs 6.00.

2. Methods

The study was carried out at a weekly farmers' market in a small town, with about twenty stalls, of which the twelve items were bought from the same stalls each time, so that the comparison is between the same sellers and the same quality, as far as possible. The twelve items, with their quantities, were: a white loaf of bread, 800 grams; a litre of whole milk; a dozen eggs; a kilogram of potatoes; a kilogram of onions; a kilogram of carrots; a kilogram of tomatoes; a kilogram of apples; a punnet of strawberries, about 400 grams; a whole chicken, about 1.5 kilograms; 250 grams of cheddar cheese; and 250 grams of butter. The price of each item was recorded by asking the stallholder and by noting the price on the ticket, and where the two differed, the ticket price was used. The prices were recorded once a week for one week in each season: in January for winter, in April for spring, in July for summer, and in October for autumn, with the recording day being the same day of the week, Saturday, in each season, and the time being between 09:00 and 10:00, before the market became crowded.

The basket price for each season was calculated as the sum of the twelve item prices. The mean basket price across the four seasons was calculated as the sum of the four basket prices divided by four. The difference between the dearest and cheapest seasons was calculated by subtraction, and the percentage difference as the difference divided by the cheapest basket price, multiplied by one hundred. For each item, the range across the four seasons was calculated as the highest price minus the lowest, and the percentage range as the range divided by the lowest price, multiplied by one hundred. The items were ranked by their percentage range, from largest to smallest, to identify the most and least seasonal items. The prices were recorded in pounds and pence, and the calculations were made to the nearest penny. No adjustment was made for quality, which is a limitation noted below, though the stallholders were asked whether the produce was local or imported, and that information was recorded.

3. Results

The basket prices for the four seasons were as follows. In winter, the twelve items cost: bread 1.20 pounds, milk 1.30, eggs 1.90, potatoes 1.00, onions 1.10, carrots 1.00, tomatoes 2.40, apples 1.80, strawberries 4.80, chicken 6.20, cheddar 2.30, and butter 2.60, giving a basket of 27.60 pounds, since the twelve prices sum to 27.60. In spring, the prices were 1.20, 1.25, 1.85, 0.95, 1.00, 0.90, 1.80, 1.60, 3.20, 6.00, 2.25, and 2.60, giving a basket of 24.60 pounds. In summer, they were 1.20, 1.20, 1.80, 0.90, 0.90, 0.80, 1.10, 1.40, 1.90, 5.80, 2.20, and 2.50, giving a basket of 21.70 pounds. In autumn, they were 1.20, 1.25, 1.85, 0.95, 1.00, 0.90, 1.60, 1.50, 2.90, 6.00, 2.25, and 2.55, giving a basket of 23.95 pounds. The mean basket price across the four seasons was 24.46 pounds, since 27.60 plus 24.60 plus 21.70 plus 23.95 equals 97.85, and 97.85 divided by 4 equals 24.46. Summer was the cheapest season, and winter the dearest, by 5.90 pounds, since 27.60 minus 21.70 equals 5.90, or 27.2 per cent, since 5.90 divided by 21.70, multiplied by one hundred, equals 27.2. The winter basket was 3.14 pounds above the mean, since 27.60 minus 24.46 equals 3.14, which is 12.8 per cent above the mean, and the summer basket was 2.76 pounds below it, since 24.46 minus 21.70 equals 2.76, which is 11.3 per cent below.

The items ranked by their percentage range across the four seasons, from largest to smallest, were as follows. Strawberries ranged from 1.90 pounds in summer to 4.80 in winter, a range of 2.90 pounds, which is 152.6 per cent of the lowest price, since 2.90 divided by 1.90, multiplied by one hundred, equals 152.6. Tomatoes ranged from 1.10 to 2.40, a range of 1.30, which is 118.2 per cent. Apples ranged from 1.40 to 1.80, a range of 0.40, which is 28.6 per cent. Carrots ranged from 0.80 to 1.00, a range of 0.20, which is 25.0 per cent. Onions ranged from 0.90 to 1.10, a range of 0.20, which is 22.2 per cent. Potatoes ranged from 0.90 to 1.00, a range of 0.10, which is 11.1 per cent. Milk ranged from 1.20 to 1.30, a range of 0.10, which is 8.3 per cent. Chicken ranged from 5.80 to 6.20, a range of 0.40, which is 6.9 per cent. Eggs ranged from 1.80 to 1.90, a range of 0.10, which is 5.6 per cent. Cheddar ranged from 2.20 to 2.30, a range of 0.10, which is 4.5 per cent. Butter ranged from 2.50 to 2.60, a range of 0.10, which is 4.0 per cent. Bread stayed at 1.20 pounds in all four seasons, a range of 0.00, or 0.0 per cent.

The contribution of each item to the winter-summer gap of 5.90 pounds shows which items drive the basket. Strawberries contributed 2.90 pounds, which is 49.2 per cent of the gap, since 2.90 divided by 5.90, multiplied by one hundred, equals 49.2. Tomatoes contributed 1.30 pounds, which is 22.0 per cent. Together, the two seasonal fruits contributed 4.20 pounds, which is 71.2 per cent of the gap, since 4.20 divided by 5.90, multiplied by one hundred, equals 71.2. Apples and chicken each contributed 0.40 pounds, or 6.8 per cent, carrots and onions 0.20 each, or 3.4 per cent, and milk, eggs, potatoes, cheddar, and butter 0.10 each, or 1.7 per cent, while bread contributed nothing. The seasonal swing in the basket is therefore driven by two items, strawberries and tomatoes, which together account for almost three-quarters of it, while the remaining ten items together account for barely a quarter. The stallholders confirmed the reason: the winter strawberries were imported from Spain and Morocco, and the winter tomatoes from the Netherlands and Spain, grown under glass, while the summer produce was grown locally in open fields, with lower costs for heating, lighting, and transport.

4. Discussion

The results confirm all three expectations. The basket was cheapest in summer, at 21.70 pounds, and dearest in winter, at 27.60, a difference of 5.90 pounds or 27.2 per cent, and the mean across the seasons was 24.46 pounds. The seasonal items varied most, with strawberries at 152.6 per cent and tomatoes at 118.2 per cent, while the staples varied least, with bread at 0.0 per cent, butter at 4.0, cheddar at 4.5, and eggs at 5.6. The two seasonal fruits together accounted for 71.2 per cent of the winter-summer gap, which shows that the seasonal swing is not a general rise in all prices but a sharp rise in a few items that are out of season. The findings agree with the theory of Smith, Jevons, and Marshall: the price follows the cost of supply, and the cost is highest when the good must be grown under glass or imported, and lowest when it grows locally in the open. They also agree with Stigler and Kindahl's finding that prices are sticky: the staples, bread, butter, and cheddar, changed by only 0.10 pounds or not at all, because their costs change little with the season, and the sellers hold their prices steady, while the seasonal items, whose costs change sharply, changed by pounds.

The comparison with the national price index puts the local result in perspective. The consumer price index for food rises by about 2 to 3 per cent over a year in normal times, and its seasonal variation is small, because it averages over the whole country, over all items, and over the year, and because it adjusts for the changing composition of the basket. The present study, by contrast, holds the basket fixed and measures it at one market, and it finds a seasonal swing of 27.2 per cent between the cheapest and dearest seasons, which is ten times the annual change in the index. The difference is not a contradiction but a difference of method: the index measures the average change in the cost of living, while the study measures the change in the price of a fixed basket at a fixed place, and the fixed basket exposes the seasonal swing that the index averages away. A household that buys the same basket every week will feel the swing directly, paying 27.60 pounds in January and 21.70 in July, a difference of 5.90 pounds a week, or about 300 pounds a year if the winter price held all year, though in practice the household adapts, buying fewer strawberries in winter and more in summer, which is exactly the substitution that Jevons and Marshall described, and which the fixed basket does not capture.

Several limitations must be stated. First, only one market and twelve items were studied, for one week per season, so the basket prices carry an uncertainty of about plus or minus 0.50 pounds, from the choice of week and from the day-to-day variation within a week, and the difference of 5.90 pounds between winter and summer is about twelve times that uncertainty, which is convincing, but the smaller differences, between spring at 24.60 and autumn at 23.95, a difference of 0.65 pounds, are close to the uncertainty and should not be overinterpreted. Second, the quality of the produce was not held constant: the winter strawberries were larger and more uniform than the summer ones, because they were grown under glass, and the winter tomatoes were firmer, so part of the price difference may reflect quality rather than season alone, which is the problem Gordon identified in his work on price measurement, and a full adjustment for quality would reduce the measured seasonal swing, though not by much, because the winter produce was not better in taste, only in appearance. Third, the prices were observed on one day per season, a Saturday morning, and prices within a week may vary, especially for perishable items that are discounted at the end of the day. Fourth, the market is a farmers' market, where prices are higher and more variable than in a supermarket, and the seasonal swing may be smaller in a supermarket, which sources globally and smooths its prices. Fifth, the study covered one year, and the seasonal pattern may differ in a year with a cold spring or a wet summer, which would change the local supply. None of these limitations changes the finding that the basket was dearest in winter and cheapest in summer by 5.90 pounds, driven by strawberries and tomatoes, which was consistent across the four seasons and confirmed by the stallholders' account of where the produce came from, but they mean that the exact figures should be read as the prices at this market in this year, not as universal values.

The practical lesson is that a household can save a substantial amount by eating seasonally. The winter basket cost 27.60 pounds and the summer basket 21.70, a difference of 5.90 pounds a week, and the two items that drove the difference, strawberries and tomatoes, cost 2.90 and 1.30 pounds more in winter than in summer. A household that buys strawberries and tomatoes only in season, and substitutes apples and carrots in winter, would save most of the 5.90 pounds, while a household that insists on strawberries in January pays for the glasshouse and the air freight. The lesson is not new, it is the lesson Adam Smith taught, that the price reflects the cost of bringing the good to market, but the numbers make it concrete: 152.6 per cent more for strawberries in winter, 118.2 per cent more for tomatoes, and 27.2 per cent more for the basket as a whole. The market, in its weekly prices, teaches the seasons, and the shopper who listens to it eats more cheaply and more locally.

The household budget impact of the seasonal swing is worth calculating, because the basket in this study is only a small part of a weekly shop, and the swing scales with the shop. The basket of twelve items cost 24.46 pounds on average across the four seasons, and a typical family spends about 100 pounds a week on food, so the basket is about a quarter of the weekly shop. If the seasonal swing of 27.2 per cent applied to the whole shop, the winter shop would cost about 27 pounds more than the summer shop, but it does not apply to the whole shop, because much of the shop, the bread, the milk, the meat, the tinned goods, varies little with the season, and only the fresh fruit and vegetables swing sharply. A better estimate is to apply the swing to the fresh produce portion, which is about a third of the shop, or 33 pounds, and the seasonal items within it, the strawberries and tomatoes and their like, are about half of that, or 16 pounds, with a swing of about 100 per cent, so the winter excess is about 8 pounds a week, or about 400 pounds a year if the household bought the same produce all year. In practice the household adapts, buying less of the expensive items in winter, so the true excess is smaller, perhaps 3 to 4 pounds a week, or 150 to 200 pounds a year, which is still a meaningful sum for a family on a tight budget, and it is the price of eating strawberries in January. The import routes explain the cost: the winter strawberries came from Spain and Morocco, by road and by sea, and the winter tomatoes from the Netherlands, grown under glass with gas heating, and the transport and the heating are the costs that the summer produce, grown in open fields a few miles from the market, does not bear. The carbon cost follows the same pattern: the winter strawberry has travelled a thousand miles and been grown with heating, while the summer strawberry has travelled ten miles and been grown by the sun, and the shopper who eats seasonally saves money and carbon together. The market, in its weekly prices, is therefore a guide to both, and the stallholder who tells the shopper where the produce came from is giving them the information they need to decide.

The history of the market adds a further dimension. The town's market was chartered in the thirteenth century, and it has operated weekly ever since, with the same rhythm of seasons that the present study measured, though the goods have changed: the medieval market sold grain, wool, and livestock, and the modern market sells strawberries in January, which the medieval market could not have done at any price. The seasonal swing measured here, 27.2 per cent, is therefore a modern phenomenon in part, because the winter produce exists only because of glasshouses and imports, and a medieval shopper would not have faced a choice between a dear winter strawberry and a cheap summer one, but between no strawberry at all and a summer one. The availability of out-of-season produce is a benefit, and the price is the cost of that benefit, and the study measures the cost precisely, so that the shopper can decide whether the benefit is worth it.

The comparison with the supermarket shows how the market differs, and why the study was done at a market rather than in a supermarket. The supermarket's prices are lower than the market's, by about 10 to 15 per cent for the same items, because the supermarket buys in bulk and sources globally, and its prices are steadier through the year, because it smooths its buying across seasons and across countries, so that the seasonal swing in a supermarket is smaller than the 27.2 per cent measured here, perhaps 10 to 15 per cent. The market's prices are higher and more variable, but the produce is fresher and more local, and the stallholder can say where it came from, which the supermarket cannot. The shopper who buys at the market therefore pays more and sees the seasons more clearly, while the shopper who buys at the supermarket pays less and sees them less, and the study's numbers are the market's numbers, not the supermarket's. The national consumer price index for food, which averages across all outlets, shows a seasonal swing of only a few per cent, because it weights the supermarket heavily, and the market's 27.2 per cent is the swing that the index hides. A household that shops at the market will feel the seasons in its budget, and a household that shops at the supermarket will feel them less, but the seasons are there in both cases, in the cost of growing and transporting the food, and the market makes them visible. The study's value is that it makes the invisible visible, by holding the basket fixed and measuring it in one place, and the reader who shops at a supermarket should not dismiss the numbers as irrelevant, but should recognise that the same forces operate there, muted by scale and by sourcing, and that the cheapest week to buy strawberries is July, wherever they are bought, and that a shopper who plans the weekly menu around the season will eat better for less.

5. Conclusion

Twelve common items were priced at a local market for one week in each season. The basket cost 27.60 pounds in winter, 24.60 in spring, 21.70 in summer, and 23.95 in autumn, with a mean of 24.46 pounds. Summer was cheapest and winter dearest, by 5.90 pounds or 27.2 per cent. Strawberries varied most, from 1.90 pounds in summer to 4.80 in winter, a range of 152.6 per cent, and tomatoes from 1.10 to 2.40, a range of 118.2 per cent, while bread did not change at all, and butter, cheddar, and eggs changed by less than 6 per cent. Strawberries and tomatoes together accounted for 71.2 per cent of the winter-summer gap. Food prices follow the seasons, driven by the cost of growing out of season and of importing, and the seasonal swing is large for fresh fruit and vegetables and small for staples. Future work should price the basket monthly rather than seasonally, adjust for quality, and compare the market with a supermarket.

References

Smith, A. (1776). An Inquiry into the Nature and Causes of the Wealth of Nations. London: W. Strahan and T. Cadell.
Jevons, W. S. (1871). The Theory of Political Economy. London: Macmillan.
Marshall, A. (1890). Principles of Economics. London: Macmillan.
Stigler, G. J., & Kindahl, J. K. (1970). The Behaviour of Industrial Prices. New York: National Bureau of Economic Research.
Gordon, R. J. (1990). The Measurement of Durable Goods Prices. Chicago: University of Chicago Press.
Office for National Statistics (2024). Consumer Price Inflation, UK. London: Office for National Statistics.

Program

baskets = {"winter": 27.60, "spring": 24.60, "summer": 21.70, "autumn": 23.95}
mean = sum(baskets.values()) / 4
gap = baskets["winter"] - baskets["summer"]
print(f"mean basket = {mean:.2f} pounds")
print(f"winter-summer gap = {gap:.2f} pounds = {gap/baskets['summer']*100:.1f}%")
print(f"strawberries {2.90/gap*100:.1f}% + tomatoes {1.30/gap*100:.1f}% = {(2.90+1.30)/gap*100:.1f}% of the gap")
