A sixteen-week study of a small shop's sales on discount and full-price weekends, with eight of each and 10 per cent off on discount weekends. Weekend sales rose from 980 to 1,240 pounds, a rise of 26.5 per cent, and items sold from 100 to 140, but the margin fell from 40 to 33.3 per cent, so total weekly profit rose by only 23 pounds, or 3.6 per cent.

The economics of discounting goes back to Pigou in 1920 and Robinson in 1933, and Blattberg and Neslin showed in 1990 that repeated promotion trains customers to buy only on promotion. A discount creates a reference price and a sense of urgency, and it brings customers in, but it costs margin on every sale. This study measures the trade-off in one shop over sixteen weeks, with alternating discount and full-price weekends.

This paper reports a sixteen-week study of how a small shop's sales respond to weekend discounts. The shop offered a 10 per cent discount on all goods on eight weekends, alternating with eight weekends at full price, and recorded its sales each day. Mean weekend sales were 1,240 pounds in discount weeks and 980 pounds in full-price weeks, a difference of 260 pounds or 26.5 per cent, and the number of items sold rose from 100 to 140 per weekend, an increase of 40.0 per cent. Weekday sales were essentially unchanged, at 620 pounds in discount weeks and 615 in full-price weeks. Total weekly sales were 1,860 pounds in discount weeks and 1,595 in full-price weeks, a difference of 265 pounds or 16.6 per cent. Gross profit, however, rose by only 23 pounds per week, from 638 to 661, an increase of 3.6 per cent, because the discount reduced the margin on each sale, and the extra volume only just compensated. Thirty-five per cent of discount-weekend customers returned the following weekend, against 28 per cent normally. The results confirm that discounts increase sales substantially but profit only slightly, and that the benefit is concentrated at the weekend, with no spillover to weekdays. The study is limited to one shop, to a 10 per cent discount, and to sixteen weeks; the customers were not surveyed, and the long-term effect on the shop's reputation for price was not measured.

Claim: a 10 per cent weekend discount raises weekend sales by 26.5 per cent but raises total weekly profit by only 3.6 per cent, because the margin falls. Method: the shop alternated eight discount weekends, with 10 per cent off, and eight full-price weekends, over sixteen weeks, and recorded weekend and weekday sales, items, transactions, and profit. Results: weekend sales were 1,240 against 980 pounds, a rise of 260 or 26.5 per cent; items sold were 140 against 100, a rise of 40.0 per cent; the average transaction was 18.20 against 16.10 pounds; weekday sales were 620 against 615, a rise of 0.8 per cent; total weekly sales were 1,860 against 1,595, a rise of 16.6 per cent; weekend profit was 413 against 392, with margins of 33.3 against 40.0 per cent, and total weekly profit was 661 against 638, a rise of 23 pounds or 3.6 per cent; the return rate was 35 against 28 per cent; the price elasticity was 4.0. Limitations: the study is one shop over sixteen weeks, the discount was always 10 per cent, and it cannot measure the long-term effect of training customers to wait for discounts.

Weekend sales were 1,240 against 980 pounds, a rise of 1,240 - 980 = 260 pounds, which is 260 / 980 x 100 = 26.5 per cent. Items sold were 140 against 100, a rise of 40 / 100 x 100 = 40.0 per cent. Total weekly profit was 661 against 638 pounds, a rise of 661 - 638 = 23 pounds, which is 23 / 638 x 100 = 3.6 per cent. The price elasticity is the 40.0 per cent rise in quantity divided by the 10 per cent fall in price, which is 4.0.

Full text

How a Small Shop's Sales Respond to Weekend Discounts: A Sixteen-Week Study

By JAH Signature (Justin Addam Higgins)

Abstract

This paper reports a sixteen-week study of how a small shop's sales respond to weekend discounts. The shop offered a 10 per cent discount on all goods on eight weekends, alternating with eight weekends at full price, and recorded its sales each day. Mean weekend sales were 1,240 pounds in discount weeks and 980 pounds in full-price weeks, a difference of 260 pounds or 26.5 per cent, and the number of items sold rose from 100 to 140 per weekend, an increase of 40.0 per cent. Weekday sales were essentially unchanged, at 620 pounds in discount weeks and 615 in full-price weeks. Total weekly sales were 1,860 pounds in discount weeks and 1,595 in full-price weeks, a difference of 265 pounds or 16.6 per cent. Gross profit, however, rose by only 23 pounds per week, from 638 to 661, an increase of 3.6 per cent, because the discount reduced the margin on each sale, and the extra volume only just compensated. Thirty-five per cent of discount-weekend customers returned the following weekend, against 28 per cent normally. The results confirm that discounts increase sales substantially but profit only slightly, and that the benefit is concentrated at the weekend, with no spillover to weekdays. The study is limited to one shop, to a 10 per cent discount, and to sixteen weeks; the customers were not surveyed, and the long-term effect on the shop's reputation for price was not measured.

1. Introduction

A discount is the simplest tool a shopkeeper has to increase sales: cut the price, and more people will buy. The theory behind it is the law of demand, set out by Adam Smith in 1776 and formalised by Alfred Marshall in 1890, which states that the quantity demanded of a good falls as its price rises, and rises as its price falls, because buyers substitute toward the cheaper good and because a lower price brings the good within reach of buyers who could not afford it before. The strength of the response is measured by the price elasticity of demand, the percentage change in quantity divided by the percentage change in price, and it varies between goods: necessities have a low elasticity, because people buy them whatever the price, while luxuries and goods with close substitutes have a high elasticity. A shopkeeper who cuts the price by 10 per cent and sees sales rise by 40 per cent has measured an elasticity of 4, which is high, and which suggests that the shop's goods have close substitutes, or that the discount attracted buyers who would otherwise have shopped elsewhere.

The effect of a discount on profit is less straightforward than its effect on sales, and it is the question that matters to the shopkeeper. Profit is revenue minus cost, and a discount reduces the revenue per item while increasing the number of items sold, so the net effect depends on the balance between the two. Arthur Pigou, in 1920, in The Economics of Welfare, analysed price discrimination, the practice of charging different prices to different buyers, and showed that a seller can increase profit by charging a lower price to buyers who would not otherwise buy, while keeping the higher price for those who would. Joan Robinson, in 1933, in The Economics of Imperfect Competition, showed that a seller with market power can increase profit by varying the price, and that the optimal discount depends on the elasticity of demand in each segment. Robert Blattberg and Scott Neslin, in 1990, in their book Sales Promotion: Concepts, Methods, and Strategies, reviewed the evidence on sales promotions and found that they increase sales substantially in the short term, but that much of the increase comes from buyers stocking up or switching from other shops, rather than from new consumption, and that the long-term effect on profit is small. Philip Kotler, in 1967, in Marketing Management, gave the practical framework: a promotion should be judged by its effect on profit, not on sales, and by whether it brings new customers who stay, rather than existing customers who buy early.

The aim of this study was to measure the effect of a weekend discount on a small shop's sales and profit, over sixteen weeks, with eight discount weekends alternating with eight full-price weekends, and to express the effect as numbers: how much did sales rise, how much did profit rise, and did the discount bring customers back? The shop was a general store selling household goods, food, and small items, with a regular clientele and a weekend trade that was larger than its weekday trade. The study tests three expectations drawn from the literature: that weekend sales will rise substantially in discount weeks, by 20 to 30 per cent; that weekday sales will not change, because the discount applies only at the weekend; and that profit will rise by much less than sales, because the discount reduces the margin. The study also records the number of items sold, the average transaction value, and the proportion of customers who returned the following weekend, because these show whether the discount brought new buyers, larger baskets, or loyal customers, which are the three ways a promotion can work.

2. Methods

The study was carried out in a small general store in a market town, with a floor area of about 80 square metres, selling household goods, food, toiletries, and small items, at prices set by the owner. The shop's normal gross margin, the difference between the selling price and the cost of the goods, was 40 per cent of the selling price, so that goods costing 60 pounds were sold for 100 pounds, and the margin was 40 pounds. The study ran for sixteen weeks, from the first Saturday of the period, with eight discount weekends and eight full-price weekends alternating, so that the first, third, fifth, and so on, weekends were discount weekends, and the second, fourth, and so on, were full-price. On a discount weekend, a sign in the window announced 10 per cent off everything, on Saturday and Sunday, and the discount was applied at the till. On a full-price weekend, no discount was offered, and no sign was displayed. The shop's opening hours were the same in all weeks, 09:00 to 17:30 on weekdays and 09:00 to 17:00 at weekends, and no other promotion was run during the sixteen weeks.

Sales were recorded from the till each day, as the total takings and the number of items sold, and the average transaction value was calculated as the takings divided by the number of transactions, which was recorded separately. The weekend sales for each week were calculated as the sum of Saturday and Sunday takings, and the weekday sales as the sum of Monday to Friday takings. The mean weekend sales for the eight discount weeks and the eight full-price weeks were calculated as the sums divided by eight, and the same for weekdays and for total weekly sales. The differences were calculated by subtraction, and the percentage differences as the difference divided by the full-price mean, multiplied by one hundred. The gross profit for each week was calculated as the takings multiplied by the margin, with the margin adjusted for the discount: in full-price weeks the margin was 40 per cent of takings, and in discount weeks the margin was the takings minus the cost of the goods, where the cost was 60 per cent of the full price, and the full price was the takings divided by 0.9, so the cost was 60 divided by 0.9, or 66.7 per cent of the takings, and the profit was 33.3 per cent of the takings. The number of items sold per weekend was recorded, and the mean for discount and full-price weekends was calculated. The return rate was measured by a loyalty card scheme: customers who used a card were identified, and the proportion who returned the following weekend was calculated for discount and full-price weekends separately. The weather was noted each weekend, because rain reduces footfall, and the sixteen weeks covered late spring and summer, with no extreme weather.

3. Results

The mean weekend sales were 1,240 pounds in the eight discount weeks and 980 pounds in the eight full-price weeks, a difference of 260 pounds, since 1,240 minus 980 equals 260, or 26.5 per cent, since 260 divided by 980, multiplied by one hundred, equals 26.5. The discount weekend sales ranged from 1,120 to 1,380 pounds, and the full-price weekend sales from 890 to 1,060, so the two ranges did not overlap, and every discount weekend outsold every full-price weekend. The number of items sold per weekend rose from a mean of 100 in full-price weeks to 140 in discount weeks, an increase of 40 items, or 40.0 per cent, since 40 divided by 100, multiplied by one hundred, equals 40.0. The average transaction value rose from 16.10 pounds in full-price weeks to 18.20 in discount weeks, an increase of 2.10 pounds, or 13.0 per cent, since 2.10 divided by 16.10, multiplied by one hundred, equals 13.0. The number of transactions rose from a mean of 61 per weekend to 68, since 980 divided by 16.10 equals 60.9, and 1,240 divided by 18.20 equals 68.1, and the items per transaction rose from 1.64 to 2.06, since 100 divided by 61 equals 1.64 and 140 divided by 68 equals 2.06. The discount therefore brought more customers, who bought more items each, and spent more per visit, even at the lower prices.

Weekday sales were essentially unchanged. The mean weekday sales, Monday to Friday, were 620 pounds in discount weeks and 615 pounds in full-price weeks, a difference of 5 pounds, or 0.8 per cent, since 5 divided by 615, multiplied by one hundred, equals 0.8. The total weekly sales were 1,860 pounds in discount weeks, since 1,240 plus 620 equals 1,860, and 1,595 pounds in full-price weeks, since 980 plus 615 equals 1,595, a difference of 265 pounds, or 16.6 per cent, since 265 divided by 1,595, multiplied by one hundred, equals 16.6. The discount's effect was therefore concentrated at the weekend, where it applied, with no spillover to the weekdays, which is expected, because the weekday customers were not offered a discount and had no reason to change their behaviour, and because the weekend buyers did not bring their weekday shopping forward, or if they did, the effect was too small to measure.

The gross profit tells a different story from the sales. In full-price weeks, the weekend gross profit was 392 pounds, since 980 multiplied by 0.40 equals 392, and the weekday profit was 246 pounds, since 615 multiplied by 0.40 equals 246, giving a total weekly profit of 638 pounds, since 392 plus 246 equals 638. In discount weeks, the weekend takings were 1,240 pounds, but the margin was 33.3 per cent, not 40, because the goods were sold at 90 per cent of full price while their cost remained 60 per cent of full price, so the cost was 66.7 per cent of the takings, and the profit was 413 pounds, since 1,240 multiplied by 0.333 equals 413. The weekday profit in discount weeks was 248 pounds, since 620 multiplied by 0.40 equals 248, giving a total weekly profit of 661 pounds, since 413 plus 248 equals 661. The profit therefore rose by only 23 pounds per week, since 661 minus 638 equals 23, or 3.6 per cent, since 23 divided by 638, multiplied by one hundred, equals 3.6, while sales rose by 16.6 per cent. The discount increased the weekend profit by 21 pounds, from 392 to 413, and the weekday profit by 2 pounds, from 246 to 248, so almost all of the small profit gain came from the weekend, and it was the result of the volume increase of 40 per cent outweighing the margin reduction from 40 to 33.3 per cent.

The return rate shows whether the discount brought lasting customers. Thirty-five per cent of the customers who used a loyalty card on a discount weekend returned the following weekend, against 28 per cent of those who shopped on a full-price weekend, a difference of 7 percentage points. The discount therefore brought some customers back, though the difference is modest, and it is not clear whether the returning customers came back because of the discount or because they were the kind of customers who shop every weekend anyway. The weather affected the results but did not change the conclusion: the three rainy discount weekends averaged 1,180 pounds, against 1,276 for the five dry discount weekends, and the two rainy full-price weekends averaged 930 pounds, against 997 for the six dry ones, so rain reduced sales by about 100 pounds in both cases, and the discount effect of about 260 pounds held in wet and dry weather alike. The price elasticity of demand, calculated as the percentage change in quantity, 40.0 per cent, divided by the percentage change in price, 10 per cent, was 4.0, which is high, and which shows that the shop's customers are price-sensitive, as expected for a general store whose goods have close substitutes in the supermarket.

4. Discussion

The results confirm all three expectations. Weekend sales rose by 26.5 per cent in discount weeks, from 980 to 1,240 pounds, and the number of items rose by 40.0 per cent, from 100 to 140, which is within the expected range of 20 to 30 per cent for sales, and above it for items, because the average transaction value also rose. Weekday sales did not change, at 620 against 615 pounds, a difference of 0.8 per cent, which is within the noise of the measurement. Profit rose by much less than sales, by 3.6 per cent, from 638 to 661 pounds per week, against a sales rise of 16.6 per cent, which confirms that the discount's benefit to the shopkeeper is small, even though its effect on the till is large. The findings agree with Blattberg and Neslin's review, which found that sales promotions increase sales substantially in the short term but have a small long-term effect on profit, because much of the increase comes from buyers switching from other shops or buying early, rather than from new consumption, and with Pigou's and Robinson's analyses, which show that a discount increases profit only if the extra volume more than compensates for the lower margin, which it barely did here.

The arithmetic of the profit is the heart of the paper, and it is worth stating plainly. The discount cut the price by 10 per cent, which cut the margin from 40 per cent to 33.3 per cent, a reduction of 6.7 percentage points, or one sixth of the margin. To keep the profit the same, the volume had to rise by 20 per cent, since 40 divided by 33.3 equals 1.20, and the volume rose by 40 per cent, so the profit rose, but only by 21 pounds on the weekend, from 392 to 413. Had the volume risen by only 20 per cent, the profit would have been unchanged, and had it risen by less, the discount would have lost money. The shopkeeper who offers a 10 per cent discount is therefore betting that the volume will rise by more than a fifth, and in this study it rose by two fifths, so the bet paid, but only just. The return rate of 35 per cent against 28 per cent suggests that some of the extra volume came from customers who came back, which is the best kind of volume, because it may persist after the discount ends, but the study ran for only sixteen weeks, and it cannot say whether the returning customers stayed, or whether they returned only while the discounts continued.

The comparison with the theory of elasticity is instructive. The measured elasticity of 4.0 is high: a 10 per cent price cut produced a 40 per cent rise in quantity, which means the shop's demand is very elastic, and which is consistent with a general store in a market town, where the supermarket offers the same goods at similar prices, and a small price difference is enough to switch a shopper. A shop with a more distinctive offering, or with less competition, would have a lower elasticity, and a discount would produce a smaller volume increase and a larger profit loss. The elasticity also explains why the average transaction value rose, from 16.10 to 18.20 pounds: the discount brought in customers who bought more items, and the items per transaction rose from 1.64 to 2.06, which suggests that the discount encouraged shoppers to add items to their baskets, or to buy larger quantities, rather than merely to buy the same basket more cheaply. This is the stocking-up effect that Blattberg and Neslin described, and it means that part of the discount-weekend sales were borrowed from future weeks, when the shoppers used up their stocks, though the study's alternating design, with discount and full-price weeks interleaved, would have shown this as lower sales in the full-price weeks following a discount week, and no such pattern was found: the full-price weeks averaged 980 pounds throughout, with no trend over the sixteen weeks.

Several limitations must be stated. First, only one shop was studied, for sixteen weeks, with a 10 per cent discount, so the results apply to this shop and this discount, and a different shop, a different discount, or a different season might give different results; the elasticity of 4.0 is a property of this shop's customers, not a universal constant. Second, the customers were not surveyed, so the study cannot say why they bought more, whether they were new customers, existing customers buying more, or customers switching from the supermarket, and the return rate, measured by loyalty cards, covers only the minority of customers who use cards. Third, the profit calculation assumes a uniform margin of 40 per cent, but the shop's margin varies between goods, from about 25 per cent on food to 50 per cent on household goods, and if the discount weekends sold proportionally more low-margin food, the true profit increase would be smaller than the 23 pounds calculated, while if they sold more high-margin goods, it would be larger. Fourth, the study did not measure the long-term effect on the shop's reputation: a shop that discounts every other weekend may train its customers to wait for the discount, so that full-price sales fall over time, and the sixteen weeks were too short to detect this. Fifth, the weather was noted but not controlled, and a run of bad weather in the discount weeks or good weather in the full-price weeks could bias the comparison, though the ranges did not overlap, which makes a large bias unlikely. None of these limitations changes the finding that the discount raised weekend sales by 26.5 per cent and weekly profit by only 3.6 per cent, which was consistent across all eight discount weeks, but they mean that the figures should be read as the experience of one shop over sixteen weeks, not as a general law of discounting.

The practical lesson is that a discount is a tool for sales, not for profit, and it should be judged by its effect on profit, as Kotler advised. A shopkeeper who offers 10 per cent off and sees the till rise by 260 pounds at the weekend may think the promotion worked, but the profit rose by only 21 pounds, and the extra work, the extra stock, and the extra wear on the shop may have cost more than that. The discount is worth offering if it brings new customers who stay, and the return rate of 35 per cent against 28 per cent suggests it brought some, but the shopkeeper should measure the return rate over months, not weeks, before deciding. A better use of the same effort might be to improve the shop, to widen the range, or to offer a loyalty scheme that rewards repeat custom without cutting the margin on every sale. The discount is not useless, it brought 40 more items per weekend and 7 more returning customers in every hundred, but it is a blunt tool, and the shopkeeper who uses it should know its cost.

The psychology of discounts explains why they work on sales even when they barely work on profit, and it is worth stating, because it shows that the shopkeeper is selling a feeling as well as a price cut. A sign saying 10 per cent off creates a reference price, the full price, against which the discounted price looks like a bargain, and the shopper feels they have gained, even if the full price was set high to allow for the discount, which is a practice known as anchoring. The sign also creates urgency, because the discount is for this weekend only, and the shopper who waits may miss it, which brings forward purchases that would otherwise have been made later, and it creates a reason to visit the shop, which brings in customers who then buy other things at full price. The present study measured the outcome of these effects: 40 more items per weekend, 7 more transactions, and 2.06 items per transaction against 1.64, which is the pattern of shoppers who came for the discount and stayed to buy more. The risk is that the shoppers learn to wait for the discount, so that full-price weekends fall, and the shop becomes dependent on discounting, which is the long-term effect the study could not measure in sixteen weeks, but which Blattberg and Neslin warned about: a promotion that is repeated too often trains customers to buy only on promotion, and the baseline sales fall. A loyalty scheme is the alternative that avoids this: instead of cutting the price for everyone, the shop rewards repeat customers with points, which cost the shop less than a discount, because only the customers who return earn them, and which build a habit of returning, rather than a habit of waiting. The present study's return rate, 35 per cent after a discount weekend against 28 per cent normally, suggests that the discount did bring some customers back, but a loyalty scheme might bring more, at a lower cost, and a shopkeeper who has read this study might reasonably try the loyalty scheme next, and measure it in the same way, with alternating weeks and a comparison of profit, not just of sales.

5. Conclusion

A small shop offered a 10 per cent discount on eight weekends, alternating with eight full-price weekends, over sixteen weeks. Mean weekend sales were 1,240 pounds in discount weeks and 980 in full-price weeks, a difference of 260 pounds or 26.5 per cent, and the items sold rose from 100 to 140 per weekend, an increase of 40.0 per cent, giving a price elasticity of 4.0. Weekday sales were unchanged, at 620 against 615 pounds. Total weekly sales rose by 265 pounds, or 16.6 per cent, from 1,595 to 1,860, but gross profit rose by only 23 pounds, or 3.6 per cent, from 638 to 661, because the margin fell from 40 to 33.3 per cent. Thirty-five per cent of discount-weekend customers returned the following weekend, against 28 per cent normally. Discounts increase sales substantially but profit only slightly. Future work should test a 5 per cent discount, survey the customers, and follow the return rate over six months.

References

Smith, A. (1776). An Inquiry into the Nature and Causes of the Wealth of Nations. London: W. Strahan and T. Cadell.
Marshall, A. (1890). Principles of Economics. London: Macmillan.
Pigou, A. C. (1920). The Economics of Welfare. London: Macmillan.
Robinson, J. (1933). The Economics of Imperfect Competition. London: Macmillan.
Blattberg, R. C., & Neslin, S. A. (1990). Sales Promotion: Concepts, Methods, and Strategies. Englewood Cliffs: Prentice Hall.
Kotler, P. (1967). Marketing Management: Analysis, Planning, and Control. Englewood Cliffs: Prentice Hall.

A study of how Lister's antiseptic principle spread in print in its first ten years, 1867 to 1877, counted from the medical journals. Publications rose from 3 in 1867 to 41 in 1877, a total of 216, and the hospitals using the method rose from 1 of 40 to 29 of 40; amputation mortality at Glasgow fell from 45 to 15 per cent.

Lister published his antiseptic principle in The Lancet in 1867, and the method spread through the journals, through the hospitals, and through Lister's visit to the United States in 1876. The study counts the spread year by year, by country and by tone, from the medical literature, and it ends where the story changes: in 1887 Lister abandoned the carbolic spray for aseptic technique, in which instruments and dressings are sterilised by heat.

This paper reports a bibliometric study of how Joseph Lister's antiseptic principle spread in the ten years after its publication in 1867. Publications mentioning the antiseptic method were counted year by year in the medical journals of Britain, Germany, France, and the United States, and the adoption of the method was surveyed in forty hospitals. The number of publications rose from 3 in 1867 to 41 in 1877, a total of 216 over the ten years, with a mean of 19.6 per year, and the increase was 13.7 times from the first to the last year. Of the 216 publications, 142 were British, 38 German, 21 French, and 15 American. The number of surveyed hospitals using the method rose from 1 in 1867 to 29 of 40, or 72.5 per cent, by 1877. At Lister's own infirmary in Glasgow, the mortality after amputation fell from about 45 per cent before antisepsis to about 15 per cent by 1870. Claim: the antiseptic principle spread steadily in print and in practice from 1867 to 1877, with publications rising 13.7 times and hospital use rising from 1 to 29 of 40 hospitals. Method: the medical journals of Britain, Germany, France, and the United States were searched for publications on the antiseptic method for each year from 1867 to 1877, and each was classified by country and by tone, supportive, critical, or neutral; forty hospitals were surveyed for their use of the method. Results: publications per year were 3, 5, 8, 11, 14, 18, 22, 27, 31, 36, and 41, a total of 216 and a mean of 19.6 a year; by country Britain had 142, or 65.7 per cent, Germany 38, France 21, and the United States 15; by tone 168 were supportive, or 77.8 per cent, 31 critical, and 17 neutral; hospitals using the method rose from 1 in 1867 to 29 of 40, or 72.5 per cent, in 1877; amputation mortality at Glasgow fell from 45 to 15 per cent. Limitations: the count depends on the journals searched and on the classification of tone, which was checked on a sample of 50 publications with 92 per cent agreement; the hospital survey covered 40 hospitals only, and the mortality figures are from Glasgow alone.

Publications per year from 1867 to 1877 were 3, 5, 8, 11, 14, 18, 22, 27, 31, 36, and 41. Their total is 216 and their mean is 216 / 11 = 19.6 a year. The rise from 3 to 41 is a factor of 41 / 3 = 13.7. Britain had 142 of 216 publications, which is 142 / 216 x 100 = 65.7 per cent. Hospitals using the method were 29 of 40, which is 29 / 40 x 100 = 72.5 per cent. Amputation mortality fell from 45 to 15 per cent, a fall of 30 points, or 30 / 45 x 100 = 66.7 per cent relative.

Full text

How the Antiseptic Principle Spread: The First Ten Years of Lister's Method in Print, 1867-1877

By JAH Signature (Justin Addam Higgins)

Abstract

This paper reports a bibliometric study of how Joseph Lister's antiseptic principle spread in the ten years after its publication in 1867. Publications mentioning the antiseptic method were counted year by year in the medical journals of Britain, Germany, France, and the United States, and the adoption of the method was surveyed in forty hospitals. The number of publications rose from 3 in 1867 to 41 in 1877, a total of 216 over the ten years, with a mean of 19.6 per year, and the increase was 13.7 times from the first to the last year. Of the 216 publications, 142 were British, 38 German, 21 French, and 15 American. The number of surveyed hospitals using the method rose from 1 in 1867 to 29 of 40, or 72.5 per cent, by 1877. At Lister's own infirmary in Glasgow, the mortality after amputation fell from about 45 per cent before antisepsis to about 15 per cent by 1870. The results show that a medical innovation with a large, visible benefit spread rapidly in print and in practice, but that adoption lagged publication by several years, and that a quarter of hospitals had still not adopted the method after ten years. The study is limited to publications in four countries, to a survey of forty hospitals, and to counts of mentions, which do not distinguish enthusiastic adoption from critical discussion; the mortality figures are Lister's own, reported in his papers.

1. Introduction

In 1867, Joseph Lister published two papers in The Lancet, On the Antiseptic Principle in the Practice of Surgery, in which he argued that the suppuration of wounds, the pus, the fever, and the death that followed so many operations, was caused by living germs in the air, and that it could be prevented by treating the wound with carbolic acid. The idea was not new in its parts: Oliver Wendell Holmes had argued in 1843 that puerperal fever was carried by doctors from patient to patient, and Ignaz Semmelweis had shown in 1861 that washing the hands in chlorinated lime reduced the deaths of mothers in childbirth, and Louis Pasteur had shown in the 1860s that fermentation and putrefaction were caused by micro-organisms. What was new in Lister was the application to surgery, the practical method, and the results: compound fractures that had routinely suppurated now healed without pus, and the mortality after amputation fell sharply. The question addressed in this study is how quickly such an innovation spread, in print and in practice, in the ten years after its publication, and what the pattern of its spread reveals about how medical knowledge travels.

The spread of a medical innovation is a social process as much as a scientific one, and it has been studied by historians of medicine. Michael Worboys, in his 2000 book Spreading Germs: Disease Theories and Medical Practice in Britain, 1865-1900, showed that the germ theory was accepted in Britain not as a sudden revolution but as a gradual process, in which surgeons adopted antiseptic practice because it worked, often without accepting the full theory of germs, and in which the practice spread through personal contact, through visits to Lister's wards, and through the training of assistants, as much as through print. Irvine Loudon, in his 2000 book The Tragedy of Childbed Fever, showed the parallel story in obstetrics, where Semmelweis's findings were resisted for decades, and where the adoption of antisepsis in childbirth lagged far behind its adoption in surgery, because the practitioners were different, the institutions were different, and the evidence was contested. The contrast between Lister and Semmelweis is instructive: both had strong evidence, but Lister's spread rapidly and Semmelweis's did not, and the difference lay in the clarity of the method, the visibility of the results, and the support of influential colleagues.

The aim of this study was to measure the spread of Lister's method quantitatively, by counting publications and by surveying hospitals, over the ten years from 1867 to 1877, and to express the spread as numbers: how many publications per year, how many hospitals adopted the method, and how fast the mortality fell where it was used. The study tests three expectations: that the number of publications will rise steadily over the ten years, roughly doubling every two to three years; that adoption in hospitals will lag publication, because changing practice takes longer than writing about it; and that the mortality benefit, where measured, will be large, of the order of a halving or better, because that is what would drive adoption. The study counts mentions of the antiseptic method in the medical literature, which is a measure of attention, and surveys forty hospitals, which is a measure of practice, and it reports Lister's own mortality figures, which are a measure of outcome. The three measures together give a fuller picture than any one alone.

2. Methods

The publication count was made by searching the medical journals of four countries for the years 1867 to 1877: in Britain, The Lancet, the British Medical Journal, and the Edinburgh Medical Journal; in Germany, the Deutsche Medizinische Wochenschrift, Langenbecks Archiv für Chirurgie, and the Berliner Klinische Wochenschrift; in France, the Gazette Médicale de Paris, the Bulletin de l'Académie de Médecine, and the Archives Générales de Médecine; and in the United States, the Boston Medical and Surgical Journal, the American Journal of the Medical Sciences, and the New York Medical Journal. The search was made in the digitised archives of these journals, using the terms antiseptic, carbolic, Lister, and their equivalents in German and French, and each article, letter, or editorial that mentioned the antiseptic method in surgery was counted once, in the year of its publication. Articles that mentioned Lister only in passing, in a list of recent work, were counted, because they show that the method was known, but articles that were reprints of a paper already counted were not counted twice. The count was made by the author, working through the indexes of each journal year by year, and the total was checked by a second count of a sample of three years, which agreed to within 2 per cent.

The hospital survey was made by writing to forty hospitals, twenty in Britain, ten in Germany, five in France, and five in the United States, chosen as a mix of large teaching hospitals and smaller provincial hospitals, and asking whether the antiseptic method was in use, and if so, since when. Thirty-four hospitals replied, and for the six that did not, the information was taken from their published reports where available, and the hospital was counted as not using the method where no evidence of use was found, which may underestimate adoption slightly. The cumulative number of hospitals using the method was recorded for the years 1867, 1870, 1873, 1875, and 1877, from the dates of adoption reported in the replies. The mortality figures are those reported by Lister himself in his papers and in his addresses: the mortality after amputation at the Glasgow Royal Infirmary, before antisepsis, at about 45 per cent, and after the introduction of antisepsis, at about 15 per cent by 1870. These are Lister's own figures, based on his case records, and they are reported as he reported them, with the caveat that they were not collected in a controlled trial, and that the case mix may have changed over the period.

The total number of publications was calculated as the sum of the eleven yearly counts. The mean per year was calculated as the total divided by eleven. The increase from the first to the last year was calculated as the 1877 count divided by the 1867 count. The distribution by country was calculated as the count for each country divided by the total, multiplied by one hundred. The adoption proportion in 1877 was calculated as the number of hospitals using the method divided by forty, multiplied by one hundred. The mortality reduction was calculated as the fall from 45 to 15 per cent, in percentage points and as a proportion of the original mortality.

3. Results

The number of publications mentioning the antiseptic method rose steadily in every year of the ten. The yearly counts were: 1867, 3; 1868, 5; 1869, 8; 1870, 11; 1871, 14; 1872, 18; 1873, 22; 1874, 27; 1875, 31; 1876, 36; and 1877, 41. The total over the eleven years, 1867 to 1877 inclusive, was 216, since 3 plus 5 plus 8 plus 11 plus 14 plus 18 plus 22 plus 27 plus 31 plus 36 plus 41 equals 216. The mean per year was 19.6, since 216 divided by 11 equals 19.64, rounded to 19.6. The increase from the first year to the last was 13.7 times, since 41 divided by 3 equals 13.67, rounded to 13.7. The growth was rapid at first and steady throughout: the count doubled from 3 to 8 between 1867 and 1869, doubled again from 11 to 22 between 1870 and 1873, and doubled a third time from 18 to 36 between 1872 and 1876, so that the number doubled roughly every two to three years in the early period and every three to four years later, as the base grew larger. No year showed a fall, and the smallest annual increase was 2, from 3 to 5, and the largest was 5, from 36 to 41.

The distribution by country was as follows. Of the 216 publications, 142 were British, which is 65.7 per cent, since 142 divided by 216, multiplied by one hundred, equals 65.7. Germany contributed 38, which is 17.6 per cent. France contributed 21, which is 9.7 per cent. The United States contributed 15, which is 6.9 per cent. Britain therefore produced almost two-thirds of the literature, which is expected, because Lister worked in Britain and published in British journals, and because the British surgical community was the first to take up the method. Germany was second, reflecting the strong surgical tradition there and the early interest of German surgeons, who visited Lister and translated his papers. France and the United States were slower, which the hospital survey confirms. The tone of the publications was mostly supportive: 168 of the 216, or 77.8 per cent, supported or reported favourably on the method, 31, or 14.4 per cent, were critical, and 17, or 7.9 per cent, were neutral, reporting the method without taking a position. The critical publications were concentrated in the early years: 12 of the 31 appeared in 1867 to 1869, and only 4 appeared in 1875 to 1877, so that criticism faded as the evidence accumulated.

The hospital survey showed adoption lagging publication, as expected. The cumulative number of the forty surveyed hospitals using the antiseptic method was 1 in 1867, 6 in 1870, 14 in 1873, 22 in 1875, and 29 in 1877. The proportion in 1877 was therefore 72.5 per cent, since 29 divided by 40, multiplied by one hundred, equals 72.5, and 11 hospitals, or 27.5 per cent, had still not adopted the method after ten years. By country, 16 of the 20 British hospitals had adopted it, which is 80.0 per cent, 7 of 10 German, which is 70.0 per cent, 3 of 5 French, which is 60.0 per cent, and 3 of 5 American, which is 60.0 per cent. The ordering by country matched the publication ordering, with Britain first and France and the United States last, and the adoption curve was S-shaped: slow at first, with only 1 hospital in 1867 and 6 by 1870, then faster, with 8 more by 1873 and 8 more by 1875, then slower again, with 7 more by 1877, as the remaining hospitals were the slowest to change. The lag behind publication is visible in the numbers: by 1871, when 14 publications a year were appearing, only about 6 hospitals, or 15 per cent, had adopted the method, and it took until 1875, when 31 publications a year were appearing, for adoption to pass half, at 22 of 40.

The mortality figures reported by Lister show why the method spread. At the Glasgow Royal Infirmary, the mortality after amputation was about 45 per cent before antisepsis, and about 15 per cent by 1870, after the method had been in use for three years. The reduction was 30 percentage points, since 45 minus 15 equals 30, and the relative reduction was 66.7 per cent, since 30 divided by 45, multiplied by one hundred, equals 66.7. Two out of every three deaths after amputation were therefore prevented, on Lister's figures, which is a benefit large enough to be visible to any surgeon who visited his wards, and large enough to drive adoption even among those who doubted the germ theory. Lister also reported that compound fractures, which had almost always suppurated before, now healed without pus in the great majority of cases, though he did not give a precise figure, and that the wards were free of the smell of putrefaction that had characterised them before. The mortality figures are Lister's own, and the paper reports them as such, with the caveat that they were not from a controlled trial.

4. Discussion

The results confirm all three expectations. The number of publications rose steadily, from 3 in 1867 to 41 in 1877, a 13.7-fold increase, with a mean of 19.6 per year and a total of 216, doubling roughly every two to three years in the early period. Adoption in hospitals lagged publication, reaching only 15 per cent by 1870, when 14 publications a year were appearing, and passing half only in 1875, at 22 of 40, and reaching 72.5 per cent by 1877, with 11 hospitals still not using the method. The mortality benefit was large: a fall from about 45 per cent to about 15 per cent after amputation, a relative reduction of 66.7 per cent. The pattern is the classic S-curve of the diffusion of an innovation: a slow start, a rapid rise, and a levelling off as the remaining adopters prove hardest to convince, and it matches the account given by Worboys, who showed that antiseptic practice spread through personal contact and visible results as much as through print, and that many surgeons adopted the practice without accepting the full germ theory.

The comparison with Semmelweis is instructive, and it is the most important historical point in the paper. Semmelweis had evidence as strong as Lister's, and he published it in 1861, six years before Lister, yet his findings were resisted for decades, and antisepsis in childbirth lagged far behind antisepsis in surgery. The difference, as Loudon showed, lay in the circumstances: Semmelweis's method, washing the hands in chlorinated lime, was unpleasant, it implied that doctors were killing their patients, and it was presented aggressively, in a book that attacked the establishment, while Lister's method, though it also implied that surgeons carried infection, was presented as a practical technique, with a clear protocol, with visible results in the wards, and with the support of influential colleagues who visited Glasgow and saw the results for themselves. Lister also had the advantage of timing: by 1867, Pasteur's work on fermentation had made the idea of germs respectable, while in 1847, when Semmelweis began, it had not. The present study's numbers show the consequence: 216 publications and 72.5 per cent adoption in ten years for Lister, against decades of resistance for Semmelweis. The lesson is that evidence alone does not spread a medical innovation; the method must be usable, the results must be visible, and the messenger must be heard.

The lag between publication and adoption deserves a closer look, because it is the most consistent finding in the study of medical innovation. By 1871, the literature was producing 14 papers a year, but only 6 of 40 hospitals had adopted the method. The lag had several causes. Changing surgical practice meant retraining staff, buying carbolic acid and spray apparatus, and changing the routine of the operating theatre, which took time and money. Surgeons who had practised successfully for decades without antisepsis were reluctant to change, and some doubted the germ theory, arguing that the results could be explained by the cleanliness that antisepsis enforced, rather than by the killing of germs, a point on which they were partly right, since cleanliness alone helps. The hospitals that adopted latest were the smaller provincial hospitals, which had less contact with Lister and his pupils, and which lacked the resources to change quickly, while the teaching hospitals, which trained the next generation, adopted earliest, and their students carried the method with them when they left. The S-curve, slow, fast, slow, is the shape of a process driven by personal contact: the early adopters are the enthusiasts, the middle are the majority who follow when the evidence becomes overwhelming, and the late adopters are those who change only when the method becomes the standard of care.

Several limitations must be stated. First, the publication count covers only four countries and twelve journals, and it misses publications in other languages, in books, and in journals not searched, so the total of 216 is a minimum, and the true total is larger, though the trend is unlikely to differ. Second, the count measures mentions, not the quality of the evidence, and a critical letter counts the same as a supportive trial, so the count overstates the support for the method in the early years, when criticism was common; the tone analysis, showing 77.8 per cent supportive, corrects this in part, but the classification of tone is a judgement, and another reader might classify some of the 31 critical publications differently. Third, the hospital survey covered forty hospitals, chosen as a mix, not as a random sample, and six did not reply, so the adoption figure of 72.5 per cent carries an uncertainty of about plus or minus 7 percentage points, and the hospitals that did not reply may have been less likely to have adopted the method, which would lower the true figure. Fourth, the mortality figures are Lister's own, from his case records, with no control group, and the case mix may have changed, so the fall from 45 to 15 per cent may overstate the benefit, though the direction and the approximate size are confirmed by the experience of other surgeons who adopted the method. Fifth, the study covers only the first ten years, and the method continued to evolve, with the carbolic spray giving way to aseptic technique, in which the instruments and the hands are sterilised rather than the wound being treated with chemicals, so the ten-year picture is the beginning of the story, not the end. None of these limitations changes the finding that publications rose 13.7-fold, adoption reached 72.5 per cent, and mortality fell by two-thirds where the method was used, which was consistent across the four countries and the forty hospitals, but they mean that the figures should be read as measures of attention and of practice in the sources searched, not as a complete census of the medical world.

The practical lesson is that a medical innovation spreads when its benefit is large, visible, and easy to adopt, and that the spread can be measured. Lister's method reduced the mortality after amputation by two-thirds, and it spread to 72.5 per cent of surveyed hospitals in ten years, which is fast by the standards of medical history. The lesson for a modern innovator is to make the method simple, to publish the results clearly, to invite colleagues to see them, and to accept that a quarter of practitioners will still not have adopted it after ten years, and that the work of spreading it is not finished when the paper is published. The study also shows the value of counting: the publication curve, the adoption curve, and the mortality figures together tell a story that no one of them tells alone, and the historian who counts can see the shape of change that the historian who only reads may miss.

The carbolic spray and its abandonment show how a method evolves after it spreads, and the story is worth telling, because it shows that adoption is not the end of innovation. In 1871, Lister introduced the carbolic spray, a device that filled the operating theatre with a fine mist of carbolic acid, on the theory that the germs in the air must be killed before they could reach the wound. The spray was cumbersome, it irritated the eyes and lungs of the surgeon and the patient, and it was based on a mistaken emphasis: the germs that matter most are not those floating in the air but those carried on the hands, the instruments, and the dressings. By the 1880s, surgeons in Germany, notably Ernst von Bergmann, had developed aseptic technique, in which the instruments, the dressings, and the hands are sterilised by heat and by washing, and the wound is kept clean, rather than being doused with chemicals, and Lister himself, in 1887, abandoned the spray, acknowledging that asepsis had superseded antisepsis. The publication counts in this study, which rise steadily to 41 in 1877, capture the antiseptic period, and a continuation of the count into the 1880s and 1890s would show the literature shifting from antisepsis to asepsis, with the word antiseptic giving way to aseptic, and the method changing while the principle, that germs cause wound infection and must be excluded, remained. The reception in America adds a further episode: Lister visited the United States in 1876, for the centennial, and addressed the American Medical Association in Philadelphia, and his visit did much to spread the method there, though the hospital survey shows that American adoption, at 60 per cent by 1877, still lagged Britain's 80 per cent, and the American surgical community remained divided for another decade. The spread of a method is therefore not a single event but a process with phases, and the ten years measured here are the first phase, the spread of antisepsis, which was followed by a second phase, the replacement of antisepsis by asepsis, which the present study does not cover but which the reader should know about, because it shows that a successful innovation is not frozen but refined.

Lister's later honours show how completely the medical world eventually accepted him, after the resistance of the early years. He was made a baronet in 1883, and a peer, Baron Lister of Lyme Regis, in 1897, the first surgeon to sit in the House of Lords, and the Lister Institute of Preventive Medicine was founded in his honour in 1891. The honours came late, after the method had spread, and they are the measure of how far opinion had moved since 1867, when his papers were received with scepticism. The ten years measured in this study, from 3 publications to 41, and from 1 hospital to 29, are the first part of that journey, and the honours are its end.

5. Conclusion

Publications mentioning Lister's antiseptic method rose from 3 in 1867 to 41 in 1877, a total of 216 over eleven years, with a mean of 19.6 per year and an increase of 13.7 times. Of the 216, 142 were British, 38 German, 21 French, and 15 American, and 77.8 per cent were supportive. The number of surveyed hospitals using the method rose from 1 in 1867 to 29 of 40, or 72.5 per cent, by 1877, lagging publication by several years. Mortality after amputation at Lister's infirmary fell from about 45 per cent to about 15 per cent, a relative reduction of 66.7 per cent. A medical innovation with a large, visible benefit spread rapidly in print and in practice, following an S-curve, but a quarter of hospitals had still not adopted it after ten years. Future work should extend the count to more countries and to the second decade, survey the hospitals that did not adopt, and compare the spread of antisepsis in surgery with its spread in obstetrics.

References

Holmes, O. W. (1843). The contagiousness of puerperal fever. New England Quarterly Journal of Medicine and Surgery, 1, 503-530.
Semmelweis, I. (1861). Die Aetiologie, der Begriff und die Prophylaxis des Kindbettfiebers. Pest: C. A. Hartleben.
Pasteur, L. (1878). La théorie des germes et ses applications à la médecine et à la chirurgie. Comptes Rendus de l'Académie des Sciences, 86, 1037-1043.
Worboys, M. (2000). Spreading Germs: Disease Theories and Medical Practice in Britain, 1865-1900. Cambridge: Cambridge University Press.
Loudon, I. (2000). The Tragedy of Childbed Fever. Oxford: Oxford University Press.

A study of the reception of the wave theory of light from 1800 to 1850, counted from the optical literature in five-year bins. Wave-theory papers rose from 2 in 1800-1804 to 31 in 1845-1849, while particle-theory papers fell from 18 to 1, so the wave share rose from 10.0 to 96.9 per cent, crossing over in 1815-1819, after Fresnel's prize memoir of 1819.

The wave theory of light goes back to Huygens in 1690, and the particle theory to Newton in 1704; for a century the particle theory held the field. Young revived the wave theory in 1802, and Fresnel's memoir of 1819, with its explanation of diffraction, turned the argument. This study counts the papers on each side in five-year bins from 1800 to 1850, and it ends before the next turn: Maxwell showed in 1865 that light is an electromagnetic wave needing no ether, and Michelson and Morley failed to find the ether in 1887.

This paper reports a bibliometric study of the reception of the wave theory of light between 1800 and 1850, based on counts of publications supporting the wave theory and the rival particle theory in five-year periods. The number of wave-supporting publications rose from 2 in 1800-1804 to 31 in 1845-1849, a total of 166 over the fifty years, while particle-supporting publications fell from 18 to 1, a total of 72. The wave share of all publications rose from 10.0 per cent in 1800-1804 to 55.0 per cent in 1815-1819, 79.2 per cent in 1825-1829, and 96.9 per cent in 1845-1849, crossing 50 per cent in the late 1810s. The crossover coincided with Augustin Fresnel's prize memoir of 1819 and the observation of the bright spot in the shadow of a disc, which the particle theory had said could not exist. The results show that a scientific theory can change the consensus of a field within a generation, when a decisive experiment and a mathematical theory arrive together, but that the change is gradual, taking thirty years from the first papers to near-unanimity. The study is limited to counts of publications in the journals of Britain and France, to a classification by the author of this paper, and to the wave-particle debate, which was not the only question in optics; the counts measure attention, not the quality of the arguments.

Claim: the wave theory of light rose from 10.0 per cent of the optical literature in 1800-1804 to 96.9 per cent in 1845-1849, crossing the particle theory in 1815-1819. Method: the optical literature was searched for each five-year bin from 1800 to 1850, and each paper was classified as wave or particle by its stated theory; a sample of 50 papers was re-classified independently, with agreement on 46, or 92 per cent. Results: the wave counts were 2, 4, 6, 11, 16, 19, 23, 26, 28, and 31, a total of 166, and the particle counts were 18, 15, 12, 9, 6, 5, 3, 2, 1, and 1, a total of 72, so the grand total was 238 and the overall wave share was 69.7 per cent; the wave shares by bin were 10.0, 21.1, 33.3, 55.0, 72.7, 79.2, 88.5, 92.9, 96.6, and 96.9 per cent; the crossover bin was 1815-1819, with 11 wave against 9 particle. Limitations: the classification depends on the search and on the classifier's judgment, checked at 92 per cent agreement; the study counts papers, not influence, and it ends in 1850, before the ether itself was abandoned.

The wave counts by five-year bin were 2, 4, 6, 11, 16, 19, 23, 26, 28, and 31, a total of 166, and the particle counts were 18, 15, 12, 9, 6, 5, 3, 2, 1, and 1, a total of 72. The grand total is 166 + 72 = 238, and the overall wave share is 166 / 238 x 100 = 69.7 per cent. The wave share rose from 10.0 to 96.9 per cent, a rise of 86.9 points over 50 years, or 86.9 / 50 = 1.74 points per year. The wave count grew 31 / 2 = 15.5 times, and the particle count fell 18 / 1 = 18 times.
