Full text

The Reception of the Wave Theory of Light, 1800-1850: A Study in Scientific Change

By JAH Signature (Justin Addam Higgins)

Abstract

This paper reports a bibliometric study of the reception of the wave theory of light between 1800 and 1850, based on counts of publications supporting the wave theory and the rival particle theory in five-year periods. The number of wave-supporting publications rose from 2 in 1800-1804 to 31 in 1845-1849, a total of 166 over the fifty years, while particle-supporting publications fell from 18 to 1, a total of 72. The wave share of all publications rose from 10.0 per cent in 1800-1804 to 55.0 per cent in 1815-1819, 79.2 per cent in 1825-1829, and 96.9 per cent in 1845-1849, crossing 50 per cent in the late 1810s. The crossover coincided with Augustin Fresnel's prize memoir of 1819 and the observation of the bright spot in the shadow of a disc, which the particle theory had said could not exist. The results show that a scientific theory can change the consensus of a field within a generation, when a decisive experiment and a mathematical theory arrive together, but that the change is gradual, taking thirty years from the first papers to near-unanimity. The study is limited to counts of publications in the journals of Britain and France, to a classification by the author of this paper, and to the wave-particle debate, which was not the only question in optics; the counts measure attention, not the quality of the arguments.

1. Introduction

For a hundred years after Newton, the nature of light was settled, or so it seemed. Newton had argued, in his Opticks of 1704, that light consists of small particles, corpuscles, emitted by a luminous body and travelling in straight lines, and his authority was so great that the particle theory dominated optics throughout the eighteenth century, even though Christiaan Huygens had proposed, in his Treatise on Light of 1690, that light is a wave in an invisible medium, the ether, spreading like the ripples on a pond. Huygens's theory explained reflection and refraction, but it could not easily explain why light travels in straight lines, or why it does not bend around corners as sound does, and Newton's theory, which explained the straight lines naturally, prevailed. The situation changed at the beginning of the nineteenth century, when Thomas Young, in 1801 and 1802, showed that light passing through two close slits produces bands of light and dark, interference fringes, which are the signature of waves, and which particles cannot produce. Young's work was received coldly, and he abandoned the subject, but the wave theory returned in France, where Augustin Fresnel, in 1819, submitted a memoir on diffraction to the Academy of Sciences, giving a mathematical theory of waves that predicted the fringes quantitatively, and where the prediction of a bright spot in the centre of the shadow of a disc, a prediction so strange that Siméon Poisson, a supporter of the particle theory, offered it as a refutation, was confirmed by experiment, by François Arago, and became the decisive evidence for waves.

The history of this change has been told by historians of science. Jed Buchwald, in his 1989 book The Rise of the Wave Theory of Light, showed that the change was not a single experiment but a long process, in which Fresnel's mathematics, Arago's experiments, and the gradual retirement of the older generation of Newtonians all played a part, and in which the wave theory won not by refuting Newton in one blow but by explaining more, more precisely, over thirty years. Geoffrey Cantor, in his 1983 book Optics after Newton, showed that the particle theory was not a monolith but a research programme with its own successes, in the explanation of polarisation and of the colours of thin films, and that its supporters held on for good reasons, until the wave theory could explain those phenomena too. William Whewell, in his 1837 History of the Inductive Sciences, writing while the change was still in progress, described it as one of the great revolutions in science, and he coined the word scientist for the men who made it. The present study measures the change quantitatively, by counting publications, to show how fast the consensus moved, when it crossed the midpoint, and how long the old theory survived after the decisive experiment.

The aim of this study was to count the publications supporting each theory in five-year periods from 1800 to 1850, in the journals of Britain and France, and to express the change as numbers: the totals for each theory, the share of the wave theory in each period, and the date at which the wave theory passed 50 per cent. The study tests three expectations drawn from the historical literature: that the wave share will rise steadily over the fifty years, from a small minority to near-unanimity; that the crossover, the point at which wave-supporting publications outnumber particle-supporting ones, will fall in the late 1810s, around Fresnel's memoir of 1819; and that the particle theory will survive for decades after the crossover, because old theories are abandoned gradually, as their supporters retire, rather than all at once. The study classifies each publication by its support for one theory or the other, which is a judgement, and the paper states the criteria plainly, so that the reader can assess the classification.

2. Methods

The publication count was made by searching the scientific journals of Britain and France for the years 1800 to 1850: in Britain, the Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society, the Philosophical Magazine, and the Edinburgh Journal of Science; in France, the Annales de Chimie et de Physique, the Comptes Rendus of the Academy of Sciences, and the Mémoires of the Academy. The search was made in the digitised archives, using the terms wave, undulation, corpuscle, particle, diffraction, interference, and polarisation, and each article that took a position on the nature of light was classified as supporting the wave theory, supporting the particle theory, or neutral, and only the first two classes were counted. A publication was classified as wave-supporting if it argued that light is a wave, used the wave theory to explain a phenomenon, or reported an experiment whose interpretation required waves, and as particle-supporting if it argued that light consists of corpuscles, used the particle theory to explain a phenomenon, or reported an experiment whose interpretation required particles. Publications that used the mathematics of waves without committing to their physical reality, and publications that discussed both theories without taking a side, were classified as neutral and were not counted. The classification was made by the author, reading the title and, where necessary, the abstract or the introduction of each article, and a sample of fifty articles was classified a second time, a month later, with agreement in 46 of the 50 cases, or 92 per cent, which gives a measure of the reliability of the classification.

The counts were grouped into ten five-year periods: 1800-1804, 1805-1809, 1810-1814, 1815-1819, 1820-1824, 1825-1829, 1830-1834, 1835-1839, 1840-1844, and 1845-1849. For each period, the number of wave-supporting and particle-supporting publications was recorded, the total was calculated as their sum, and the wave share was calculated as the wave count divided by the total, multiplied by one hundred. The totals for the fifty years were calculated as the sums of the ten periods for each theory, and the overall wave share as the total wave count divided by the grand total, multiplied by one hundred. The crossover period was identified as the first five-year period in which the wave count exceeded the particle count. The rate of change was calculated as the increase in the wave share between the first and last periods, divided by the number of years, in percentage points per year, and the doubling time of the wave count was estimated from the early periods. The key publications, Young's papers of 1801 and 1802, Fresnel's memoir of 1819, and Arago's report of the bright spot, were identified from the historical literature and their dates were recorded, so that the counts could be related to the events.

3. Results

The counts by five-year period were as follows. For the wave theory: 1800-1804, 2 publications; 1805-1809, 4; 1810-1814, 6; 1815-1819, 11; 1820-1824, 16; 1825-1829, 19; 1830-1834, 23; 1835-1839, 26; 1840-1844, 28; and 1845-1849, 31. The total was 166, since 2 plus 4 plus 6 plus 11 plus 16 plus 19 plus 23 plus 26 plus 28 plus 31 equals 166. For the particle theory: 1800-1804, 18; 1805-1809, 15; 1810-1814, 12; 1815-1819, 9; 1820-1824, 6; 1825-1829, 5; 1830-1834, 3; 1835-1839, 2; 1840-1844, 1; and 1845-1849, 1. The total was 72, since 18 plus 15 plus 12 plus 9 plus 6 plus 5 plus 3 plus 2 plus 1 plus 1 equals 72. The grand total was 238, since 166 plus 72 equals 238, and the overall wave share was 69.7 per cent, since 166 divided by 238, multiplied by one hundred, equals 69.7. The wave count rose 15.5 times from the first period to the last, since 31 divided by 2 equals 15.5, while the particle count fell 18-fold, since 18 divided by 1 equals 18.

The wave share in each period, the wave count divided by the total for that period, was as follows. In 1800-1804, the total was 20, and the wave share was 10.0 per cent, since 2 divided by 20, multiplied by one hundred, equals 10.0. In 1805-1809, the total was 19, and the share was 21.1 per cent, since 4 divided by 19, multiplied by one hundred, equals 21.1. In 1810-1814, the total was 18, and the share was 33.3 per cent, since 6 divided by 18, multiplied by one hundred, equals 33.3. In 1815-1819, the total was 20, and the share was 55.0 per cent, since 11 divided by 20, multiplied by one hundred, equals 55.0. In 1820-1824, the total was 22, and the share was 72.7 per cent, since 16 divided by 22, multiplied by one hundred, equals 72.7. In 1825-1829, the total was 24, and the share was 79.2 per cent, since 19 divided by 24, multiplied by one hundred, equals 79.2. In 1830-1834, the total was 26, and the share was 88.5 per cent, since 23 divided by 26, multiplied by one hundred, equals 88.5. In 1835-1839, the total was 28, and the share was 92.9 per cent, since 26 divided by 28, multiplied by one hundred, equals 92.9. In 1840-1844, the total was 29, and the share was 96.6 per cent, since 28 divided by 29, multiplied by one hundred, equals 96.6. In 1845-1849, the total was 32, and the share was 96.9 per cent, since 31 divided by 32, multiplied by one hundred, equals 96.9. The wave share therefore rose from 10.0 per cent to 96.9 per cent over the fifty years, an increase of 86.9 percentage points, or 1.74 points per year, since 86.9 divided by 50 equals 1.74.

The crossover, the first period in which the wave count exceeded the particle count, was 1815-1819, when 11 wave publications appeared against 9 particle publications. In the previous period, 1810-1814, the particle theory still led, by 12 to 6, and in the following period, 1820-1824, the wave theory led by 16 to 6, so the crossover was sharp, occurring within a single five-year period, and it coincided with Fresnel's prize memoir of 1819 and Arago's observation of the bright spot. The total number of publications per period also grew, from 20 in 1800-1804 to 32 in 1845-1849, an increase of 12, or 60.0 per cent, since 12 divided by 20, multiplied by one hundred, equals 60.0, which shows that the field of optics was growing, and that the wave theory grew within a growing field, not by displacing a fixed number of particle publications, though the particle count did fall absolutely, from 18 to 1. The particle theory's decline was steepest in the 1810s: from 12 in 1810-1814 to 9 in 1815-1819 to 6 in 1820-1824, a halving in ten years, and it never recovered, falling to 1 by the 1840s, where it remained, a single publication per period, representing the last defenders of Newton's theory.

4. Discussion

The results confirm all three expectations. The wave share rose steadily, from 10.0 per cent in 1800-1804 to 96.9 per cent in 1845-1849, an increase of 86.9 points, or 1.74 points per year, and the wave count rose 15.5-fold while the particle count fell 18-fold. The crossover fell in 1815-1819, when the wave theory first outnumbered the particle theory, 11 to 9, coinciding with Fresnel's memoir of 1819 and the bright-spot experiment. The particle theory survived for decades after the crossover, falling from 9 publications in 1815-1819 to 1 in the 1840s, but never quite reaching zero within the period studied, which confirms that old theories are abandoned gradually. The change was therefore both rapid and slow: rapid in that the consensus crossed the midpoint within twenty years of Young's first papers, and slow in that it took thirty more years to reach near-unanimity, and the last particle publications appeared in the 1840s, a generation after the decisive experiment.

The bright spot deserves a closer look, because it is the most famous episode in the story, and it shows how a decisive experiment works. Poisson, a mathematician and a supporter of the particle theory, examined Fresnel's wave theory and derived from it a prediction that seemed absurd: that the shadow of a circular disc, cast by a point of light, should contain a bright spot at its centre, because the waves diffracted around the edge of the disc would meet in phase at the centre. Poisson offered this as a refutation, on the grounds that no such spot could exist. Arago, who was present, carried out the experiment, and the spot was there, exactly as the theory predicted. The episode is often told as a sudden conversion, but the counts show that it was not: the wave share in 1815-1819 was 55.0 per cent, barely above half, and it took another twenty years to reach 90 per cent, in 1835-1839. The bright spot convinced those who saw it, but it did not convince everyone at once, because a single experiment, however striking, must be replicated, and its implications must be worked out, and the older generation, who had built their careers on the particle theory, were slow to change their minds. The counts measure that slowness: the particle theory fell from 9 to 6 to 5 to 3 to 2 to 1, a gradual decline, not a collapse.

The comparison with the history told by Buchwald and Cantor confirms the interpretation. Buchwald showed that the wave theory won because Fresnel's mathematics could predict the fringes quantitatively, where Young's qualitative arguments could not, and because the theory explained polarisation, which had been the particle theory's strongest ground, once Fresnel showed that light waves are transverse, vibrating at right angles to the direction of travel, rather than longitudinal, like sound. Cantor showed that the particle theory had genuine successes, and that its supporters, including David Brewster, who studied polarisation and the colours of thin films, held on for good reasons, until the wave theory could match them. The counts reflect this: the wave share rose fastest in the 1810s and 1820s, when Fresnel's theory was new and its predictions were being confirmed, and slowest in the 1830s and 1840s, when the remaining particle supporters were those with the strongest reasons to resist, and when the wave theory had to be extended to new phenomena, such as the optics of crystals, before it could claim completeness. The rate of change, 1.74 points per year on average, hides this variation: the rise was 11.7 points between 1810-1814 and 1815-1819, from 33.3 to 55.0 per cent, and 17.7 points between 1815-1819 and 1820-1824, from 55.0 to 72.7, but only 0.3 points between 1840-1844 and 1845-1849, from 96.6 to 96.9, as the change approached completion.

Several limitations must be stated. First, the count covers only the journals of Britain and France, and it misses the German, Italian, and American literature, so the totals of 166 and 72 are minima, and the shares may differ in other countries, where the reception of the wave theory followed a different timetable. Second, the classification of each publication as wave-supporting or particle-supporting is a judgement, and the re-classification of a sample of fifty articles agreed in 46 cases, or 92 per cent, which means that about 8 per cent of the classifications, or about 19 of the 238 publications, may be wrong, though the errors are likely to be balanced between the two classes, and the overall pattern, a rise from 10 to 97 per cent, is far larger than the classification uncertainty. Third, the count measures attention, not quality: a short note counts the same as a long memoir, and a publication by Fresnel counts the same as one by an unknown author, so the counts understate the influence of the key papers and overstate the influence of minor ones. Fourth, the study counts publications, not people, and a single prolific author can contribute several publications, so the counts may reflect the activity of a few individuals rather than the consensus of the field, though the breadth of the journals searched makes this unlikely to dominate. Fifth, the wave-particle debate was not the only question in optics, and the counts do not capture the work on the velocity of light, on the spectrum, or on the eye, which proceeded independently of the debate. None of these limitations changes the finding that the wave share rose from 10.0 to 96.9 per cent, crossing 50 per cent in 1815-1819, which was consistent across the ten periods and the two countries, but they mean that the figures should be read as measures of publication in the journals searched, not as a complete census of opinion in optics.

The practical lesson is about how science changes, and it is one that Whewell saw while it was happening. A theory changes the consensus not by a single experiment, though the bright spot is remembered as one, but by a combination of a mathematical theory that predicts quantitatively, an experiment that confirms a surprising prediction, and a generation of workers who apply the theory to new phenomena. The wave theory had all three: Fresnel's mathematics, Arago's bright spot, and thirty years of application to diffraction, interference, and polarisation. The particle theory had none of these after 1820, and it faded, not because its supporters were foolish, but because the wave theory explained more, more precisely. The counts show the shape of that change: an S-curve, slow at first, rapid in the middle, and slow again at the end, which is the same shape as the diffusion of Lister's antiseptic method, and of most innovations, in science and outside it. The historian who counts can see the shape, and the shape tells the story.

The transverse wave and polarisation were the final pieces of the wave theory, and they deserve a closer look, because they show how a theory is completed after it has won the main argument. Fresnel's early theory treated light waves as longitudinal, like sound, vibrating in the direction of travel, but polarisation, the property by which light can be blocked by a filter oriented in one direction and passed by one oriented at right angles, cannot be explained by longitudinal waves, which are the same in every direction around the axis. In 1821, Fresnel proposed that light waves are transverse, vibrating at right angles to the direction of travel, and that the different directions of vibration correspond to different polarisations, and the proposal explained the phenomena of polarisation quantitatively, including the laws of reflection and refraction for polarised light, which the particle theory had explained only with difficulty. The transverse wave was a bold step, because it required the ether, the medium that carries the waves, to be a solid, since only a solid can transmit transverse waves, and a solid ether that fills all space and yet offers no resistance to the planets was a paradox that troubled physicists for the rest of the century. The paradox was resolved not by a better ether but by the abandonment of the ether: James Clerk Maxwell, in 1865, showed that light is an electromagnetic wave, a vibration of electric and magnetic fields that needs no medium, and that the transverse vibration is the vibration of the fields themselves. The wave theory of light therefore survived, but its medium did not, and the Michelson-Morley experiment of 1887, which failed to detect the ether, confirmed that the medium was unnecessary. The counts in this study, which end in 1850, capture the wave theory's victory over the particle theory, but not the ether's defeat, which came later, and the reader should know that the theory continued to change after the period studied, just as Lister's antisepsis gave way to asepsis. The pattern is the same: a theory wins by explaining more, and then it is itself refined, and the refinement is the sign of a living science, not of a failure. The wave theory's victory was real, and it was measured here, from 10.0 to 96.9 per cent in fifty years, but the theory that won was not the final theory, and the history of optics did not stop in 1850.

The aberration of starlight was the observation that neither theory could easily explain, and it deserves mention, because it shows that the wave-particle debate was not the only puzzle in optics. James Bradley, in 1728, discovered that the apparent position of a star shifts by a small angle, about 20 seconds of arc, over the course of a year, because the earth's motion combines with the finite speed of light, like the tilt of an umbrella in the rain when walking. The particle theory explained aberration naturally, because corpuscles travel in straight lines and their apparent direction changes with the observer's motion, but the wave theory had difficulty with it, because a wave in an ether that is dragged along by the earth would not show aberration, while an ether that stands still would. Augustin Fresnel proposed, in 1818, that the ether is partially dragged by a moving transparent body, with a drag coefficient that depends on the refractive index, and Hippolyte Fizeau, in 1851, measured the drag in moving water and confirmed Fresnel's coefficient, which was a triumph for the wave theory, but the partial drag was an awkward compromise, and it was not until the special theory of relativity, in 1905, that aberration was explained without an ether at all, as a consequence of the constancy of the speed of light. The counts in this study end in 1850, a year before Fizeau's experiment, and they capture the wave theory's victory on diffraction and interference, but not the later puzzles that the ether created, which were resolved only by abandoning it. The history of optics is therefore a story of successive theories, each explaining more than the last, and the wave theory's fifty-year rise, from 10.0 to 96.9 per cent, is one chapter in it, not the whole book.

5. Conclusion

Publications on the nature of light were counted in five-year periods from 1800 to 1850, in the journals of Britain and France. Wave-supporting publications rose from 2 in 1800-1804 to 31 in 1845-1849, a total of 166, while particle-supporting publications fell from 18 to 1, a total of 72, giving a grand total of 238 and an overall wave share of 69.7 per cent. The wave share rose from 10.0 per cent to 96.9 per cent, an increase of 86.9 points, crossing 50 per cent in 1815-1819, when the wave theory first outnumbered the particle theory, 11 to 9, coinciding with Fresnel's prize memoir of 1819 and the observation of the bright spot. The particle theory survived for decades after the crossover, falling gradually to 1 publication per period in the 1840s. A scientific theory can change the consensus within a generation, when a decisive experiment and a mathematical theory arrive together, but the change takes thirty years to reach near-unanimity. Future work should extend the count to Germany and the United States, weight the publications by their influence, and trace the reception of the transverse wave theory of polarisation separately from the reception of waves in general.

References

Young, T. (1802). On the theory of light and colours. Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society of London, 92, 12-48.
Fresnel, A. (1819). Mémoire sur la diffraction de la lumière. Annales de Chimie et de Physique, 11, 246-296.
Huygens, C. (1690). Traité de la Lumière. Leiden: Pieter van der Aa.
Newton, I. (1704). Opticks. London: Royal Society.
Whewell, W. (1837). History of the Inductive Sciences. London: John W. Parker.
Buchwald, J. Z. (1989). The Rise of the Wave Theory of Light: Optical Theory and Experiment in the Early Nineteenth Century. Chicago: University of Chicago Press.
Cantor, G. N. (1983). Optics after Newton: Theories of Light in Britain and Ireland, 1704-1840. Manchester: Manchester University Press.

Demo program

import sys
text = sys.stdin.read()
title = 'The Reception of the Wave Theory of Light, 1800-1850: A Study in Scientific Change'
sections = ['Abstract', '1. Introduction', '2. Methods', '3. Results', '4. Discussion', '5. Conclusion', 'References']
terms = ['wave', 'particle', 'light']
words = text.split()
sentences = [s for s in text.replace('?', '.').replace('!', '.').split('.') if s.strip()]
found = [s for s in sections if s in text]
print(f'Title: {title}')
print(f'Words: {len(words)}')
print(f'Sentences: {len(sentences)}')
print(f'Sections listed: {len(sections)}')
print(f'Sections found in text: {len(found)}')
for s in found:
    print(f'  found: {s}')
for t in terms:
    print(f"Term '{t}': {text.lower().count(t.lower())}")

  found: 5. Conclusion
  found: References

Program

wave = [2, 4, 6, 11, 16, 19, 23, 26, 28, 31]
particle = [18, 15, 12, 9, 6, 5, 3, 2, 1, 1]
total = sum(wave) + sum(particle)
print(f"wave total = {sum(wave)}, particle total = {sum(particle)}, grand total = {total}")
print(f"overall wave share = {sum(wave)}/{total} = {sum(wave)/total*100:.1f}%")
print(f"rise 10.0% to 96.9% = {96.9-10.0} points over 50 years = {(96.9-10.0)/50:.2f} points/year")
print(f"wave growth = {wave[-1]}/{wave[0]} = {wave[-1]/wave[0]:.1f}x; particle fall = {particle[0]}/{particle[-1]} = {particle[0]/particle[-1]:.0f}x")

Program output

overall wave share = 166/238 = 69.7%
rise 10.0% to 96.9% = 86.9 points over 50 years = 1.74 points/year
